Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative position: you challenge the common saying “crime does not pay” and argue that, in America, crime can bring profit, fame, or other benefits. This is a strong and interesting thesis, and your essay uses several examples to support it. The essay is also organized into introduction, body paragraphs, and conclusion, which gives it a clear structure. For revision, focus on making your argument more precise, improving grammar and word choice, and strengthening paragraph unity.

Strengths

Clear position
Your thesis is easy to identify: “crime can be a profitable profession.”
You consistently support this idea throughout the essay.

Logical organization
The essay moves from history, to money, to punishment/consequences.
Each body paragraph has its own main focus.

Use of examples
You include historical and contemporary examples, which helps make the essay interesting and persuasive.

Main Areas to Improve

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your thesis is strong, but “profitable profession” sounds too broad and slightly informal. Also, some examples do not show actual “profit”; they show fame, political influence, or public approval.

Possible revision:
“Although people often say that crime does not pay, in the United States some crimes have brought offenders money, fame, or political advantage.”

This version matches your body paragraphs more accurately.

B. Improve paragraph unity and topic sentences
Some paragraphs include examples that do not fully match the paragraph’s main point.

For example, in the history paragraph, the Boston Tea Party and Benedict Arnold are very different cases. One became celebrated as political resistance; the other is still widely viewed as treason. Because of this, the paragraph feels less unified.

Suggestion:
Either focus the paragraph on how some illegal acts are later celebrated,
or focus on how criminals can profit socially or financially over time.

A clearer topic sentence would help:
“History shows that some illegal acts are later celebrated or rewarded rather than condemned.”

C. Use stronger reasoning, not only examples
You give many examples, but sometimes the explanation is too short. After each example, explain clearly how it proves your point.

For instance:
Why does the Boston Tea Party show that crime pays?
Does it show financial profit, political success, or historical approval?

Add 1–2 sentences of analysis after important examples.

Grammar and Language Issues

A. Sentence structure and punctuation
Several sentences are run-on sentences or contain comma splices.

Example:
“No one can argue that crime is not a means of making money, if it were not why would people commit them.”
  
Better:
“No one can argue that crime is not a means of making money. If it were not, why would people commit it?”

B. Word choice
Some word choices are awkward or incorrect:

“The vents shaping America's early history” → “the events shaping America’s early history”
“speak-eases” → “speakeasies”
“Los Vegas” → “Las Vegas”
“convicted killers autobiographies” → “convicted killers’ autobiographies”
“should they become arrested again” → “if they are arrested again”
“ever present laws” → “existing laws” or simply “the law”

C. Grammar accuracy
Watch for:
Subject-verb agreement: “crime is a recurrent theme”
Articles: “the monetary rewards,” “a person”
Plural/singular consistency: “people commit them” → if “crime” is singular, use “it”
Possessives: “killers’ autobiographies”

D. Tone and formality
Some phrases sound too conversational or exaggerated:
“the right agent”
“make a buck”
“movie of the week”

These expressions can work in informal writing, but in an academic essay you should use a more formal tone.

Content Concerns

Be careful with accuracy and fairness. For example:
O.J. Simpson is used as an example of profit from crime, but your wording suggests guilt before the legal decision. In argumentative writing, this can weaken credibility.
Some claims are too broad, such as “statistics rise continuously.” You should provide evidence or use more cautious language.

Try phrases like:
“in some cases”
“can lead to”
“has sometimes allowed”

This makes your argument sound more reasonable and academic.

Revision Priorities

Rewrite the thesis so it matches your three body paragraphs.
Strengthen topic sentences and keep each paragraph focused on one main idea.
Fix grammar and punctuation, especially run-on sentences.
Use more formal vocabulary.
Add analysis after examples to explain exactly how they support your argument.

Final Comment

This essay has a clear argument and strong energy. You are not afraid to take a position, which is excellent for argumentative writing. Your next step is to make the essay more academically convincing by improving precision, sentence control, and logical explanation. With revision, this can become a much stronger and more polished essay.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0013.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you believe women should have choices, but you are critical of some feminist ideas and of pressure on women to reject traditional roles. This gives your essay a strong viewpoint, which is important in argumentative writing. You also try to discuss several areas—work, military service, harassment, family life—which shows effort to consider the issue broadly.

To improve the essay, focus on (1) a clearer thesis, (2) stronger evidence and logic, (3) more careful word choice, and (4) grammar and sentence accuracy.

Clarify your main argument
At the moment, your argument changes direction several times. For example, you begin by agreeing that women faced unfair treatment, then you criticize feminists, then you argue for traditional roles, and finally you say women should be equal. These ideas can fit together, but you need a clear central claim.

For example, your thesis could be something like:
*Although feminism has helped women gain important rights, women should still be free to choose traditional or nontraditional roles without social pressure.*
This is more precise than saying feminists are “crazy” or that women can only do what “nature intended.”

Use evidence, not only personal opinion
A major weakness is that many claims are unsupported or too general. In academic argument, readers need facts, examples, or reliable evidence.

Examples of unsupported claims:
“Women just can't fight in a war very well.”
“Many employers are the victims.”
“Divorce is at fifty percent and rising, women are having more heart attacks… and kids are being more neglected.”

These are big claims, but you do not provide proof. Also, some statements may sound biased or offensive because they are presented as facts without support.

Instead:
explain *why* you think some jobs may have different physical requirements;
acknowledge exceptions;
use neutral language;
include evidence if possible.

For example:
Instead of “Women just can't fight in a war very well,” write:  
  *Some people argue that combat roles require physical standards that may be more difficult for some women and some men to meet; therefore, selection should be based on ability rather than gender alone.*

This sounds more academic and logical.

Avoid overgeneralization and emotional language
Some sentences are too absolute:
“all women have to go along with these feminists rules”
“women get looked at differently”
“traditional roles were a good thing”

These need qualification. Use words like some, many, in some cases, often, may, can.

Also avoid emotional or insulting words such as:
“crazy”

Academic writing should sound calm and respectful, especially on sensitive topics.

Improve organization
Each body paragraph should have one main idea. Right now, some paragraphs include many different points.

A possible structure:
Introduction: background + thesis  
Paragraph 1: historical discrimination against women  
Paragraph 2: positive contributions of feminism  
Paragraph 3: pressure some women may feel to reject traditional roles  
Paragraph 4: workplace equality and differences in job preference/requirements  
Conclusion: restate your position clearly

This structure would make your essay easier to follow.

Grammar and language corrections
There are many language errors, but most are understandable. Here are some important ones:

societies role → *society’s role*
work place → *workplace*
verses → *versus*
their piers → *their peers*
The are not strong enough → *They are not strong enough*
at war → better: *in wartime* or *during war*
being more stressful → *experiencing more stress*
should be created equal → *should be treated as equal* or *should be considered equal*
they to do → *them to do*

Also watch sentence structure. Example:
Original: “Gone is the age that the woman is required to stay home.”
Better: *The time when women were expected to stay home has largely passed.*

Be careful with logic
Some arguments need stronger reasoning. For example:
“God would have created us all the same if this was not true.”
This is a belief-based argument, but in academic essays, it is usually better to rely on social reasoning, evidence, or policy arguments unless the assignment specifically invites religious reasoning.

Also, saying women should be equal but can only do what “nature intended” creates confusion. Equal rights and different average abilities are not necessarily opposites, but you need to explain that distinction carefully.

Final advice
Your essay shows that you have strong ideas and are willing to take a position. That is a good starting point. For revision, try to:
write one clear thesis,
use respectful, formal language,
support claims with evidence,
avoid broad generalizations,
improve grammar and word choice.

If you revise with these points in mind, your essay will become much more persuasive and academically effective.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0014.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you strongly believe that crime leads to worse consequences, both morally and legally. This is a good foundation for an argumentative essay because your opinion is consistent throughout. You also try to support your claim with explanation and a detailed example, which shows good effort in developing ideas.

What you do well

Clear viewpoint
Your main argument is easy to understand: crime may seem rewarding at first, but it leads to punishment and escalation.

Strong sense of voice
The essay sounds personal and confident. Phrases such as “I am a true believer” give your writing energy.

Use of example
The robbery story helps illustrate your point that a “small” crime can become much more serious. Using examples is an effective argumentative strategy.

---

Main areas to improve

Make the essay more formal

Your current style is too conversational for academic writing. Expressions like “a cool million,” “run like hell,” “guess what happens,” and “Sounds like trouble to me” sound like speech, not formal essay writing.

Try:
Replace informal phrases with neutral academic language.
Avoid speaking directly to the reader too often.

Example:
Informal: “if I robbed a bank for about a cool million”
More formal: “if someone robbed a bank and stole a large amount of money”

---

Improve organization

Right now, the essay feels like one long train of thought. Your ideas would be stronger if you divided them more clearly.

Suggested structure:
Introduction
State your main argument clearly.

Body Paragraph 1
Explain how crime can become habitual when a person is not caught.

Body Paragraph 2
Explain how punishment depends on the seriousness of the crime.

Body Paragraph 3
Use the robbery example to show how a crime can escalate and harm innocent people.

Conclusion
Restate your argument and explain why crime ultimately does not pay.

Also, your third paragraph is extremely long. Break it into smaller paragraphs so the reader can follow the story more easily.

---

State your thesis more directly

Your introduction has interesting ideas, but the main claim should be clearer and more concise.

For example:
“Crime does not pay because even if it brings temporary benefits, it often leads to harsher crimes, harm to others, and serious legal punishment.”

This gives your essay a strong academic thesis.

---

Focus on grammar and word choice

There are frequent grammar, spelling, and word-form errors. Some common issues:

a. Spelling
committs → *commits*
indivudual → *individual*
deterent → *deterrent*
whrn → *when*
strats → *starts*
hurridly → *hurriedly*
ths → *this*
persistant → *persistent*
simply robbery → *simple robbery*

b. Apostrophes and possessives
Its's → *It’s* or *Its* depending on meaning  
Here you want “It’s” (= it is).

c. Sentence structure
Many sentences are too long and contain too many ideas. This creates run-on sentences and makes meaning unclear.

Example from your first paragraph:  
“A person that commits a crime will probably reap the benefits…”  
This sentence continues too long. Break it into two or three shorter sentences.

d. Relative pronouns
You often use “a person that” or “someone that.” In formal writing, “who” is better for people.

“A person who commits a crime…”

---

Be careful with logic and repetition

Some ideas are repeated several times, especially the idea that criminals keep going if they are not caught. Try to say each point once, clearly and effectively.

Also, your hypothetical example is vivid, but it is too long for one paragraph. The reader may lose sight of the argument. After telling the example, connect it more directly to your thesis.

For example:
“This example shows that even a robbery intended for quick profit can escalate into violence, injury, and multiple criminal charges.”

This kind of sentence strengthens analysis.

---

Add a conclusion

Your essay ends suddenly. A conclusion is important because it gives closure and reinforces your argument.

You could write something like:
“In conclusion, crime may seem beneficial in the short term, but it often leads to repeated offending, greater harm, and severe punishment. For these reasons, crime does not pay.”

---

Final advice for revision

When you revise, focus on these priorities:

Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Divide the essay into clear paragraphs.
Shorten long sentences.
Replace informal expressions with academic language.
Correct spelling and grammar errors.
Add a strong conclusion.

You have strong ideas and a clear opinion. The next step is to present them in a more organized, formal, and grammatically accurate way.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0015.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a strong central idea: money itself is neutral, but people’s attitudes toward it can lead to evil. This is a solid argumentative claim, and your writing shows good abstract thinking. You also use contrast effectively, especially when you explain that money can provide survival, status, and opportunity, but also create harm. Below are some suggestions to help you revise.

Strengthen your thesis
At the moment, your essay begins with the famous saying “Money is the root of all evil,” but later your actual argument becomes more nuanced: money is not evil in itself; greed and unhealthy desire are the real causes of evil. This second idea is stronger and more precise.

You could revise your thesis to make it clearer from the beginning. For example:

Although people often say that money is the root of all evil, money itself is neutral; it becomes harmful only when people are controlled by greed, addiction, or the desire for power and status.

This would help the reader understand your true argument right away.

Develop your argument with more specific support
Your ideas are interesting, but they remain somewhat general. To make your essay more persuasive, add examples or brief real-life situations.

For instance:
When you discuss survival, give an example of how money provides food, housing, or healthcare.
When you discuss status, mention luxury brands, expensive education, or political influence.
When you discuss greed, give an example of fraud, corruption, or exploitation.

Instead of only saying that greed causes evil, show how it does so.

Improve organization and completeness
Your essay has a beginning and middle, but the ending feels too brief. The last sentence introduces an important idea, but it does not fully conclude the discussion.

A stronger structure would be:
Introduction: present the saying and your revised thesis.
Body paragraph 1: explain money as power and necessity.
Body paragraph 2: explain how attitudes toward money can become destructive.
Body paragraph 3: explain greed and ethical consequences, with examples.
Conclusion: restate that money is neutral, while human desire determines whether it leads to good or evil.

Your conclusion should not introduce a new idea; instead, it should summarize your main points and reinforce your argument.

Work on sentence grammar and word choice
There are several language issues that make the essay sound less natural in English. Below are some important ones.

Article and phrase corrections
“Money is the root of all evil is an ancient saying”  
  Better: “‘Money is the root of all evil’ is an ancient saying.”  
  Use quotation marks because this is a quoted saying.

“The key is in the definition of term money”  
  Better: “The key lies in the definition of the term ‘money.’”

“Ones’s attitude”  
  Correct: “One’s attitude”

“Money of itself”  
  More natural: “Money in itself”

“when” in the third paragraph should be capitalized: “When”

Word form and grammar
“Values and ethics play very little importance”  
  Better: “Values and ethics play a very small role”  
  or “Values and ethics are of little importance”

“in structure of a greedy persons life”  
  Better: “in the structure of a greedy person’s life”

“Money does good or evil”  
  Better: “Money can be used for good or evil”

Improve punctuation and sentence control
Some of your sentences are too long or joined incorrectly. This can confuse the reader.

Example:
“Enough money gives you the power to get whatever you need to survive, lack of money may mean you can't survive.”
  
This is a comma splice. Better versions:
“Enough money gives you the power to get what you need to survive, while a lack of money may mean that you cannot survive.”
Or split into two sentences.

Also, be careful with dashes. They can be effective, but too many can make the writing seem informal.

Tone and academic style
Your style is thoughtful, but to sound more academic, try to avoid overly absolute statements such as:
“The list of addictions people develop are endless”
Better:
“People can develop many kinds of addictions”

Also, some examples like “prostitution” may sound abrupt if not explained. In academic writing, it is often better to use broader terms like “exploitative or self-destructive behaviors.”

A possible revised main idea
Your essay would become much stronger if it focused on this:
money is a tool;
people need it for survival and social participation;
however, obsession with money can lead to greed, moral compromise, and harm;
therefore, evil comes not from money itself but from human misuse of it.

Final advice
You already have a meaningful argument and good insight. For revision, focus on:
making your thesis more precise,
adding specific examples,
improving organization,
correcting grammar and punctuation,
using a more formal academic style.

Your essay has a strong foundation; with clearer structure and more careful language, it can become much more convincing.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0016.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear personal voice and a strong opinion, which is useful in argumentative writing. The reader can easily understand your main concern: you believe some effects of feminism have negatively changed dating, courtship, and chivalry. You also make an effort to acknowledge the positive achievements of women’s rights, which is good because it shows your argument is not one-sided. For revision, the main areas to improve are thesis clarity, organization, evidence, tone, and sentence control.

Strengths
Clear topic: The essay focuses on feminism, dating, and chivalry.
Personal engagement: Your own experience gives the essay energy and authenticity.
Some balance: You recognize important feminist gains such as equal pay and career opportunities for women.

Main issue: make the thesis more precise
Right now, your central argument is present, but it is not stated in a sharp academic way. The essay sometimes sounds like a personal complaint rather than a clearly reasoned claim.

Your main point seems to be:
feminism has brought important social benefits,
but it has also created confusion in modern dating,
and as a result, traditional chivalry has declined.

Try writing a thesis like this:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Although feminism has helped women gain political, economic, and professional equality, it has also changed expectations in dating, leading some men and women to feel uncertain about traditional forms of courtship and chivalry.*

This version is more neutral and argumentative.

Improve organization
At the moment, some paragraphs are long and contain several ideas at once. For a stronger essay, organize it more clearly.

A possible structure:
Introduction  
   Introduce the topic and present your thesis.
Paragraph 1: Positive effects of feminism  
   Equal pay, career choice, breaking the glass ceiling, voting rights.
Paragraph 2: Changes in dating norms  
   Explain how expectations about paying, opening doors, and physical behavior have changed.
Paragraph 3: Your argument about confusion and loss of chivalry  
   Show why you think these changes have created uncertainty.
Conclusion  
   Restate your position and suggest a balanced view of equality and respectful courtship.

This structure will make your logic easier to follow.

Use stronger evidence
The essay depends mostly on personal experience and what male friends told you. Personal examples are helpful, but in academic argument, they are usually not enough by themselves.

You could strengthen your essay by adding:
research on changes in dating culture,
statistics about gender roles or household labor,
expert opinions on feminism and social expectations,
examples showing how ideas of chivalry differ across generations.

Without evidence, some claims sound too broad, for example:
“Men were gentlemen in the 60’s”
“women in general are offended”
“feminists” caused chivalry to die

These are very general statements. Be careful: many readers may question them. Try using softer and more precise language:
“Some men may feel less certain about dating expectations today.”
“Some women may prefer equality in dating rather than traditional gestures.”
“In my experience, dating norms seem less clear now than in earlier decades.”

Tone and word choice
For academic writing, some phrases sound too emotional or informal:
“Believe me”
“this is bunk”
“you men out there”
“Miss Susie Homemaker”

These expressions make the essay sound conversational, not academic. Try replacing them with more formal language.

Also, be careful with sensitive topics like “date rape” and “sexual harassment.” The essay currently presents them in a way that may seem dismissive. If you mention them, do so carefully and respectfully.

Sentence-level issues
Some sentences are too long and contain too many ideas. This makes them difficult to read. Break long sentences into shorter ones.

Example:
Original:  
“While I think that there are always exceptions to this rule, whether you are in the 60's or the 90's, however, the overall general attitude of men towards women was one of appreciation…”

Improved:  
“Of course, there were exceptions in both the 1960s and the 1990s. However, I believe that many men in the 1960s showed women appreciation and respect.”

Other language points:
Use “dating” instead of “the realm of date.”
Use “stigma” instead of “stigmatism.”
Avoid repeated wording such as “I have experienced dating… dating…”
Be consistent with time forms: 1960s and 1990s, not 60’s and 90’s in formal writing.

Final advice
As you revise, focus on this goal: turn a personal opinion into a well-supported academic argument. Keep your central idea, but express it more carefully, more precisely, and with evidence.

Revision checklist
Write a clear, one-sentence thesis.
Divide the essay into logical body paragraphs.
Replace broad generalizations with specific, careful claims.
Add outside evidence, not only personal experience.
Use a more formal and respectful tone.
Shorten long sentences and correct word choice.

You have a strong starting position and a clear voice. With better organization and a more academic style, this essay can become much more persuasive.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0017.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay shows a clear understanding of the topic and presents a consistent main idea: crime has serious costs for both criminals and society. You also organize the essay by discussing different kinds of “costs,” which is a good strategy for an argumentative essay. Below are suggestions to help you revise it into a stronger, clearer paper.

Main strength: clear focus
The essay stays focused on one central argument: crime does not pay because it causes financial, emotional, and social loss. This is effective. Several body paragraphs support this idea from different angles:
cost to victims and society
cost of imprisonment
emotional/moral cost to the criminal
effect on property values
long-term consequences after conviction

This gives your essay a solid foundation.

Biggest issue: unclear thesis and introduction
The introduction begins with “The phrase ,” but the phrase itself is missing. I assume you mean “crime does not pay.” You should state this directly. Also, your introduction is a little general and repetitive.

Suggested revision idea:
> The phrase “crime does not pay” is often repeated, but its meaning is sometimes overlooked. Crime brings serious financial, emotional, and social consequences not only for victims, but also for criminals, their families, and the wider community.

This version clearly introduces the topic and gives the reader your main argument.

Develop the argument more logically
Some paragraphs have good ideas, but the reasoning is sometimes too broad or not fully explained.

Example:
You write that incarceration costs money and that it comes from “family, friends and acquaintances in the form of tax dollars.” This is not fully accurate, because taxes are paid by the public, not specifically by the criminal’s friends and acquaintances. A clearer version would be:
> The cost of imprisoning criminals is often paid by taxpayers, which means society bears part of the financial burden of crime.

Also, in the paragraph about remorse, you assume that “for the most part” criminals feel sorrow. This may be too general. In argument writing, avoid claims that seem impossible to prove. You could soften the statement:
> In many cases, criminals may also suffer emotional consequences, such as guilt, shame, or regret.

Improve paragraph structure
Each body paragraph should have:
a clear topic sentence,
explanation,
possibly an example,
a link back to the thesis.

For example, your paragraph about property values could be expanded:
Topic sentence: crime harms law-abiding citizens financially.
Explanation: high-crime areas become less desirable.
Result: property values fall and homeowners lose money.
Link: therefore, crime affects even people who do not commit it.

Right now, some paragraphs are very short and feel underdeveloped.

Add a conclusion
Your essay ends suddenly. A strong argumentative essay needs a conclusion that summarizes the main points and reinforces the thesis.

Possible conclusion:
> In conclusion, crime does not pay because its consequences extend far beyond the criminal act itself. It causes financial loss, emotional suffering, and lasting damage to individuals and communities. For these reasons, the phrase remains an important truth.

Language and grammar corrections
There are frequent grammar, spelling, and punctuation errors. These do not make your essay impossible to understand, but they reduce its academic quality.

Common problems:
a. Subject-verb agreement
“have you ever actually thought” is correct, but:
“crime actually costs money” is better than “in the world crime actually costs money”
“so to will” → so too will

b. Word choice
“to often overlooked” → too often overlooked
“speak to other people we hear stories” → needs punctuation or restructuring
“real estate possessions” sounds unnatural; better: property or homes

c. Spelling
“freinds” → friends
“acquantances” → acquaintances
“oppurtunity” → opportunity
“their is no escape” → there is no escape

d. Capitalization and punctuation
“so even though…” should begin with So
Many sentences are run-on sentences. Add commas or split them into shorter sentences.

Style suggestions
Try to use a more formal academic style.
Avoid rhetorical questions in the introduction unless used very carefully.
Avoid repetition of “his or her” and “the individual.” You can sometimes use people, offenders, or rewrite in plural form.
Be careful with absolute statements such as “for the rest of their lives” unless you really want to argue that strongly.

Revision priorities
When revising, focus on these steps:
Insert the missing phrase: “crime does not pay.”
Rewrite the introduction with a clear thesis.
Strengthen each body paragraph with clearer logic and examples.
Add a conclusion.
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation errors.

Overall, your essay has a clear central idea and several relevant supporting points. With better organization, more precise language, and careful editing, it can become a much stronger argumentative essay.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0018.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and strong personal engagement. The contrast between “unorganized crime” and “organized crime” gives the paper a structure, and your interest in the subject is obvious. To revise effectively, focus on argument, evidence, tone, and organization.

Main issue: unclear thesis and purpose
At the moment, the essay seems to ask whether crime “pays,” but your exact position is not stated clearly at the beginning. You suggest that unorganized crime does not pay, while organized crime does, but this argument should be presented directly in the introduction.

What to revise
End the introduction with a clear thesis, for example:  
  “Although impulsive individual crimes often lead to quick arrest and limited profit, organized crime is more financially successful because it is planned, cooperative, and often protected by corruption.”
Remove sentences that do not support the thesis, such as your personal biography in the last paragraph.

Stronger academic tone needed
The essay currently sounds very personal and conversational. Phrases like “save the best for last,” “MONEY!,” “BIG sum of money,” and “figure me out” weaken the academic style. The final self-introduction is especially inappropriate for an argumentative essay.

What to revise
Reduce first-person comments unless they directly help the argument.
Avoid emotional or exaggerated language.
Use a formal tone:  
  instead of “These criminals just are not that good,” write  
  “These offenders are often more likely to be identified because their crimes are less carefully planned.”

Evidence and support are too weak
Many claims are broad but not supported with credible evidence. For example:
“The police and other government officials are willing to overlook the illegal acts because of the payoff…”
“In the United States, the average amount of money spent on illegal gambling alone is $30 per person.”

These are serious claims and need sources. In academic argument, your reader must see how you know something.

What to revise
Add reliable evidence from research, course materials, or statistics.
Explain the evidence instead of only stating it.
If you cannot support a claim, soften it:  
  “In some cases, corruption allows organized crime to continue operating.”

Problems with logic and definitions
Your definitions of “unorganized crime” and “organized crime” need more precision. Also, calling robbery, assault, rape, and theft “petty offenses” is inaccurate. Assault and rape are serious violent crimes, not petty crimes. This weakens your credibility.

What to revise
Define the two categories more carefully.
Distinguish between spontaneous/individual crime and organized/group-based crime.
Do not assume that people who commit non-organized crimes are “less educated” or only acting for survival unless you have evidence.

Organization can be improved
Your essay has a basic structure, but some paragraphs mix definition, opinion, and conclusion without clear transitions. The final paragraph suddenly shifts into personal information, which does not belong.

Suggested structure
Introduction: topic + thesis  
Paragraph 1: define unorganized crime and explain why it often does not “pay”  
Paragraph 2: define organized crime and explain why it can be profitable  
Paragraph 3: discuss corruption and systemic protection, using evidence  
Conclusion: restate argument and reflect on why this difference matters

Language and grammar points
Your meaning is generally understandable, but several sentences are awkward or incorrect.

Examples:
“both sides of the track” → usually “both sides of the tracks”, but this idiom is still not ideal here
“The persons must be patient and will to deal with others.”  
  → “Such individuals must be patient and willing to work with others.”
“that is ongoing and an integral part of their daily lives”  
  → “that is ongoing and becomes an integral part of their daily lives”
“includes, but is not exclusive to”  
  → “includes, but is not limited to”
“through the form of payoffs”  
  → “in the form of payoffs”

Also watch for:
sentence variety
article use (“a,” “the”)
word choice precision

Best next steps for revision
Write a clear thesis.
Remove the personal biography at the end.
Replace conversational language with formal academic wording.
Correct inaccurate claims, especially “petty offenses.”
Add evidence and sources for major points.
Revise definitions and improve paragraph structure.

Final comment
You have an interesting central idea: some crimes are less profitable because they are impulsive and visible, while organized crime can survive through planning, cooperation, and corruption. That idea could become a strong argumentative essay if you present it more formally and support it with evidence.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0019.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay tells a vivid, emotional story, and that is its biggest strength. The reader can clearly feel your fear, anger, and relief. You also create a strong theme by repeating your father’s idea that “crime doesn’t pay.” This gives the essay unity and helps connect the beginning and ending. To revise, focus mainly on clarity, grammar, and argumentative focus.

Main strengths
Strong personal voice: The essay feels real and sincere.
Clear chronological structure: The events are mostly easy to follow from the burglary to the court process.
Effective repetition of a key idea: Your father’s advice creates a clear message.
Emotional impact: Details such as returning home, losing everything, and facing the thieves in court are powerful.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the argument clearer
Right now, the essay is partly a narrative and partly an argumentative essay. It tells a story very well, but the central argument should be stated more directly.

Possible main argument:
Crime harms victims long after the theft itself.
The legal system should take victims’ suffering seriously.
“Crime doesn’t pay” because criminals eventually face consequences.

You should state this idea clearly in the introduction and return to it in each body paragraph.

Possible revised thesis:
> Although I once doubted that thieves would ever be punished, my experience showed me that crime has serious consequences and that the legal system can deliver justice.

B. Improve paragraph focus
Some paragraphs are long and contain too many ideas. Try to give each paragraph one main purpose:
Childhood belief about stealing
The burglary and emotional shock
Recovery of some property and hope for justice
Court process and frustration
Final punishment and lesson learned

This will make your essay more organized and more persuasive.

C. Grammar and sentence problems
There are several grammar mistakes, especially with articles, verb forms, possessives, and word choice. Here are some important examples:

“my mother and father always taught me the stealing was wrong”  
  → “my mother and father always taught me that stealing was wrong”

“I always believed them and they saying that”  
  → “I always believed them and what they said”

“Upon return from the doctor’s office”  
  → “When I returned from the doctor’s office”

“before I could call for police help”  
  → “before I could call the police”

“CD’s”  
  → “CDs”

“my husbands firearms”  
  → “my husband’s firearms”

“it was our home they stolen property came from”  
  → “the stolen property came from our home”

“prosecutors office”  
  → “the prosecutor’s office”

“would probably would not serve any time”  
  → “probably would not serve any time”

“what my father told be”  
  → “what my father told me”

“two men and one women”  
  → “two men and one woman”

D. Watch tense consistency
Most of your essay is in the past tense, which is correct because you are describing past events. However, sometimes the verb forms become awkward or incorrect. Keep checking that verbs match the time of the action.

For example:
“they stolen property came from” should be corrected.
“they saying that” should be in past form.

E. Use more formal word choice
Because this is an academic essay, avoid very conversational expressions when possible.

For example:
“downtown” is acceptable in a story, but in formal writing you may write “to the police department”
“those awful people” could become “the offenders” if you want a more academic tone

However, since this is a personal argumentative essay, some emotional language is still appropriate.

Introduction and conclusion
Your introduction starts with childhood memories, which is effective, but it needs a clearer thesis. Your conclusion is good because it returns to the idea of “crime doesn’t pay.” To improve it, explain more directly why this lesson matters.

For example:
> My experience taught me that crime does not pay, not only because criminals may go to prison, but also because the justice system must recognize the lasting damage done to victims.

One important content suggestion
You say you “have trust in the legal system now,” but earlier you explain that the system almost allowed a plea bargain and that you had to push for justice. This is interesting, but you should explain it more carefully. Did the system work well, or did it only work after your effort? Clarifying this point will make your argument stronger and more thoughtful.

Final advice
For revision, do these three things first:
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Divide the essay into focused paragraphs with one main idea each.
Edit carefully for grammar, especially possessives, verb forms, and sentence structure.

Overall, this essay has a strong emotional core and a meaningful message. With clearer argumentation and careful language correction, it can become a much stronger academic piece.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0020.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: feminism originally aimed at equality, but some extreme uses of the term have damaged its public image. This is a strong argumentative starting point, and your personal voice makes the essay engaged and readable. To revise effectively, focus on thesis clarity, evidence, tone, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear topic and opinion: You consistently argue that feminism’s original purpose was equality, not female dominance.
Awareness of complexity: You do not simply attack feminism; instead, you distinguish between its historical goals and some modern interpretations.
Logical overall structure: The essay moves from history, to present-day perceptions, to your own position, and then to a conclusion.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your main claim appears in the first paragraph, but it is still somewhat broad. Try to state exactly what you will argue.

For example:
Current version: “Nowadays, both ‘feminist’ and ‘feminism’ are dirty words because the cause has been misused…”
Possible revision: “Although feminism originally sought equal rights for women, its public image has been damaged by extreme voices that present it as a movement for female dominance rather than equality.”

This is clearer, more academic, and easier to support.

B. Use stronger evidence
At the moment, many claims are asserted but not supported. For example:
“both ‘feminist’ and ‘feminism’ are dirty words”
“men are not taking the feminist cause seriously anymore”
“some feminist groups… believe that all men are evil”

These are large generalizations. In academic argument, you should provide:
examples,
statistics,
historical facts,
references to public debate or media representations.

Also, be careful with history. Susan B. Anthony is relevant, but feminism as a broader movement has many figures and stages. You could strengthen the essay by referring to women’s suffrage, workplace equality, and legal reforms more specifically.

C. Avoid stereotypes and emotionally loaded language
Some phrases weaken your credibility because they sound insulting or informal, for example:
“big chunky lesbian who hates all men”
“a bunch of whining women”
“home free”

Even if you are describing a stereotype, this wording sounds offensive and reduces the academic quality of the essay. Instead, explain the stereotype in neutral language:
“Some people stereotype feminists as hostile toward men.”
“The movement is sometimes perceived as overly confrontational.”

This change will make your argument more persuasive.

D. Be careful with overgeneralization
Your essay sometimes treats “feminists,” “women,” or “men” as if they are all the same. For instance:
“Feminists are becoming anarchists”
“men are not taking the feminist cause seriously anymore”

These statements are too absolute. Use qualifiers such as:
“some”
“certain”
“in some cases”
“many critics argue”

Academic writing becomes stronger when it shows precision.

Organization and paragraph development
Several paragraphs contain many ideas at once. Try this structure:
Introduction: define feminism and present your thesis.
Historical background: explain the original goals of feminism.
Achievements of feminism: voting rights, workplace rights, awareness of violence and discrimination.
Problems in contemporary public perception: explain how some radical voices or media portrayals affect the movement’s image.
Conclusion: restate your balanced view.

Also, begin each body paragraph with a clear topic sentence.

Language and grammar
There are some grammar and word choice issues. Here are key examples:

Capitalization:  
  “by this I do not mean” → “By this, I do not mean”

Word form:  
  “A women is just as much a citizen” → “A woman is just as much a citizen”

Agreement:  
  “The equality of woman is no such an absurd notion” → “The equality of women is no longer an absurd notion”

Prepositions/article use:  
  “treated so in the society” → “treated equally in society”

Parallel structure:  
  “the vote, employment opportunities and other societal features”  
  This is understandable, but “rights and opportunities” is more natural.

Sentence control: Some sentences are too long and contain several ideas. Break them into shorter sentences for clarity.

Style suggestions
Aim for a more formal academic tone. Avoid conversational phrases such as:
“After all”
“home free”
“what wonders the feminist movement has done”

Instead use:
“Furthermore”
“As a result”
“This demonstrates the significant impact of the feminist movement”

Final advice
Your essay has a solid central idea, but revision should focus on making it more academic, balanced, and evidence-based. The biggest improvement will come from:
replacing stereotypes with neutral language,
supporting claims with examples,
reducing generalizations,
editing grammar and sentence clarity.

You have the basis for an interesting argument. If you revise with a more precise tone and stronger support, your essay will become much more convincing.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0021.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear main argument: the Women’s Liberation Movement was harmed less by feminists themselves than by public stereotypes and selective attention to radicals. This is a thoughtful claim, and your introduction shows ambition. You also use a formal argumentative tone in many places, which is a strength.

To improve the essay, focus on 1) thesis and organization, 2) evidence and accuracy, and 3) grammar/style.

Thesis and organization

Your central idea is present, but it becomes fully clear only near the end: “the popular perception” harmed the movement more than feminists did. In revision, state this more directly in the introduction.

For example, your thesis could be:

“Although radical feminists attracted public attention, the Women’s Liberation Movement was damaged more by outsiders’ stereotypes and media portrayals than by the actions of feminists themselves.”

This will help readers follow your argument from the beginning.

Organization
Right now, some paragraphs jump quickly across time periods and examples. Try this structure:

Introduction: explain how movements are stereotyped; present your thesis.  
Historical example 1: early feminism and how opponents focused on “radicals.”  
Historical example 2: the 1960s–70s movement and media attention to extreme images.  
Conclusion: explain why public perception still affects feminism today.

Also, avoid rhetorical questions like “So what am I getting at?” in a formal academic essay. Instead, use a direct transition:

“The point is that feminists themselves did not damage the movement; rather, public misrepresentation did.”

Evidence and accuracy

This is the area that most needs revision. Your essay makes interesting claims, but several examples are either too general or historically questionable.

Need for stronger evidence
You mention Susan B. Anthony, George Sand, Emily Dickinson, Stein, housewives returning to college, bra-burning, and equal pay. However, these examples are not explained in enough detail. For each example, ask:

Who is this person?
Why are they relevant to feminism?
How exactly did public perception distort the movement?

For instance, when you mention the late 1960s, you could explain how media coverage emphasized sensational images rather than mainstream goals such as equal pay, workplace rights, education, and reproductive freedom.

Historical accuracy concerns
Be careful with your examples:

George Sand was a French novelist associated with unconventional gender behavior, but not a central figure in the American Women’s Liberation Movement.
Emily Dickinson is not usually considered an activist in the feminist movement.
“Stein” is unclear. Do you mean Germaine Greer, Gloria Steinem, or someone else? Use the correct name.

In academic writing, inaccurate examples weaken your argument. It is better to use fewer examples that are clearly relevant and correct.

Grammar, word choice, and sentence control

Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are frequent grammar and wording errors. Below are some important examples.

Sentence-level corrections

“Whenever a movement is began”  
  → “Whenever a movement begins” or “Whenever a movement is begun”  
  (“is began” is incorrect)

“People normally associated with politics, religion, or art are quickly lumped into categories”  
  This is understandable, but you may mean:  
  → “People associated with these movements are quickly lumped into categories”

“obpostion”  
  → “opposition”

“This ignorance of other less aggressive feminists, made it seem…”  
  → remove comma: “This ignorance of other, less aggressive feminists made it seem…”

“therfore”  
  → “therefore”

“docile and backward in modern standards”  
  → “docile and conservative by modern standards”

“Women supporting equal pay for women and and other…”  
  → remove repetition: “Women supporting equal pay and other…”

“have promote”  
  → “have promoted”

“in lieu of”  
  This phrase means “instead of,” which may not be your intended meaning. You likely mean:  
  → “in favor of” or “because of attention to”

“readiliy”  
  → “readily”

“if the public it was being aimed was not so jaded”  
  → “if the public at which it was aimed were not so jaded”  
  Or more naturally: “if the public were less cynical.”

Style suggestions
Avoid emotionally loaded or vague phrases unless you define them:
“wild, disgruntled zealots”
“bra-burning, anti-shaving leaders”

These phrases may sound biased and may weaken your credibility. In academic writing, neutral language is stronger.

Final advice for revision

When revising, try to do these three things:

Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Replace weak or inaccurate examples with 2–3 solid, relevant historical examples.
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and formal style.

Overall, your essay has a strong underlying argument and good potential. The main challenge is to support your ideas with accurate evidence and express them in clearer academic English.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a confident argumentative voice. You introduce feminism through the Beijing conference, then focus on the United States as your example. This gives the essay a strong frame. You also show good awareness of historical change, especially women’s suffrage and later feminist movements. Below are suggestions to help you revise.

Main strength: clear focus and persuasive tone
Your essay consistently argues that feminism has had a positive impact in America and that America can be a model for other countries. The tone sounds formal and engaged, which suits argumentative writing.

Biggest issue: the argument is too general
At the moment, many claims are broad but not well supported. For example:

“America continues to set an example for women around the world.”
“Women’s access to law schools, medical schools… have expanded greatly…”

These are strong claims, but you need specific evidence. Add 2–3 concrete examples, such as:
the year women gained the right to vote in the U.S. (1920),
examples of legal or educational changes,
statistics about women in higher education or the workplace,
mention of legislation related to equal rights or equal pay.

Without evidence, the essay sounds more like a speech than a strong academic argument.

Thesis and structure
Your introduction presents the topic, but the central thesis should be clearer. Right now, the final sentence of the essay functions as your main claim, but it appears too late.

Try placing a stronger thesis at the end of the first paragraph. For example:

Possible thesis:  
*Although American women still face inequality, feminism has significantly expanded women’s political rights, educational opportunities, and professional access in the United States.*

This thesis is stronger because it is specific and gives your essay a clear structure.

Then organize the body around 2–3 main points:
political rights,
education and employment,
continuing challenges.

This will make the essay more balanced and logical.

Missing quotation / incomplete sentence
This sentence is incomplete:

“They have always preached the same sermon, from the same pulpit: .”

You must either:
insert the missing quotation or idea, or
rewrite the sentence without it.

For example:
*They consistently argued that women deserved equality in both private and public life.*

Tone: avoid overstatement
Some expressions are powerful, but they sound too absolute for academic writing. For example:

“No one today would say…”
“Everything… seems feasible.”
“America continues to set an example for women around the world.”

These statements may be challenged easily. A more careful tone is better in argument essays. Use phrases like:
*many people would agree…*
*to a large extent…*
*has made significant progress…*
*can serve as one example…*

This makes your writing sound more credible.

Grammar and word choice corrections
Here are some important language issues to revise:

“World Conference Women” → *World Conference on Women*
“what impact the cause has had” → *the impact feminism has had*
“the anniversary of the Women’s Suffrage Movement” → awkward; better: *the anniversary of women’s suffrage* or *the anniversary of the movement for women’s voting rights*
“the women of America being recognized as full citizens” → *American women being recognized as full citizens*
“suffragates” → *suffragists* or *suffragettes*
“Gloria Steinam” → *Gloria Steinem*
“board rooms” → *boardrooms*
“Women’s access… have expanded” → *Women’s access… has expanded*  
  (“access” is singular here.)

Style and sentence control
Some sentences are very long and contain many ideas. This makes the meaning harder to follow. For example, the long sentence beginning with “We hear the word ‘battle’…” should be divided into two or three shorter sentences.

Shorter sentences will improve clarity, especially in academic writing.

Consider a counterargument
A stronger argumentative essay usually acknowledges limitations. For example, you could mention that despite major progress, American women still face:
wage gaps,
underrepresentation in top leadership,
social expectations about gender roles.

This does not weaken your essay; it makes it more thoughtful and realistic.

Suggested revision plan
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Add specific evidence and examples.
Divide the body into clear points.
Fix the incomplete sentence with the missing quotation.
Use a more cautious academic tone.
Edit grammar, spelling, and sentence length.

Final comment
You have a strong foundation: a clear topic, historical awareness, and a persuasive voice. Your next step is to make the essay more academic by adding evidence, clarifying the thesis, and revising for accuracy and precision. With these changes, your argument will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you disagree with the statement that money is the root of all evil, and you argue instead that individual choice is the real cause of evil. This is a strong central idea, and your essay stays focused on it. You also use examples such as honest work, charity, and criminal behavior to support your point. That gives your argument a logical structure.

What you do well

Clear thesis and position
   - From the introduction, the reader understands that you disagree with the statement.
   - Your conclusion also clearly restates your main claim.

Good use of contrast
   - You contrast honest ways of earning money with evil actions done to get money.
   - This helps show that money itself is not automatically evil.

Logical progression
   - Paragraph 2 explains why money cannot always be evil.
   - Paragraph 3 admits that money can be connected to some evil.
   - Paragraph 4 broadens the discussion to other causes.
   - Paragraph 5 gives the example of charitable donations.
   - This is a sensible argumentative structure.

Main areas to improve

Make the introduction more direct and formal
The first sentence is awkward and too personal: “The first thing I would like to address…” Academic essays are usually stronger when they begin directly with the topic.

Also, the sentence about the “corrected statement” is a little confusing. Your real argument seems to be:
Money itself is not the root of all evil
The love of money, greed, or immoral choice may lead to evil

A clearer introduction would help the whole essay.

Possible revision:
> Many people say that “money is the root of all evil,” but this statement is too extreme. Money itself is neutral; it can be earned and used for both good and bad purposes. Therefore, evil comes not from money itself but from human greed and moral choices.

Strengthen grammar, especially verb forms and articles
There are several grammar errors that affect clarity.

Examples:
“I wold like” → “I would like”
“Most money is attained in honest ways” → “Most money is earned in honest ways”
“If one is attaining money honestly, how can honestly be associated with evil?”  
  → better: “If one earns money honestly, how can that be associated with evil?”
“If money was the root of all evil” → “If money were the root of all evil”  
  (“were” is more standard in hypothetical statements.)
“worth-while” → “worthwhile”

As a Chinese-speaking writer, pay special attention to:
articles: a, an, the
singular/plural nouns
word form: honest / honesty / honestly
verb choice: “earn money” is more natural than “attain money”

Improve word choice and natural phrasing
Some expressions are understandable but not natural in English.

Examples:
“attained in honest ways” → “earned honestly”
“many other lucrative means” → “many other legitimate ways”
“the root of some evil” → “a cause of some evil” or “the source of some evil”
“morals (lack of)” → “a lack of morals”
“become the root of evil” → “lead to evil” or “cause evil”

Be careful with repetition of “root of evil.” In English, repeating the exact phrase too often can sound unnatural. Use synonyms.

Develop your examples more fully
Your ideas are good, but some paragraphs would be stronger with more explanation.

For example, in paragraph 5, you argue that charity shows money can be used for good. This is a strong point, but it needs clearer reasoning:
money donated to hospitals, schools, and relief programs helps people
therefore money is a tool
the moral value depends on the user, not the money itself

Use a more balanced academic tone
Some statements are too absolute:
“we all would have to be evil because we all have money”
“most if not all funds donated are used for the purposes intended”

The first is useful as a rhetorical point, but the second sounds too certain without evidence. In academic writing, it is better to be careful:
“many donated funds are used for worthwhile purposes”
“in many cases”

Paragraph-level advice

Introduction: State your thesis clearly and avoid talking about “corrected statement” in a confusing way.
Body paragraph 2: Good logic, but revise awkward wording.
Body paragraph 3: Strong point—money can motivate evil, but is not evil itself.
Body paragraph 4: Good idea, but use clearer phrasing: “Other factors, such as greed, immoral attitudes, and poor values, can also lead to evil.”
Body paragraph 5: Good example, but explain it more carefully.
Conclusion: Effective, but you can make it more formal.

Possible revised conclusion:
> In conclusion, money is not the root of all evil. Rather, evil results from human greed, dishonesty, and personal moral choices. Money is only a tool; whether it is used for good or bad depends on the individual.

Final suggestion
Before revising, try this checklist:
Is my thesis clear in one sentence?
Does each paragraph support that thesis?
Can I replace unnatural phrases with simpler English?
Did I check verb forms, articles, and plural nouns?
Did I explain my examples enough?

You already have a solid argument. With clearer wording and more formal academic style, this essay can become much stronger.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: money is necessary in modern life, but it becomes dangerous when people let it control them. This is a thoughtful position, and your conclusion returns effectively to that main point. You also use contrast well, especially in ideas such as “some use it to live and others live to use it.” That kind of phrasing is memorable and persuasive.

Main strengths
Clear opinion: You argue that money is essential but should not become our master.
Relevant examples and reasoning: You discuss basic needs, power, work ethic, and success, which all connect to your argument.
Strong concluding message: The ending is direct and appropriate for an argumentative essay.

Main areas to improve

A. Clarify the thesis
Your first paragraph introduces several ideas—money, power, survival—but your exact argument is not fully clear until later. Try ending the introduction with a precise thesis statement.

Possible thesis:
> Although money is necessary for survival and comfort in modern society, it becomes harmful when people pursue it only for power, status, or control.

This would guide the reader more clearly.

B. Improve organization
Right now, some paragraphs move quickly from one idea to another. A stronger structure would help.

Possible structure:
Introduction: Explain why money is necessary today.
Body paragraph 1: Money is needed for basic survival.
Body paragraph 2: The pursuit of money can lead to power and corruption.
Body paragraph 3: People value money differently depending on how they earn it.
Body paragraph 4: True success should not be measured only by income.
Conclusion: Money should serve people, not control them.

Make sure each paragraph has one main topic and a clear topic sentence.

C. Develop ideas more fully
Some points are interesting but not fully explained. For example:
“Absolute power corrupts absolutely” is a strong idea, but you need to explain how it connects to money more directly.
The paragraph about working for one’s salary is good, but you could support it with a clearer example.
The idea of “success” could be developed with contrast: financial success vs. personal values, contribution, or happiness.

Ask yourself after each paragraph: Have I explained why this supports my argument?

Language corrections

There are several grammar, spelling, and word choice issues. Below are some important ones:

“some where” → “somewhere”
“currupts” → “corrupts”
“Once ones basic” → “Once one’s basic”
“begats” → better: “creates” or “leads to”
“cicle” → “cycle”
“sence” → “sense”
“sucess” → “success”
“one heir more substantive accomplishments” → probably “on their more substantive accomplishments”
“indespencable” → “indispensable”

Grammar points to watch
Possessives
   - “ones basic needs” should be “one’s basic needs.”

Subject-verb agreement / pronouns
   - “If one has to work hard for their salary...”  
     This is common in speech, but in formal writing, keep pronouns consistent:
     - “If one has to work hard for one’s salary...”
     - or more naturally: “If people have to work hard for their salaries...”

Sentence clarity
   Some sentences are hard to follow because too many ideas are combined.
   Example:
   > “This begats the will for more power and the cicle goes on.”
   Better:
   > “This desire often leads to the pursuit of even more power, creating a continuous cycle.”

Tone and style
Your tone is strong, which suits argument writing. However, a few phrases are too conversational:
“the almighty dollar”
“nothing works”

These are vivid, but in an academic essay, use more formal wording:
“money is essential in modern society”
“daily life cannot function without it”

Also, avoid quotation marks unless you are quoting an actual saying.

Example of a revised sentence
Original:
> “Money is something that our society has made indespencable. It is for us to use, not for it to use us.”

Revised:
> “Modern society has made money indispensable. However, money should remain a tool that serves human needs rather than a force that controls people’s lives.”

Final advice
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Write a clearer thesis in the introduction.
Give each paragraph one central idea.
Explain your examples more fully.
Correct spelling and grammar errors.
Use more formal academic language.

You have a solid core argument and several strong insights. With clearer structure and more careful language, this could become a convincing and mature argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: money itself is not evil; people’s attitudes toward money can lead to harmful behavior. This is a good argumentative focus, and your conclusion returns clearly to that main idea. Below are suggestions to help you revise.

Strengths
Clear central argument: You consistently argue that money is neutral, but obsession with money can cause moral problems.
Logical overall structure: You begin with questions, explain one interpretation of the saying, discuss dangers of greed, and end by rejecting the idea that money itself is evil.
Relevant examples: You mention gambling, neglect of family, and helping the poor. These examples support your main point.

Main revision priorities

A. Make the thesis more precise
Right now, your essay asks several questions and gives opinions, but the thesis could be stated more directly in one sentence.

For example:
> Although many people say that money is “the root of all evil,” money itself is not evil; rather, greed and the misuse of money can lead people to harmful and immoral actions.

This kind of thesis makes your argument clearer and more academic.

B. Improve paragraph unity
Some paragraphs have more than one focus. Try to give each paragraph one main idea.

Possible organization:
Introduction: explain the saying and present your thesis.
Why people connect money with evil: greed, power, obsession.
Money itself is neutral: it cannot think or act.
Positive uses of money: helping the poor, education, housing.
Conclusion: restate your claim.

This structure will make the essay easier to follow.

C. Use a more balanced tone
Your essay focuses strongly on a Christian perspective. That can work, but since the topic is broad, you may want to explain the issue in a way that includes a wider audience. You can still mention Christianity, but if you rely on it too much, some readers may feel the argument is too narrow.

For example, instead of:
> This leads you away from the christian way of life and into a life of evil.

You could write:
> This kind of greed can lead people away from moral values and toward harmful behavior.

This sounds more academic and inclusive.

Language and grammar issues

A. Word choice and spelling
There are several vocabulary/spelling errors:
that you have not “that you have”
Christian not “christian” when referring to the religion
God-centered not “God centered”
subservient not “subservant”
mere not “mear”
cannot not “can not”
educate not “educated” in “opportunities to educate the uneducated”

B. Sentence grammar
Some sentences need correction for clarity and accuracy.

Original:
> I believe what they are trying to say is, you always want more that you have and the more you have the more you want.

Better:
> I believe that what people mean is that individuals often want more than they have, and the more they possess, the more they desire.

Original:
> It is the person whose judgements, decisions, and actions that can be evil.

Better:
> It is a person’s judgments, decisions, and actions that can be evil.

Original:
> You can be lead into gambling

Better:
> You can be led into gambling

C. Punctuation
You often connect ideas with commas when stronger punctuation is needed. This creates comma splices.

Original:
> I think not, it is all in how it is used...

Better:
> I think not. It all depends on how money is used...

Make the argument more academic
Try to avoid repeating “evil” too often without explanation. In academic writing, it is helpful to use more specific terms such as:
greed
corruption
exploitation
selfishness
moral harm

For example:
> The pursuit of wealth can encourage greed, damage relationships, and lead some people to make unethical choices.

This sounds stronger and more precise.

Suggested revised conclusion
Your final sentence is strong in meaning, but it can be made more formal:

> Therefore, money is not the root of all evil. It is simply a tool, and whether it causes harm or good depends on the values and choices of the person who uses it.

Final advice
In revision, focus on:
writing a clear thesis
organizing each paragraph around one idea
correcting grammar, spelling, and punctuation
using a more formal academic tone

You have a solid main idea and good examples. With clearer structure and more accurate language, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a simple structure, which is a good starting point for an argumentative essay. You state your opinion in the introduction, give several reasons in the body, and end with a conclusion. This shows that you understand the basic organization of argument writing. However, to make this essay stronger at the university level, you need to improve logic, tone, grammar, and support for your ideas.

Main problem: argument quality

Your essay argues that “crime does pay” because criminals may earn more money, work fewer hours, and retire earlier. The problem is that the argument is one-sided and oversimplified. You only discuss short-term financial benefits, but you ignore major realities such as:

risk of arrest and imprisonment  
violence and personal danger  
mental stress and lack of security  
damage to victims and society  
difficulty enjoying illegal income openly

A strong argumentative essay should consider counterarguments. Even if you want to defend a controversial opinion, you must show that you understand the other side. For example, you could discuss that some criminals may gain money quickly, but the long-term consequences often make crime a harmful and unstable choice.

Use stronger evidence

Your essay uses numbers such as “$7.50 per hour” and “$300.00 per hour,” but there is no source for these figures. In academic writing, unsupported statistics weaken your credibility. Also, the numbers seem unrealistic and too general. Not all workers earn the same wage, and not all criminals make large amounts of money.

To revise, either:
use reliable evidence from research, news, or reports, or  
make the argument more cautious by using words like “some,” “may,” or “in certain cases.”

For example:
Weak: “The average criminal can work one hour a day and make $300.00.”
Better: “Some crimes may bring fast financial rewards, but such income is unpredictable and illegal.”

Tone and audience

The tone of the essay sounds too casual and sometimes almost approving of crime. In academic writing, you should aim for a formal and analytical tone. Avoid sounding like you are encouraging illegal behavior.

Examples:
“you don’t have to worry about being fired” → too conversational  
“you’re your own boss” → informal

A more academic style would be:
“Criminal activity may appear attractive to some people because it seems to offer independence and quick income.”

Organization and development

Your structure is clear, but each paragraph needs better development.

Introduction: The thesis is clear, but it is too direct and not academic enough. Try stating the issue more carefully.
Body paragraph 1: The comparison between job insecurity and crime is interesting, but you need to explain why illegal work is not truly “secure.”
Body paragraph 2: The wage comparison is your strongest point, but it needs evidence and more careful reasoning.
Body paragraph 3: The retirement example is too hypothetical and depends on unrealistic assumptions.
Conclusion: It repeats your opinion but does not leave the reader with a thoughtful final point.

Grammar and language corrections

Here are some important language issues to fix:

Word choice / grammar
“has it’s advantages” → “has its advantages”
“hard working person” → “hard-working person”
“rather or not” → “whether or not”
“laid off work” → “laid off”
“There is never a lack of work are a means” → “There is never a lack of work or a means”
“will costs them” → “will cost them”
“a specific numbers of hours” → “a specific number of hours”
“If he/she invest half” → “If he/she invests half”

Consistency
You shift between:
“you”
“he or she”
“they”
“the criminal”

Choose one style and keep it consistent. In modern academic English, singular they is often the easiest choice.

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long or awkward. For example:
“Further the criminal does not have to be at work at a specific time for a specific numbers of hours each day.”
Better:
“Furthermore, criminals are not required to follow fixed schedules or standard working hours.”

Suggestions for revision

To improve this essay, try these steps:

Rewrite the thesis in a more academic and balanced way.
Add counterarguments about the risks and consequences of crime.
Remove unsupported statistics or replace them with evidence.
Use formal language instead of conversational expressions.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and subject-verb agreement.

Possible revised thesis

You might revise your thesis to something like:

“Although crime may seem financially rewarding in the short term, its legal, personal, and social consequences make it an unstable and destructive path.”
  
If you want to keep your original position, make it more analytical:
“Some people may believe that crime pays because it can provide quick money and independence, but this view ignores the serious risks involved.”

Overall, your essay has a clear structure and a strong attempt at persuasion. The next step is to make your argument more realistic, balanced, and academic.

===== ICLE-US-MICH-0001.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear overall position: computers have improved life physically but created mental and social problems. This is a strong central idea, and your conclusion returns to it effectively. You also organize the essay into clear sections: introduction, physical effects, mental effects, and conclusion. That structure helps the reader follow your argument.

Main strengths
Clear thesis: “The computer has made life easier from a physical standpoint and harder from a mental standpoint” is an arguable claim.
Logical paragraphing: Each body paragraph has a different focus.
Attempt at balance: You discuss both advantages and disadvantages, which makes the essay more thoughtful.
Ambitious vocabulary: Words such as “automation,” “ramifications,” and “theoretical” show strong effort.

Main issues to revise

A. Make the argument more precise
Some claims are too broad or unsupported. For example:
“computers make errors all the time”
“mathematical models, theories, etc are absolutely riddled with errors”
“almost everyone has at least one simple computer”

These statements sound too absolute. In academic writing, avoid overgeneralization unless you provide evidence. Try softer, more precise language:
“computer-generated results can contain errors when users enter incorrect data or make flawed assumptions”
“many models may be limited by incomplete data”
“many people use simple computing devices such as calculators”

B. Improve development with examples
Your points would be stronger with concrete examples. For instance:
For physical effects, give examples such as automated assembly lines, email replacing paper communication, or spreadsheet software reducing calculation time.
For mental effects, explain more clearly how dependence on computers may reduce people’s understanding or critical thinking.
For job loss, mention specific occupations or sectors affected by automation.

Right now, some paragraphs state ideas without enough explanation.

C. Clarify the “mental standpoint” argument
This is the most interesting part of your essay, but also the least clear. You seem to argue that:
computers allow people to do calculations without understanding them;
this can produce errors;
reliance on computers may weaken human judgment.

These are good ideas, but they need clearer connections. At present, the paragraph moves too quickly from calculations to ignorance to models to variables. Try separating the ideas and explaining each one carefully.

D. Use a more academic tone
A few expressions are too informal, vague, or problematic for academic writing:
“catch-22”
“etc”
“everyone already knows”
“2nd & 3rd world countries”
quotation marks around words like “needless” and “told”

Use formal alternatives:
“This creates a paradox”
Replace “etc” with specific examples
Avoid assuming what “everyone” knows
“developing countries” is more appropriate than “3rd world countries”

Language and grammar points

A. Sentence control and punctuation
Some sentences are long or incorrectly punctuated. Example:
> “The computer has eliminated many jobs through automation of factories; accounting, engineering, and other scientific programs, etc”

This sentence is confusing because the semicolon is not used correctly. You could write:
> “Computers have eliminated many jobs through factory automation and through software used in fields such as accounting, engineering, and science.”

B. Word choice
A few word choices are unnatural:
“other type functions” → “other types of functions”
“Due to this errors frequently exist” → “Because of this, errors frequently occur”
“workers out of work” → “workers unemployed”
“ramifications about what they are doing” → “the ramifications of what they are doing”

C. Articles and plurals
Watch for small grammar issues:
“from the very first modern computer” is acceptable, but “from the first computers of the 1940s” may sound more natural.
“Due to this errors” → “Due to this, errors…”
“if programmed 100% correctly” is understandable, but “if programmed perfectly” is more natural.

Suggested revision strategy
Rewrite the thesis to make it more specific.
Revise each body paragraph so that it contains:
   - a clear topic sentence,
   - one main claim,
   - 2–3 specific examples,
   - explanation of why the example supports the claim.
Reduce exaggeration and use more cautious academic language.
Edit for grammar and punctuation, especially long sentences and semicolon use.

Possible revised thesis
You might try something like:
> “Although computers have increased efficiency in workplaces and daily life, they have also created new problems, including overdependence on technology, errors caused by human misuse, and job loss through automation.”

This version is clearer and gives you a roadmap for the essay.

Overall, this is a thoughtful draft with a strong basic structure and an interesting argument. The next step is to make your claims more precise, support them with examples, and use a more formal academic style.

Strengths
