Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: the microwave was a revolutionary invention that changed cooking and modern life. The writing is energetic and imaginative, especially the extended sea/storm metaphor in the introduction. You also include useful supporting ideas such as speed, safety, and a break from traditional fire-based cooking. These are strong starting points. For revision, focus on clarity, accuracy, organization, and grammar.

Main strengths
Clear argument: You consistently argue that the microwave transformed food preparation.
Strong voice: The essay sounds confident and persuasive.
Good use of comparison: You compare older cooking methods with the microwave effectively.
Relevant supporting points: You mention time-saving, convenience, and safety.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your central idea is good, but it should be stated more directly and formally. Right now, the introduction is very metaphorical, but the exact argument appears only later.

Possible revised thesis:
> The microwave revolutionized cooking because it introduced a new heating method, reduced cooking time, and made food preparation more convenient for modern families.

This kind of thesis helps guide the whole essay.

B. Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases are too dramatic or informal for an argumentative essay:
“they would have thought you crazy”
“under the influence of heavy drugs”
“slaving in the kitchen”
“marvel of technology”

These expressions create personality, but they weaken the academic style. Try replacing them with more neutral language.

For example:
“would have found the idea unbelievable”
“greatly reduced the time required for meal preparation”

C. Improve paragraph organization
Each body paragraph should focus on one main reason and explain it clearly.

A stronger structure could be:
Introduction + thesis
Body paragraph 1: The microwave changed the basic method of cooking by using radiation instead of fire.
Body paragraph 2: The microwave saves time and fits modern lifestyles.
Body paragraph 3: The microwave increases convenience and safety, especially for families.
Conclusion

At the moment, some ideas are mixed together, especially in paragraph 3.

D. Develop your evidence more carefully
Some claims are too broad:
“cuts cooking time in half”
“Children are able to use this simple device without parental fear”

These may be partly true, but you should avoid absolute statements unless you can prove them. Use more cautious language:
“often reduces cooking time significantly”
“can be safer than open flames for supervised children”

Also, explain why your examples matter. For instance, how exactly did the microwave change the lives of working parents? Add one or two specific examples.

Language corrections

Here are some important sentence-level issues:

Spelling / word choice
culunary → *culinary*
consistant → *consistent*
move → *moved*
rythm → *rhythm*
changed → *change*
twentyth → *twentieth*
comodity → *commodity*
dizzing → *dizzying*
fool of mankind → probably *food of mankind*
differents perspectives → *different perspectives*

Grammar
“the constant rhythm of the waves have showed little changed”  
  → *the constant rhythm of the waves has shown little change*
“As History progressed”  
  → *As history progressed*  
  (“history” is not usually capitalized in the middle of a sentence.)
“Modern times saw gas and electric ranges as to staple.”  
  → *In modern times, gas and electric ranges became staples.*
“A mother who works, can come home can whip up something”  
  → *A working mother can come home and prepare something quickly*
“The microwave is excellent for re-heating making it easier”  
  → *The microwave is excellent for reheating, making it easier...*

Punctuation
You have several run-on sentences and spacing issues. Read each sentence carefully and ask:
Does it contain two complete ideas?
Should I use a period, comma, or semicolon?

For example:
> “However with the daybreak of the twentyth century a storm has brewed.”

Better:
> “However, at the dawn of the twentieth century, a storm began to brew.”

Style advice for a Chinese-speaking writer
A common issue for Chinese learners of English is writing long, image-rich sentences that sound powerful but become grammatically unclear. In English academic writing, clear and direct sentences are usually more effective than highly poetic ones. You do not need to remove all your creativity, but balance it with precision.

Final revision advice
When you rewrite, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Organize each paragraph around one reason.
Replace informal/dramatic wording with formal academic language.
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation.
Support claims with specific and careful explanation.

You have a strong idea and an engaging style. With clearer structure and more accurate language, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Conclusion

Today, television is one of the most common forms of entertainment in many households.

radio as a tool of political control, or

For example:
“the 2 most important inventions” → the two most important inventions

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a personal voice. You answer the prompt by focusing on one invention—the microwave—and you support your opinion with examples from your family and your great-uncle. This makes the essay easy to relate to. To improve it, focus on organization, argument development, and grammar/word choice.

Main strengths
Clear focus: You stay on one invention instead of discussing too many ideas.
Relevant examples: Your family routine and the story about your great-uncle make the essay interesting and concrete.
Good awareness of another viewpoint: You mention that not everyone thinks a microwave is necessary. This helps make the essay more balanced.

Main areas to revise

A. Make your argument clearer
Right now, the essay feels more like a discussion of microwaves than a strong argument. Your main claim should be stated more directly in the introduction.

For example, instead of:
> “During the 20th century there have been many advances in science and modern technology that have made a significant impact on peoples lives.”

You could write:
> “Among the many inventions of the twentieth century, I believe the microwave has had a significant positive impact on people’s lives because it saves time and makes daily meal preparation easier.”

This gives the reader a clear thesis and two reasons.

B. Improve organization
Your essay would be stronger with a more obvious structure:

Introduction: mention the topic and state your thesis  
Body paragraph 1: explain why microwaves are useful in modern family life  
Body paragraph 2: give your personal example  
Body paragraph 3: mention the opposing view and respond to it  
Conclusion: restate why the microwave is valuable

At the moment, the essay jumps quickly from general statements to personal story. Add topic sentences so each paragraph has one main purpose.

C. Develop the opposing view more carefully
You say that a microwave “isn’t right for everyone,” which is a fair point. However, since this is an argumentative essay, you should explain why the microwave is still valuable despite some people not liking it.

For example:
> “Although some older people may prefer traditional cooking methods, microwaves remain highly useful because they help busy families save time without completely replacing home-cooked meals.”

This shows that you understand the other side, but you still defend your main argument.

Language corrections

A. Grammar and sentence structure
Some sentences need correction:

“peoples lives” → “people’s lives”
“For instance a microwave” → “For instance, a microwave”
“left overs” → “leftovers”
“we each take turns cook” → “we each take turns cooking” or “we take turns cooking”
“they’ re” → “they’re”
“recieved” → “received”
“The point is, is that” → “The point is that”

B. Word choice
A few expressions sound informal or awkward for academic writing:

“brilliant addition” → better: “useful invention” or “valuable appliance”
“pop them into the microwave” → better: “put them into the microwave”
“little story” → better: “example” or “personal anecdote”
“The long and short of the story” → too conversational; use “In the end” or “As a result”

C. Avoid repetition
You repeat “microwave” many times in close sentences. Try using:
this appliance
this invention
it

Punctuation and mechanics
Be careful with:
Commas after introductory phrases: “For instance,” “In fact,” “On the other hand,”
Apostrophes: “people’s,” “today’s”
Hyphenation: usually microwave, not micro-wave
Quotation marks: use them only when necessary

Style advice for a stronger academic tone
Because this is an argumentative essay, try to sound a little more formal. Personal examples are good, but they should support a broader point. You can connect your example to society more clearly:

> “In many households, both parents work full-time, so appliances that reduce cooking time are especially important.”

This kind of sentence helps move from your personal experience to a general argument.

Suggested revision goal
When you revise, try to do these three things:
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Organize each paragraph around one main idea.
Replace informal phrases with more academic language.

Possible revised thesis
> “I believe the microwave is one of the most important twentieth-century inventions because it saves time, supports busy families, and makes meal preparation more convenient.”

Overall, you have a good foundation: a clear topic, relevant examples, and a recognizable opinion. With stronger structure and more formal language, this could become a much more effective argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a practical topic. You argue that ATMs are an important invention because they save time, improve access to money, and make daily life more convenient. The essay is easy to follow overall, and your use of personal examples helps make the argument relatable. Below are suggestions to help you revise it.

Strengths

Clear thesis and purpose  
You clearly state that ATMs have significantly changed people’s lives and explain why you value them.

Relevant supporting ideas  
Your main reasons are logical: convenience, access in emergencies, time-saving, and safety.

Personal experience  
The example about forgetting your wallet or not having enough money at the grocery store makes the essay more vivid and persuasive.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your opening sentence is understandable, but it is long and slightly awkward. Try to make your thesis direct and specific.

Original:  
“An invention of the 20th century which I feel has significantly changed people's lives is the introduction of Bank-cash machines or Automatic teller machines.”

Better:  
“One important invention of the twentieth century is the automated teller machine (ATM), which has greatly changed people’s daily lives.”

This version is clearer and more formal.

B. Improve organization
Your essay has good ideas, but the body paragraphs could be more clearly separated by topic. A stronger structure would be:

Introduction: introduce the invention and thesis  
Body paragraph 1: convenience and 24-hour access  
Body paragraph 2: time-saving for working people  
Body paragraph 3: safety and personal example  
Conclusion: restate your main argument

This will make your argument more persuasive.

C. Use more formal academic style
Some sentences sound too conversational for an argumentative essay.

Examples:
“I can not comprehend how difficult it would be…”  
“This is when I really appreciate my ATM card.”  
“I do not know what I would do without my card.”

These are natural in speech, but in academic writing, try to sound more objective.

Better alternatives:
“It is difficult to imagine modern life without this convenient access to money.”
“In such situations, the usefulness of an ATM card becomes clear.”
“For these reasons, ATM cards have become essential in modern life.”

D. Develop the counterargument
You mention that some people argue ATMs reduce bank employment, but you do not explain this point or answer it fully. Since this is an argumentative essay, it would be stronger if you addressed the opposing view more carefully.

For example:  
“Although some people argue that ATMs reduce employment opportunities in banks, their benefits to the public—especially convenience, efficiency, and accessibility—are much greater.”

This shows you understand both sides of the issue.

Language issues to revise

A. Word choice
Some phrases are unnatural in English:

“Bank-cash machines” → “bank machines” or simply “ATMs”
“This machinery” → “these machines” or “this technology”
“assigned banking hours” → “regular banking hours”
“appear in all areas of the World” → “are found all over the world”
“promote danger” → “increase the risk of theft” or “create danger”

B. Grammar and mechanics
Pay attention to these points:

Spelling / spacing
“can not” → “cannot”
“every day life” → “everyday life”

Capitalization
“Automatic teller machines” should not capitalize each word unless it begins a sentence.
“World” should not be capitalized in this context.

Apostrophes
“1900's” → “1900s”

Redundancy
“ATM machines” is repetitive because “M” already means “machine.”  
Use “ATMs” or “ATM machines,” but in formal writing, “ATMs” is better.

C. Sentence clarity
Some sentences need smoother wording.

Original:  
“It is also a convenience which helps to save time, which is very important to working men and women of the late 1900's.”

Better:  
“ATMs also save time, which is especially important for working men and women.”

This is shorter and clearer.

Suggested revision of your conclusion
Your conclusion has a good idea, but the final sentence is separated awkwardly by dashes and sounds incomplete.

Possible revision:  
“In conclusion, ATMs are an excellent invention because they provide convenient, fast, and safe access to money. The development of computerized banking technology has made everyday life less stressful and more efficient.”

Final advice
When revising, focus on:
writing a clearer thesis,
organizing each paragraph around one main idea,
using more formal academic language,
correcting small grammar and word choice issues.

You already have a solid foundation and a clear argument. With better organization and more polished language, this essay can become much stronger.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and an engaging personal voice. You introduce the cellular telephone as an important 20th-century discovery, and you discuss both its benefits and drawbacks. The example about calling your family from Madrid is vivid and helps make your topic interesting. For revision, the main areas to improve are thesis and argument, organization, style, and grammar/punctuation.

Thesis and Argument
Your essay is called an argumentative essay, but your position is not fully consistent. In the introduction, you say cellular telephones are “a complete luxury and not a necessity,” but later you also describe them as “truly amazing” and “a mark of great expansion in technology.” These ideas are not impossible to combine, but you need a clearer central claim.

Ask yourself: What exactly am I arguing?
Possible thesis directions:
Cellular telephones are an impressive invention, but they are still more of a luxury than a necessity.
Cellular telephones have improved communication, but their cost and danger while driving limit their value.
Although cellular telephones are expensive and sometimes dangerous, they represent a major technological advance.

Choose one main argument and make sure every paragraph supports it.

Organization
The essay has several good points, but the structure can be smoother.

A possible structure:
Introduction: introduce the invention and give a clear thesis.
Benefit paragraph: explain how cell phones improve communication and help in emergencies.
Drawback paragraph: explain dangers while driving and high cost.
Example paragraph: use the Madrid example to show the impressive reach of the technology.
Conclusion: restate your argument and end with a broader thought about technology.

Right now, some ideas are mixed together in one paragraph. For example, in the third body paragraph, you discuss both driving accidents and high cost. These are two separate disadvantages, so they would be stronger if developed more clearly.

Also, your final sentences about robots are interesting, but they feel slightly disconnected from the main argument. Try linking them more directly to your thesis about technological progress.

Development of Ideas
Some of your points would be stronger with more explanation.
For example:
You say cell phones are “a luxury and not a necessity.” Why? Is it because public phones and home phones already exist? Because only wealthier people can afford them?
You mention accidents caused by drivers using phones. Can you explain more clearly how divided attention affects safety?
You say cell phones are expensive. Compared with what? A home phone? A pay phone?

In argumentative writing, each point should have:
a clear topic sentence,
explanation,
and, if possible, an example.

Style and Tone
Your voice is lively and personal, which is a strength. However, some expressions are too informal for academic writing:
“&” → use and
“a bit ridiculous” → perhaps somewhat excessive
“lots of people” → many people
“The other day” → For example or Recently
“The scary thing” → What is striking is that

Also, avoid too many exclamation marks. In formal essays, they can make the tone sound emotional rather than analytical.

Grammar and Sentence-Level Issues
Here are some important corrections:

“20th century discoveries” → 20th-century discoveries  
  (Use a hyphen when it comes before a noun.)

“the main way being the availability to be reached at any time”  
  This is awkward. Better: “The main change is that people can now be reached at any time.”

“if they are stranded or broken down”  
  Better: “if they are stranded or their car breaks down.”

“friends houses” → friends’ houses

“people who are talking on their car phone”  
  Better: “people who talk on their cell phones while driving”

“Although most people who can afford to buy a cellular phone, can afford to pay the bills!”  
  This sentence has punctuation and logic problems. Better:  
  “However, most people who can afford to buy a cellular phone can probably afford the monthly bills as well.”

“from Madrid - On his ‘party line’”  
  The dash and capitalization are incorrect here. Rewrite more clearly:  
  “Recently, I called my father at his office from Madrid. Using a conference line, he connected my sister in Santa Barbara and my mother in Los Angeles, who was in her car.”

“it’ s” → it’s

Final Advice
For your revision, focus on:
Writing a clear, consistent thesis.
Organizing each paragraph around one main idea.
Using formal academic language.
Correcting grammar and punctuation carefully.

You already have strong ideas and a good example. With a clearer argument and more polished language, this essay will become much more effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a consistent focus: you argue that schools should use a “two-model approach” and teach both evolution and creation. The paper also shows effort to include counterarguments, examples, and outside sources. These are important strengths in argumentative writing. To revise effectively, however, you need to improve thesis clarity, organization, evidence use, and academic tone.

Main strengths
Clear topic and purpose: The reader quickly understands that the essay is about whether creation and evolution should both be taught.
Argumentative structure: You attempt to present claims, support, and counterarguments.
Attention to audience: You try to persuade readers by asking important educational and social questions.

Most important revision priorities

A. Strengthen the thesis
Your thesis appears at the end of the introduction, but it is too long and repetitive:

> “Those who support a two-model approach... present a strong, effective argument...”

This sentence mostly evaluates the argument rather than stating your own precise claim. Make the thesis direct and specific.

Possible revision:
> Schools should teach evolution and creation in a comparative, non-dogmatic way because doing so encourages critical thinking, broadens students’ perspectives, and improves their understanding of controversial questions about human origins.

This version clearly states what should happen and why.

B. Improve organization
Many body paragraphs begin with broad statements, but the internal logic is sometimes unclear. Try this structure for each paragraph:
Topic sentence: one clear reason supporting your thesis  
Evidence: source, example, study, or fact  
Explanation: how the evidence proves your point  
Link: connect back to thesis

For example, one paragraph could focus only on critical thinking, another on student tolerance/perspective, and another on educational outcomes.

Also, the essay repeats similar ideas several times, especially “questioning accepted beliefs” and “broadening perspective.” Combine overlapping points.

C. Use evidence more carefully
This is the biggest academic issue. Your essay includes many placeholders like , which means important support is missing. Without those quotations or details, the argument feels incomplete.

Also, some evidence needs better explanation or stronger reliability:
The Newton/Einstein example is interesting, but it does not directly prove that creation should be taught in science classes. It shows that questioning ideas can be valuable, but you must explain the connection more carefully.
The Bliss study is potentially useful, but you need to identify it more fully and explain why it is trustworthy.
The Darwin quote could be persuasive, but only if accurately quoted and clearly analyzed.

In revision:
Add full quotations or paraphrases where the placeholders are.
Identify who the source is, what the source argues, and why the source is credible.
Distinguish between scientific evidence, educational evidence, and personal opinion.

D. Address counterarguments fairly
You include opposing views, which is good, but sometimes they are presented too vaguely:
> “Some people feel .”
> “The most common argument against the two-model approach is probably that .”

To sound academically strong, state the opposing view clearly and respectfully. Then respond with evidence. For example:
Opponents argue that creationism is religious, not scientific.
Opponents argue that teaching creation in science class may violate the separation of church and state.

Then answer these points directly, rather than only saying the other side is “weak.”

Style and language

A. Use a more academic tone
Some wording is too emotional or informal:
“forceful and aggressive”
“surprise”
“incredible claims”
“infamous equation”

Academic argument is usually calmer and more precise.

Better choices:
“clear and balanced”
“notably”
“central claims”
“equation”

B. Avoid rhetorical questions
Your introduction uses several rhetorical questions. In academic essays, strong statements are usually more effective than many questions.

C. Be careful with word choice and grammar
A few examples:
“where as” → whereas
“fro ma” → from a
“the student's perspective” / “students’ perspectives” → keep number consistent
“What about attempts made by teachers to indoctrinate students? the opposition asks.”  
  → capitalize The

Also, some sentences are too long. Break them into shorter sentences for clarity.

Content issue to consider
One important issue is that your essay often treats creation and evolution as if they are simply two equal scientific theories. In an academic context, you may need to define your terms more carefully:
Is your argument about teaching both in science class?
Or teaching both in a comparative religion, philosophy, or social studies context?

Clarifying this will make your argument more precise and academically credible.

Suggested revision plan
Write a clearer thesis.
Replace all  with real evidence.
Reorganize body paragraphs by main reason.
Add clearer explanations after each example.
Present counterarguments more specifically and fairly.
Edit for tone, grammar, and sentence clarity.

Final comment
You have a strong foundation because your essay clearly takes a position and attempts real argumentation. The next step is to make the essay more focused, evidence-based, and academic. If you revise the structure and support, your argument will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report on Your Essay about Euthanasia

Your essay shows clear engagement with a difficult ethical issue, and you take a definite position: although euthanasia raises moral concerns, you believe personal suffering and individual choice matter. This is a good starting point for an argumentative essay because you are not just summarizing opinions; you are trying to make a claim.

Strengths
Strong topic choice and seriousness  
   You treat euthanasia as a complex moral and social issue, which gives your essay depth.

A clear personal voice  
   Phrases such as “I feel that…” and “I believe…” show your position clearly. This can be effective in an argument, especially when supported by evidence.

Awareness of different perspectives  
   You mention supporters, opponents, religion, law, medicine, and family feelings. This shows you understand the issue has many sides.

Main areas to improve

Clarify your thesis
Your position is not fully consistent. In the introduction, you seem to support euthanasia as a personal freedom. Later, you say allowing mercy killing widely would be “outrageous,” and you also argue that life is God’s gift and not society’s choice to end it. At the end, you again support patient choice in some cases.

What to do:  
Revise your thesis so the reader knows exactly what you argue. For example:

If you support euthanasia in limited cases:  
  “Although euthanasia raises serious moral and religious concerns, competent patients suffering from incurable and extreme pain should have the right to choose it under strict legal controls.”

This would help unify the essay.

Improve organization
Right now, several paragraphs repeat similar ideas about values, beliefs, and morality. The essay would be stronger with a clearer structure.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: define euthanasia and state your thesis
Body Paragraph 1: arguments in favor of euthanasia
Body Paragraph 2: arguments against euthanasia
Body Paragraph 3: legal/medical example (such as Dr. Kevorkian)

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that affirmative action is still necessary, and you try to consider both supporters’ and opponents’ views. criticisms such as “reverse discrimination” and “quotas weaken merit” are not fully convincing.

What to do

Body 2: legality
Body 3: why it is still necessary
Body 4: respond to major counterarguments
Conclusion: restate argument and significance

Also, each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence and stay focused on one idea.

Evidence and source use
This is the biggest weakness. Many quotations are incomplete: “”. Also, some claims are broad but unsupported, such as statements about what opponents believe or what “blacks probably got into these situations” because of.

Identify all sources clearly.
Explain how each quotation supports your point.
Avoid unsupported generalizations about groups.

Instead of:
> “Opponents state that it is up to blacks to raise their morals…”

You could write:
> “Some opponents argue that social problems within Black communities should be solved without race-conscious hiring policies. However, this view overlooks the structural effects of long-term discrimination in housing, education, and employment.”

This sounds more precise and academic.

Tone and academic style
At times the tone becomes too absolute or emotional. Phrases like “nearly infallible” weaken your credibility because they sound exaggerated. Some wording also risks stereotyping.

What to do
Use cautious academic language:
“suggests” instead of “proves”
“some opponents argue” instead of “opponents state”
“may strengthen” instead of “will make infallible”

Also, avoid direct conversational phrases like:

Feedback Report

You have a clear position: you argue that the case against teaching New Age beliefs is stronger than the case for it. Your essay also shows effort in comparing two sides of an issue, which is an important argumentative skill. To revise effectively, focus on thesis clarity, evidence quality, organization, and academic tone.

Strengthen the thesis and focus
Your main claim appears near the end of the introduction:

> “...the case against the teaching of new age beliefs is stronger than the case for it...”

This is a workable thesis, but the introduction is too long and unfocused before you reach it. The opening about “Yoda/yoga” is humorous, but it does not help an academic argument. Also, much of the introduction discusses yoga, “new age” people, political correctness, and American society in ways that are not directly connected to your central claim.

Revise by:
Removing the joke opening.
Defining the exact issue early: Are you arguing about teaching New Age beliefs in schools, in society, or in general?
Making your thesis more precise.

For example:
“This essay argues that the case against teaching New Age beliefs in public schools is stronger than the case in favor because opposing sources use more credible evidence, clearer consequences, and a more consistent value system.”

Improve organization
Right now, some paragraphs drift between topics. For example, one paragraph starts by discussing definitions, then moves to practices such as yoga and meditation. Later paragraphs mix source evaluation, your personal reaction, and consequences.

Try a clearer structure:
Introduction + thesis
Define “New Age” and explain the debate
Weaknesses in arguments supporting New Age teaching
Strengths in arguments opposing New Age teaching
Conclusion

Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that clearly supports your thesis.

Example:
“Arguments in favor of teaching New Age beliefs are weakened by vague definitions and limited source credibility.”
“Arguments against teaching New Age beliefs appear stronger because they present concrete examples of perceived harm.”

Use stronger and more academic evidence
This is the biggest issue in the essay. You often judge credibility based on whether a source agrees with your beliefs. For example, you treat the Bible as automatically stronger evidence because it is “true and established.” In university writing, you need to explain why a source is reliable in academic terms: author expertise, publication quality, evidence, logic, and verifiability.

Also, relying heavily on Christian magazines such as *Christianity Today* and *Moody* may weaken your argument if your audience does not already share those beliefs. These are not neutral sources on this topic. Likewise, saying a source is powerful because “more people” read it is not a strong measure of truth.

Revise by:
Evaluating all sources by the same standard.
Distinguishing between belief-based authority and academic evidence.
Using more neutral language such as “from a Christian perspective” rather than assuming universal agreement.

Remove unsupported personal reactions
Your paragraph about running out of your room to ask your neighbors is lively, but it is not appropriate evidence in an academic essay. Personal reactions can appear in reflective writing, but here they distract from the argument.

Instead of:
> “I honestly ran out of my room…”

Write:
“This claim appears questionable because the author provides no statistical evidence to support it.”

That sounds more formal and persuasive.

Address logic more carefully
Some claims need stronger reasoning. For example:
“Because the case against the teaching of New Age ideas reaches more people, the argument is more powerful.”  
This confuses popularity with validity.
“Because of the large amount of ‘personal’ values…the objective of arguing this topic as a question of value must be thrown out.”  
This is too absolute and unclear.

Try to explain *how* a source fails: Does it generalize? Lack evidence? Use emotional language? Misrepresent the opposing side?

Fix style, grammar, and word choice
There are many sentences that are long, repetitive, or awkward. Some examples:
“the te aching” → spacing/typing error
“that one authors opinion” → that one author’s opinion
“furthermore” should be capitalized when it begins a sentence
“can not” → usually cannot
“many time made reference” → many times made reference
“attaching it legally” → probably challenging it legally

Also watch capitalization: New Age should be used consistently.

Important content issue
Several places contain , which suggests missing quotations or incomplete evidence. You must either insert the missing quotations properly or remove references to them. Right now, the essay feels incomplete.

Final advice
Your revision should aim to sound more precise, more academic, and less personal. You have a strong opinion, but the essay will improve most if you:
narrow the topic,
state the thesis earlier and more clearly,
organize paragraphs logically,
evaluate sources fairly,
and replace personal belief with analyzable evidence.

You have the foundation of an argument; now the goal is to make it more rigorous and credible.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that supporters of nuclear power make a stronger case than opponents. This is a good foundation for an argumentative essay because your purpose is clear and you consistently return to it. You also organize the body around major points such as cost, safety, cleanliness, and waste, which helps the reader follow your ideas.

Strengths

Clear overall argument
   Your thesis appears in the introduction: opponents appeal to values and fears, while proponents use facts and figures. This gives the essay direction.

Logical body structure
   Each body paragraph focuses on one main supporting point. This is effective argumentative organization.

Awareness of audience and persuasion
   You are not only discussing nuclear power itself; you are analyzing *how* each side argues. That is a more thoughtful approach than simply listing pros and cons.

Main Areas to Revise

Clarify the essay’s focus
At times, the essay seems to argue for nuclear power, but at other times it seems to analyze the rhetoric of supporters and opponents. These are not exactly the same task.

For revision, decide on one central purpose:
Option A: “Nuclear power is beneficial and should be supported.”
Option B: “Supporters of nuclear power make a more persuasive argument than opponents.”

Your current thesis suggests Option B, so make sure all paragraphs support that focus. For example, when you discuss cost or safety, explain not only the facts but also why these facts make the proponents’ argument more persuasive.

Improve evidence and source use
You mention many statistics (“50 percent more expensive,” “20 percent cheaper,” “twenty and one hundred lives are saved”), but there are no sources. In academic writing, unsupported numbers weaken credibility.

For revision:
Add sources if this was based on research.
If no sources are available, avoid precise numbers and use more cautious wording.
Explain the evidence more fully instead of simply stating it.

Avoid overgeneralization and biased language
Some sentences sound too absolute or emotional, which can reduce academic credibility. For example:
“the general public becomes instantly closed minded”
“the one sided way Americans have been brought up”
“the media has obtained the reputation of blowing issues out of proportion”

These statements are broad and may seem unfair. In academic style, use more balanced language:
“many people are hesitant about nuclear power”
“media coverage often emphasizes accidents”
“public opinion may be shaped by fear of radiation and nuclear weapons”

Strengthen paragraph unity and topic sentences
Some paragraphs begin well but then become too broad or repetitive. For example, the safety paragraph spends a lot of time criticizing the media rather than directly explaining the supporters’ argument.

Try this pattern for each body paragraph:
Topic sentence
Evidence/example
Explanation
Link back to thesis

Edit grammar and word choice carefully
There are frequent grammar and vocabulary problems that make some sentences hard to read. Common issues include:

Word form: “analyzation” → analysis
Spelling/confusion: “do to” → due to
Comparison: “more cost-efficient then” → more cost-efficient than
Agreement: “a large group of opponents... are formed” → is formed
Word choice: “counter act” → counteract
Preposition/article use: “educating the public on the fact around nuclear power” → educating the public about the facts of nuclear power
Past participle: “the amount of precaution took” → the amount of precaution taken
Countable/uncountable and spelling: “meggawatts” → megawatts, “radio active” → radioactive

Also watch for run-on sentences and comma splices. Example:
“The arguments are strong in both favors.”
This should be revised to:
“Arguments on both sides are strong.”

Revise the introduction and conclusion
The opening about “a nuclear bomb” is dramatic, but it does not directly fit an essay about nuclear power generation. Because bombs and power plants are different, this opening may confuse the reader.

A better introduction would begin with public debate over nuclear energy, safety, and energy independence.

Your conclusion should summarize your main argument more formally. The final phrase “our revolutionary country of life, liberty, property” sounds emotional and not closely connected to your analysis. End by restating why the proponents’ evidence is more convincing.

Suggested Revision Priorities

Rewrite the thesis so the essay has one clear focus.
Add or improve evidence and explain it.
Replace emotional or overly general language with formal academic language.
Edit sentence structure, grammar, and word choice.
Strengthen the conclusion.

Final Comment

You have strong ideas and a solid basic structure. The most important next step is to make the essay more academic: clearer focus, stronger evidence, and more precise language. Once revised, your argument will become much more persuasive and credible.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows several strengths. You address an important social issue, consider both benefits and drawbacks of school integration, and include both outside sources and personal experience. This gives your argument depth. You also show awareness that the issue is complex rather than simply “good” or “bad,” which is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay.

Main issue: make your thesis clearer
Your introduction gives background, but the central argument is not fully clear. At the end, you write that the debate concerns “value and consequence,” but you do not clearly state your own position.

Try to write a thesis that directly answers the question. For example:

School integration can improve academic opportunities and race relations, but it succeeds only when schools and communities provide strong social support for students.

This kind of thesis is clear, arguable, and matches your later discussion.

Organization: improve paragraph focus
Your essay generally follows a logical order:
educational benefits,
possible harms,
race relations,
community influence.

This is good. However, some paragraphs are too long and include several ideas at once. For revision:

Make sure all sentences in the paragraph support that one main idea.
End paragraphs by linking the idea back to your thesis.

For example, the paragraph beginning “Even though there are some individuals…” could be shortened and focused specifically on self-esteem and adjustment difficulties.

Use of evidence: explain and cite more carefully
You include studies, books, and personal experience, which is excellent. But sometimes the evidence is incomplete or not explained enough.

Problems:
Some quotations or references are missing: `""`.
Some sources are mentioned too generally, such as “a study done by two researchers from New York.”
After presenting evidence, you should explain how it supports your argument.

Suggestion:
Use this pattern:
make a claim,
give evidence,
explain why the evidence matters.

Example:
Claim: Early integration can help academic success.
Evidence: Students entering desegregated schools in kindergarten or first grade achieved more than those transferred later.
Explanation: This suggests that younger children adapt more easily to new academic and social expectations.

Also, your personal example is interesting, but be careful not to generalize too broadly from one experience.

Tone and fairness: avoid overgeneralization
This is the most important revision area. Some sentences may sound too sweeping or may unintentionally stereotype groups. For example:

“many of them were rebellious”
“Many black students took advantage…”
“the black community” / “the white community” as if each group thinks the same way

These statements are too broad and may weaken your credibility. In academic writing, use more precise and cautious language.

For example:
Instead of “Many black students took advantage…”
Try “Some students appeared to take advantage of administrators’ reluctance to discipline behavior that might be misinterpreted as racial bias.”

This version is more specific, balanced, and academic.

Language and grammar
Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are several grammar and word-choice issues. Below are common patterns to revise.

Word choice
“infested with crime and drugs” → too emotional; try “affected by crime and drug-related problems”
“conductive to learning” → “conducive to learning”
“hinderance” → “hindrance”
“implemented into the program” → “added to the program” or “enrolled in the program”

Sentence structure / grammar
“The ethical dispute over school integration has based itself…”
  → awkward; try “The ethical debate over school integration is based on questions of values and consequences…”
“As can seen”
  → “As can be seen”
Watch comma use. Some sentences are very long and should be divided into two shorter sentences.

Style
Try to reduce repetition of phrases like “black students” and “white students” when possible. Use alternatives such as students, transfer students, participants in the program, or children from different racial backgrounds, when the meaning remains clear.

Conclusion
Your final paragraph has a good main point: school integration needs support from the wider community. However, the essay would be stronger if the conclusion:
restated your thesis more directly,
summarized the main benefits and challenges,
ended with a clear final judgment.

For example, you could conclude that integration is not harmful by nature, but its success depends on thoughtful implementation, teacher support, and community commitment.

Revision priorities
When you revise, focus on these in order:
Write a clear thesis stating your position.
Replace broad or stereotypical statements with precise academic language.
Fix missing source information and explain evidence more fully.
Shorten long sentences and correct grammar/word choice.
Strengthen the conclusion.

Overall, this essay has a strong topic, relevant ideas, and a thoughtful attempt to examine both sides. With clearer argumentation, more careful wording, and stronger source integration, it can become a much more persuasive academic essay.

   - Explain the “renting a womb” / “baby selling” debate
   - Clarify why you think this is unethical

Counterargument + rebuttal
   - Present the strongest pro-surrogacy view fairly
   - Then explain why you still disagree

Conclusion
   - Restate thesis
   - Summarize strongest reasons

Logic and clarity
Some claims need more careful reasoning.

Examples
“Biologically there are only two parents, excluding the adoptive mother.”
  - This is too absolute and confusing. In surrogacy, legal, social, and biological parenthood may differ. Be precise.
“They do not see the surrogate mother as a person who has feelings…”
  - This is a strong claim. Not all intended parents think this way. Say “in some cases” or “surrogacy can risk reducing…”
“There are many people in the world whose lives have not been altered because they are infertile.”
  - This does not strongly prove your point. Infertility can be deeply painful, even if people cope with it differently.

Try to avoid overgeneralizing. Strong arguments are usually more careful and qualified.

Language issues to revise
Your meaning is usually understandable, but grammar and word choice need attention.

Common issues
Subject-verb agreement
  - *Health services, however, argues* → *Health services, however, argue*  
  - *Others feels* → *Others feel*
Possessive forms
  - *the infertile couples lives* → *the infertile couple’s lives*
  - *the fathers* → *the father’s*
  - *the surrogate mothers* → *the surrogate mother’s*
Word form / spelling
  - *mother hood* → *motherhood*
  - *substancial* → *substantial*
  - *had bore* → *had borne*
Pronoun/reference clarity
  - *it* and *they* are sometimes unclear. Make sure each pronoun clearly refers to one noun.
Articles and plurals
  - *who has no feeling* → *who has no feelings*
  - *just as much the surrogate mothers as it is the fathers* → *just as much the surrogate mother’s as it is the father’s*

Style and tone
Your tone is passionate, which helps the essay, but sometimes the wording is too emotional or informal for academic writing.

Example
*“just a walking womb”* is vivid, but in an academic essay it may sound too dramatic unless it is a quoted phrase from a source.
Instead, you could write:
  - *Surrogacy may reduce the surrogate mother to a reproductive function rather than recognizing her full humanity.*

A possible revised thesis
Your current thesis is understandable but repetitive. You might write:

“Surrogate motherhood is ethically problematic because it can create serious emotional harm, blur parental rights, and risk treating women and children as commodities.”

Final advice
For revision, focus first on:
Completing the missing evidence
Reorganizing each paragraph around one main point
Making claims more precise and less absolute
Editing grammar and word forms carefully

You already have a strong opinion and a good ethical topic. With clearer organization and stronger evidence, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a strong sense of urgency. Your essay argues consistently that drug prices are too high and that federal regulation is needed. This gives your paper a strong argumentative purpose. You also try to use examples and statistics, which is important in persuasive writing.

What is working well

Clear thesis
   Your main claim appears at the end of the first paragraph: federal intervention is necessary through a price regulation review board. This gives the essay direction.

Strong engagement with the topic
   Your voice is passionate and committed. The final paragraph, especially from a nursing student’s perspective, shows why the issue matters in real life.

Use of evidence and examples
   You include statistics, comparisons with Canada and Europe, and specific drug/company examples. This helps move the essay beyond opinion.

Main revision priorities

Improve accuracy and credibility
Some of your examples seem inaccurate or confused, which weakens the argument. For example:
Depo-Provera is not an oral contraceptive; it is an injectable contraceptive.
Clozapril appears to be the wrong drug name; perhaps you mean clozapine.
Clozapine is mainly used for schizophrenia, not senile dementia.
The sentence about cancer and AIDS patients depending on “Depo-Provera and AZT” does not make sense together.

Because this is an argumentative essay, factual accuracy is essential. Check every drug name, use, company name, and statistic carefully.

Fill in incomplete ideas
There are places where the essay is unfinished:
“This price-gouging shows in high hospital bills, which .”
“This happens partly because .”

These gaps interrupt your logic. Make sure every paragraph contains a complete explanation.

Strengthen paragraph structure
Several body paragraphs begin with examples before making the main point clear. A stronger structure is:

Topic sentence: state the claim of the paragraph.
Evidence: give statistics or examples.
Explanation: show how the evidence supports your thesis.
Link: connect back to your argument for regulation.

For example, your paragraph about Canada and Europe should begin with a clearer claim such as:  
“International comparisons suggest that price review boards can reduce drug costs without eliminating access.”

Use a more formal academic tone
Some wording is vivid, but too emotional for academic argument:
“mind boggling”
“price blackmail”
“a crunch in the pocketbooks”
“skyrocketing of already outrageous prices”

Try more neutral language:
“very high”
“coercive pricing practice”
“financial burden”
“rapid price increases”

A formal tone will make your argument sound more credible.

Language and grammar issues to revise

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long or awkward. Example:

“Drugs promoted the longevity of many patients who otherwise wouldn't have lived otherwise.”

Better:
“Many drugs have extended the lives of patients who might otherwise have died.”

Word choice
A few phrases are not natural in English:
“people victimized by cancer” → “people suffering from cancer”
“made the quality of life for several people increase” → “improved the quality of life for many people”
“recup the cost” → “recoup the cost”

Grammar and mechanics
Be careful with:
subject-verb agreement: “industry argues,” not “agrues”
article use: “a federal price regulatory board”
plural/singular: “these Federal price regulatory board are” should be “this federal price regulatory board is” or “these federal price regulatory boards are”
capitalization: do not capitalize words like “federal” or “mothers, fathers, and children” unless necessary
spelling: “receive,” “proper,” “can’t,” “European,” “painkillers”

Punctuation
You often join ideas with commas when they should be separate sentences. Read each sentence aloud and check whether it needs a period or semicolon.

Suggestions for organization

A possible revised outline:

Introduction
   - background on medical advances
   - problem of rising drug prices
   - thesis: the federal government should create a drug price review board

Body Paragraph 1
   - rising drug prices compared with inflation
   - explain burden on consumers, especially elderly people

Body Paragraph 2
   - international comparison: Canada/Europe
   - explain how review boards can lower prices

Body Paragraph 3
   - company practices that increase prices
   - one or two accurate, well-researched examples only

Body Paragraph 4
   - impact on patients and healthcare providers
   - include your nursing perspective

Conclusion
   - restate thesis
   - emphasize fairness, access, and public health

Final advice
Your essay has a strong argument, but it needs more careful research, clearer organization, and more formal language. Focus first on correcting factual errors and incomplete sections. Then revise paragraph structure and grammar. You have the basis for a persuasive essay; with better accuracy and clearer writing, it will become much stronger.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position and a logical overall structure. You introduce the topic, present three main reasons, and restate your argument in the conclusion. This is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. Your tone is generally formal, and your ideas are easy to follow. For revision, the main areas to improve are evidence, precision, paragraph development, and language accuracy.

Strengths

Clear thesis: Your introduction clearly states your position that the death penalty should be abolished.
Logical organization: The essay is divided into three major points: ineffectiveness, constitutional problems, and ethics.
Awareness of opposing views: You mention the other side before arguing against it, which is important in academic argument.

Most important revision issue: missing or incomplete evidence

The biggest problem is that many quotations or facts are incomplete and marked as “”. Because of this, your body paragraphs often make claims without fully supporting them. In academic writing, your argument becomes convincing only when the reader can see the exact evidence.

What to do
Replace every “” with the full statistic, quotation, or explanation.
Give the source, and if possible, the year.
After each source, explain how it supports your point.

Example
Instead of:
> “This study also showed that, .”

Write:
> “This study also showed that states with higher execution rates did not have lower murder rates. This finding weakens the claim that the death penalty deters crime.”

Improve paragraph development and analysis

Some paragraphs list studies one after another, but do not always explain them enough. In academic argument, evidence should not just be presented; it should be interpreted.

Try this structure for body paragraphs:
Topic sentence  
Evidence/example  
Explanation of the evidence  
Link back to thesis  

For example, in the first body paragraph, you mention several studies. After each study, add 1–2 sentences explaining why the study matters and how it proves the death penalty is ineffective.

Address counterarguments more fully

You mention supporters’ views, but the response is a little brief. A strong argumentative essay should fairly present the opposing side and then refute it with evidence.

For example, in the ethics section, instead of only saying “fair is fair” is popular, explain why some people believe retribution is just, and then show why you think life imprisonment is a better alternative.

Language and style corrections

Your writing is generally understandable, but some grammar and style problems should be corrected.

A. Capitalization
“both sides cite statistics...” → “Both sides cite statistics...”

B. Word choice
“put a stop to it all together” → “put a stop to it altogether”
“nu merous” should be corrected to “numerous”

C. Avoid informal or vague phrases
“fair is fair” is acceptable if discussing common opinion, but in formal writing, explain it more academically as retributive justice.
“The fact is” can be replaced with a more formal phrase such as In reality or Evidence shows that.

D. Sentence clarity
Some sentences are awkward or too absolute.

Example:
> “Therefore, it is clear that capital punishment should be abolished…”

This is understandable, but sounds too certain too early. A more academic version:
> “Therefore, the evidence suggests that capital punishment should be abolished…”

Be careful with factual accuracy

You mention Roger Keith Coleman and say he “was executed on May 20, 1982,” but this appears questionable. Dates and facts must be checked carefully. Incorrect details damage credibility.

Also, some sources seem old (for example, a 1935 study). Older evidence can be useful, but you should also include more recent sources if possible.

Strengthen the introduction and conclusion

Introduction
Your introduction is clear, but it is a little long. You can make it more concise and direct.

Conclusion
Your conclusion mostly repeats earlier points. Try ending with a stronger final thought, such as the importance of justice, human rights, or the risk of irreversible error.

Final advice for revision

When revising, focus on these priorities:
Complete all missing evidence
Check facts and sources carefully
Explain each example more clearly
Use more formal academic language
Proofread for grammar, capitalization, and word choice

Overall, your essay has a solid argumentative structure and a strong central claim. With fuller evidence, more careful source use, and clearer analysis, it can become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: Prozac should continue to be prescribed because it helps people medically and socially. This is a good foundation for an argumentative essay. You also try to include counterarguments, which strengthens your discussion. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Strengths
Clear thesis: Your main argument appears at the end of the introduction.
Logical organization: The essay moves from medical benefits, to safety, to social effects, then to criticism and rebuttal.
Use of examples: Cases such as Sam and Tess help make the topic concrete.
Counterargument included: You mention suicide reports and side effects, which is important in argument writing.

Main problems to revise

A. Missing evidence and incomplete quotations
Several places contain “”, which means key evidence is missing. This is the most serious problem. Because your essay depends heavily on expert opinions, the missing quotations make the argument feel incomplete and less credible.

What to do:
Replace every “” with the full quotation or paraphrase.
Introduce the source clearly.
Explain how each quotation supports your point.

Example:
Instead of:
> One doctor commented that Prozac .

Try:
> One doctor argues that Prozac improves patients’ daily functioning because it reduces depressive symptoms without causing severe sedation. This supports the claim that the drug can help patients return to normal life.

B. Argument sometimes becomes too one-sided
You do mention criticism, but your replies are sometimes too quick or unsupported. For example, saying suicide cases are “a minute percentage” is not enough unless you explain why raw prescription numbers are meaningful. Also, “only a few confirmed Prozac deaths” is a serious claim and needs careful evidence.

What to do:
Acknowledge risks more carefully.
Avoid sounding dismissive.
Explain that medication should be prescribed with medical supervision, not as a perfect solution.

A stronger argument would be:
> Although Prozac may cause side effects in some patients and may not be suitable for everyone, current evidence suggests that under professional supervision it remains a valuable treatment option for many people with depression.

C. Overgeneralization and informal tone
Some sentences are too broad or conversational for academic writing.

Examples:
“people cured disease with known home remedies”
“simply ask a physician ‘for something’”
“what is wrong with making yourself better?”

These sound informal or imprecise.

What to do:
Use a more formal and specific style.

For example:
“Before modern medicine, many people relied on home remedies.”
“Today, patients often expect physicians to prescribe medication.”
“If a treatment is both safe and effective, patients should have access to it under medical guidance.”

Language and grammar issues

A. Word choice
Some phrases are unnatural or incorrect:
“banishment” → “ban”
“an architect name Sam” → “an architect named Sam”
“prescribed with Prozac” → “prescribed Prozac”
“pas social lives” → “past social lives” or better “normal social lives”
“non-addicting effects” → “non-addictive effects”

B. Sentence clarity
Some scientific explanations are confusing:
> “The drug causes the serotonin to slow in spaces between the nerve cells”

Better:
> “The drug increases the availability of serotonin in the spaces between nerve cells, which may improve communication in the brain.”

C. Grammar and style
Watch for:
article use: “the symptoms of depression” / “a physician”
verb forms: “has had” instead of “has made” in “the positive effect it already has made”
punctuation and spacing: “patients com plain” should be “patients complain”

Paragraph-level advice

Introduction
The opening is too broad. Move more quickly to the topic of antidepressants and Prozac.

Body paragraphs
Each paragraph should begin with a clear topic sentence. For example:
“Prozac is effective because it reduces symptoms of depression and related disorders.”
“In addition to its effectiveness, Prozac is considered safer than many older antidepressants.”

Conclusion
Your conclusion should restate the argument in a formal way. Avoid ending with rhetorical questions. Instead, summarize your main reasons and note that treatment decisions should be made carefully by doctors and patients.

Suggested revision priorities
Fill in all missing quotations and citations.
Improve scientific accuracy and clarity.
Use a more academic tone.
Strengthen the counterargument and rebuttal.
Edit grammar, word choice, and sentence structure.

Final comment
You already have a solid argumentative structure and a clear opinion. Your next step is to make the essay more credible, precise, and academic. Focus especially on missing evidence, formal tone, and careful handling of counterarguments. With revision, this can become a much stronger essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a recognizable argumentative structure: you introduce the debate, compare two authors, and end with a firm conclusion. This is a good foundation. To revise successfully, focus on (1) argument quality, (2) use of evidence, and (3) language accuracy/style.

Main strengths
Clear thesis: You argue that Pattullo’s article is more convincing than Wall’s.
Organized structure: The essay moves from introduction to body paragraphs to conclusion.
Comparative approach: You do not only summarize; you compare how each author handles the issue.

Biggest problem: weak critical analysis
At the moment, the essay mainly accepts Pattullo’s claims as true rather than analyzing whether his reasoning is logically strong and well supported. In academic argument, you should evaluate:
What evidence does each author use?
Are the claims based on facts, assumptions, or emotions?
Are there any stereotypes, exaggerations, or logical fallacies?

For example, you often say Pattullo’s argument is “sound,” “realistic,” or “convincing,” but you do not fully prove this. Why is it sound? What evidence supports it? Could there be another interpretation?

Also, some of Pattullo’s claims rely on assumptions about homosexual people causing discomfort or damaging unity. In revision, consider whether these are proven consequences or predictions based on fear. A stronger essay would examine that critically.

Missing evidence and quotations
Several places contain “”, which suggests missing quotations or evidence. This is a serious problem because your reader cannot judge the articles without seeing the authors’ actual words.

When revising:
Insert the exact quotations.
Introduce each quotation clearly.
Explain how the quotation supports your point.

A good pattern is:
Claim → Quote/Example → Explanation
For instance: “Pattullo argues that ____. He writes, ‘____.’ This suggests that… However, this reasoning depends on the assumption that….”

Improve fairness and academic tone
Some sentences sound too absolute or too personal:
“The obvious answer may be…”
“it will be ultimately decided…”
“Wall attacks the issue with a lighthearted attitude”

These phrases can sound biased. In academic writing, try to sound more careful and objective:
“Wall argues that…”
“Pattullo presents the issue as…”
“This claim may persuade readers who value military cohesion…”

Also, your conclusion says Pattullo changed “the opinions of many people,” but you have not provided evidence for that. If you mean your own opinion, say so directly.

Logical issues to reconsider
Some parts of the essay make comparisons that are not fully convincing. For example, comparing homosexual soldiers sharing barracks with “one woman sharing with one hundred men” is not a parallel example. The issue is not exactly the same, so this weakens the logic.

Also, be careful with statements like:
“Therefore, it would be difficult to control what goes on in the barracks.”
“This would offend many heterosexuals and result in the disintegration of camaraderie.”

These are strong conclusions, but the essay does not prove them. Ask yourself:
Is there evidence?
Is this speculation?
Could there be a counterargument?

Including one counterargument and responding to it would make your essay much stronger.

Language and grammar corrections
There are many sentence-level errors. Here are common patterns to revise:

Grammar
“there should be a ban gays serving” → “there should be a ban on gays serving” or better, “gay people should be banned from serving”
“will make on change their mind” → “will make one change one’s mind”
“Wall’s articles does not” → “Wall’s article does not”
“There would be a real concern of homosexuals interacting sexually” → “There would be concern about homosexuals engaging in sexual relationships”
“there was not reason” → “there was no reason”
“claims has had an affect” → “claims have had an effect”

Word choice
Use more precise and neutral terms:
Instead of repeatedly using “homosexuals,” consider “gay men and lesbians” or “homosexual service members,” depending on context.
“whomever” is incorrect here; use “whoever.”

Sentence structure
Some sentences are too long and awkward. For example, your introduction contains one very long sentence with several ideas. Break it into shorter sentences for clarity.

Suggested revision strategy
Add the missing quotations.
Rewrite the thesis so it clearly states *why* Pattullo is more persuasive.
Revise each body paragraph to include:
   - one main point,
   - one quotation from Pattullo or Wall,
   - your analysis of the logic and evidence.
Consider counterarguments instead of only agreeing with Pattullo.
Edit grammar and word choice for correctness and academic tone.

Possible stronger thesis
You might write something like:
> Although Pattullo presents concerns about privacy and military cohesion more concretely than Wall, many of his claims rely on assumptions rather than evidence; therefore, his argument is emotionally persuasive but not fully convincing.

This kind of thesis shows deeper critical thinking.

Overall, your essay has a solid structure, but to become a strong academic argument, it needs more evidence, more critical evaluation, and more careful language.

Strengths

   Your introduction presents the two opposing groups and ends with your own claim that the “moment of silence” is the best solution.

Organized body paragraphs

Analytical intention

Stronger analysis needed

  Example: “The author assumes that school prayer would reduce crime and teenage pregnancy, but he gives no evidence that these social problems are caused by the absence of prayer.”

reaons → *reasons*
ow → *how*

titles → *titled*

Evidence from article

Connection to your thesis

Your essay has a clear position: you oppose the death penalty because it is ineffective, immoral, and flawed in practice. Strengths

Logical organization: The essay follows a three-part argument: deterrence, morality, and problems in the legal system.
Engagement with the issue: Your writing sounds passionate and thoughtful, which helps persuade readers.

Main Areas to Revise

Complete and support your evidence

insert the exact quotation/information, or
paraphrase the source clearly in your own words.

Also, give enough information about each source. Instead of “a scientist named Forst,” write something like:

This sounds more academic and credible.

Improve formal academic tone

“Well, I believe…”
“Basically…”
“Well it has been proven…”
“right around”
“something to talk about”

Use a more formal style:
“I argue that…”
“Supporters claim that…”
“Research suggests that…”

Also avoid rhetorical questions in formal argument unless used very carefully. For example:
“If somebody… then is it moral…?”  
This can be revised to:
“If someone commits murder, it does not necessarily follow that the state acts morally by executing that person.”

Strengthen grammar and word choice

Article use:  
  “the increases crime” → “the increase in crime”  
  “a death penalty” → usually “the death penalty” or “capital punishment”

Word form:  
  “it necessity” → “its necessity”  
  “full-proof” → “foolproof”

Sentence structure:  
  “Thus, when serious criminal cases occur, and the state that they occur in does not have the death penalty, a debate occurs over it necessity.”  
  Better: “Thus, when a serious crime occurs in a state without the death penalty, public debate often arises over its necessity.”

Parallel structure:  
  “because it will not stop our criminal problems, it is immoral, and may even hinder…”  
  Better: “because it does not prevent crime, it is immoral, and it may hinder…”

Prepositions and collocations:  
  “sway governments into their own direction” → “sway governments in their direction”  
  “debating over” → “debating”  
  “murdering of the murderer” → “execution of the murderer”

Make your claims more precise
Some statements are too broad or unsupported:
“the crime rate has continued to increase”
“many politicians, especially Liberal Democrats…”
“too many cases”
“states really do not require enough [evidence]”

These need either evidence or more careful wording. In academic argument, avoid exaggeration. For example:
“Some supporters argue…”
“There have been cases in which innocent people were wrongly convicted…”

Work on paragraph development
Some paragraphs include good ideas but need clearer topic sentences and stronger explanation. A useful pattern is:

Topic sentence → evidence → explanation → link to thesis

For example, in the third body paragraph, you mention inequality, appeals, delays, and wrongful convictions. These are all useful, but the paragraph feels crowded. You could separate it into:
one paragraph on unequal legal representation,
one paragraph on wrongful conviction and systemic delay.

Suggested Revision of Thesis
Your current thesis is understandable, but you can make it more concise:

“The death penalty should be abolished because it does not effectively deter violent crime, it is morally unjustifiable, and it produces serious inequalities and errors in the justice system.”

Final Advice
When revising, focus on these priorities in order:
Fill in all missing evidence ()
Make the tone more formal
Correct grammar and wording
Add more precise support for broad claims
Improve paragraph unity

You already have a solid argumentative foundation. With clearer evidence, more formal language, and careful editing, this essay can become much more persuasive and academic.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and shows strong effort to engage both sides of a controversial issue. You also organize the paper in a recognizable argumentative structure: introduction, opposing claims, refutation, and conclusion. This is a good foundation. For revision, focus on (1) clarity of argument, (2) use of evidence, and (3) language accuracy/style.

Strengths

Clear thesis: Your main claim is evident: prayer should not be reintroduced into public school classrooms.
Awareness of counterarguments: You do not ignore the opposing side; instead, you summarize supporters’ arguments and respond to them.
Logical paragraphing: Most body paragraphs focus on one main point, which helps readability.
Appropriate academic tone: In general, the essay sounds formal and argumentative.

Most Important Revision: Missing and Incomplete Evidence

The biggest problem is the many placeholders such as “”. Because of these missing quotations or details, several paragraphs feel incomplete and your argument loses credibility. In an academic argument, readers must see exactly what source says what, and then how you interpret it.

What to do:
Replace every “” with the exact quotation, paraphrase, or evidence.
Introduce each source clearly: author, title, and relevance.
After each quotation, explain how it supports your point.

Example:
Instead of:
> As the writer of "Say a Little Prayer" states .

Write:
> In “Say a Little Prayer,” [author’s name] argues that school prayer could restore moral values by encouraging respect and discipline. However, this claim assumes that religious ritual directly causes moral behavior, an assumption the author does not prove.

This way, your essay becomes analytical, not just descriptive.

Argument Development

Sometimes your reasoning is promising but not fully developed.

Example 1: Polling argument
You correctly question the “75% of Americans” statistic, but you should go further:
Who was surveyed?
Were they parents, voters, or students?
Does majority opinion justify policy in a constitutional matter?

This is an important point: constitutional rights are not decided only by majority preference. Adding this would make your rebuttal much stronger.

Example 2: “25%” argument
Your point about minority students feeling excluded is good, but the paragraph becomes unclear because of the missing source material. Also, instead of saying this is “not a healthy attitude,” explain the principle more directly:
public schools serve students from many religions and non-religious backgrounds;
compulsory prayer may pressure students to conform;
this undermines religious freedom.

Example 3: Morality and social problems
Your statement “correlation is not causation” is strong. Develop it with one or two concrete examples or explanation. For instance:
social violence may be connected to poverty, family instability, mental health, access to weapons, etc
therefore, school prayer alone cannot reasonably be presented as a solution.

Organization and Cohesion

The overall structure is solid, but some transitions are repetitive or confusing.

Issues:
You use “A second factor” and later “A second claim”; the numbering becomes inconsistent.
Some paragraphs introduce a source but do not clearly connect it back to your thesis.
The essay spends a long time on Klinghoffer’s examples; this section could be shortened and tied more directly to your argument.

Suggestions:
Use clearer topic sentences.
Make sure each paragraph answers: What is the claim? What is the evidence? Why does it matter?
Consider grouping ideas more simply:
  1. constitutional/religious freedom issue  
  2. weakness of “moral improvement” argument  
  3. practical problem of non-sectarian prayer  
  4. better alternative: voluntary student religious groups

Language and Grammar

Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are several grammar and word-choice issues. Below are common patterns to fix.

Word choice / spelling
writes → writers
first Amendment → First Amendment
wide-spread → widespread
intrest → interest
oppotunity → opportunity
parishoners → parishioners
at the sake of → at the expense of

Sentence clarity
Example:
> “These numbers, according to the supporters, are to reflect the desire to return prayer to public schools…”

Better:
> “Supporters argue that these numbers reflect public desire to return prayer to public schools.”

Punctuation / grammar
Example:
> “This prayer according to opponents is anything but the perfect solution.”

Better:
> “According to opponents, this prayer is anything but the perfect solution.”

Example:
> “The prayer is void of any feeling or sincerity  As Klinghoffer writes…”

Final Advice for Revision

Fill in all missing evidence (“”).
Clarify your strongest reasons: freedom from imposed religion, weak causal link between prayer and morality, and problems with non-sectarian prayer.

Your essay has a clear position: government-sponsored prayer in public schools should not be instituted. Main Areas to Revise

Clarify and narrow your thesis

You could make it more precise:
Suggested revision:

This version gives readers a clear roadmap.

Improve paragraph unity
Some paragraphs begin with one idea but shift to another. For example, the paragraph about parents teaching morals also moves into the role of schools and the seriousness of prayer. Try to keep one main point per paragraph.

A possible structure:

Paragraph 2: moral education should primarily come from family/community

Either insert the real quotation,

provide the quotation or paraphrase
analyze it
