Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that animal testing should continue because it protects human life. You also try to include the opposing view, which is a good feature of argumentative writing. The paper shows strong engagement with the topic and uses examples to support your claims. For revision, the main goals are to improve organization, clarity, evidence, tone, and language accuracy.

Strengthen your thesis and focus
Your main argument appears in several places, but it should be stated more clearly in the introduction. Right now, the first paragraph is very broad and spends too much time on general ideas about morals and dilemmas.

Suggestion: end the introduction with a direct thesis, for example:  
“Although animal testing causes suffering, it remains ethically justifiable when it is necessary to save human lives and when no effective alternative exists.”

This thesis is more precise and gives your essay a clear direction.

Improve organization
At the moment, the essay feels repetitive. You return many times to the same point: animal testing saves humans. Some paragraphs also lack clear topic sentences.

A stronger structure could be:

Introduction: present the issue and thesis  
Opposing view: why animal rights activists object  
Your argument 1: medical benefits and life-saving research  
Your argument 2: lack of adequate alternatives  
Your argument 3: limits—some testing should be banned  
Conclusion: restate thesis and explain your final judgment

This structure would make your reasoning easier to follow.

Use evidence more carefully
You include examples such as Timmy Baker, Dr. Traystman, thalidomide, and the Draize test. These examples help, but some are not fully explained, and some claims seem uncertain or inaccurate.

For example:
The thalidomide example is confusing and may be factually incorrect. Be careful: in argument essays, inaccurate evidence weakens your credibility.
The Timmy Baker story is emotional, but you should explain more clearly how it proves the necessity of animal testing.
Several quotations are missing and marked only with . These must be completed or removed.

Advice: use fewer examples, but explain them more fully and make sure they are reliable.

Develop the counterargument fairly
You do mention the views of animal rights activists, which is good. However, sometimes you present them too briefly or dismissively.

For instance, instead of only asking, “How can this be ethical?”, explain the ethical principle behind their view:
animals can feel pain
causing suffering requires strong moral justification
some experiments are unnecessary or cruel

Then respond to that argument directly. This will make your essay more persuasive because it shows you understand the opposition.

Use a more academic tone
Some expressions are too emotional or informal for academic writing. Examples:
“Thank God for little Timmy”
“as they please”
“who’s life is more sacred”
“superior species”

These phrases sound more like speech or personal opinion than academic argument. Try to use neutral, formal language.

For example:
Instead of “Thank God for little Timmy”, write “This case suggests that such research may produce life-saving treatments.”
Instead of “Animals were put on earth for humans to do with as they please”, write “Some religious perspectives interpret human dominion over animals as permitting their use for human benefit.”

Address weak reasoning
Some arguments need more careful logic. For example:
Saying humans are the “superior species” does not by itself justify animal testing.
Comparing animal testing with hunting or bullfighting is not a strong defense; the fact that other harmful practices exist does not make testing ethical.
The argument about viruses and bacteria being “animals” is incorrect and weakens the essay.

Try to focus on stronger ethical reasoning:
human survival and public health
necessity
regulation and minimizing suffering
lack of alternatives

Language and grammar
Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are many grammar and word-choice errors. Common problems include:

Sentence structure
“Some of them easily justified”  
  → “Some of them are easily justified”

Possessives
“which friends side to take”  
  → “which friend’s side to take”

Word choice
“decision making tactics”  
  → “decision-making processes”
“base this argument of experience”  
  → “base this argument on experience”
“Pain less”  
  → “painless”
“my not be conscious”  
  → “may not be conscious”
“a medicine aides in the healing off birth defects”  
  → this sentence needs complete rewriting for accuracy and grammar

Agreement and article use
“if human’s want”  
  → “if humans want”
“Animal researches also argue”  
  → “Animal researchers also argue”

Also check spelling carefully: insure/ensure, controversy, researchers, Raystman/Traystman.

Conclusion
Your conclusion clearly restates your opinion, but ending with a rhetorical question makes the argument feel simplified. A stronger conclusion would summarize your reasoning:
animal testing is morally troubling
some forms should be restricted or eliminated
however, medically necessary testing remains justified when it saves human lives and alternatives do not exist

Final advice

writing a clear thesis  
organizing paragraphs by main points  
replacing emotional language with formal argument  
correcting grammar and incomplete quotations  
using accurate, well-explained evidence

With revision, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and strong passion. You argue consistently that women’s college basketball deserves equal respect, resources, and opportunities. Strengths

   Your main claim is easy to identify: women’s basketball teams should be treated equally to men’s teams.

Engaging introduction
   The hypothetical TV-switching example is dramatic and effective. It creates emotional impact and introduces the issue of unequal attention in sports media.

Strong sense of purpose
   Your voice is confident, and your commitment to the topic is clear throughout the essay.

Use of comparison
   You often compare men’s and women’s teams directly. This helps support your argument about inequality.

Main Areas to Revise

Complete and strengthen your evidence
The biggest weakness is that many sentences contain “” placeholders where evidence, quotations, or examples should be. Because of these gaps, some paragraphs feel unfinished and less convincing. In argumentative writing, claims need support.

Introduce sources clearly.
Explain how each piece of evidence proves your point.

For example, instead of:

Paragraph 5: Counterarguments about pressure and academics

Examples:
“at least we don’t belong in the kitchen anymore”
“the men screwed things up first”
“lower end of the totem pole”

These expressions are vivid, but they weaken the formal tone. Replace them with more neutral academic language.

Word form / spelling

“otherhand” → other hand

“fell” → feel
“Summit” → likely Summitt

Articles and plurals

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose and a recognizable structure. You introduce the issue, present opposing views, and try to refute them one by one. This is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. Your position is also consistent throughout: you argue that distributing condoms in high schools is necessary to help prevent AIDS. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Strengthen the thesis and focus
Your thesis appears at the end of the first paragraph, which is good. However, it can be made more precise and academic. For example:

Current idea: “the distribution of condoms to sexually active teens in high school is a step which needs to be taken...”

Possible revision:  
“Despite moral and practical objections, distributing condoms in public high schools is a necessary public-health measure because it can reduce the risk of HIV transmission, support students who are already sexually active, and complement sex education.”

This version clearly previews your main reasons.

Replace missing evidence
The biggest problem is the many places marked with . These gaps weaken your credibility because key claims have no support. Since this is an argumentative essay, every major point needs evidence.

When revising, do one of the following:
insert the exact statistic or quotation from a reliable source, or
if you no longer have the source, remove the placeholder and rewrite the sentence more generally.

For example, instead of “according to the Centers for Disease Control, ,” write a complete sentence with the actual statistic and year.

Also, be careful not to rely too heavily on vague references like “According to Time.” Academic writing is stronger when you cite more authoritative sources such as CDC reports, medical journals, or government studies.

Improve paragraph development
Your body paragraphs follow a useful pattern: objection → response. That is effective. However, some paragraphs need clearer topic sentences and stronger explanation after the evidence.

For instance, in paragraph 3, after mentioning the Johns Hopkins study and Paul Epstein, explain more clearly how those examples support your claim. Do not assume the evidence speaks for itself. Add 1–2 sentences of analysis:
What exactly do the results show?
Why do they weaken the argument that condom distribution promotes promiscuity?

This will make your argument more persuasive.

Avoid overstatement
Some sentences are too absolute, which can make your argument sound less reasonable.

Example:
“This shows that the idea of abstinence is a mere fantasy.”

This is too strong. A better version would be:
“This suggests that abstinence-only messages are not sufficient for many teenagers.”

Academic argument is usually stronger when it sounds balanced rather than extreme.

Work on style and tone
Your tone is passionate, but some wording is too emotional for academic writing. Phrases like:
“this horrible epidemic”
“should be reason enough”
“mere fantasy”

can be revised into a more formal tone. Try using neutral language:
“serious public-health problem”
“strong evidence in support of”
“unlikely to be effective for all students”

A formal tone will make your essay sound more credible.

Sentence-level grammar and word choice
Your meaning is usually clear, but there are several grammar and word-choice errors. Here are some important examples:

“grounds to pre-marital sex” → grounds related to premarital sex / because they oppose premarital sex
“steer away form” → steer away from
“John Hopkins Medical School” → Johns Hopkins Medical School
“the rate in which AIDS is spreading” → the rate at which AIDS is spreading
“than anyone” → then anyone? Actually here it should be then only if indicating result, but your sentence needs: then anyone who engages... should have easy access; however, the sentence is better as: then sexually active people should have easy access to them
“more apt to practice safer sex” → acceptable, but more likely to practice safer sex is more formal

Also, watch article use and plural forms, which are common difficulties for Chinese speakers:
“staff volunteers hand out condoms” is fine
“to any student that wanted them” → to any student who wanted them
“teens in high schools” may be better than “teens in high school” depending on meaning

Improve coherence
Use transitions more carefully. You already use “Another criticism” and “The final argument,” which helps. To improve flow, connect ideas more explicitly:
“Although abstinence is the only completely effective way to avoid infection, this approach alone does not address the reality that many teenagers are already sexually active.”
“Therefore, condom distribution should be understood as a harm-reduction strategy rather than an encouragement of sexual activity.”

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion mostly repeats earlier points. Try to do more than restate. Briefly summarize the argument and emphasize why the issue matters in policy terms.

For example:
“Because many teenagers are sexually active despite abstinence-based messages, schools have a responsibility to provide both education and practical protection. Condom distribution does not replace moral guidance; it addresses a real health risk.”

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:
Fill in or remove all  placeholders.  
Make your claims more precise and less absolute.  
Add analysis after each piece of evidence.  
Correct grammar and use a more formal academic tone.

You have a solid argumentative structure. With stronger evidence and more careful language, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a consistent argumentative focus: you argue that organized prayer should not return to public schools because it conflicts with fairness, equality, and religious freedom. Your tone is serious and persuasive, and you often try to anticipate the opposing side’s claims, which is a strong feature in argumentative writing.

What is working well

Clear thesis
Your introduction states your main claim clearly: supporters of school prayer rely on weak reasoning, while opponents offer a stronger argument based on fairness and equality.

Logical organization
Most body paragraphs follow a useful pattern: present the supporters’ claim, then explain why it is weak, and finally offer your own counterargument.

Strong sense of audience
You write as if you are addressing readers who may disagree with you, which is effective in argumentative essays.

Main areas to revise

Missing content
Several sentences contain “”, which leaves important ideas incomplete. This is the biggest problem because it makes some paragraphs impossible to fully understand. Before revising style, first restore the missing claims, evidence, or quotations.

Develop evidence more carefully
Many claims are strong, but some need clearer support. For example:

“A prayer alone cannot subdue major societal problems.”
“No data shows that prayer actually causes a better educational or social environment.”

These are reasonable points, but they need evidence or examples from law, history, or research. Try adding:
a court case about school prayer,
statistics or scholarly evidence,
a specific example showing the difference between private prayer and school-sponsored prayer.

Avoid overstatement
Some sentences are too absolute or emotional, which can weaken your credibility. For example:
“This view holds no validity.”
“The only logical and fair answer…”
“constant threat to their morals or beliefs”

In academic argument, it is usually stronger to use more precise language:
“This view is unconvincing because…”
“A more equitable solution is…”
“may create pressure on students whose beliefs differ…”

Improve paragraph unity
A few paragraphs mix several ideas at once. For example, the second body paragraph discusses teen pregnancy, violence, broken homes, segregation, lynching, and women’s oppression. These are interesting points, but the paragraph needs a clearer central topic sentence and smoother connections. Try focusing each paragraph on one main reason.

Language and grammar issues

Sentence-level corrections

Original: “Those who oppose prayer in public schools focus on fairness and equality to everybody…”  
Better: “Those who oppose prayer in public schools focus on fairness and equality for everyone…”

Original: “Trying to get a troubled youth out of a gang for example, with a prayer in school compares to…”  
Better: “Trying to persuade a troubled youth to leave a gang through a school prayer, for example, is like…”

Original: “These facts do not accurately show that prayer lead to moral virtue.”  
Better: “These facts do not prove that prayer leads to moral behavior.”

Original: “During the time when prayer existed in public schools moral tarnishes such as segregation…”  
Better: “When prayer existed in public schools, moral problems such as segregation…”

Original: “The concept of God is not discourage in a public school.”  
Better: “The concept of God is not discouraged in public schools.”

Original: “he or she should not encounter any determent.”  
Better: “he or she should not face any obstruction” or “should not be prevented from doing so.”

Some vocabulary sounds advanced but unnatural. For example:
“fanaticism” may be too emotionally charged.
“apostasy in the classroom” is not the right word here.
“digest the religious philosophy” sounds awkward.

Use simpler academic words when possible:
“extremism,” “exclusion,” “accept,” “impose,” “pressure,” “coercion.”

Articles and prepositions
As a Chinese speaker, this is a predictable challenge. Pay special attention to:
in public schools / at school
lead to
equality for everyone
allow its inclusion in public schools

Style suggestions

Try to distinguish clearly between:
private prayer by students, which is protected, and  
school-sponsored public prayer, which is the real issue.

This distinction is important and will make your argument more precise and persuasive.

Also, your conclusion has a good overall message, but the final sentence is grammatically unclear:

Original: “Because America's vast religious diversity and the lack of apparent good resulting from discouraging religion diversity, public schools need to remain free of public prayer.”

Possible revision:

Revision priorities

Fill in all missing “” sections.  
Add specific evidence and examples.  
Reduce emotional or absolute wording.  
Edit grammar, especially articles, verb forms, and prepositions.

You already have a solid argumentative foundation. With clearer evidence, more precise language, and careful editing, this essay can become much stronger and more academically convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a strong sense of moral purpose. You argue consistently that discrimination against people with HIV/AIDS is wrong, and you use examples to support your claim. This gives the essay energy and conviction. For revision, the main goals are: improving accuracy, strengthening organization, using evidence more effectively, and making the tone more academic.

Strengths
Clear argument: Your thesis is easy to identify: discrimination by employers and insurers is morally and legally wrong.
Relevant examples: The Macy’s case, the nurse, and the pharmacist help show that workplace discrimination exists.
Strong engagement with the issue: Your writing shows empathy and commitment, which is valuable in argumentative writing.

Most important revision priorities

A. Improve factual and medical accuracy
Some statements are too absolute or inaccurate, which weakens your credibility.

Examples:
“Aids is the most common disease of our world today” — this is too broad and likely false.
“when someone becomes infected with HIV, it is going to develop into AIDS, and they are going to die” — this is medically outdated and inaccurate, especially today.
“The government has recognized that a person with AIDS has a disability… This is not true” — this is confusing. Legally, HIV/AIDS may be treated as a disability for protection under the law; do not say it is “not true” without explanation.

Advice: Use cautious academic language:
“HIV/AIDS has been one of the most stigmatized diseases in modern history.”
“At the time of the cases discussed, many people with HIV/AIDS faced severe social and legal discrimination.”

B. Replace emotional overstatement with academic tone
Your passion is strong, but sometimes the language becomes too dramatic:
“martyr to the AIDS community”
“ousted by corporate America”
“bury someone with AIDS alive”

This kind of wording can make readers feel you are exaggerating. In academic argument, calm and precise language is more persuasive.

Advice: Instead of emotional claims, explain the effect clearly:
“This case illustrates how stigma can deny people both employment and dignity.”

C. Strengthen organization
Right now, the essay repeats the same idea several times. Try a clearer structure:

Introduction  
   Background on HIV/AIDS and stigma; thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: Misunderstanding of transmission  
   Explain why casual contact is not a risk.
Body Paragraph 2: Employment discrimination  
   Use one or two well-explained examples.
Body Paragraph 3: Insurance/healthcare discrimination  
   Explain why denying coverage is unjust.
Body Paragraph 4: Legal and moral argument  
   Discuss ADA and human dignity.
Conclusion  
   Restate argument and broader social importance.

D. Use evidence more carefully
Many quotations are missing and marked as “.” This makes the essay incomplete. Also, some examples are introduced, but not fully analyzed.

Advice:
Add the actual quotations or remove the quotation marks.
After each example, explain how it supports your thesis.
Avoid relying only on individual stories; include at least one general legal or medical source if possible.

Language and grammar issues

A. Word choice and form
“Aids” → AIDS
“spokes person” → spokesperson
“full-blown” is informal; better: advanced AIDS
“hospice car” → probably hospice care
“final fays” → final days
“mo rally” → morally
“We societies of the world” → Societies around the world or We as a society

B. Grammar
“The instances… has been” → have been
“who and who not to hire” → whom to hire and whom not to hire
“therefore is not fully understood” → acceptable, but “therefore remains imperfectly understood” sounds more academic.

C. Sentence style
Many sentences are long and overloaded with commas. This can confuse readers. Try shorter, more direct sentences.

Example:
Original: “It is morally and legally wrong for people with HIV, or AIDS to be discriminated against by employers and insurance companies simply on the basis that they are afflicted with the disease.”
Better: “It is morally and legally wrong for employers and insurance companies to discriminate against people with HIV/AIDS solely because of their condition.”

Thesis revision suggestion
Your introduction is long and repetitive. Try a shorter thesis like this:

“Because HIV cannot be transmitted through casual contact, discrimination against people with HIV/AIDS in employment and insurance is based on fear rather than fact, and it is both morally unjust and legally unacceptable.”

Final advice
For your next draft, focus on:
Correcting factual inaccuracies
Using a more formal academic tone
Adding complete evidence
Reducing repetition
Editing grammar and sentence structure carefully

You have a strong foundation: a clear argument and meaningful topic. If you make the essay more precise, balanced, and organized, it will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that orphanages should be reconsidered as one response to child welfare problems. This is a strong argumentative topic, and you show awareness of opposing views such as family breakup and cost. The essay also has a recognizable structure: introduction, body paragraphs with reasons, and conclusion. That is a good foundation.

Main strengths

You consider counterarguments: this is important in argumentative writing.
You try to use evidence and sources: quoting historians and writers is appropriate for this kind of essay.
You stay focused on children’s welfare: this gives the essay a moral and logical center.

Most important problems to revise

A. Missing evidence and incomplete quotations
The biggest issue is that many places contain “”, so key evidence is missing. Because of this, the argument cannot fully persuade the reader.

What to do:
Insert the full quotations or paraphrases.
Introduce each source clearly.
Explain how each piece of evidence supports your claim.

Example pattern:
*According to historian Nurith Zmora, …*
*This evidence suggests that some orphanages offered structure, education, and preparation for adult life.*

Do not let quotations stand alone; always analyze them.

B. Thesis is too broad and sometimes inconsistent
Your thesis says orphanages should be “re instituted,” but later you say children should only be removed in abuse/neglect cases and that financial support may still be needed. This creates some inconsistency.

Suggestion: make the thesis more precise:

Or for limited, carefully regulated residential care as one option among others?

A narrower thesis will sound more reasonable and be easier to defend.

C. Logic and fairness
Some claims are too general, especially about welfare mothers “continuing to have children to gain financial support.” This may sound biased unless you provide strong evidence. In academic argument, avoid sweeping statements about social groups.

Also, “orphanage” may not be the best term if you mean children removed for abuse, neglect, or homelessness. Many such children are not orphans. You may need to clarify the definition.

Organization
Your essay has paragraphs, but some ideas jump too quickly. Try this structure:

Introduction: background + clear thesis  
Historical misunderstanding of orphanages  
Why some children need alternatives to biological families  
Problems with foster care and homelessness  
Counterarguments: family separation and cost  
Your proposed solution: limited, regulated use of residential institutions  
Conclusion

At the start of each paragraph, write a topic sentence that clearly connects to your thesis.

Style and academic tone
Some sentences are too emotional or informal for academic writing.

Examples:
“has a dark ring to it” → a bit conversational
“The truth is in the numbers” → dramatic but vague
“Something needs to be done” → too general

Use more precise academic language:
*The term “orphanage” often carries negative historical associations.*
*Statistical and historical evidence suggests…*

Also avoid overstatement such as “the vast majority were a pleasant place.” That is a very strong claim and needs strong proof.

Grammar and wording corrections
Here are some common issues in your draft:

publics attention → public attention
no where → nowhere
re instituted → reinstituted
Opposition … refute it mainly because of two reasons → Opponents reject the idea mainly for two reasons
difficult to content with → difficult to contend with
provide and alternative → provide an alternative
it's own → its own
the foster care can't deal → the foster care system cannot deal
disparity may be the wrong word; perhaps isolation or abandonment
with he use → with the use

Also check sentence structure. Some sentences are long and unclear. Try shorter sentences when expressing complex ideas.

Use of sources
Be careful with phrases like:
*Stated by Nicholas Scoppetta… states…*

This is repetitive and grammatically incorrect. Use one reporting verb:
*Nicholas Scoppetta, chairman of the Children’s Aid Society, argues that…*

If possible, use a consistent citation style required by your class.

Final revision advice
When you revise, focus on these priorities in order:

Add the missing evidence
Clarify your thesis
Make your argument more balanced and precise
Improve paragraph structure and transitions
Edit grammar and word choice

Possible revised thesis
You might use something like this:

This version is more specific, realistic, and academically persuasive.

You have a strong topic and a clear desire to argue for children’s well-being. With fuller evidence, clearer logic, and more careful language, this essay can become much more convincing.

You have a solid foundation and a meaningful argument. With clearer structure and stronger evidence, this essay could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: you compare two arguments about prayer in public schools and argue that Shriver’s is stronger than Methvin’s. This is a good analytical task, and your position is consistent throughout the essay. You also show awareness of important concepts in argumentation, such as claims, evidence, opposition, and fallacies. These are strong foundations.

What is working well

Clear overall thesis
   Your thesis appears at the end of the introduction: Shriver’s argument is stronger because he uses facts and avoids fallacies. This gives the essay direction.

Logical organization
   The essay generally follows a useful structure:
   - introduction and background
   - critique of Methvin
   - analysis of Shriver
   - conclusion

Good comparative focus
   You do not only summarize both authors; you try to explain *why* one is more persuasive. That is an important academic skill.

Main areas to revise

Replace summary with precise analysis
Sometimes your paragraphs describe what each author does, but do not fully analyze *how* that affects the argument. For example, instead of saying “this is a very powerful quote,” explain why it is powerful:
Does it appeal to fairness?
Does it support the claim with evidence?
Does it address a counterargument?

Try to use this pattern:
point -> evidence -> explanation -> connection to thesis

Use actual quotations or paraphrases
You have many places marked with . These are major gaps. Since your essay evaluates the writers’ claims and evidence, you must include the exact quote or a clear paraphrase. Without that, the reader cannot judge your analysis.

Also, when you mention a fallacy such as appeal to unqualified authority, you need to prove it:
Who is the authority?
Why is this person unqualified?
How does that weaken Methvin’s argument?

Make your standards for “strong argument” more specific
You say a strong argument needs an arguable claim and supporting evidence. Good start, but you can make this more precise. For example:
clear claim
relevant and sufficient evidence
explanation of evidence
response to opposing views
logical reasoning without fallacies

Then apply these standards consistently to both authors.

Avoid overgeneral statements
Some sentences sound too absolute:
“people learned through war and consequence, that church and state do not mix”
“it is hard to argue against”
“the reader is lead to believe that he is hiding something”

These statements need more caution. Academic writing is stronger when it avoids exaggeration. Use phrases like:
“suggests”
“may weaken”
“appears less convincing”
“could lead readers to question”

Language and grammar issues

Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are frequent grammar and word choice problems. Focus especially on these:

Articles and singular/plural
“an influential factor”
“the foundation of society”
“public school prayer” or “prayer in public schools”

Verb forms
“This ruling has become”
“people learned”
“the author conducted a survey”
“the reader is led to believe”

Sentence structure and punctuation
Some sentences are too long or joined incorrectly. Example:
“But even though it has been instrumental in the development of societies and states, people and learned through war and consequence, that church and state do not mix.”
This sentence has grammar and logic problems. Revise for clarity:
“Although religion has been instrumental in the development of societies and states, history has shown that combining church and state can lead to abuses of religious freedom.”

Word choice
A few phrases are awkward:
“modern day society” -> “modern society”
“public atmosphere” -> “public setting”
“attributes to poor argumentation” -> “contributes to weak argumentation”
“corroborates with his claim” -> “supports his claim”

Suggestions for revision

Strengthen the introduction
   Shorten the historical background and move more quickly to your thesis.

Add concrete textual evidence
   Fill in every missing quote/paraphrase and cite the authors clearly.

Develop deeper comparison
   In each body paragraph, compare the two writers directly where possible.

Revise topic sentences
   Make them analytical, not just descriptive.  
   Example:  
   - “Methvin’s argument is weakened by unclear claims and unsupported appeals to authority.”

Proofread carefully
   Check verb tense, articles, punctuation, and sentence boundaries.

Final thought

You have a solid argument and a clear comparison, which is the hardest part. Your next step is to make the essay more convincing by using exact evidence, explaining your reasoning more fully, and revising for grammar and clarity. With those changes, this can become a strong academic analysis.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: the public should be informed about executions, and reporters should be allowed to witness them, even if cameras should be restricted for safety reasons. This is a strong, arguable thesis, and you use the Robert Alton Harris case as relevant evidence. The paper also shows good awareness of opposing views, which is important in argumentative writing.

Strengths
Clear opinion: Your main claim is easy to understand.
Relevant example: The Harris case gives the essay a concrete focus.
Acknowledgment of counterargument: You mention the “high ratings” objection and respond to it.
Logical compromise: Your distinction between allowing reporters but restricting cameras is thoughtful and nuanced.

Main areas to revise

Complete missing evidence
Several sentences contain “”, which leaves important evidence unfinished. Because your essay depends heavily on legal and factual support, these missing parts weaken your argument. Before revising style or grammar, first fill in all quotations and source information.

For example:
“Vasquez reasoned that .”
“...on the grounds that it would .”
“...said he was .”
“...which states .”

Without these details, readers cannot fully evaluate your claims.

Strengthen the thesis and organization
Your introduction gives your opinion, but it is long and slightly repetitive. Try stating the thesis more directly:

*The media should be allowed to witness and report on executions because the public has a right to information, but cameras and recording devices may be restricted for safety and security reasons.*

This version is clearer and more formal.

Also, your body paragraphs would be stronger if they followed a more obvious structure:
Background on the Harris case  
The legal challenge to the media ban  
Counterargument: “media seeks ratings”  
Your rebuttal  
Why cameras can still be restricted  

This will help the essay feel less like a list of facts and more like a developed argument.

Develop analysis, not just summary
Some paragraphs mostly report what happened. In argumentative writing, facts should be followed by analysis explaining why they matter.

For example, after describing Judge Schnacke’s decision, explain how it supports your argument:
Does it show that a total media ban was unjustified?
Does it suggest that written reporting is different from filming?
Does it balance constitutional rights and prison safety?

Add 1–2 sentences of explanation after key facts.

Tone and word choice
Some phrases are too absolute or informal:
“anything and everything”
“clear right”
“turn it off; it is that simple”

These expressions sound emotional rather than academic. A more formal tone will make your argument stronger.

For example:
Instead of “anything and everything,” write “matters of public concern.”
Instead of “turn it off; it is that simple,” write “those who object to such coverage are not required to watch it.”

Grammar and sentence-level corrections
There are several grammar errors, many involving word choice, verb form, and possessive/confusing homophones. Here are some important ones:

“there access should be restricted” → “their access should be restricted”
“One such case which receive national attention” → “One such case that received national attention”
“had made to effort” → “had made no effort”
“inmate are more tense” → “inmates are more tense”
“they are not reason to exclude” → “they are not reasons to exclude”

Also check article use:
“safety of the people involved” → better as “the safety of the people involved”

Original:

This is hard to follow. Try:

*Schnacke ruled this way because Vasquez had made no effort to justify a total ban on reporters. No evidence showed that journalists using notebooks and pencils posed a security risk.*

Shorter sentences often sound clearer and more persuasive.

Final advice
Your essay has a solid central argument and useful supporting material, but it needs revision in three key areas:  
complete the missing quotations/evidence,  
add more explanation of how each fact supports your claim, and  
edit carefully for grammar and formal style.

If you revise those areas, your essay will become much more convincing and academically effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear personal voice and a strong sense of purpose. The opening story is engaging, and it helps readers understand why this topic matters to you. You also make your position clear: because many parents and churches do not provide enough guidance, schools should teach sex education that includes values, relationships, and safety. This is a strong foundation. To revise effectively, focus on clarity of thesis, organization, use of evidence, tone, and sentence-level grammar.

Main argument and thesis
Your essay’s central claim is understandable, but the thesis should be stated more clearly and consistently. Right now, your paper argues several things at once:
schools should teach sex education,
schools should teach morals and values,
abstinence and marriage are the ideal,
teens need information about disease prevention.

These ideas can work together, but you need one precise thesis. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Because many parents do not discuss sex openly, schools should provide comprehensive sex education that teaches relationship responsibility, consent, and disease prevention.*

This version is clearer and more academic. It also avoids sounding overly judgmental.

Organization
The essay would be stronger with a more logical structure. At present, some paragraphs repeat similar ideas about parents, schools, and morality.

A possible structure:
Introduction: personal anecdote + thesis  
Problem: many parents/churches/media do not provide healthy guidance  
Why schools should help: schools reach all students  
What schools should teach: safety, consent, relationships, responsibility  
Counterargument: some say morals should stay at home  
Response: schools can teach respect and health without replacing parents  
Conclusion: return to personal experience and explain what should change

Try to make each paragraph focus on one main point.

Evidence and support
This is the area that needs the most improvement. Several places contain missing citations (“”), which weakens your credibility. Also, some claims are too broad, such as “casual sex is unacceptable” or “teens inevitably will go with the flow.” In an academic essay, readers expect reliable support.

Suggestions:
Replace missing statistics with real sources.
Use current research, not only older data “before 1989.”
Explain how the evidence supports your argument.
Avoid relying only on personal experience, because one experience does not prove a general rule.

For example, instead of saying:
*Parents are failing to talk to their children about sex because of embarrassment…*

You could write:
*Many parents feel unprepared or uncomfortable discussing sex, which can leave teenagers to learn from peers or media instead of trusted adults.*

This sounds more balanced and academic.

Tone and word choice
Some language is very emotional or judgmental, which may alienate readers. Phrases like “degrading, animalistic,” “hiding behind their bedroom doors,” and “turning their backs on our youth” sound accusatory. In argumentative writing, a calmer tone is usually more persuasive.

Also, be careful when discussing “morals.” Schools can teach responsibility, respect, consent, and health, but claiming that schools should teach one specific moral code may raise objections. You may want to distinguish between:
personal/family beliefs, and
public education goals such as safety, respect, and informed decision-making.

Development of counterargument
You briefly mention that some people think teaching sexual morals in schools is unethical. This is good, but the counterargument needs more development. Explain *why* some parents object, then answer them fairly.

For example:
Some parents believe sexual values should be taught only at home.
However, not all students receive accurate guidance at home.
Therefore, schools should provide factual, respectful education while allowing families to discuss personal beliefs.

This approach makes your argument stronger and more balanced.

Grammar and style
Your meaning is usually clear, but there are many grammar and usage errors. Common issues include:
capitalization: “Father” and “Him” should usually be lowercase unless used as proper names.
apostrophes/plurals: “bird's and the bee's” → “birds and the bees”
word choice: “class mate” → “classmate”; “its” → “it’s”
sentence boundaries/comma splices: “We were wrong, after a few difficult years…” should be divided or revised.
awkward phrasing: “Educations of sex in school” → “Sex education in schools”
agreement and formality: “some one” → “someone”; “Aids” → “AIDS”

Example revision
Original:  
*The lack of parental guidance regarding sexual relationships for youths' emphasizes the need for schools to teach children morals and values of sexual relationships.*

Revised:  
*The lack of parental guidance about sexual relationships for young people highlights the need for schools to provide education about sexual health, responsibility, and relationships.*

Final advice
Your essay has an honest and compelling personal foundation. For revision, concentrate on:
writing a clear thesis,
organizing paragraphs around one idea each,
adding reliable evidence,
using a more balanced academic tone,
correcting grammar and sentence structure.

You have an important topic and a sincere perspective. With clearer structure and stronger evidence, your essay can become much more persuasive.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0029.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay addresses an important ethical issue and shows clear personal engagement. The central position is consistent: the right to refuse life support should belong to the individual. This is a strong basis for an argumentative essay. Below are the main areas to revise.

Strengths

Clear topic and stance: From the first paragraph, readers understand the essay is about life support and personal choice.
Thoughtful discussion: You consider both sides—people who want life support and people who do not.
Personal voice: The paragraph beginning “I personally consider…” is effective because it makes your position concrete and sincere.
Relevant ethical focus: Questions about life, death, suffering, and autonomy are appropriate for an argumentative essay.

Main revision priorities

A. Strengthen the argument structure
Right now, the essay often raises questions instead of fully developing claims. In an argumentative essay, each body paragraph should make a clear point and support it with reasoning or examples.

For example, your overall argument could be organized like this:
Individuals should decide about life support because definitions of a meaningful life differ from person to person.
Medical definitions of death are complex and not purely technical.
Because the consequences are deeply personal, the patient’s wishes should have priority.
Doctors should advise, but not replace, the patient’s decision when the patient’s wishes are known.

Try to begin each paragraph with a clear topic sentence and then explain it.

B. Add evidence and examples
The essay is mostly reflective. To become more persuasive, include:
a real or hypothetical case,
reference to advance directives / living wills,
ethical principles such as autonomy, dignity, or quality of life.

For instance, instead of only asking “Where do you draw the line between life and death?”, explain why different people draw the line differently and how this supports patient choice.

C. Improve coherence
Some paragraphs move quickly from one idea to another. Add transitions so the reader can follow your logic:
“First,”
“In addition,”
“By contrast,”
“Therefore,”
“For this reason,”
“Ultimately.”

Also, the essay contains several places marked , which suggest missing sentences. These gaps make the argument feel incomplete. Make sure each paragraph is fully developed.

Language and style issues

A. Word choice and collocation
Some expressions sound unnatural in English:
“re-think” → rethink
“need re-defining” → need to be redefined
“tried and true” is acceptable, but here well established may fit better
“price of emotions” → emotional cost
“what makes our life important to use” → probably what makes life important to us
“people o want” → people who want
“imminent death” is possible, but inevitable death may be clearer

B. Grammar
Watch for sentence structure, agreement, and punctuation.

Examples:
“Not only does this pose a dilemma to the physicians, it provokes thought…”  
  Better: Not only does this pose a dilemma for physicians, but it also provokes thought in every individual.
“Our memories, our personality, other events in our lives determine…”  
  Better parallel form: Our memories, our personalities, and other life experiences determine…
“Would we want someone else to make this decision without regarding our wish(es).”  
  This should be a question: Would we want someone else to make this decision without considering our wishes?
“this is our life, our death that we are talking about”  
  Better: this is our life and our death that we are talking about

C. Avoid too many rhetorical questions
Questions can be effective, but there are too many here. If every paragraph asks questions, the essay can sound uncertain. In revision, turn many questions into statements.

For example:
“Who can say that a person in a coma does not feel pain?”  
  Better: Because we cannot fully know what a person in a coma experiences, decisions about prolonged treatment become even more difficult.

Content suggestions

One important issue is missing: what happens when the patient cannot speak? Since your argument is about individual choice, you should mention:
advance directives,
living wills,
medical power of attorney,
family members and doctors interpreting previously expressed wishes.

This would make your argument more practical and complete.

Possible revision of your thesis

Your current thesis is understandable but broad. A stronger version could be:

Because ideas of dignity, suffering, and meaningful life differ from person to person, the decision to refuse life support should primarily belong to the individual, with physicians serving as advisors rather than decision-makers.

Final advice

When revising, focus on:
completing the missing parts,
making each paragraph argue one clear point,
replacing many questions with explanations,
correcting grammar and awkward phrasing,
adding one or two concrete examples.

You have a strong topic and a sincere position. With clearer organization and more developed support, this essay could become a convincing argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: welfare reform should strengthen families and provide support, not simply cut benefits. You also show strong engagement with the topic, and your personal example makes your argument feel sincere and memorable. These are important strengths in argumentative writing.

What is working well

Clear opinion
   Your thesis is understandable: welfare reform will fail unless it addresses family stability, work support, and values.

Strong voice
   The opening is energetic and grabs attention. The essay sounds committed and persuasive.

Use of example
   Your personal experience with social services is one of the strongest parts because it gives concrete evidence.

Organization by topic
   The essay generally moves from background, to causes, to criticism of current proposals, to your own solutions. This helps readers follow your ideas.

Main revision priorities

Make the thesis more precise
Right now, the main claim is broad and repeated in different ways. Try to state it once, clearly and specifically, near the end of the introduction.

For example:
> Welfare reform should combine work requirements with government support such as job training, childcare, counseling, and temporary family-centered housing, because cutting benefits alone will deepen poverty and family instability.

This is stronger because it tells readers what you argue and why.

Improve logical support
Some claims are too general or too absolute. For instance:
“families have forgotten all about pride and self worth”
welfare dependence has caused crime and violence
“This is how the American Family… began to deteriorate”

These statements may sound judgmental and need stronger evidence. In academic argument, avoid blaming whole groups unless you can prove it carefully. Instead, use more cautious language:
“long-term dependence may weaken incentives to work in some cases”
“family instability can contribute to social problems”
“some welfare policies may unintentionally discourage work”

This will make your essay sound more fair and academic.

Distinguish evidence from opinion
You use personal experience effectively, but one story cannot prove a general national problem. Add other kinds of support:
historical facts
policy examples
statistics
expert opinions
research on childcare, job training, or intergenerational poverty

Also, some historical points may need checking. For example, the welfare system was not simply “begun by Herbert Hoover.” Be careful with factual accuracy.

Reduce emotional and politically loaded language
Phrases like “driven by a growing coldness toward the poor” or “greatest Country in the World” sound emotional rather than analytical. In an academic essay, a calmer tone is usually more persuasive.

For example:
Instead of “coldness toward the poor,” write “a punitive approach toward welfare recipients.”
Instead of “greatest Country in the World,” write “social cohesion and economic stability.”

Language and grammar issues

Confusion of its and it’s
This error appears many times.
its = possessive
it’s = it is

Examples:
“The Welfare System and its problems”
“the family and its values”
“allow its people”

Wrong word choice
“air programs” is probably incorrect. Did you mean aid programs?
“chemical abusers” is awkward; better: substance abusers or people struggling with addiction
“rear them” is understandable but sounds old-fashioned; raise them is more natural.

Punctuation and sentence control
You often use too many commas, dashes, and long sentences. This can make the argument hard to read.

For example:
> “My reply, was, ‘Thanks, but no thanks’.”
should be:
> “My reply was, ‘Thanks, but no thanks.’”

Try to shorten long sentences into two sentences when possible.

Capitalization
Do not capitalize common nouns unnecessarily:
reform the welfare system
country
society

But capitalize proper nouns:
House of Representatives
Contract with America
Newt Gingrich

Organization suggestions

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction: issue + thesis
Background: brief history of welfare policy
Problem with current reform proposals
Why family stability and work support matter
Your policy recommendations
Conclusion

Also, the numbered list is useful, but introduce it more formally and explain each point with evidence.

Final advice for revision

When revising, focus on these three goals:
State one precise thesis
Support big claims with evidence, not only emotion
Use a more formal and careful academic tone

You have a strong core argument and a genuine voice. If you make your claims more precise, reduce overgeneralization, and edit grammar carefully, this essay can become much more convincing and academically effective.

explain why it matters
connect it back to your thesis

“Think about it”

These phrases create energy, but they weaken academic credibility. Aim for a more formal tone. For example:

“gesus” → likely Jesuits

Final advice

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that the Illinois Supreme Court made the wrong decision in the “Baby Richard” case. This is a strong starting point. You also show engagement with the emotional, psychological, and social dimensions of the case, which helps make your argument compelling. Below are suggestions to help you revise more effectively.

Strengths

Clear thesis:  
Your main claim is easy to identify: the court made a mistake by transferring custody to the biological father.

Strong sense of purpose:  
You consistently focus on persuading the reader, and your emotional investment gives the essay energy.

Good use of multiple perspectives:  
You discuss Richard, the adoptive parents, adoption as a social institution, and the legal system. This broadens the argument.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the structure more logical
Right now, some paragraphs feel repetitive or loosely connected. Try organizing the essay like this:

Introduction: explain the case briefly and present your thesis.  
Argument 1: Richard’s psychological well-being.  
Argument 2: problems with the court’s reasoning/process.  
Argument 3: wider effects on adoption and public trust.  
Counterargument and response: eg, the biological father’s rights.  
Conclusion: restate your position clearly.

This will make the essay easier to follow.

B. Strengthen evidence and explanation
Some claims are asserted strongly but need more support. For example:

“Richard has learned to love and be loved.”
“A two and a half year old child cannot yet comprehend abstract concepts such as love.”
“He would feel he is at fault.”

These are important claims, but they need reliable evidence. If you mention Erik Erikson, explain his theory accurately and connect it carefully to the case. Also, avoid overstating psychological certainty unless your source clearly supports it.

C. Address the opposing side fairly
A strong argumentative essay should consider the other side. In this case, the strongest counterargument is the biological father’s legal and parental rights. If you acknowledge this and then explain why Richard’s best interests should still come first, your argument will become more persuasive and balanced.

Style and tone

A. Reduce emotionally charged language
Phrases like “ripped away,” “tug of war battle,” and “if he knew what was best for his son” are powerful, but too much emotional language can weaken academic credibility. Keep some emotional force, but balance it with reasoned analysis.

B. Use a more academic voice
Some sentences sound conversational:
“Think back to when you were four years old.”
“I’m not sure I can understand the ruling…”

In a formal argumentative essay, it is usually better to avoid speaking directly to the reader or relying too much on personal reaction. Instead, write more objectively:
“A four-year-old child is likely to have formed deep attachments to primary caregivers.”
“The ruling is difficult to justify because…”

Language corrections

Because you are a Chinese-speaking writer, pay special attention to articles, relative pronouns, verb forms, and word choice.

Here are some specific examples:

“when Daniela Janikova gave birth” → “When Daniela Janikova gave birth”  
  Capitalize the first word of a sentence.

“whom are referred to as Jane and John Doe” → “who are referred to as Jane and John Doe”  
  Use who as the subject.

“wouldn’t be quite as messy” → acceptable, but more formal: “would not have become so complicated.”

“ripping him away form” → “from”

“the emotional bonds are the most strong” → “the emotional bonds are strongest”

“These laws will no longer very from state to state” → “These laws will no longer vary from state to state”

“in regards to” → “with regard to” or “regarding”

“suffer the loss of their child, a child that they raised up to this point”  
  Better: “suffer the loss of a child they have raised for four years.”

Also check sentence boundaries. Some sentences are too long and would be clearer if divided into two shorter ones.

Content issues to reconsider

Be careful with this sentence:  
“A mother who cannot afford to keep her baby won't have to kill it…”  
This wording is too extreme for an academic essay and may offend readers. If you want to discuss abortion, do so in a more neutral and precise way.

Also, avoid universal statements such as:
“Nobody likes to be hurt, so why go through with it?”
This is too informal and too general. Replace it with a more analytical claim about how such cases may discourage prospective adoptive parents.

Final revision advice

In your next draft, focus on:
clearer organization,
more reliable evidence,
fair treatment of counterarguments,
more formal academic style,
grammar and word-choice corrections.

You already have a strong opinion and a meaningful topic. With better structure and a more academic tone, this essay can become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that euthanasia should be more restricted in the United States. You also organize the paper around three main reasons, which gives the essay a visible structure. That is a good foundation. For revision, the main goals are to improve accuracy, strengthen logic, and make the evidence more reliable.

Main strengths
Clear stance: The reader can understand that you oppose broad legalization of euthanasia.
Organized structure: The introduction previews three arguments, and the body paragraphs generally follow that plan.
Engagement with ethics and law: You try to connect moral questions with legal consequences, which is appropriate for an argumentative essay.

Most important revision issues

A. Clarify key terms
A major problem is that the essay uses “euthanasia” very broadly and sometimes inaccurately. In the U.S., active euthanasia is generally illegal, while some states allow physician-assisted dying/assisted suicide under strict conditions. Also, withdrawing life support is different from directly ending a life. If you do not define the term clearly, your argument becomes confusing.

Revise by:
Defining euthanasia in the introduction.
Explaining exactly what practice you are discussing.
Avoiding statements like “Euthanasia is practiced throughout the U.S.” unless you can prove that precisely.

B. Improve factual accuracy
Some claims are too broad or may be incorrect:
“the Declaration of Independence states life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness as rights that all American possess” → should be “all Americans possess.”
“Since the ruling on euthanasia took place in 1988” → this is unclear and likely inaccurate. You need to name the specific case or law.
“Currently, euthanasia has little restrictions on its practice” → this is likely false or oversimplified.

In argumentative writing, factual errors weaken credibility quickly.

Revise by:
Checking all legal claims carefully.
Naming exact laws, court cases, or states.
Using current and credible sources.

C. Strengthen the logic of your arguments
Some arguments rely on weak comparisons or emotional predictions.

Public opposition argument
Your first body paragraph argues that because some Americans opposed animal euthanasia, America is not ready for human euthanasia. This is not a strong comparison. Animal euthanasia and human end-of-life decisions are ethically and legally different.

Abortion comparison
The abortion paragraph is also weak because it depends on analogy rather than direct evidence. Even if both issues involve ending life, that does not prove euthanasia will lead to the same violence.

Legal vagueness argument
This is potentially your strongest point, but it needs better evidence. You raise important questions about patient competence, family involvement, and legal safeguards. Develop this paragraph more carefully with actual laws and expert sources.

Best strategy: Make the essay focus mainly on whether current safeguards are sufficient, rather than predicting social violence.

Use evidence more effectively
Several quotations are incomplete: “” appears in the essay. This makes the paper look unfinished and prevents the reader from evaluating your sources.

Also, some source use is vague:
Who is “American Against Human Suffering”?
What article, year, or publication?
What exactly did McKown say?

Revise by:
Completing every quotation.
Introducing each source clearly.
Explaining how each piece of evidence supports your point.
Prefering scholarly, legal, or medical sources over emotional examples.

Style and language corrections
Your writing is understandable, but there are many grammar and word-choice problems. Here are common patterns:

Subject-verb agreement:  
  “all American possess” → all Americans possess
Wrong word:  
  “there own lives” → their own lives  
  “there is too much gray areas” → there are too many gray areas  
  “a live is worth living” → a life is worth living  
  “full of wholes” → full of holes
Countable/uncountable nouns:  
  “no black or white answers” → no black-and-white answers
Sentence structure:  
  “Simply because that there are few people…” is not a complete or clear sentence.
Articles and plurals:  
  “the patient is not competent” / “families only need to be notified of the patient’s request”

You also often use phrases that sound repetitive:
“worth living”
“gray area”
“the people of America”

Try varying vocabulary and using more formal academic phrasing, such as “many Americans,” “legal safeguards,” “decision-making capacity,” and “ethical ambiguity.”

Organization revision suggestion
A stronger structure could be:

Introduction
   - Define euthanasia/assisted dying
   - State your position clearly
   - Preview 2–3 specific arguments

Body Paragraph 1: Legal safeguards
   - Explain current laws
   - Show why they may be insufficient

Body Paragraph 2: Patient competence and mental health
   - Discuss depression, coercion, informed consent
   - Use medical or legal evidence

Body Paragraph 3: Family/doctor oversight
   - Argue for stronger review procedures
   - Propose specific restrictions

Conclusion
   - Summarize
   - Emphasize reform, not emotional prediction

Final advice
Your essay shows strong interest in the topic, but it needs revision to become a persuasive academic argument. Focus on:
defining terms accurately  
correcting factual/legal claims  
using stronger evidence  
improving grammar and sentence clarity

If you revise in those areas, your argument will become much more convincing.

Explanation

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a serious argumentative purpose. You argue consistently that abortion is morally wrong, and you try to support this claim with biology, law, and medical ethics. This gives your paper a strong sense of direction. For revision, focus on three main areas: completeness of evidence, organization, and language accuracy/style.

Main strengths
Clear thesis and stance: Your position is never confusing.
Use of different types of support: You refer to biological facts, scholars, doctors, and the Hippocratic Oath.
Formal tone: In many places, your vocabulary sounds appropriately academic.

Most important revision issue: incomplete and missing evidence
The biggest problem is that many quotations or examples are missing and replaced by “”. Because of this, readers cannot evaluate your evidence. In an argumentative essay, claims must be supported fully and precisely.

What to do
Insert the full quotations or paraphrases.
Identify each source clearly: author, title, and why the source is credible.
Explain each quotation after you present it. Do not let quotations stand alone.

Example
Current:
> “According to Germain Grisez… .”

Better:
> “According to philosopher Germain Grisez, the fetus has its own genetic identity from conception. This supports the claim that the fetus is biologically distinct from the mother.”

Organization and logic
Your essay generally follows a logical order:
introduce the debate,
argue that the fetus is alive,
argue that it is a separate being,
discuss doctors’ moral responsibility,
conclude.

This is a good structure. However, some paragraphs are too long and contain several ideas at once. Also, some transitions are abrupt.

Suggestions
End the introduction with a specific thesis statement.
Begin each body paragraph with a clear topic sentence.
Make sure each paragraph develops only one main point.

Possible revised thesis
> Abortion is morally wrong because the fetus is a living human being, an individual distinct from the mother, and a life doctors have an ethical duty to protect.

This thesis is clearer and previews your body paragraphs.

Argumentation: address counterarguments fairly
Your essay presents the pro-choice side, but mostly in a simplified way. To sound more academic, you should represent the opposing view more fairly and then respond to it.

For example, instead of saying pro-choice supporters believe a woman can “do what she wants with her body,” explain their argument more precisely:
bodily autonomy,
privacy rights,
difficult medical or social circumstances.

Then explain why you disagree. This will make your argument stronger and more credible.

Language issues to revise
There are many grammar and style problems, but most are fixable.

A. Sentence form
Some sentences are incomplete or awkward:
“Or ‘endure’ the right word?”
  - Better: “But is ‘endure’ the right word?”
“For in reality the procedure is not one of necessity for more ninety-eight percent...”
  - Better: “In reality, the procedure is not medically necessary for more than ninety-eight percent of these women...”

B. Word choice
Some words are too emotional or imprecise for academic writing:
“senseless killing”
“turn their backs”
“property of that woman”

Try using more neutral language when presenting analysis. Strong claims are acceptable, but overly emotional phrasing can weaken academic credibility.

C. Grammar
Watch for:
articles: “is the morally decision” → “is the moral decision”
prepositions: “taken form the oath” → “taken from the oath”
agreement: “any argument that pro-choice activists have stating” → “any argument made by pro-choice activists stating”
word form: “follo wing” → “following”
repetition: “decision the woman decides” → revise to avoid repeating the same word

D. Punctuation and spacing
There are several formatting issues, such as broken words (“re main”), missing commas, and inconsistent quotation marks. Carefully proofread the final version.

Style and academic tone
Your essay often uses rhetorical questions:
“Can one really justify…?”
“Is it moral for doctors…?”

A few rhetorical questions can be effective, but too many can make the essay sound more persuasive than analytical. In academic writing, direct statements are usually stronger.

For example:
> “Can one really justify an innocent child being denied a chance at life as moral?”

could become:
> “From this perspective, denying the fetus the chance to live cannot be considered morally justified.”

Conclusion
Your conclusion returns to the larger debate, which is good, but it becomes vague at the end. Make sure the final paragraph:
restates your thesis,
summarizes your key reasons,
leaves the reader with a clear final idea.

Also, the final line, “You can’t legislate morality,” seems to complicate your argument. If you keep it, explain how it relates to your position.

Revision priorities
Replace all “” with complete evidence.
Write a clearer thesis.
Improve paragraph focus and transitions.
Use more precise, less emotional academic language.
Proofread for grammar and sentence clarity.

Your essay has a strong argumentative foundation. With fuller evidence and more careful language, it can become much more persuasive and academically effective.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0036.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear ambition: you are questioning a deeply accepted assumption—that preserving life at all costs is always good. This is a strong argumentative topic, and your essay shows intellectual courage. You also use a range of examples (PKD, genetic counseling, antibiotics, AIDS, health-care costs), which helps create the sense of a broad social issue.

To revise effectively, focus on argument clarity, evidence, organization, and tone.

Clarify your main claim
At the moment, your thesis is interesting but too broad and sometimes unclear. You seem to argue that:

modern medicine focuses too much on prolonging life rather than eliminating causes of disease;
this may weaken the human gene pool and increase future suffering;
it also wastes resources.

These are three large claims. Try to state them more precisely in one thesis statement near the end of the introduction. For example:

> Modern medicine’s emphasis on prolonging life at all costs can create ethical, genetic, and economic problems; therefore, society should place greater emphasis on prevention, responsible genetic counseling, and treatment that addresses root causes rather than only delaying death.

This gives your essay a clearer direction.

Improve organization
Right now, some paragraphs feel disconnected. For example, the essay moves from PKD to genetic counseling, then to antibiotics, then to AIDS and immunity. The reader can follow your general concern, but the logic needs stronger structure.

A possible structure:

Introduction: present the social belief in preserving life and your thesis.
Body 1: medicine often treats symptoms rather than causes.
Body 2: genetic disease example (PKD) and ethical implications.
Body 3: misuse/limits of antibiotics and mutation/resistance.
Body 4: economic costs of life-prolonging treatment.
Conclusion: restate your argument and propose a better approach.

Also, each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that connects clearly to the thesis.

Use stronger evidence and explain it carefully
You make bold claims, but many need reliable support. For example:

“The medical community derives its existence solely on the preservation of life.” This is too absolute and probably inaccurate.
The Darwin/evolution argument about people with PKD surviving and reproducing is controversial and needs careful scientific and ethical support.
Claims about weak immune systems being passed on also require evidence and should not be stated too generally.

Also, the essay contains placeholders (“”) where evidence or quotations are missing. These gaps seriously weaken the paper. You need to complete them with properly introduced and explained sources.

When using sources, do not only quote them. Explain:
what the source says,
why it matters,
how it supports your argument.

Be careful with tone and ethics
Some sentences sound too judgmental or insensitive, which may alienate readers. For example:

“I see no honor to life in this parent’s selfish act.”
“by all means adopt a child already born with a genetic defect...”

These statements sound harsh and dismissive of difficult personal decisions. In argumentative writing, especially on medical ethics, a more balanced tone is stronger. You can criticize decisions or systems without attacking individuals. Try language like:

> This decision raises difficult ethical questions about parental responsibility and the use of genetic information.

That sounds more academic and persuasive.

Address counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge the other side. Why do people support life-preserving medicine? Because it relieves suffering, respects human dignity, and reflects compassion. If you briefly recognize these values and then respond to them, your essay will become more convincing.

Sentence-level language issues
Your vocabulary is strong, but some sentences are too long and difficult to follow. Shorten them and remove unnecessary repetition.

Examples:
“runs the gamut” → acceptable, but slightly informal here.
“our own fallibility to disease and illness” → better: “our vulnerability to disease and illness.”
“Doctors” should not be capitalized in the middle of a sentence.
“uniformed people” should be uninformed people.
“highly advance defense mechanism” should be highly advanced defense mechanism.
“over thousands years” should be over thousands of years.
“one occasion t decide” should be one occasion to decide.
“some particularly anomaly” should be some particular anomaly.

Also, check article use and plural forms carefully.

Correct factual accuracy
A few medical details need revision:
Antibiotics do not treat viral infections.
A finger infection from a cut would usually be bacterial, not viral.
Bacteria do not “recognize” an antibiotic; rather, resistant bacteria survive and reproduce.

Improving scientific precision will make your argument more credible.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong central concern and shows independent thinking. For revision, concentrate on:
writing a precise thesis,
organizing paragraphs more logically,
adding credible evidence,
using a more academic tone,
correcting factual and language errors.

This topic can become a very compelling essay if you make the argument narrower, better supported, and more balanced.

Do you mainly oppose euthanasia? Support it in limited cases? Support more regulation? Your conclusion suggests society needs a “median,” but this idea is not fully developed.

Possible revision of thesis:
“Although supporters of assisted suicide emphasize mercy and patient choice, the stronger argument is that legalizing these practices may create serious ethical and medical consequences unless strict limits are established.”
Or: “Assisted suicide for the terminally ill may be justified in limited cases, but only with strong legal and medical safeguards.”

A clearer thesis will help the whole essay feel more unified.

---

Organization and paragraph development

At present, many body paragraphs follow this pattern: present an argument, explain why it is strong, then briefly mention a weakness. This is useful, but sometimes the paragraph becomes repetitive.

Suggestions:
Begin each body paragraph with a clear topic sentence.
Focus each paragraph on one main point.
Use a more logical structure, such as:

Paragraph 1: Opponents’ slippery-slope argument  
Paragraph 2: Opponents’ argument about better alternatives (hospice/pain care)  
Paragraph 3: Opponents’ argument about trust in doctors  
Paragraph 4: Supporters’ argument about mercy and ending suffering  
Paragraph 5: Your evaluation: which side is stronger and why

This structure will make your ideas easier to follow.

---

Use of evidence and quotations

You include places for quotations, but many are written as “”, so your evidence is incomplete. In academic writing, this is a major problem because readers need to see the actual source material.

When revising:
Insert the full quotation.
Introduce the source clearly.
Explain how the quotation supports your point.

Example:
Instead of:  
“Hendin wrote, .”

Write:  
“As Herbert Hendin argues, ‘[quotation],’ suggesting that euthanasia may gradually expand beyond extreme medical cases.”

Then explain why this matters.

Also, some claims are too broad and need support, such as:
suicide becoming “popular”
people with HIV/AIDS choosing death immediately
loss of public trust in doctors

These ideas can work, but they need evidence or more careful wording.

---

Style and tone

Your tone is serious, but sometimes the language becomes too emotional or exaggerated. Academic argument is stronger when it is precise and measured.

Examples:
“play God” is powerful, but should be presented as opponents’ view, not as fact.
“mercy killings” is emotionally loaded; use it carefully.
“as popular as fast food” sounds exaggerated and weakens your credibility.

Try using neutral academic phrasing:
“critics argue that…”
“supporters contend that…”
“this may lead to…”
“one possible consequence is…”

---

Grammar and sentence-level issues

There are frequent grammar and word-choice problems. These do not hide your meaning, but they reduce clarity.

Common patterns to revise:

1) Capitalization
“when his case was first released” → “When his case was first reported…”
“Dr Kevorkian” → usually “Dr. Kevorkian”

2) Word form
“neglection” → “failure” or “neglect”
“sufferings” → usually “suffering”
“assisted suicides” → better “assisted suicide”

3) Articles and singular/plural
“a doctors stands for” → “what a doctor stands for”
“the acceptance of these methods” is fine, but be careful with count/noncount nouns.

4) Sentence structure
“This makes it seems” → “This makes it seem”
“they are af raid” → “they are afraid”
“improve i the care” → “improve the care”

5) Awkward phrasing
“in their reliance of the belief” → “because they rely on the belief”
“helps to adequately debate” → “helps frame the debate”

---

Final advice

For revision, focus on these priorities:

State a clear thesis with your own position
Complete and integrate quotations properly
Improve organization by giving each paragraph one clear purpose
Use more formal, precise language
Edit grammar carefully

You have a strong foundation: the essay is thoughtful, engaged, and clearly interested in ethical complexity. With a clearer thesis and more precise evidence, it can become much more persuasive.
