Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose: you argue that opponents of corporal punishment in schools make an ineffective case because they use unclear definitions and show a wavering position. This is a focused claim, and your opening anecdote about Tommy is vivid and attention-grabbing. You also try to organize the essay around two main reasons, which is a good structure for an argumentative essay.

Strengths

Engaging introduction  
   The Tommy story creates interest and gives readers an emotional entry into the topic.

Clear thesis direction  
   Your main claim appears at the end of the introduction: the opponents’ argument is ineffective because of ambiguous terminology and inconsistency. This gives the essay a clear line of development.

Logical paragraphing  
   The body paragraphs generally follow your thesis: first definition, then inconsistency, then consequences.

Main Revision Priorities

Strengthen your evidence
The biggest problem is that the essay often makes claims without enough concrete proof. You mention articles and authors, but some quotations are missing, marked as . Without those quotations, readers cannot judge your analysis.

What to do:
Insert the actual quotations or paraphrases from the sources.
After each quotation, explain clearly how it supports your point.
Avoid general statements like “some authors” or “most of the opponents” unless you provide examples.

For instance, instead of saying:
> “Some authors make reference to behavioral reactions...”

say something more specific:
> “For example, Author X argues that corporal punishment causes short-term obedience, while Author Y suggests its effects are unclear. These differing claims weaken the overall anti-corporal-punishment position because they do not present a consistent conclusion.”

Clarify your argument
Sometimes your essay seems to criticize the writing quality of anti-corporal-punishment authors, but sometimes it sounds like you may support corporal punishment. That position is not fully clear.

Ask yourself:  
Are you arguing that corporal punishment is acceptable?  
Or only that its opponents argue poorly?

If it is the second, make that distinction explicit. Otherwise readers may misunderstand your purpose.

Improve analysis
At times, the essay repeats the idea that the opponents are unclear, but does not go deeply enough into why that matters.

For example, your point about definition is promising. You could develop it by explaining:
Do different authors define corporal punishment differently because they include different actions?
Does this create confusion about whether they mean paddling, spanking, striking, or all physical punishment?
Why does such variation weaken persuasion?

The same applies to “wavering position.” Show exactly where the contradiction happens and why it damages credibility.

Style and Language Suggestions

Your writing is often expressive, but some sentences are awkward or grammatically incorrect. Below are common issues to revise.

Word choice and collocation
“The bell... loudly sounded its warning” → more natural: “The bell rang loudly to signal the start of third period.”
“what would be in store for him” is understandable but a bit informal.
“incorrigible” is not the right word here. You probably mean “unacceptable,” “harmful,” or “indefensible.”

Grammar
“Tommy, with his head hung low responded” → “Tommy, with his head hanging low, responded...”
“took he seat” → “took his seat”
“Even if definition of the focal term had been clearly achieved” → “Even if the central term had been clearly defined...”
“the opponents to the educational use of corporal punishment” → more natural: “opponents of corporal punishment in schools”
“subjected the paddle” → “subjected to the paddle”

Capitalization and naming
You write “tommy” later in lowercase, but the character’s name is “Tommy.” If you mean a general type of student, explain that clearly.

Sentence control
Some sentences are too long and difficult to follow. Break them into shorter units. For example:

> “The authors who define the term do not apply the same definition, several of the authors fail to make any attempt at defining.”

This is a comma splice. Better:
> “The authors who define the term do not use the same definition. Several others do not attempt to define it at all.”

Organization Advice

Consider revising into this structure:

Introduction: anecdote + background + thesis  
Body Paragraph 1: unclear definitions, with specific source examples  
Body Paragraph 2: inconsistent claims, with quotations and analysis  
Body Paragraph 3: why these weaknesses reduce persuasiveness  
Conclusion: restate argument and explain broader significance

Final Comment

You have a strong central idea and a good instinct for argumentative structure. Your next step is to make the essay more academically persuasive by adding precise evidence, clarifying your position, and revising grammar and sentence structure. With stronger support and cleaner language, this could become a much more convincing essay.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and strong commitment to your argument. Your essay consistently argues that television censorship should increase to protect children, and you use several kinds of support: studies, examples of TV shows, counterargument, and public opinion. This gives your paper persuasive potential. For revision, focus on clarity, organization, and language accuracy so your ideas become more convincing.

Main strengths
Clear stance: Your opinion is obvious from the beginning.
Relevant topic sentences: Many paragraphs begin with a clear idea about children, parents, networks, or society.
Use of support: You include research, examples, and opposing views.
Awareness of audience: You try to persuade readers by emphasizing children’s future and social consequences.

Most important revision priorities

A. Make your thesis more direct and specific
Right now, the introduction is passionate, but the thesis is repetitive and not sharply stated. Try to end the introduction with one sentence like:

Example revision:  
*Television censorship should be strengthened because violent and sexual content influences children’s behavior, weakens parental control, and gradually makes society less sensitive to harmful material.*

This gives your essay a roadmap.

B. Improve organization
Many paragraphs repeat “the advocates for censorship” instead of developing new points. This makes the paper sound circular. Organize by your own claims, not by what “advocates” do.

A stronger structure:
Introduction + thesis
Children are influenced by violent content
Sexual content also affects children
Parental control alone is not enough
Lack of censorship affects wider society
Counterargument + rebuttal
Conclusion

C. Use your own voice
You often write phrases like:
“the advocates for censorship realize…”
“the proponents prove…”
“the supporters show…”

This distance weakens your authority. Since this is your argument, say:
“This evidence shows…”
“These examples suggest…”
“I argue that…” (if first person is allowed)

Language issues to correct

A. Word choice
Several words are incorrect or unnatural:
effect / affect: usually affect is the verb, effect is the noun  
  - Wrong: “what they view effects their behavior”  
  - Correct: “what they view affects their behavior”
suppose → supposed
peak → pique
caniving → probably conniving
deminish → diminish
fore shadowing → foreshadowing

B. Grammar and sentence structure
You have many run-on sentences and sentence fragments. For example:

Original:  
*Children are very impressionable, the information that is instilled into them, whether it be good or bad becomes a part of them.*

Better:  
*Children are very impressionable. Information instilled in them, whether good or bad, becomes part of who they are.*

Use shorter sentences more often. This will make your argument sound more confident.

C. Articles, plurals, and prepositions
Because your first language is Chinese, watch for common English patterns:
“increase in censorship” not “censorships”
“a policy of control or standards for something” not “standards upon something institution”
“the minds of children” not “the mind’s of children”

Evidence and logic
Some evidence is useful, but some claims are too broad or emotional. Try to explain evidence more carefully.

For example, instead of:
*television needs to be censored… because whether it's 9:00 PM or 8:30 AM it's still public television and content of that nature will effect any body's mind whether you're a child or an adult.*

Try:
*Because broadcast television is widely accessible, harmful material can reach children even when parents are not supervising closely.*

Also, be careful with claims like “society is becoming numb.” This can work, but you need clearer explanation and stronger proof.

Tone and academic style
Your essay sometimes sounds informal or dramatic:
“birds and the bees”
“back in the day”
“so they say”

In academic writing, use more formal wording:
sexuality / sex education
in earlier decades
according to network executives

Also, avoid overstatement unless you can prove it. Instead of “the networks will destroy censorship,” say “the networks may continue to push boundaries.”

Conclusion advice
Your conclusion repeats earlier ideas but can be more concise. Restate:
your thesis,
your key reasons,
why the issue matters.

Possible model:  
*In conclusion, stronger television censorship is necessary because children are especially vulnerable to violent and sexual media content, parental supervision is not always enough, and repeated exposure can reduce society’s sensitivity to harmful material. For these reasons, stricter standards on broadcast television would better protect both children and the public.*

Final suggestion
When revising, focus first on big issues:
thesis
paragraph organization
removing repetition
clearer topic sentences

Then edit for:
grammar
word choice
punctuation

You have strong passion and many ideas. If you reorganize the essay and simplify the language, your argument will become much more powerful.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong effort and a clear interest in the ethical debate around genetic research. You understand the topic well and present several relevant concerns: eugenics, privacy, discrimination, and gene manipulation. These are good ideas for an argumentative essay. To revise successfully, focus on clarifying your thesis, strengthening organization, and improving sentence-level accuracy.

Main issue: the essay argues about “how to argue,” not the issue itself
The biggest problem is that your essay often explains how someone could make an argument against genetic research, instead of directly making that argument yourself.

For example, you write:
“One possible way of presenting the most effective argument…”
“One should present…”
“This would be effective in an argument…”

This makes the essay sound like an analysis of argument strategy rather than an argumentative essay. Instead, take a clear position and argue it directly.

Revise your thesis
Your current introduction gives background well, but the thesis is indirect. Try a thesis like:

“Although genetic research may improve medicine, its risks—such as misuse for eugenics, loss of privacy, discrimination, and unethical gene manipulation—mean that it should be strictly limited and carefully regulated.”

This kind of thesis is direct, arguable, and easier to support.

Improve overall organization
Your essay has useful points, but some paragraphs repeat the same pattern: introducing what “could be used” in an argument. A stronger structure would be:

Introduction
   - Background on genetics
   - Clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Historical dangers
   - Eugenics and the Nazi example
   - Explain why this history matters today

Body Paragraph 2: Limits of the pro-genetics argument
   - Screening for disease
   - Problem of removing useful genes and reducing diversity

Body Paragraph 3: Privacy and discrimination
   - Employers, insurers, military
   - Genetic information misuse

Body Paragraph 4: Genetic manipulation and “designer babies”
   - Difference between therapy and enhancement
   - Ethical consequences

Body Paragraph 5: Weak regulation
   - International disagreement
   - Lack of effective control

Conclusion
   - Restate position
   - Emphasize need for caution/regulation

Use evidence more effectively
You refer to several sources (Sinsheimer, Maddox, Annas, Aldhous), which is excellent. However, many quotations are missing and marked with . This creates major gaps in the argument. You need to:
insert the actual quotations or paraphrases,
explain each source clearly,
connect the evidence back to your thesis.

After each source, ask:
What does this evidence mean?
Why does it support my argument?
How does it affect the reader?

Also, be careful with the Nazi comparison. It is powerful, but it should be used precisely and responsibly, not only for emotional effect.

Develop counterargument more fairly
You do mention supporters of genetic research, which is good. But the rebuttal can be stronger if you acknowledge real benefits more clearly first. For example:
early detection of hereditary diseases,
possible treatment development,
improved medical knowledge.

Then explain why these benefits do not remove the ethical risks. This makes your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Language and grammar corrections
Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are repeated grammar and word-choice problems. Here are the most important ones:

Word choice / accuracy
“Mendal” → Mendel
“Gregorian monk” → Augustinian monk (more accurate)
“disease carrying genes” → disease-carrying genes
“malevolent” is not the best word for genes; use harmful
“do three doings” is unnatural; use “do three things”
“layman” is awkward here; use “the general public”

Grammar
“to see future, untreatable, hereditary conditions” → “to predict future hereditary conditions that are currently untreatable”
“one’s argument” not “ones argument”
“One consequences deals with privacy” → “One consequence deals with privacy”
“if genetic information is be kept private” → “whether genetic information can be kept private”
“if the governments of the world cannot control the research than it should not be done” → “if the governments of the world cannot control the research, then it should not continue”

Style
Try to reduce repeated use of:
“one should”
“this would be effective”
“could be used”

These phrases weaken your authority. Write more directly.

Stronger academic tone
Some sentences are too informal or general:
“what will stop employers…” → better as “There is little to prevent employers…”
“An issue this great should not be taken lightly” → “An issue of this importance requires careful ethical scrutiny.”

Final revision advice
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Replace the “how to argue” approach with a direct argument.
Add the missing evidence/quotations.
Strengthen the thesis and paragraph topic sentences.
Correct grammar and word choice.
Conclude with a clear position: not just fear, but a reasoned claim about limits or regulation.

You have a solid foundation and important ideas. With a clearer thesis and more direct argumentation, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central topic and shows good effort at rhetorical analysis. You compare JAMA and Newsweek in terms of audience, context, and attitude, and this is an appropriate basis for an argumentative essay. The paper is organized into introduction, body paragraphs, and conclusion, which gives it a strong overall structure. To revise effectively, focus on making your thesis more precise, supporting claims with clearer evidence, and improving language accuracy.

Strengths

Clear comparison: You consistently compare the two publications rather than discussing them separately.
Awareness of audience: You correctly recognize that audience affects style, tone, and use of evidence.
Logical organization: Most body paragraphs focus on one main idea, such as audience, physical/social context, or attitude.
Confident voice: Your writing sounds engaged and analytical.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the thesis more specific and balanced
Your thesis is interesting, but it becomes too judgmental when you say popular magazines “exploit” the topic to increase readership. This is a strong claim, but the essay does not fully prove it. A better thesis would be more precise and easier to support.

Current idea:  
“Newsweek and JAMA utilize context and attitude to describe their two different viewpoints…”

Possible revision:  
“Because JAMA and Newsweek address different audiences, they present the issue of college binge drinking in different ways: JAMA emphasizes balanced data and scientific structure, while Newsweek uses emotionally engaging examples and selective statistics to attract general readers.”

This version is clearer and more arguable.

B. Use more specific evidence from the articles
You often make strong claims, but some examples are vague or incomplete. For instance, you write “such as ” or mention words from the article without quoting them. These placeholders make the analysis unfinished.

To improve:
Include actual quotations or exact examples from each text.
After each example, explain how it supports your point.
Avoid repeating general statements like “this appeals to the audience” without analysis.

Example pattern to follow:
Make a claim.  
Give a quotation/example.  
Explain its effect on the intended audience.

C. Strengthen analysis, not just summary
Sometimes the essay summarizes what each article contains, but rhetorical analysis should explain why those choices matter.

For example, instead of only saying JAMA includes charts and methods, explain:
why a medical audience values transparency,
how formal structure builds credibility,
how this creates an objective tone.

Similarly, for Newsweek, explain how anecdotes and dramatic details create urgency or emotional involvement.

Some statements are too broad, such as:
“People are for some reason drawn to look at the dark side of issues.”
“Making those assumptions is what makes a good writer.”
“The problems… are really not of concern to them.”

These claims sound too absolute and may weaken your credibility. Use more cautious language:
“Many readers may be drawn to dramatic stories.”
“The writers seem to assume that emotional examples will attract readers.”
“The article appears to prioritize reader interest over balance.”

Language and grammar issues

Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are several sentence-level errors. Here are common patterns:

Word choice / form
“any give time” → “any given time”
“todays” → “today’s”
“56% or men” → “56% of men”
“has an affect on” → “has an effect on”
“take in to account” → “take into account”
“this make” → “this makes”
“hook it audience” → “hook its audience”

Grammar
“The writers for Newsweek and JAMA utilize…”  
  Better: “The writers of Newsweek and JAMA use…”
“They only look at facts…”  
  If referring to the article, use singular: “It only looks at facts…”
“The authors fact-based, no emotion approach”  
  → “The authors’ fact-based, unemotional approach”

Sentence style
Some sentences are too long and repetitive. Try to shorten them and remove repeated ideas. For example, you mention audience influence many times in similar wording. Combine overlapping points.

Organization suggestions

Final revision priorities

Rewrite the thesis to be more precise and supportable.  
Replace all placeholders with real quotations or details.  
Add analysis after each example.  
Reduce overgeneralizations and use more academic wording.  
Proofread for grammar, articles, possessives, and word choice.

You have a solid foundation and a clear understanding of the assignment. With stronger evidence and more careful language, this essay can become much more persuasive and academically polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that comprehensive sex education is more effective and more honest than abstinence-only education. This is a strong and debatable thesis, and you repeatedly return to the idea that misinformation causes teenagers to distrust sex education. That is the essay’s main strength.

Main revision priorities

A. Clarify your thesis early
Your introduction gives background, but the central claim is not stated clearly enough. Try to make your position direct and specific.

For example, you could revise the thesis to something like:

Comprehensive sex education should be taught in schools because abstinence-only programs often rely on misleading information, weak evidence, and fear-based reasoning, which reduces their credibility and effectiveness.

This version tells the reader exactly what your essay will prove.

B. Improve organization
Right now, some paragraphs feel repetitive because they all argue that abstinence-only supporters use weak reasoning. That point is valid, but each body paragraph should focus on one distinct reason.

A possible structure:
Introduction + thesis
Body paragraph 1: misleading or scientifically inaccurate claims
Body paragraph 2: weak or unreliable studies/statistics
Body paragraph 3: fear-based rhetoric and its effects on teenagers
Conclusion

This structure will make your argument easier to follow.

C. Explain your evidence more fully
You include many references to studies, speakers, and organizations, which is good. However, sometimes the quotations are not explained enough. After each example, ask:
What exactly does this example show?
Why does it weaken the abstinence-only position?
How does it support comprehensive sex education?

Your analysis should be more important than the quotation itself.

Use of sources and argumentation

A. Avoid overstating
Some sentences are too absolute, such as suggesting that abstinence-only advocates only use fallacies. A stronger academic style is more precise:
Instead of “proponents have used illogical reasoning”
Try “some proponents rely on questionable reasoning or unsupported claims”

This sounds more balanced and credible.

B. Be careful with fairness
You do a good job presenting the opposing side, but sometimes the wording sounds emotional:
“become enraged”
“taking full advantage”
“their own fallacies and misfortunes”

In argumentative writing, neutral language is usually more persuasive. Try to sound analytical rather than angry.

Language issues to revise

Your meaning is often understandable, but grammar and word choice sometimes make sentences unclear. Below are common patterns to fix.

A. Word choice
“more than recent” → “has existed for many years” or “is not new”
“prevelant” → “prevalent”
“facile reasoning” is possible, but “oversimplified reasoning” may be clearer.

B. Grammar
“teens will not being informed” → “teens will not be informed”
“studies have beared this scrutiny” → “studies have withstood this scrutiny”
“she consider a joke” → “she considers a joke”
“The proponents case” → “The proponents’ case”

C. Sentence structure
Some sentences are too long and difficult to follow. For example:

Original:  
“Accordingly, opponents of ‘abstinence-only’ argue that most teens know neither of those ideas is true and since, why should teens even listen to proponents…”

This can be clearer as:

Revision:  
“Opponents of abstinence-only education argue that many teenagers know these claims are false. As a result, teenagers may stop listening to advocates altogether.”

Shorter sentences will make your argument stronger.

Style and academic tone

A. Reduce repetition
You repeat “opponents” and “proponents” very often. That creates a mechanical style. You can vary your language:
supporters of abstinence-only education
critics of abstinence-only programs
advocates of comprehensive sex education

B. Use transitions more carefully
You use transitions such as “Yet,” “Therefore,” “Hence,” and “At the same time.” This is good, but sometimes the logic between sentences is not fully clear. Make sure each transition accurately reflects the relationship:
contrast: however, nevertheless
cause/effect: therefore, as a result
addition: furthermore, in addition

Final advice
For revision, focus on these three goals:
Write a sharper thesis
Organize each paragraph around one main point
Edit for clarity, grammar, and a more formal tone

You already have a strong argumentative direction and relevant evidence. If you make the structure cleaner and the language more precise, your essay will become much more persuasive.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0043.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong effort in analyzing both sides of a difficult issue. You clearly understand that adoption disputes involve law, morality, emotions, and media influence. You also do a good job noticing weaknesses in the arguments used by both sides. This gives your essay an analytical direction rather than a purely emotional one. Below are suggestions to help you revise.

Main argument and thesis

Your thesis is interesting, but it is not fully clear. You seem to argue not that biological or adoptive parents should win, but that both sides use weak arguments, so no simple answer is possible. This is a valid position, but you should state it more directly and consistently.

For example, your introduction could end with something like:

“Although both biological and adoptive parents claim they have the stronger right to the child, the public debate is weakened by emotional examples, inconsistent values, and unsupported predictions; therefore, custody disputes should be judged case by case rather than by broad generalizations.”

This would help readers understand your focus immediately.

Organization

Your essay has many good points, but the structure is sometimes hard to follow. You move back and forth between:
biological parents’ arguments,
adoptive parents’ arguments,
media influence,
legal issues,
hypothetical consequences.

Try to organize the body more clearly. For example:

Arguments for biological parents
   - legal rights
   - moral guilt / blood ties
   - weaknesses in these arguments

Arguments for adoptive parents
   - emotional attachment
   - child welfare / psychology
   - public opinion and media
   - weaknesses in these arguments

Why the debate remains unresolved
   - both sides rely on extreme cases
   - generalizations are misleading
   - each case should be considered individually

This would make your reasoning easier to follow.

Use of evidence

A major weakness is that many examples are mentioned but not fully explained. Sometimes you refer to a case, statistic, or article, but do not show exactly how it proves your point. Also, there are several places marked , which leave important information missing. These gaps seriously weaken credibility.

When you revise:
complete all missing quotations and statistics,
explain each example in detail,
connect evidence directly to your claim.

For instance, instead of saying a case is “rare” or “extreme,” explain why using rare cases creates a weak argument. You should also avoid repeating that both sides are weak unless you provide specific proof.

Analysis

Your strongest skill in this essay is critique: you often ask whether an argument is too emotional, too general, or too hypothetical. That is good academic thinking. However, sometimes the essay becomes repetitive because many paragraphs make the same point: “this example is rare, so it is weak.”

Try to deepen the analysis by asking:
What kind of evidence would be stronger?
Should the child’s best interests be more important than legal parenthood?
When should emotional bonds matter more than biology?
How does media framing distort ethical judgment?

Answering these questions would make your essay more original and persuasive.

Style and tone

Your tone is mostly formal, but there are moments where wording is awkward or imprecise. Examples:

“proponents for biological parents” → better: “supporters of biological parents”
“opponents to biological parents” → better: “supporters of adoptive parents” or “opponents of biological parents’ claims”
“could possible happen” → “could possibly happen”
“in each cases” → “in each case”
“on could argue” → “one could argue”

Be careful with sentence length. Some sentences are too long and include too many ideas. Shorter sentences will make your argument clearer.

Grammar and language patterns

As a Chinese-speaking writer, you may want to pay special attention to:
articles: “the child,” “a case,” “the courts”
singular/plural: “each case is,” not “each cases are”
word form: “legality,” “psychological,” “premature”
parallel structure: “more concrete evidence, clearer consequences, and stronger values”

Also check pronouns. You switch between him, his/her, and their. Choose one consistent form. In modern academic English, singular they/their is often best.

Conclusion

Your conclusion repeats earlier ideas, but it could be sharper. Instead of only saying no answer exists, explain what follows from that. For example: courts should evaluate each adoption dispute individually, with more attention to evidence and the child’s long-term welfare.

Revision priorities

Clarify the thesis
Reorganize the body by categories
Fill in missing evidence and quotations
Reduce repetition
Edit grammar and wording for precision

Overall, this essay shows thoughtful engagement with a complex ethical issue. Your next step is to make the argument more focused, better organized, and more evidence-based.

Since this is an argumentative essay, your thesis should clearly state your position.

Possible revision of thesis:
“Although stricter academic penalties can reduce cheating, establishing a strong honor code is the most effective way for universities to create a lasting culture of academic honesty.”

This thesis is better because it:
shows your position clearly,
acknowledges another viewpoint,
sets up the essay’s comparison.

Also, some paragraphs lose focus. For example, the paragraph about faculty involvement seems important, but it is not well connected to your main claim about honor codes. Decide whether this is:
a third solution, or
a supporting condition that makes honor codes work.

If it is only support, explain that clearly.

Develop evidence more fully
You mention useful examples, especially the University of Virginia and the “XF” grade. However, several places feel incomplete because of the  gaps or because the point is not fully explained.

For each body paragraph, try this structure:
Topic sentence
Evidence from a source
Explanation of why the evidence matters
Link back to your argument

For example, in the honor code paragraph, you mention Virginia’s practices, but you should explain why those practices are effective. Is it because students learn the value of honesty before entering the university? Is it because the code becomes part of campus culture? Spell this out.

Also, be careful when using personal experience. Your sentence “As a student, I believe…” adds voice, but your essay will be stronger if personal opinion supports source-based reasoning rather than replacing it.

Improve coherence and transitions
Sometimes ideas jump too quickly. For example, in one paragraph you support honor codes, then suddenly discuss peer pressure. That is a good counterargument, but it needs a clearer transition.

Useful transition phrases:
“However, this method has limitations.”
“One weakness of honor codes is…”
“Even so, this problem can be reduced if…”

You should also make sure your conclusion does more than repeat earlier points. It should explain why your preferred solution is better overall, despite weaknesses.

Correct grammar and wording
Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are many grammar and word-choice problems that reduce clarity. Here are common patterns to revise:

Word choice / collocation
“a major question of value” → “a major moral issue”
“enlisted the use of” → “adopted” or “implemented”
“turn in others whom they believe have cheated” → “report students they believe have cheated”
“when taken in account” → “when taken into account”
“just stands” → “firm positions” or “fair stands”
“the ignorant cheater” → “students who cheat unintentionally”
“eliminated this problem” → “eliminate this problem”

Grammar
“it encourages” should match “Honor codes require…”  
  Better: “Honor codes require students to sign a pledge not to cheat and encourage them to report suspected cheating.”
“students will not cheat if the range of punishments are” → “the range of punishments is”
“have been to reluctant” → “have been too reluctant”
“on a first hand basis” → “firsthand”

Articles and prepositions
As a Chinese speaker, you may want to pay special attention to a/the, singular/plural nouns, and fixed prepositions:
“presented to the school’s honor code” → “introduced to the school’s honor code”
“write an essay about the honor code on their application” is okay, but “in their application” may sound more natural.

Make the style more academic
Avoid overstatements such as:
“cheating has become an aspect of college life”
“cheating is becoming out of control”

These are strong claims, so they need evidence or more careful wording.

More academic alternatives:
“cheating has become a common concern on college campuses”
“academic dishonesty appears to be increasing”

Also, refer to sources more precisely. Instead of “The authors show…,” try naming the source or explaining the study briefly.

Revision priorities
Write a clearer thesis stating that honor codes are the best solution.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one has one clear purpose.
Add explanation after each example.
Fix grammar, especially article use, verb agreement, and word choice.
Replace informal or vague phrases with more academic language.

You already have a strong foundation: a relevant issue, a clear interest in the topic, and useful examples. With better organization and more precise language, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong engagement with a complex legal and ethical issue. You clearly understand that the debate is not only about law, but also about how each side persuades readers. This is a real strength. You also organize the essay in a generally logical way: introduction, background, argument for one side, argument for the other side, and conclusion.

What you do well

Clear topic and context
   You introduce the Fourth Amendment and explain why “exigent circumstances” is open to interpretation. This gives readers useful background.

Awareness of rhetoric
   A major strength is that you do not just summarize the case; you analyze how each side uses emotion, credibility, and logic. For example, you notice how the prosecution asks readers to sympathize with detectives, while the opposition attacks the detectives’ credibility.

Good use of contrast
   The essay repeatedly compares “proposition” and “opposition,” which helps structure your discussion.

Main areas to revise

Clarify your thesis
Your introduction gives background, but your main claim is still somewhat unclear. Are you arguing:
that both sides argue effectively?
that the opposition is more persuasive?
that illegally obtained evidence should not be admitted?

Your conclusion says “both sides argue their points effectively,” so your thesis should state that earlier and more directly.

Possible revision of thesis:
> In the debate over the admissibility of evidence in the O.J. Simpson case, both supporters and opponents of the search use persuasive rhetorical strategies, but the opposition relies more effectively on challenges to police credibility and procedure.

Make your purpose more academic
Some sentences sound too conversational or opinion-based, such as:
“After all, the detectives have earned their badge…”
“the opposition did a little homework…”
“scratching to find anything…”

These phrases weaken your academic tone. Try to sound more neutral and analytical.

Example:
Instead of “the detectives have earned their badge,” write:
> the articles encourage readers to trust the detectives’ professional judgment.

Use evidence more precisely
You refer to articles and judges, which is good, but several quotations are missing and shown as . These must be filled in or removed. Without them, some paragraphs feel incomplete.

Also, sometimes you make strong claims without enough support:
“This is a definite strength in the opposition’s argument.”
“The strategy … works very well…”

Explain why it is effective and base that judgment on specific evidence.

Improve sentence clarity and grammar
There are many good sentences, but some are awkward or incorrect. Common problems include:

Word choice
  - “illegal search and seizure is unlawful” → repetitive
  - “causes much skepticism in the validity” → better: “causes skepticism about the validity”
  - “revolved from one attempting” → should be “evolved from one attempting”

Article/preposition use
  - “except for in” → “except in”
  - “question the validity” → “questions the validity”
  - “lured them into the house” may be better as “prompted them to enter the house”

Capitalization/spelling
  - “Los Angelas” → “Los Angeles”
  - “Goldwin” may need checking
  - “one way” should be capitalized at the beginning of a sentence

Agreement
  - “the opposition … question” → “questions”

Strengthen paragraph unity
Some body paragraphs begin with a clear point, but then move into summary. Try this structure:

topic sentence with one clear analytical point
example/evidence
explanation of how that evidence persuades readers
link back to thesis

For example, in the paragraph about Fuhrman, the main point should be:
> The opposition undermines the prosecution by attacking Fuhrman’s credibility.

Then all details in the paragraph should support that one idea.

Organization suggestions

In the introduction, define your focus more clearly: legal issue or rhetorical analysis?
In body paragraphs, use clearer topic sentences such as:
  - “Supporters of the search appeal to readers’ fear that another victim may have been inside the house.”
  - “Opponents focus less on constitutional interpretation and more on police credibility.”

Style suggestions

“most people still remember…”
“sure to pull compassion out of most readers”

These claims are too broad unless you can support them. More academic alternatives:
> This argument is designed to evoke sympathy in readers.
> This strategy may be persuasive to readers who prioritize public safety.

Final advice
Your essay has a solid foundation and strong analytical potential. For revision, focus on:
writing a sharper thesis,
keeping a formal academic tone,
correcting grammar and word choice,
replacing missing quotations,
deepening analysis instead of summarizing.

With these changes, your essay will become much clearer and more persuasive.

Analytical tone

This will reduce repetition and improve flow.

Also avoid broad claims like:

avoid moralizing statements unless you are analyzing others’ views

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You chose an important and serious topic, and your essay shows effort to use sociological theory to explain adolescent suicide. That is a strong idea. You also include statistics and theorists such as Durkheim and Marx, which gives your paper an academic direction. For revision, focus on making your argument clearer, more accurate, and more organized.

Main strengths
Important topic: You explain that adolescent suicide deserves public attention.
Use of theory: Connecting suicide to Structural Functionalism and Conflict Theory is ambitious and thoughtful.
Use of sources/statistics: Numbers and references help create authority.

Biggest issues to revise

A. Clarify your thesis
Your introduction gives background, but the main argument is not fully clear. You seem to argue that:
adolescent suicide is a serious social problem, and  
sociological theories help explain why it happens.

Make this explicit in one sentence near the end of the introduction.

Possible revision:
> This essay argues that adolescent suicide is a major social problem and that Durkheim’s structural functionalism and Marx-inspired conflict theory help explain some of its social causes.

B. Improve organization
Right now, some paragraphs are long and repetitive. A clearer structure would help:

Introduction: topic, importance, thesis  
Background: key statistics and trends  
Durkheim / Structural Functionalism  
Application to adolescents: egoistic and anomic suicide  
Conflict theory / Marx  
Comparison of theories  
Conclusion

Also, each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that tells the reader the paragraph’s main point.

C. Be careful with argument and evidence
Some claims are too broad or unsupported. For example:

“Suicide used to be just an adult problem” — this is too absolute.
“There are no theories… that argue adolescent suicide isn’t a direct result of depression” — this is also too absolute and probably inaccurate.
“Everyone experiences some type or form of depression” — this needs more careful wording.

In academic writing, avoid overgeneralization. Use phrases like:
many researchers argue
one important factor is
studies suggest
adolescent suicide is often associated with

D. Use theory more accurately
Your essay discusses Durkheim, but some explanations are unclear or inaccurate.

For example:
Durkheim is most known for linking suicide to social integration and social regulation.
Egoistic suicide = low social integration.
Anomic suicide = weak social regulation, often during social disruption.
Altruistic suicide = excessively high integration.

Try to explain these concepts in simpler, more precise language, then show clearly how they apply to adolescents.

Your Marx/conflict theory paragraph is weaker than the Durkheim section because the connection to adolescent suicide is less direct. You need to explain more specifically how social inequality, family stress, alienation, or social change may increase risk.

Language issues to correct

A. Grammar
There are many subject-verb agreement and word form problems:
“the worst problem concerning are young people” → “concerning young people”
“its” → “it’s” when you mean “it is”
“These works and studies has looked” → “have looked”
“adolescents who has moved” → “adolescents who have moved”

B. Word choice
Some words are incorrect or unnatural:
“contraversal” → controversial
“publically” → publicly
“phenomenal” is not the right word for numbers of suicide attempts; use alarming, striking, or high
“bondness” → connectedness or sense of belonging

C. Sentence style
Many sentences are too long and difficult to follow. Break them into shorter sentences. Avoid starting too many sentences with “And.”

For example:

Original:
> And being that Structural Functionalist argues that society is made up of functional parts...

Better:
> Structural functionalism argues that society is made up of interrelated parts. Durkheim’s theory of suicide fits this perspective because he examined how social forces affect individual behavior.

Citation and academic style
Your references appear as symbols like `<R>` and , but they are not explained. Make sure you use a consistent citation style required by your teacher (MLA, APA, etc). Also, introduce sources more clearly.

For example:
> According to Rabkin, adolescents who feel isolated may view themselves as “different” from others.

Conclusion
Your final sentence is interesting, but too vague. A stronger conclusion should:
restate the thesis,
summarize the two theories,
explain why understanding social causes matters.

Possible model:
> In conclusion, adolescent suicide should be understood not only as an individual tragedy but also as a social problem. Durkheim’s ideas about social integration and regulation, together with conflict theory’s focus on social change and pressure, help explain why some adolescents are especially vulnerable.

Revision priorities
Write a clear thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs by theory and application.
Correct major grammar errors.
Replace overgeneralizations with cautious academic language.
Explain Durkheim and Marx more precisely.
Fix citation format.

You have a strong topic and good ambition. With clearer structure and more careful language, this can become a much stronger academic essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a strong sense of urgency. You successfully show that water pollution is both an environmental and a social problem, and you try to connect it to sociological theory, which is a good argumentative goal. For revision, focus on three main areas: thesis and argument, organization, and language accuracy/style.

Strengthen the thesis and central argument
Right now, the paper introduces water pollution and then discusses Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory, but the main claim is not stated clearly enough. Your reader needs one sentence early in the essay that explains exactly what you will argue.

For example, you could write something like:

“Water pollution can be better understood through Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory because both theories explain how individual decisions, rewards, and consequences shape harmful environmental behavior.”

This would give the paper a clear direction. Then each body paragraph should help prove that claim.

Also, some parts of the essay become more descriptive than argumentative. For instance, the paragraph listing types of pollutants gives useful background, but it should connect more directly to your argument about human behavior and theory. Ask yourself: How does this information support my main point?

Improve organization and paragraph focus
The essay would be stronger with a more logical structure. A possible structure:

Introduction
   - Background on water pollution
   - Importance of freshwater
   - Clear thesis

Background on water pollution
   - Types and sources of pollution
   - Why the issue matters socially

Rational Choice Theory
   - Define the theory clearly
   - Explain how it applies to companies and individuals
   - Use evidence

Exchange Theory
   - Define the theory clearly
   - Explain costs, rewards, and reinforcement
   - Apply to pollution behavior

Conclusion
   - Restate argument
   - Explain why understanding behavior matters for solving pollution

Some paragraphs are too long and contain many ideas at once. Try to make one main idea per paragraph. Use clear topic sentences. For example:

“Rational Choice Theory helps explain water pollution because people and companies often act according to perceived self-interest.”
“Exchange Theory emphasizes rewards and costs, which can explain why polluting practices continue.”

Clarify your use of theory
This is the most important content issue. At times, your explanation of the theories is too general or not fully accurate. You need to define each theory more precisely and then apply it carefully.

For example:
Rational Choice Theory usually suggests that people make decisions based on perceived benefits and costs.
Exchange Theory focuses on social behavior as an exchange process involving rewards, punishments, and reinforcement.

When applying the theories, be specific:
Why might a company pollute? Lower disposal costs, less regulation, more profit.
What would change behavior? Fines, public pressure, education, legal consequences, cleaner incentives.

This will make your argument more analytical and persuasive.

Deal with missing citations and placeholders
Your essay includes several marks such as “ <R>” and “Contemporary Journal reports that .” These make the essay feel unfinished. Before submitting, you must:
replace all placeholders with real evidence or remove them
cite your sources consistently
make sure statistics are accurate and introduced clearly

If you use numbers, explain what they prove. Do not only list statistics.

Language and grammar corrections
Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are frequent grammar and word-choice errors. Here are some common patterns to fix:

Possessives
its’ → its
ones’ behavior → one’s behavior

Word choice
then into four major categories → them into four major categories
war pollution threat → probably water pollution threat
insure the cooperation → ensure cooperation
individualizing → individual
human race → the human race or simply humans

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are awkward or too long. Example:

Original:  
“Humans only get less than 3% of the planet's fresh water supply.”  
Better:  
“Less than 3% of the planet’s water is freshwater available to humans.”

Original:  
“The main source of these agents are human excrement which are untreated and enter the water cycle.”

Remove unnecessary hyphens: two- non-point → two: non-point
Fix repeated words: to to pollute
Be careful with commas; some are unnecessary.

Academic tone

“like the text says”
“awakened in a sense”
“dozens of AIDS patients died prematurely”

Final advice
This essay has a solid topic and an interesting interdisciplinary approach. Your next revision should focus on:

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: marijuana should be legalized for medical, personal, industrial, and economic reasons. You also show strong effort in research and include many historical and medical details. This gives your paper substance. In revision, your main goals should be to improve organization, academic tone, use of evidence, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear argumentative stance: the reader always knows what you believe.
Wide range of points: medicine, prisons, crime, judges, industry, and tax revenue.
Use of theories: trying to apply structural functionalism, exchange theory, and conflict theory is ambitious and appropriate for a sociology-style argument.
Good sense of urgency: your writing shows engagement with the topic.

Main Revision Priorities

A. Narrow and organize the argument
Right now, the essay covers too many topics, so some paragraphs feel rushed or repetitive. Consider grouping your argument into 3–4 main claims instead of many separate ones.

For example:
Marijuana should be legalized for medical use.  
Criminalization creates social harms (prisons, unequal justice, crime).  
Legalization would bring economic and industrial benefits.  

Then make each body paragraph follow this pattern:
topic sentence
evidence
explanation
connection to your thesis/theory

Your current introduction is long and includes background, history, and thesis all together. Try ending the introduction with one concise thesis statement.

Example:
> This essay argues that legalizing marijuana, especially for medical use, would reduce social harm, improve justice, and provide economic benefits.

B. Use theory more accurately and consistently
You mention several theories, but sometimes they feel added on rather than fully explained. Instead of briefly naming all three many times, explain them more clearly.

For example:
Structural functionalism: How would legalization improve social stability or institutions?
Conflict theory: How does criminalization affect social classes differently?
Exchange theory: What are the costs and rewards of legalization vs. criminalization?

Use one theory per paragraph if possible. That will make your analysis more convincing.

C. Improve evidence and citation style
You include many facts, but some are outdated, unsupported, or too absolute. Also, citation markers like “<R>” are not enough in a formal essay unless your teacher specifically requires them.

Revise by:
checking whether all facts are accurate
avoiding claims such as “not a single death” unless you can support them carefully
distinguishing between evidence and opinion
citing sources in a consistent academic style (MLA, APA, etc)

Some claims also need more careful logic. For example, “There are many thefts and break-ins because people are trying to obtain pot” needs evidence, not assumption.

Language and Style Issues

A. Word choice and spelling
There are several vocabulary and spelling errors. Examples:
cannavis sativa → *Cannabis sativa*
heroine → *heroin*
foe the DEA → *for the DEA*
mar on drugs → *war on drugs*
ideal → probably *illegal*
breakins → *break-ins*

Be careful with scientific and legal terms.

B. Grammar
Some common problems:
sentence fragments
run-on sentences
article use (“a,” “the”)
plural/singular agreement

Example:
> “Overcrowded prisons is another concern...”
should be:
> “Overcrowded prisons are another concern...”

C. Punctuation and formatting
You often have missing commas, unnecessary hyphens, and inconsistent capitalization. For example:
“Marijuana When” needs punctuation after *marijuana*.
“A Few” should not capitalize *Few* in the middle of a sentence.
Avoid overusing quotation marks and dashes.

D. Academic tone
Words like pot, weed, and getting high create an informal tone. Since this is an academic essay, prefer:
*marijuana* or *cannabis*
*recreational use*
*intoxication* or *use*

You can mention slang terms once in the introduction if relevant, but not throughout the essay.

Paragraph-Level Advice
Introduction: shorten background and end with a precise thesis.
Medical-use section: this is your strongest part; keep it, but make it more focused and better sourced.
Prison/judges/crime sections: these ideas are relevant, but they need clearer evidence and smoother transitions.
Industrial fabrics and revenue: combine these into one economic section.
Conclusion: do not just repeat points. Summarize the strongest reasons and restate your thesis more formally.

Final Suggestion
When revising, focus first on big issues:
clearer structure  
stronger thesis  
better integration of theory  
more careful evidence  

Then edit for grammar, word choice, and punctuation.

You have the basis for a persuasive essay, especially because you already have many ideas and sources. The next step is to make the paper more focused, more academic, and more logically developed.

Your essay shows clear effort, strong engagement with the topic, and a serious attempt to use sociological theory to analyze homelessness. You also include historical background, causes, and two theoretical frameworks, which gives the paper ambition and breadth. To revise effectively, focus on thesis, organization, evidence, style, and grammar.

Main strength: strong topic and serious analysis
You clearly believe homelessness is an urgent social problem, and that conviction gives your essay energy. A second strength is that you try to go beyond description by applying conflict theory and symbolic interactionism. This is a good academic move because it shows analysis rather than only opinion.

Main problem: the thesis is unclear and too broad
At the beginning, you argue that homelessness is a major problem. Later, the essay becomes mostly a sociology paper comparing theories. Because of this, the reader is unsure of your main argument.

Ask yourself: What is this essay trying to prove?
Possible revised thesis:
*Homelessness in the United States is caused not only by economic inequality but also by social labeling, and symbolic interactionism provides the most useful framework for understanding its human impact.*

This kind of thesis is better because it makes a specific claim and prepares the reader for your conclusion.

Organization: too much summary, not enough control
Right now, the essay feels like several essays combined:
history of homelessness  
causes of homelessness  
conflict theory  
symbolic interactionism  
comparison of theories

This makes the paper very long and repetitive. Try a clearer structure:

Introduction
define the issue
present your thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Brief background and causes
housing costs
unemployment
deinstitutionalization
racial inequality

Body Paragraph 2: Conflict theory
explain the theory briefly
apply it directly to homelessness
give examples

Body Paragraph 3: Symbolic interactionism
explain the theory briefly
apply labeling/stigma to homelessness
give examples

Body Paragraph 4: Which theory is more useful?
compare the two
explain why one is stronger for your purpose

Conclusion
restate your argument
suggest what understanding homelessness requires

Also, many paragraphs are too long. Each paragraph should have one clear main idea.

Evidence and support
A major weakness is lack of reliable evidence. You make many strong claims, but often without proof. For example:
“Real estate agencies have also conspired against the poor…”
“The government… tagged the homeless as lazy…”
“Drug use has increased dramatically in the homeless community.”

These claims need sources, statistics, or examples. Also, one quotation is incomplete:  
*“which states that .”*  
This must be fixed.

When revising:
include real citations from credible sources
avoid sweeping claims unless you can support them
distinguish between fact and opinion

Academic tone and word choice
Some wording is too emotional or absolute for academic writing. For example:
“immensely greedy”
“mindlessly follow”
“atrocious things”
“obvious problem”

Use a more neutral style:
instead of “immensely greedy,” say *economically privileged groups may protect their interests*
instead of “mindlessly follow,” say *often adopt dominant social attitudes*

This will make your argument sound more credible.

Grammar and sentence issues
There are frequent grammar problems, especially with:
subject-verb agreement
word form
article use
awkward phrasing
spelling

Examples:
“People living without shelter has been a problem” → “People living without shelter have been a problem” or better: “Homelessness has been a problem”
“their lobs” → “their jobs”
“a live threat” → “a real threat”
“the sociologists choice” → “the sociologist’s choice”
“Contact theorists” → “Conflict theorists”
“effects what they view” → “affects what they view”
“mayor cause” → “major cause”
“people being effected” → “people being affected”

Be careful with overgeneralization
Some statements about homeless people are too broad:
“Most of them have no knowledge of how to maintain what little they possess.”
This sounds judgmental and unsupported. Avoid generalizing about a diverse population.

Final revision advice
For your next draft, prioritize these steps:
Write a clear, arguable thesis
Cut repeated historical details
Organize around argument, not just information
Add credible evidence

Edit carefully for grammar and clarity

Overall, your essay has strong potential because it tackles an important issue and engages with theory. The next step is to make the paper more focused, better supported, and more academically precise.
