Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong effort and clear engagement with a difficult social issue. You use historical background, theory, statistics, and examples, which gives the paper academic depth. Your central contrast between Herbert Spencer and Robert Merton is especially effective because it gives the essay a clear intellectual framework. For revision, focus on thesis clarity, organization, source use, tone, and sentence-level accuracy.

Strengths
Ambitious argument: You do more than describe welfare; you compare two theories and evaluate reform vs. abolition.
Good use of structure in the middle: The Spencer section and Merton section create a useful contrast.
Range of evidence: You include laws, statistics, case examples, and sociological ideas.
Strong conclusion: The final paragraph reaches a balanced position rather than a simplistic one.

Main issue: make your thesis clearer
Right now, the essay asks many questions at the beginning, but your own position is not fully clear until near the end. In an argumentative essay, readers should know your claim early.

What to revise
At the end of the introduction, add a direct thesis, for example:

> Although welfare can create dependency in some cases, abolishing it is neither humane nor effective; instead, welfare should be reformed through realistic job training, childcare support, and stronger accountability measures.

This thesis is better because it:
states your position,
acknowledges the opposing view,
previews your main arguments.

Organization: reduce repetition and sharpen paragraph purpose
The essay is thoughtful, but it is too long and sometimes repetitive. Some paragraphs mainly list facts without clearly explaining how they support your argument.

Suggestions
Divide the essay into clearer sections:
  1. History and growth of welfare
  2. Conservative/Spencer argument
  3. Liberal/Merton argument
  4. Your position: reform, not abolition
At the beginning of each paragraph, write a topic sentence that makes a claim, not just introduces information.
After statistics or examples, explain them. Ask: What does this prove? Why does it matter?

For example, after discussing low education levels, explicitly connect this to Merton:
> These educational deficits support Merton’s view that many recipients face structural disadvantages rather than simply lacking motivation.

Source use and missing material
There are several places with  and <R>, showing missing quotations or references. These must be fixed before the essay is complete.

What to do
Insert the missing quotations or remove those sentences.
Provide full citations in the required format.
Be careful that examples are accurately presented and ethically used.

Also, some evidence is old (mainly from the 1990s). If this is a current argumentative essay, use more recent data. Otherwise, explain the historical context.

Tone and word choice
Some wording sounds too judgmental or absolute, especially when discussing poor people, unmarried mothers, or people on welfare. Since this is an academic argument, use a more neutral and precise tone.

Examples to revise
“too lazy to work” → “perceived as unwilling to work”
“illegitimate kids” → “children born outside marriage”
“their extinction” should be discussed carefully because it is extremely strong and morally loaded

You may explain Spencer’s harsh logic, but make clear when those are his views, not yours.

Logical balance
You do a good job presenting both sides, but sometimes the essay seems to accept weak generalizations too easily, especially in anecdotal examples. One or two personal stories cannot prove that most welfare recipients behave that way.

Improve your argument by:
distinguishing between individual abuse and systemic patterns
acknowledging that some conservative evidence shows real problems
then arguing why those problems support reform, not abolition

This will make your position more persuasive.

Language and grammar corrections
There are many sentence-level errors, but most are understandable. Pay attention to:

Articles and spelling
“Me United States” → “the United States”
“deferent men” → “different men”
“exploded in to” → “exploded into”

Punctuation
“Thus, to Spencer. if…” → “Thus, to Spencer, if…”
“Consider, for example. the father…” → “Consider, for example, the father…”

Word form / awkward phrasing
“base this abolition on the basis that” → “support this abolition on the grounds that”
“social reform he no useful place” → “social reform has no useful place”

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long and should be split into two shorter ones.

Final revision priorities
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Remove or fix all missing quotations/references.
Shorten repetitive sections.
Use more neutral academic language.
Explain evidence more clearly after each example.
Proofread carefully for grammar, punctuation, and spelling.

Overall, this is an intelligent and substantial essay with a strong conceptual core. With clearer argument control and cleaner language, it can become much more convincing and academically polished.

===== ICLE-US-PRB-0039.2.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong effort and clear knowledge of several sociological theories. You use symbolic interactionism, functionalism, conflict theory, and exchange theory to explain divorce, and this gives your paper an academic tone. You also try to connect causes, consequences, and possible solutions, which is a good goal for an argumentative essay.

Main strength: wide range of ideas
The biggest strength of your essay is that it is well-informed. You include statistics, scholars, and theoretical perspectives. This shows serious reading and gives your paper authority.

However, at the moment the essay reads more like a survey/report than a focused argumentative essay. You present many theories, but your own central claim is not strong enough.

Improve the thesis and argument
Your paper needs a clearer thesis early in the introduction. Right now, the essay mainly says divorce is a problem and that different theories explain it. That is descriptive, not strongly argumentative.

Possible stronger thesis:
> Although divorce has many individual causes, its rise is mainly the result of broad social changes in expectations, family roles, and economic independence; therefore, understanding divorce requires a sociological rather than purely personal explanation.

This kind of thesis helps you:
make a clear claim,
organize the body paragraphs,
and remind readers what you are arguing.

Organization
The essay is long and contains many useful points, but some paragraphs feel disconnected. Try to organize it more clearly.

Suggested structure:
Introduction
   - background on divorce
   - clear thesis

Body 1: Symbolic interactionism
   - changing expectations of love
   - changing meanings of marriage/divorce
   - changing gender roles

Body 2: Functionalism
   - industrialization
   - weakened family functions
   - reduced dependence among family members

Body 3: Conflict theory
   - inequality between men and women
   - power struggles in marriage
   - economic and social conflict

Body 4: Exchange theory
   - costs/benefits of marriage
   - barriers to divorce
   - why some people stay or leave

Body 5: Consequences and solutions
   - effects on children
   - realistic expectations
   - communication and equality in marriage

Conclusion
   - restate argument
   - end with a stronger final thought

Also, use clearer topic sentences so each paragraph has one main focus.

Use evidence more carefully
You include many references marked with <R>, but in revision you should make sure that:
all sources are properly cited,
statistics are current or clearly identified as historical,
and evidence supports your own argument.

Also, avoid making claims that may sound biased or outdated without explanation, such as the sentence mentioning homosexuality as a cause. If you keep such points, you need strong evidence and careful wording.

Style and academic tone
Some sentences are effective, but others are repetitive or too general. For example, phrases like “divorce is a problem” or “society will always have problems” are too broad. Try to be more precise.

Example revision:
Original:
> We have seen, through several different theories, how the changes in the family have caused the divorce rate to rise.

Revised:
> These sociological theories suggest that rising divorce rates are closely connected to changes in family expectations, gender roles, and economic relationships.

This sounds more academic and specific.

Grammar and language issues
Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are frequent grammar and word-choice errors. Pay special attention to:

a. Articles and prepositions
“stabilized for the time” → stabilized for the time being
“take for granted of what to expect” → take for granted what to expect

b. Word form / wrong word
“coning” → coming
“pasts of the social system” → parts of the social system
“fob” → job
“decided” → decide
“wilt always” → will always
“me changing sex roles” → the changing sex roles
“Piety needs” → probably Society needs
“has involved through history” → has evolved through history
“possible lower” → possibly lower

c. Subject-verb agreement
“there will stronger marriages” → there will be stronger marriages

d. Sentence structure
Some sentences are awkward or incomplete:
“then when dissatisfactions enter spouses tend to blame one another”
  - better: when dissatisfaction appears, spouses tend to blame one another

e. Capitalization
“it was once a symbol” → It was once a symbol

Conclusion
Your conclusion repeats earlier ideas, but it should be more concise and powerful. Do not just list what the essay discussed. Instead, restate your main argument and explain why it matters.

Possible direction:
> In conclusion, divorce should not be understood only as a private failure between two people. It is also shaped by changing social values, economic conditions, and family roles. A better understanding of these forces may help individuals build stronger marriages and help society respond more effectively to divorce.

Final advice
For revision, focus on three priorities:
Write a clear argumentative thesis.
Improve organization so each theory supports that thesis.
Edit carefully for grammar, word choice, and sentence clarity.

You have a strong base of ideas. With clearer argument and cleaner language, this essay can become much more effective.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0001.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that coaches accused of “running up the score” are often acting because of rankings, player development, awards, money, and job pressure. You also use vivid football examples, which gives the essay energy and shows strong engagement with the topic. The main revision goal is to make your argument clearer, more formal, and better organized.

Strengths
Clear opinion: You consistently argue that coaches are not really guilty.
Relevant examples: The Florida/South Carolina example helps readers understand the issue.
Good awareness of causes: You explain several motivations: rankings, Heisman voting, fan expectations, bowl games, and job security.

Main issues to revise

A. Thesis and focus
Your thesis is present, but it should be stated more directly in the introduction. Right now, the first paragraph begins dramatically, but the central claim comes later.

Possible revision of thesis:
> Although the NCAA criticizes coaches for running up the score, many college football coaches do not do it simply out of cruelty; instead, they face pressure from rankings, player evaluation, financial incentives, and fan expectations.

This sentence is clearer, more formal, and previews your body paragraphs.

B. Organization
The essay has many ideas, but some paragraphs are long and difficult to follow. Try this structure:

Introduction: explain the controversy + thesis  
Rankings pressure: why coaches keep scoring  
Second-string players / game management  
Heisman and player statistics  
Money, bowls, and job security  
Smaller schools and fan support  
Conclusion

Also, make sure each paragraph begins with a topic sentence.

For example:
> One reason coaches continue scoring is that national rankings often reward large margins of victory.

C. Development of argument
Some points need more explanation or evidence. For example, when you say “No one knows,” that weakens the argument. Instead, explain the logic more carefully.

Also, some claims sound too absolute:
“Florida was clearly the better team”
“Neither is the correct answer”

These are strong opinions, but in argumentative writing, it is better to sound reasoned rather than emotional.

D. Tone and word choice
Some phrases are too informal for an academic essay:
“getting their butts beat bad”
“jerk”
“bench warmer”
“this little pay-cut”

Use more formal alternatives:
“were being heavily defeated”
“unsportsmanlike”
“reserve player”
“financial benefit”

Also, be careful with word choice:
“unsurmountable” should be insurmountable
“to” should sometimes be too
“it’s ranking” should be its ranking

Grammar and sentence issues

A. Run-on sentences and comma splices
You often join two full sentences with only a comma.

Example:
> “It’s fourth down and just two yards to go with a forty-nine to seven lead, will they go for it?”

Better:
> “It is fourth down, and with a forty-nine-to-seven lead and only two yards to go, will they go for it?”

Or split it:
> “It is fourth down with only two yards to go and a forty-nine-to-seven lead. Will they go for it?”

B. Punctuation
You need commas after introductory phrases and between items in a series. Also, avoid random spaces before commas.

Example:
> “what can a coach do , but pass?”
Correct:
> “what can a coach do but pass?”

C. Pronouns and agreement
Example:
> “the NCAA, who discourages”
Because NCAA is an organization, use which or that, not who.

D. Verb forms
Example:
> “it all tables up”
Better:
> “it all adds up”

Example:
> “whether they are secured a position”
Better:
> “whether they can keep their position”

Content suggestions
To strengthen the essay, briefly acknowledge the other side:
> Some critics argue that continuing to score against a much weaker opponent is disrespectful. However, in practice, coaches must balance sportsmanship with the realities of rankings, recruiting, and finances.

This makes your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Sample revision of one sentence
Original:
> “I know I made Steve Spurrier sound like a jerk, but who could blame him.”

Revised:
> “Although Spurrier’s decisions may appear unsportsmanlike, they can also be understood as a response to the ranking system.”

This keeps your idea but makes it more academic.

Final advice
When revising, focus on:
Writing a stronger thesis
Dividing ideas into clearer paragraphs
Using formal vocabulary
Fixing run-on sentences and punctuation
Explaining each reason more logically

You have a strong foundation and a clear argument. With better organization and more formal language, this essay can become much more convincing.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0001.2.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and an interesting personal voice. You recognize that the Confederate flag has different meanings for different people, and you try to build a balanced argument before stating your own view. This is a good foundation for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on argument quality, organization, evidence, and language accuracy.

Main strength: clear stance
Your thesis is understandable: the Confederate battle flag should not fly over the South Carolina State Capitol. You also make a more specific claim: the battle flag represents racism, while the “real” Confederate flag represents heritage. This gives your essay a strong direction.

However, the argument becomes less convincing because some claims are repeated without enough evidence, and the final recommendation changes the focus from removing the battle flag to replacing it with another Confederate flag. This may confuse readers.

Improve the logic of your argument
The biggest issue is consistency. At first, your essay argues that the battle flag should come down because it symbolizes slavery, hatred, and racism. This is clear. But later, you argue that another Confederate flag should fly instead. This weakens your paper because many readers may see all Confederate symbols as connected to slavery and the Confederacy.

Ask yourself:  
Is your real argument “remove the battle flag”?  
Or is it “replace it with a different Confederate flag”?  

These are not the same. The second claim is much harder to defend and needs much more evidence. For a stronger essay, you may want to focus on one simpler argument: the battle flag should not fly over the state capitol because it divides citizens and is historically tied to racism.

Use stronger evidence
Several points need factual support.

Examples:
“In my mind that stands for the South fighting to keep slavery.”  
  This is a personal interpretation; it would be stronger if you added historical evidence.
“The battle flag stands for nothing but racism and hatred.”  
  This is too absolute. In argument writing, extreme language can make readers resist your point.
“This was the year that the civil rights movement started.”  
  This is historically inaccurate. The civil rights movement began before 1964. Be careful with dates and facts.

To improve, use:
historical sources,
dates that are correct,
quotations from historians, politicians, or public debates,
evidence about public reaction to the flag.

Organization and paragraph development
Your essay has a recognizable structure, but some paragraphs repeat the same idea. Try this structure:

Introduction: explain the controversy and present your thesis.  
Opposing view: acknowledge that some see the flag as heritage.  
Historical argument: explain why the battle flag is associated with slavery and racism.  
Context of 1964: explain why its placement at the capitol matters.  
Public impact: discuss how the flag affects citizens and visitors.  
Conclusion: restate your argument and suggest a solution.

Also, make sure each paragraph begins with a topic sentence and develops one main idea.

Tone and word choice
Some wording is too emotional or too general for academic argument.

Examples:
“nothing but racism and hatred”
“all the wrong reasons”
“true southerners”

These phrases sound subjective and may alienate readers. Academic writing is stronger when it is precise and measured.

You could revise like this:
“is widely associated with racism and white resistance to civil rights”
“is linked to the Confederacy’s defense of slavery”
“many Southerners”

Grammar and language corrections
Your meaning is usually clear, but there are several language issues common for non-native writers.

Word choice / form
“It holds many different beliefs” → It has many different meanings
“reflects on my history” → reflects my history or is connected to my history
“succeeded from the union” → seceded from the Union
“the civil rights movement started” → better: the flag was raised during the civil rights era

Articles and capitalization
“the South Carolina State Capitol” is correct, but later be consistent with Capitol / state capitol building
Civil War, Civil Rights Movement, Union, South

Sentence style
Some sentences are repetitive:
“I agree that… I agree because…”  
  Try combining them for smoother style.

Example revision:
> Although some people see the Confederate flag as part of Southern heritage, the Confederate battle flag should not remain over the South Carolina State Capitol because of its historical connection to slavery, racism, and resistance to civil rights.

Final advice for revision
For your next draft:
Choose one clear claim and stay consistent.
Reduce repetition.
Add reliable historical evidence.
Use a more academic tone.
Check factual accuracy, especially dates and historical terms.

You have a strong sense of the issue and a clear opinion. With better evidence and a more consistent argument, this essay could become much more persuasive.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0001.3.txt =====
Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position and strong energy. You argue consistently that premarital sex is harmful, and you try to support this claim with causes and effects, examples, and quotations from sources. This gives your paper a clear argumentative purpose. For revision, the main areas to improve are: thesis focus, logic and evidence, tone, organization, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear stance: The reader always knows your opinion.
Ambitious scope: You discuss social, moral, medical, and family effects.
Use of sources: You include quotations and statistics, which is good for an academic essay.

Main problem: thesis and argument quality
Your thesis is very broad: premarital sex causes disrespect for women, “illegitimate communities,” and sexually transmitted disease, and later you add other effects too. Because the claim is so large, some paragraphs become too general and rely on moral judgment rather than careful proof.

How to revise
Make your thesis more precise and academic. For example:

> Premarital sex can have negative social consequences when it is linked to poor sexual education, unstable relationships, and lack of responsibility.

This version is easier to defend because it does not claim that all premarital sex always destroys society.

Evidence and logic
Some of your points need stronger logical connections.

Examples:
You argue that the sexual revolution caused lower morality and loss of responsibility, but you do not fully prove this.
You connect single-parent families directly to crime and poor education, but the essay does not explain other possible causes such as poverty, lack of social support, or inequality.
Some statistics are used too quickly without analysis. After a quotation, explain how it proves your point.

How to revise
For each body paragraph:
Start with one clear topic sentence.
Present one or two pieces of evidence.
Explain the evidence in your own words.
Show how it supports your thesis.

Also, avoid unsupported absolute claims such as “without any doubt” or “the only way.” In academic writing, careful language is stronger.

Tone and audience
Your tone is often emotional and sometimes sarcastic or insulting. For example, phrases like:
“headed down the toilet”
“ritual parties to get free stuff”
“immoral slaughter of babies”
“piece of ass”

These expressions may weaken your credibility in an academic essay because they sound more like spoken opinion than reasoned analysis.

How to revise
Use a formal, respectful tone, even when discussing sensitive topics. You can describe offensive language without sounding offensive yourself.

For example:
Instead of “headed down the toilet,” write: “contributed to social decline, in the author’s view.”
Instead of “ritual parties to get free stuff,” write: “weddings may be seen by some as losing their original meaning.”

Organization
Your essay has an introduction, body paragraphs, and a conclusion, but some paragraphs are too long and mix several ideas together.

Suggested structure
Introduction: background + focused thesis
Body Paragraph 1: historical/social change
Body Paragraph 2: health risks
Body Paragraph 3: pregnancy and family structure
Body Paragraph 4: gender attitudes/objectification
Conclusion: restate argument + final reflection

Try to keep one main idea per paragraph.

Language issues
There are frequent grammar, word choice, and spelling errors. These make your ideas harder to follow. Below are common patterns:

Word form / wrong word
“lost of respect” → loss of respect
“affects” (noun) → effects
“have sex before marriage” → having sex before marriage
“there bad side affects” → their bad side effects
“planed” → planned
“at lest” → at least

Grammar
“One the most potent solvents” → One of the most potent solvents
“It is without any doubt that…” → There is little doubt that… / I argue that…
“have make” → has made
“harder calculate” → harder to calculate

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long and become confusing. Break long sentences into two shorter ones.

For example:
> “Premarital sex in itself, physically, does little or no harm in its action. The real problems come soon after and have long ranging affects.”

Could become:
> “Premarital sex may not always cause immediate physical harm. However, I argue that its long-term social and personal consequences can be serious.”

Source use
Be careful with citation accuracy and integration. Introduce sources clearly and explain them. Also check formatting such as:
quotation punctuation
page numbers
consistency of author names and titles

Final advice
Your essay shows strong engagement with the topic, but to make it persuasive in an academic setting, revise toward:
a narrower thesis
more balanced reasoning
formal tone
clear paragraph structure
careful grammar and wording

If you revise in this way, your argument will sound more credible and mature.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0001.4.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that women should be able to serve in the military, but not in combat roles. The paper also shows strong personal engagement with the topic. This is a good starting point for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on (1) strengthening your argument with evidence, (2) improving organization, and (3) editing grammar and wording for clarity and academic tone.

Argument and use of evidence
Your main claim is understandable, but much of the essay depends on personal belief rather than proof. In academic argument, readers expect reasons supported by facts, examples, or research.

For example, you say:
standards are “sometimes lowered”
women are physically weaker
women’s “emotional makeup” is different
allowing women into combat could harm national defense

These are major claims, but you do not provide evidence. To make your essay more convincing, include:
military policy examples
research on physical requirements in combat roles
statistics about performance or injury rates
expert opinions from military leaders or scholars

Also, some statements are very broad, such as “women were created different for a reason.” This may reflect your belief, but in an academic essay it is less persuasive unless you explain how this connects logically to military policy.

Revision advice
Try to make each body paragraph follow this pattern:
Topic sentence stating one reason
Evidence (fact, study, example)
Explanation of how the evidence supports your claim
Link back to your thesis

Organization and focus
Your essay has a thesis in the introduction, but the body paragraphs are sometimes repetitive. You discuss physical differences several times, and emotional differences several times, without always adding new points.

A clearer structure might be:

Introduction: present topic and thesis
Body Paragraph 1: combat requires specific physical standards
Body Paragraph 2: changing standards may affect military effectiveness
Body Paragraph 3: women can contribute strongly in non-combat roles
Counterargument Paragraph: acknowledge the argument for equal opportunity, then respond
Conclusion: restate your main point

Including a counterargument would improve the essay. For example, supporters of women in combat might argue that standards should be the same for everyone and that those who meet them, regardless of sex, should be allowed to serve. Responding to that would make your argument stronger and fairer.

Tone and word choice
Some language in the essay may sound too absolute or emotionally charged for academic writing. For instance:
“the weaker sex”
“distorts reality”
“feminists... obsessed”
“destroying the uniqueness”

These phrases may make readers feel that the essay is biased. In academic writing, try to sound measured and precise. For example:
instead of “the weaker sex,” say “women may, on average, have less upper-body strength than men”
instead of “feminists are obsessed,” say “some advocates of gender equality may place too much emphasis on identical roles”

This change will make your argument sound more thoughtful and credible.

Grammar and sentence issues
There are several grammar and editing problems that reduce clarity.

a) Repetition
One sentence in paragraph 2 is accidentally repeated:
> “when a female enters the military…”

Delete the repeated version.

b) Sentence boundaries and punctuation
You have some run-on or awkward sentences. For example:
> “The intentions seem honorable on the surface, however when reality comes into play, reality is often altered…”

Better:
“The intentions seem honorable on the surface; however, in practice, standards may be altered in the name of equality.”

c) Word form and agreement
Examples:
“as a males” → “as a male’s” or better “those of men”
“a women” → “a woman”
“women is” → “women are”
“support this equality is” → “support this equality are”

d) Clarity and conciseness
Some sentences are too long and difficult to follow. Break them into shorter sentences. For example:
> “The physical body of a female is so different from the male body…”

This could become:
“The female body differs from the male body in important ways. Because of these differences, some combat tasks may be more difficult for women on average.”

Thesis revision
Your thesis could be more specific. For example:

“Although women can serve the military effectively in many roles, they should not be assigned to combat positions because these roles require physical standards that should not be altered and because combat readiness must remain the military’s top priority.”

This gives your essay a clearer argumentative direction.

Final advice
For your next draft:
Remove repeated and unclear sentences.
Use a clearer paragraph structure.
Replace emotional language with precise academic wording.
Add evidence for each major claim.
Consider and respond to the opposing view.

You have a strong sense of your position. With better support and a more academic style, your essay can become much more persuasive.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0002.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay addresses an important topic and shows clear engagement with psychological, sociological, and economic effects of gender stereotypes. You also try to use outside sources, which is a strong academic habit. The main area for revision is clarity and consistency of argument: your introduction says American culture should maintain gender identities to a certain extent, but your conclusion argues that people should be free from traditional roles. This contradiction weakens the essay.

Main strength
You show good critical thinking by discussing both advantages and disadvantages of gender-role stereotypes.
You include examples about men, women, family life, education, and work.
Your conclusion supports individual freedom, which is a clear and meaningful position.

Most important revision: clarify your thesis
Right now, your position is mixed. Ask yourself:  
Do you believe traditional gender roles should be preserved, or do you believe they should be rejected?

Your body paragraphs mostly argue that rigid stereotypes are harmful. So a clearer thesis might be:

> Although traditional gender roles may provide some social structure, American culture should not maintain rigid gender stereotypes because they harm men and women psychologically, socially, and economically.

This would match your evidence much better.

Organization
Your essay has ideas, but the structure can be improved.

Current problem
Some paragraphs begin with one point, then move to another without clear transitions. Also, some references are incomplete: “Poilit feels that ” and “psychiatrist, as they think .”

Suggested structure
Introduction: define gender stereotypes and present a clear thesis.
Paragraph 1: explain traditional male and female stereotypes.
Paragraph 2: effects on men.
Paragraph 3: effects on women.
Paragraph 4: economic/family consequences.
Conclusion: restate argument and explain why change is necessary.

Use topic sentences. For example:
“Rigid male stereotypes harm men by discouraging emotional expression and creating unhealthy pressure to succeed financially.”
“Traditional female roles can restrict women’s education, identity, and independence.”

Development of ideas
Several claims are interesting but need more explanation or evidence.

Examples:
“Having the typical image is advantageous...”  
Explain how role models help children and then why this benefit is limited.
“These research evaluations have negative psychological and sociological effects...”  
Be specific: What effects? Depression? anxiety? emotional isolation?
“Poilit feels that...”  
This idea is unfinished, so it must be completed or removed.

Try to explain each quotation or source in your own words. Do not just mention a source; show how it supports your argument.

Use of sources
You mention Warren Farrell, William Masters, Betty Friedan, and Katha Pollitt, which is good, but the citations are incomplete and sometimes unclear.

Problems:
Some quotations or ideas are not fully introduced.
Some source information is missing.
The placeholders “<R>” and “” must be removed.
Be careful about accuracy in names and titles.

Advice:
Use a simple pattern:
Introduce source → summarize or quote → explain significance

Example:
> Betty Friedan argues that women who are defined only as wives and mothers may lose a sense of personal identity. This supports the view that restrictive gender roles can damage women’s psychological well-being.

Language issues to revise
Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are many grammar, spelling, and word-choice errors. As a Chinese-speaking writer, pay special attention to articles, sentence boundaries, and word forms.

Common issues in your essay
Word choice: “genre identities” should be gender identities
Spelling: “identitites,” “femine,” “famimlies,” “enstranged,” “abnomal,” “defination,” “shoud”
Informal style: “alot” → a lot; “afterall” → after all
Sentence fragments:  
  “On the other hand, a woman would be femine. Meaning gentle...”  
  Better: “On the other hand, a woman is stereotyped as feminine, meaning gentle, emotional, sensitive, and loving.”
Run-on sentences: several sentences are too long and need punctuation or division.
Possessives/plurals: “womans’ role” → woman’s role or women’s roles, depending on meaning.
Verb forms: “results” should often be result when the subject is plural.

Example of sentence revision
Original:
> But this unfortunately puts alot of pressure on men, as they aspire to get through college, get the best jobs with good salaries, they may then become so preoccupied with their job and success, that they neglect their families...

Revised:
> However, this stereotype places a great deal of pressure on men. As they strive to complete college and obtain high-paying jobs, they may become so preoccupied with success that they neglect their families.

Final advice
For your next draft:
Rewrite the thesis so it clearly matches your conclusion.
Complete or remove unfinished source references.
Use one main idea per paragraph.

You have a strong topic and a clear interest in the issue. With better organization and language control, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and strong feelings about the topic, which gives your essay energy. Your main claim is understandable: college residence hall rules can prevent students from developing independence and should be fairer and more flexible. You also use specific examples, such as visitation rules and quiet hours at USC, which helps support your argument.

Main strengths
Clear argument: You consistently argue that residence hall policies are too controlling.
Relevant examples: The discussion of visitation hours, roommate contracts, and quiet hours makes the essay concrete.
Strong voice: Your essay sounds engaged and confident, which is good for argumentative writing.

Most important revision goal: improve organization
Right now, the essay feels repetitive because similar ideas appear in several places. Try to make the structure more controlled.

A stronger structure could be:

Introduction
   - Background: college should help students mature.
   - Thesis: residence hall rules, especially visitation and quiet-hour policies, limit student independence and should be revised to be fairer and more student-centered.

Body paragraph 1: Visitation policies
   - Explain the rule.
   - Show how it limits independence.
   - Address counterargument: safety and roommate comfort.

Body paragraph 2: Gender fairness
   - Discuss how the policy may treat different students unequally.
   - Be careful with wording and logic here.

Body paragraph 3: Quiet hours
   - Explain why the current rule is too strict.
   - Suggest a more reasonable weekend policy.

Conclusion
   - Restate the argument.
   - Emphasize the need for balance between safety and independence.

Strengthen your thesis and argument
Your introduction presents the topic, but the thesis is not sharp enough. Make it more direct.

For example:
“Although residence halls are meant to provide safety, overly strict rules on visitation and quiet hours limit students’ ability to develop responsibility and should be revised to reflect students’ needs.”

Also, some claims need more balanced reasoning. For example, when you say rules “violate rights,” that sounds too absolute unless you explain exactly what rights are being limited. Academic argument is stronger when it avoids exaggeration.

Use a more academic tone
Your voice is lively, but some expressions are too informal for an academic essay. Examples:
“mom and dad from downstairs”
“That’s ridiculous!”
“when the clock strikes two”

These phrases are expressive, but in academic writing, use more neutral language:
“residence hall staff”
“This policy seems unreasonable”
“after 2:00 a.m.”

Also, avoid speaking directly to the reader too often:
“I know you think…”
“Think about it…”

Instead, explain your idea directly.

Develop counterarguments more fairly
You do mention the other side, such as safety and roommate concerns, which is good. But you should respond more carefully. For instance, instead of saying disturbances are rarely a concern, explain that:
safety matters,
roommates need privacy,
but universities could allow more flexibility through roommate agreements or different weekend rules.

That makes your essay more persuasive.

Be careful with sensitive claims
Your discussion of rape, gender, and sexual orientation raises an interesting fairness issue, but this section needs clearer and more careful language. Some sentences sound too general or may seem unsupported. Try to focus on the policy issue rather than making broad claims about groups of people.

For example, instead of saying one group has an “advantage,” you could say:
“If visitation policies are based only on the sex of guests, they may not be applied consistently across different relationships.”

This is more precise and academic.

Grammar and language issues to revise
There are many sentence-level errors, especially with:
subject-verb agreement
word choice
plural/singular nouns
pronouns
informal phrasing

Examples:
“the rules and regulations it has” → “the rules and regulations colleges have”
“guest of the opposite sex” → “guests of the opposite sex”
“state” → “states”
“there opinions” → “their opinions”
“Atleast” → “At least”
“Another rule in resident halls are” → “Another rule in residence halls is”
“you are suppose to be quiet” → “you are supposed to be quiet”
“What maybe loud to you” → “What may be loud to you”

Also, some sentences are run-on or awkwardly connected. Read each sentence and check whether it is complete and clear.

Word choice and phrasing
A few vocabulary choices are incorrect:
“maturing station” is unusual; better: “a place for personal growth”
“parenting rolls” should be “parenting roles”
“resident halls” is usually “residence halls”

Final advice
Your essay has a strong central idea and good real-life examples. For revision, focus on:
writing a clearer thesis,
organizing each paragraph around one main point,
using a more academic tone,
revising grammar and word choice carefully.

If you do those things, your argument will become much more convincing and polished.

Feedback Report

Main strengths

Good use of examples: The seasons 1992–93, 1993–94, and 1994 provide concrete evidence.
Strong sense of audience: You understand that readers may care about fairness, money, and fan interest.

Biggest revision priority: organization

Suggested structure

   - Briefly explain the problem.
   - End with a thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: Polls and bowl games do not always produce a clear champion
   - Use 1–2 seasons as examples.
Body Paragraph 2: A playoff system would be fairer
   - Explain why head-to-head results and unbeaten records deserve resolution on the field.
Body Paragraph 3: A playoff system would increase revenue and fan interest
Body Paragraph 4: Answer objections
   - season too long
   - tradition of bowl games
Conclusion
   - Restate why a playoff is the best solution.

Important advice

Make your analysis stronger
Often you describe what happened, but you do not always explain enough why it matters.

For example, after discussing Alabama, Florida State, and Texas A&M, add a sentence like:
“This controversy shows that polls reward reputation and scheduling circumstances rather than settling the issue through direct competition.”

This kind of sentence is analysis. Try to include it after each example.

Improve the thesis
Your thesis is already clear, but it can be more direct and formal.

Original idea
“A playoff system will generate more revenue, create more excitement… and determine a true champion…”

Possible revision
“College football should adopt a playoff system because it would determine the national champion more fairly, increase fan interest, and generate additional revenue while still preserving bowl traditions.”

This version is shorter and more academic.

Sentence-level issues

Examples

“their was some controversy” → “there was some controversy”
“have prove” → “had proved”
“just happen” → “just happened”
“unfair and unjustly” → “unfair and unjust”
“which ever way” → “whichever way”

Also watch for:
subject-verb agreement
article use (“the national championship,” “a playoff system”)
prepositions (“go to the Rose Bowl,” not “go the Rose Bowl”)
word form (“controversial,” “recognition,” “fairness”)

Tone and style
Your voice is passionate, but some phrases are too informal for an academic essay.

Examples:
“absolutely ridiculous” → “highly problematic”
“whipped” / “destroyed” → use more neutral verbs such as “defeated”
“dream match-ups” is acceptable, but use it carefully

Try to sound confident without sounding emotional.

introduced clearly
punctuated correctly
explained afterward

fairness
fan excitement
financial benefits

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: to persuade readers to follow safety rules on go-carts. The strongest part of your essay is that you use personal experience as a worker at an amusement park. This gives your argument credibility because you have seen accidents yourself. Your message is also practical and easy for readers to understand. To revise, focus on making the essay more academic, organized, and grammatically accurate.

Main strengths
Strong topic and clear position: You clearly argue that riders must follow the rules.
Good use of real experience: Examples such as seeing people get hurt and being hit by a go-cart make the essay convincing.
Specific details: You explain important rules like wearing seatbelts, using brakes, avoiding bumping, and waiting to exit the car.

Organization and argument
Right now, the essay reads more like a spoken warning or workplace explanation than a formal argumentative essay. To strengthen it, give it a clearer structure:

Suggested structure
Introduction
   - Introduce the issue.
   - State why go-cart safety rules matter.
   - End with a clear thesis.

Body paragraph 1: Seatbelts and listening to instructions
Body paragraph 2: Safe driving rules
Body paragraph 3: Protecting workers and other riders
Conclusion
   - Restate the main argument.
   - Emphasize safety and shared responsibility.

Thesis revision
Your current introduction is sincere, but the first sentence is too conversational:  
> “I don't know how to express how important it is...”

A stronger thesis would be:
“All riders at go-cart tracks should follow safety rules because these rules prevent injuries, protect workers and passengers, and reduce damage to the cars.”

This thesis is more direct and academic.

Style and tone
Your tone is passionate, but sometimes too informal for an academic essay. Avoid expressions such as:
“Believe me”
“I should know”
“you got to remember”
“real bad”

Use a more formal tone instead:
“It is important to note that…”
“My experience as an employee shows that…”
“Riders should remember that…”

Also, your essay often speaks directly to the reader using “you.” This is acceptable in some argumentative writing, but too much can sound informal. Try mixing in nouns like riders, customers, or visitors.

Paragraph development
Some body paragraphs are strong, but several ideas are repeated. For example, the essay says many times that people can get hurt. Instead of repeating this, explain how each rule prevents a different kind of danger.

For example:
Seatbelts prevent riders from being thrown forward.
Brakes prevent rear-end collisions in the pit.
No bumping protects children and avoids mechanical damage.
Staying seated protects riders when cars are still moving.

This will make your reasoning more precise.

Grammar and sentence issues
There are many grammar errors, especially with verb forms, word choice, punctuation, and spelling. Below are some important examples:

“I have seen a good bit of people get hurt”  
  Better: “I have seen many people get hurt”

“The seatbelts shouldn't be taking off”  
  Better: “The seatbelts should not be taken off”

“at anytime”  
  Better: “at any time”

“when people coming in the pit”  
  Better: “when people come into the pit”

“annoucements”  
  Correct spelling: “announcements”

“their” / “there” confusion  
  Example: “The bumpers are not their” → “The bumpers are not there”

“When ever”  
  Correct spelling: “Whenever”

“Having all these rules are”  
  Better: “Having all these rules is” or “These rules are”

You also have several run-on sentences. For example:
> “I was even lucky that when I got hit, I wasn't seriously hurt, I just bruised my arm real bad.”

This should be divided:
“I was lucky that I was not seriously hurt when I got hit. I only bruised my arm badly.”

Sentence variety and clarity
Many sentences begin with “When…” or “I have seen…”. Try varying sentence structure. Also, some long sentences are difficult to follow. Shorter, clearer sentences will improve your argument.

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Write a clearer, more formal thesis.
Organize the essay by categories of rules.
Remove repetition.
Use a more academic tone.
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation errors.
Break long sentences into shorter ones.

Model revision of one sentence
Original:  
“There are many reasons why you should follow the rules, so you don't get hurt or hurt anybody else and you don't damage the cars.”

Revised:  
“Riders should follow the rules to avoid injuries, protect other passengers, and prevent damage to the go-carts.”

Overall, you have a strong idea and useful real-life evidence. With clearer organization and more formal language, this could become a much more effective argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that Susan Smith should not receive the death penalty. You also show strong engagement with the topic and try to support your opinion with background information, psychological explanation, and religious belief. These are good starting points for an argumentative essay. To revise successfully, focus on thesis, organization, evidence, tone, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear opinion: Your main claim is understandable—you believe the jury made the right decision.
Relevant background: You include Susan Smith’s childhood trauma and mental health history to explain your reasoning.
Emotional engagement: The essay shows conviction and seriousness, which can be persuasive if controlled carefully.

Main issues to revise

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your introduction ends with: “I would like to express my opinion on the justness of the sentence…” This is too general. State your exact argument clearly.

Possible revision:
> Although Susan Smith committed a terrible crime, the jury was justified in sentencing her to life imprisonment rather than death because of her history of trauma, mental illness, and the belief that lifelong punishment is more meaningful than execution.

This gives the reader a roadmap for the essay.

B. Improve organization
Right now, some ideas repeat and the essay shifts suddenly from legal/psychological reasoning to religious reasoning. Try using a clearer structure:

Introduction + thesis
Background and mitigating factors  
   - childhood trauma  
   - depression  
   - inability to make rational choices
Why life imprisonment is a better punishment than death
Religious/moral perspective (if allowed by the assignment)
Conclusion

Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that directly supports your thesis.

C. Use evidence more carefully
Some claims are presented as facts without support, and one is inaccurate:
“These past behavior patterns are a few reasons why the Supreme Court did not impose the death penalty.”  
  This is incorrect. The jury, not the U.S. Supreme Court, decided the sentence in this case.
“She obviously cared deeply…” and “It is obvious to me…” are subjective. In argument writing, use more neutral phrasing:
  - “The evidence suggests…”
  - “Her actions may have been influenced by…”

Also, your essay includes very vivid imagined details about what Susan Smith thinks and hears in prison. These are emotionally strong, but they are speculative, not evidence. Reduce this kind of unsupported description.

D. Be careful with tone
Some parts sound more like a sermon or emotional speech than an academic argument. For example:
“Her greatest fear should be God’s wrath.”
“She is faced with making the choice of seeing her sons again in heaven…”

If this is for an academic class, you should keep a more formal and analytical tone. You may include religion as part of your moral reasoning, but do not let it replace argument and evidence. Also remember that not all readers share the same beliefs.

E. Address the opposing view
A strong argumentative essay usually mentions the other side. For example, you could briefly acknowledge why some people wanted the death penalty:
> Some people argue that murdering two children deserves the harshest possible punishment. However, life imprisonment without release may be a more appropriate sentence because…

This will make your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Language and grammar
Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are grammar and word-choice problems. Common areas to revise:

Spelling
  - recieve → receive
  - prision → prison

Sentence boundaries / comma splices
  - “The news media and many individuals have stated facts as well as opinions, I would like to express my opinion…”
  - Better: “The news media and many individuals have stated facts as well as opinions. I would like to express my own view…”

Word choice
  - “not endowed with logical mental coherence” sounds unnatural.
  - Better: “did not show sound judgment” or “was not mentally stable”.

Unnecessary repetition
  - “Her boyfriend dumped her…” is repeated in consecutive sentences.
  - Combine these ideas into one sentence.

Article/preposition problems
  - “as a child” → better “in childhood”
  - “At the age of fifteen years old” → “At the age of fifteen”

Example of a stronger style
Original:
> Death would have been quick, easy, and more economical.

Revised:
> Execution may appear to be a decisive punishment, but life imprisonment can be a harsher and more lasting consequence.

This sounds more formal and less simplistic.

Final advice
When revising, aim to:
write a clear thesis
organize each paragraph around one main reason
use accurate facts
reduce overly emotional or speculative language
maintain a more academic tone
proofread carefully for grammar and spelling

You have a strong central opinion and plenty of material. With better structure and a more formal argumentative style, this essay can become much more convincing.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0003.3.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that lowering the drinking age from 21 to 18 would reduce crime, encourage responsibility, and improve public health. The structure is also easy to follow because your introduction presents three main reasons, and the body paragraphs generally match those reasons. This is a strong starting point for an argumentative essay.

Main strengths
Clear thesis: Your main argument is easy to understand.
Organized structure: The essay follows a logical pattern: introduction, body paragraphs, conclusion.
Strong sense of purpose: You consistently support the same claim rather than changing topics.

Main problems to revise

A. Your argument needs stronger logic
Some claims are too absolute or not fully explained. For example:
“Lowering the drinking age would decrease the amount of crime among youths.”
“The common problem of binge drinking would be cured...”
“Once these rights are given back, young adults will act responsibly.”

These statements sound certain, but readers will ask: How do you know? What evidence proves this? In academic argument, avoid promising that one policy will “cure” a problem. Instead, use cautious language such as:
“may reduce”
“could help decrease”
“is likely to improve”

Also, some reasoning is weak. For example, saying fewer people would need reform programs if drinking became legal does not automatically follow. Legal drinking does not necessarily reduce harmful drinking.

B. Evidence and sources are not used effectively
You mention sources, but many citations are incomplete, unclear, or not academic. Examples:
email addresses
website links in the sentence
“Netscape. Young Drinkers Homepage”
“US Department of Justice 3-62” without full context

For revision:
Use credible academic or government sources.
Introduce each source clearly: *According to a report from the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services...*
Explain how the evidence supports your point.
Avoid listing a source after a claim without analysis.

C. Overgeneralization
Several sentences make very broad claims:
“Alcoholism is not a problem in other countries...”
“In Canada, alcohol is served with meals, regardless of a person's age.”
“Many Americans see drinking as a pastime, and as the center of social life.”

These are too sweeping and may be factually inaccurate. In argumentative writing, generalizations weaken credibility. Be more precise:
Which countries?
What evidence supports the comparison?
Are there exceptions?

D. Repetition
You repeat the same ideas many times:
drinking is “forbidden fruit”
college students binge drink because alcohol is restricted
giving rights back will make youth responsible

Try combining repeated points and developing each body paragraph more deeply instead.

Language and grammar issues

A. Word choice
Use more natural academic phrasing:
“high school and college environment” → “high school and college environments”
“decrease the amount of crime” → “reduce the number of crimes”
“improve the health of the nation” → “improve public health”
“giving back drinking rights” → “lowering the legal drinking age”

B. Grammar forms
Common errors include possessives, plurals, and verb forms:
“eighteen-year old's” → “eighteen-year-olds”
“Responsible drinking would be encourage” → “Responsible drinking would be encouraged”
“would effect the United States” → “would affect the United States”
“amoung” → “among”

Also, watch capitalization:
“on most college campuses” should begin with a capital O.

C. Sentence style
Many sentences are repetitive because they begin with “Lowering the drinking age would...” This makes the essay sound mechanical. Vary your sentence openings. For example:
“Another possible benefit is a reduction in underage drinking violations.”
“In college settings, legal access might reduce the appeal of alcohol as a forbidden activity.”

Paragraph-level advice
Each body paragraph should do this:
Topic sentence
Evidence
Explanation of evidence
Link back to thesis

Right now, some paragraphs contain many claims but not enough explanation. Focus on fewer points with deeper analysis.

Example of how to revise your thesis
Current idea:
> Changing the drinking age from twenty-one to eighteen would lower the amount of crimes among young adults, encourage a more responsible approach to alcohol in the United States and improve the health of the nation.

Possible revision:
> Lowering the legal drinking age from twenty-one to eighteen could reduce some forms of underage crime, encourage more open and responsible attitudes toward alcohol, and help address unhealthy binge-drinking culture among young adults.

This version sounds more academic because it is strong but not overstated.

Final advice
For your next draft, focus on:
Using stronger and more credible evidence
Explaining your reasoning more carefully
Avoiding overstatements
Correcting grammar and word choice
Reducing repetition

You have a clear argument and workable structure. With better evidence, more careful logic, and cleaner language, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose: you argue that swimming should be a required part of physical education because it can prevent drowning and teach water safety. This is a strong, practical topic, and your essay shows concern for public safety, which gives it real urgency. The opening story is vivid and emotionally engaging, so you already have a good instinct for catching the reader’s attention.

Main strengths
Clear position: Your thesis is understandable: schools should require swimming instruction.
Strong introduction strategy: The drowning scenario makes the issue feel real and important.
Relevant reasons: You explain several benefits: saving lives, teaching safety, using existing facilities, and creating jobs.
Good persuasive intention: You try to appeal to both emotion and logic.

Main areas to improve

A. Strengthen organization
Right now, some ideas run together, and paragraph focus is not always clear. Try this structure:

Introduction: hook + background + thesis  
Body paragraph 1: why swimming is necessary (drowning prevention, life skill)  
Body paragraph 2: why schools are the best place to teach it  
Body paragraph 3: how schools could implement it (pools, instructors, local partnerships)  
Conclusion: restate argument and emphasize importance

Also, make sure each paragraph begins with a topic sentence.

Example:
“Swimming should be required in school because it is an essential life-saving skill.”
“Schools are in a strong position to provide swimming education to all students.”
“Even schools without pools could still make a swimming program possible.”

B. Improve the thesis
Your thesis appears in the middle of the essay, but it should be more direct and polished.

Possible revision:
“Swimming should be included as a required part of physical education because it teaches a life-saving skill, improves water safety awareness, and could reduce preventable drowning deaths.”

C. Use a more formal academic tone
Some parts sound conversational, such as:
“How often have you heard of dodgeball saving someone’s life?”
“You get home from work, turn on the News…”

These are effective in speech, but in an academic essay, a slightly more formal tone is better.

For example:
“Unlike many traditional physical education activities, swimming is a skill that may save lives.”
“News reports frequently describe preventable drowning accidents during the summer.”

D. Develop evidence more fully
Your argument would be stronger with facts, statistics, or examples from reliable sources. For instance:
drowning rates among children
number of people who cannot swim
benefits of early swim instruction
recommendations from health or safety organizations

Without evidence, some claims sound like personal opinion. In academic argument, readers expect support.

Language and grammar issues to revise

A. Capitalization and sentence boundaries
There are several run-on sentences and capitalization errors.

Examples:
“your only problem now...” → Your
“one class that is common...” → One
“these organizations...” → These

Also, avoid comma splices:
“This is not uncommon, most of your friends do not know how to swim.”
Better:
“This is not uncommon. Most of your friends do not know how to swim.”

B. Spelling
Please carefully proofread. Some repeated spelling errors include:
panicing → panicking
drownding → drowning
cericulum → curriculum
evryone → everyone
instuctor → instructor
wuold → would
instuctors → instructors
thaech → teach
employent → employment
simlpe → simple
remidied → remedied

C. Word choice and phrasing
Some phrases are awkward or unclear:
“floods your head” → better: “comes to mind”
“the least little bit of air” → “even a small breath of air”
“there is not enough use” → “there is not enough demand”

D. Pronoun consistency
You often use “you” in the introduction, then shift to “I think” and more general statements. In formal essays, it is usually better to avoid directly addressing the reader too much. You can use:
students
young people
many children
the public

Content suggestions for revision
You should also consider possible counterarguments. For example:
Some schools do not have pools.
Swimming programs may be expensive.

Then respond to these points:
schools can partner with community pools
programs can be short-term
exceptions or accommodations can be provided

Including a counterargument will make your essay more balanced and persuasive.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong foundation and an important message. The biggest improvements will come from:
organizing your ideas more clearly,
making the tone more academic,
correcting grammar and spelling,
adding evidence,
acknowledging counterarguments.

You already have a persuasive central idea. With revision, this could become a much stronger academic argument.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0004.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: society should reject rigid gender identities because they are harmful and outdated. The paper also shows good argumentative structure: introduction, several body paragraphs with examples, and a conclusion. You use sources and real-world situations to support your claim, which is a strong academic habit.

What is working well

Clear thesis
   Your main argument is easy to identify: distinct gender identities should be abolished because traits should not belong only to one gender.

Logical organization
   Each body paragraph focuses on one reason:
   - changing ideas about masculinity/femininity
   - harm to women
   - harm to men
   - single-parent families as evidence  
   This gives your essay a clear progression.

Consistent focus
   Most paragraphs connect back to the central claim. This shows good control over the argument.

Main areas to revise

Clarify and refine your key term
Your essay depends on the phrase “distinct gender identities,” but this phrase is unusual and somewhat unclear in English. Readers may better understand terms such as:
rigid gender roles
traditional gender stereotypes
fixed ideas of masculinity and femininity

These alternatives are more natural and precise. If you keep your phrase, define it very clearly in the introduction.

Make the argument more nuanced
Sometimes your essay sounds too absolute. For example, “abolish distinct gender identities” may seem unrealistic or too broad. A stronger academic claim would be something like:

society should challenge rigid gender stereotypes
people should be free to express traits traditionally associated with either gender
traditional gender expectations should not limit personal identity or social roles

This sounds more persuasive because it is more precise.

Strengthen evidence and explain it
You mention important sources (Friedan, Farrell, Masters, Johnson, Kolodny), but some quotations are missing (, `<R>`), and some references are too vague. For revision:
insert the missing quotations or paraphrases
explain exactly how each source supports your point
connect the source more directly to your thesis

For example, after mentioning a source, add 1–2 sentences of analysis: *Why does this example prove that gender roles are harmful?*

Avoid overgeneralization
Some sentences assign traits too absolutely to men or women, even while arguing against stereotypes. For example:
“these are strictly male gender identity characteristics”
“these are distinct female gender identity characteristics”

These claims weaken your argument because they repeat the same rigid thinking you are criticizing. Instead, write:
“traits traditionally associated with men”
“qualities often stereotypically linked to women”

Improve grammar and word choice
There are several language issues that affect clarity. Some important ones:

ever changing → ever-changing
looked upon to be → regarded as
more healthy → healthier
it would be ridiculous → capitalize It
on order to succeed → in order to succeed
dependant → independent (You probably mean *independent*, not *dependant*.)
emanate these qualities / radiate these qualities → more natural: display or develop these qualities

Also watch article use and sentence flow. Example:
“The fact that a man possesses feminine gender qualities does not mean that he is less masculine.”
This is grammatical, but repetitive. You can simplify:
“A man who shows traits considered feminine is not therefore less masculine.”

Vary sentence structure
Many sentences begin with “Likewise,” “Also,” or “Moreover.” Try more variety. This will make the essay sound more mature and natural.

Paragraph-level suggestions

Introduction: shorten repetition and define the key term more smoothly.
Paragraph 2: explain the research more clearly; the sentence with Masters, Johnson, and Kolodny is incomplete.
Paragraph 3: good focus on women’s dissatisfaction, but explain Friedan’s idea more specifically.
Paragraph 4: strong point about pressure on men; revise grammar and avoid saying all men “must” be one way.
Paragraph 5: useful real-life example, but some wording is awkward and overly absolute.
Conclusion: effective summary, but avoid repeating earlier sentences almost word-for-word.

Possible revised thesis
You might try something like this:

In modern society, rigid gender stereotypes should be challenged because they limit personal fulfillment, create psychological pressure for both women and men, and do not reflect the realities of family and working life.

This is clear, academic, and easier to support.

Final advice
Your essay has a solid foundation and a meaningful argument. The most important revisions are:
use more natural terminology,
complete and explain your evidence,
reduce absolute statements,
edit grammar and word choice carefully.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a strong personal voice. You argue passionately that media beauty standards harm women’s health and self-image, and your conclusion returns well to your main message: true beauty should not be defined by society. This is a good foundation for an argumentative essay.

What is working well

Clear argument
   Your main claim is easy to understand: media and society create unrealistic beauty standards, and these standards can lead to harmful consequences.

Emotional engagement
   The opening with famous models catches attention, and your use of rhetorical questions helps show your concern and involvement.

Range of examples
   You discuss models, eating disorders, plastic surgery, beauty products, and discrimination against larger women. This gives your essay breadth.

Main areas to improve

Strengthen the thesis and organization
Right now, your ideas are strong, but the essay sometimes feels repetitive or loosely organized. Try giving the introduction a more precise thesis. For example:

> The media promotes unrealistic beauty standards for women, and these standards damage women’s mental health, physical health, and social status.

Then organize body paragraphs by topic:
Paragraph 1: unrealistic standards in media
Paragraph 2: health consequences (anorexia, bulimia, surgery)
Paragraph 3: economic and social discrimination against larger women
Paragraph 4: why inner beauty/self-respect matters

This structure will make your argument more logical.

Use evidence more carefully
You include many statistics, which is good, but in academic writing you should cite sources and be careful not to make unsupported claims. Some statements seem too absolute or may be inaccurate, such as:
“there is no cure” for anorexia
implants are “a leading cause of Cancer”
whitening methods “break down skin, and bone”

If you cannot support such claims with reliable sources, revise them to more cautious wording:
> Eating disorders can be very difficult to treat.
> Some cosmetic procedures involve serious health risks.

This will make your essay sound more credible and academic.

Improve tone for academic writing
Your essay is persuasive, but sometimes the tone becomes too conversational:
“yeah right”
“I mean”
“doing there job of being beautiful”

In an academic essay, use a more formal style. For example:
> Advertisements often create the illusion that a product alone can produce an ideal appearance.

Also, first person (“I believe,” “I admit”) can be effective, but use it sparingly so the essay does not rely too much on personal opinion.

Work on grammar and word choice
There are frequent language errors, especially with:
plural/singular nouns
verb agreement
word forms
spelling
pronouns

Examples:
“The 1990's base these standards” → The 1990s established these standards
“society place” → society places
“there job” → their job
“perfect women” → perfect woman / ideal woman
“average women is” → average woman is
“adolecense” → adolescence
“Naomi Cambell” → Naomi Campbell
“enwnel” → enamel

Be especially careful with:
woman / women
its / it’s
their / there / they’re
affect / effect

Improve sentence structure and punctuation
Many sentences are long and joined by commas. This creates comma splices and makes the writing harder to follow.

For example:
> “Of course this problem is not the model's fault, they are doing there job of being beautiful.”

Better:
> Of course, this problem is not the models’ fault. They are simply doing their job.

Try to separate long ideas into shorter, clearer sentences.

Example of possible revision
Original:
> “Because of all of the pressures to be beautiful that are placed on women, we are only making ourselves unhealthy, and in the long run fatally ill.”

Revised:
> Because women are pressured to meet unrealistic beauty standards, many damage their physical and mental health in the process.

This version is shorter, clearer, and more formal.

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis
Reorganize paragraphs by main topic
Remove or soften unsupported claims
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation
Use a more formal academic tone

You already have passion and a meaningful argument. With clearer structure and more precise language, this essay can become much more powerful and convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and shows strong engagement with the debate about talk shows. You present both sides of the argument, which is an important feature of argumentative writing. You also use vivid language and specific examples to keep the reader interested. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Clarify your main argument

What to do:

  - Are talk shows mostly beneficial despite some risks?  
  - Are they harmful and should be restricted?  
  - Or are they acceptable only when guests understand the consequences?
State this position clearly at the end of the introduction.

Example of a clearer thesis:
“Although many daytime talk shows are criticized for promoting conflict, they should not be dismissed entirely because they can provide entertainment, public discussion, and emotional support; however, both producers and guests must act responsibly.”

Improve organization
Your essay has interesting ideas, but some paragraphs are very long and contain several different points. This makes the argument harder to follow.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: background + clear thesis  
Body Paragraph 1: benefits of talk shows  
Body Paragraph 2: harmful features of sensational talk shows  
Body Paragraph 3: example of serious consequences  
Conclusion: restate argument and explain who is responsible

Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence and focus on one main idea.

Develop argument more logically
Some claims are strong, but they need more explanation or evidence. For example, when you say talk shows provide help with health, finance, and family counseling, explain how they help. When you describe violence and conflict, explain why this is harmful to viewers or society.

Also, the murder example is dramatic, but you should explain how it supports your argument. Does it show that producers exploit people irresponsibly? Or that guests must think carefully before appearing?

Use a more formal academic tone
Your writing is energetic, but some expressions sound too emotional or conversational for an academic essay.

Examples to revise:
“trashy” → “sensational” or “controversial”
“deep, dark secrets” → “private information”
“on the edge of their seats” → “highly engaged”
“picture perfect ending” → “positive resolution”

Try to avoid rhetorical questions such as:
“Is it because they want to shield themselves...?”
In academic writing, it is usually better to make a direct claim instead.

Example:
“Some critics may reject these programs because the shows present social realities they find uncomfortable.”

Work on grammar and sentence structure
There are frequent grammar errors that sometimes make sentences difficult to read. The most important patterns to revise are:

a) Subject-verb agreement
“These differences in opinions leads” → “These differences in opinion lead”
“The guest are willing” → “The guests are willing”

b) Plurals and possessives
“television viewers lives” → “television viewers’ lives”
“guest panelist” → “guest panelists”
“each of the guest” → “each of the guests”

c) Word choice/spelling
Check carefully for spelling errors:
“audeinces” → “audiences”
“agressively” → “aggressively”
“thier” → “their”
“heath” → “health”
“fueding” → “feuding”
“thay” → “they”
“nieghbor” → “neighbor”

d) Sentence boundaries and punctuation
You have many punctuation problems, especially periods used where commas should be, and double punctuation.

Examples:
“trash t.v..” → “trash TV”
“vulgar. and inappropriate” → “vulgar and inappropriate”
“Many faithful viewers. feel” → “Many faithful viewers feel”

Read each sentence carefully and ask:
Is this one complete sentence?
Do I need a comma, period, or no punctuation here?

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion should do more than mention guest responsibility. It should bring together the whole argument.

A stronger conclusion could:
restate your thesis
summarize benefits and risks
explain that talk shows should be judged by both content and responsibility

Revision priorities

Write a clear thesis
Reorganize paragraphs around one idea each
Make sure each paragraph supports the thesis
Replace informal language with academic language
Edit grammar, spelling, and punctuation carefully

Final comment
