Feedback Report

You have a clear position: the legal drinking age in the U.S. should be lowered to 18. Your essay also shows strong opinion and energy, which is important in argumentative writing. To revise successfully, focus on clarity, organization, grammar, and stronger evidence.

Main strengths
Clear thesis: You state your argument early: “Eighteen should be the legal age to consume alcohol in the United States.”
Relevant reasons: You give several reasons: military service, independence at 18, graduation, and possible effects on crime.
Strong voice: Your writing sounds confident and persuasive.

Main problems to revise

A. Organization and logic
Your essay has body paragraphs, but the argument is not always logical or consistent.

For example:
In paragraph 4, you argue that lowering the drinking age would reduce crime, but you do not explain this fully or provide evidence.
In the conclusion, you write: “Drinking should be prohibited to kids eighteen or older.” This means people 18+ should not drink, which is the opposite of your argument. You probably mean permitted or allowed.

How to improve
Use a simple structure:
Introduction: background + thesis  
Body 1: If 18-year-olds can serve in the military, they should have adult rights  
Body 2: At 18, people are legal adults and should make personal choices  
Body 3: Lowering the age may reduce unsafe secret drinking  
Conclusion: restate argument and explain why it matters

Also, make sure each paragraph has:
a clear topic sentence
explanation
example or evidence
connection back to the thesis

B. Evidence and support
Many claims are based only on opinion. In academic argument, you need facts, statistics, or examples.

Examples of unsupported claims:
“Most crime among teenagers is drinking and driving.”
“Maybe a higher percentage of teenagers will graduate...”
“It would just make the U.S. a better environment to live in.”

These are interesting ideas, but they need proof.

How to improve
Add evidence from reliable sources:
statistics about underage drinking
information about military age and adult rights
research on whether lower drinking ages reduce unsafe drinking
comparisons with other countries or U.S. history

Also consider the opposing side. For example, some people argue that younger drinkers are at greater risk in traffic accidents. If you mention this and respond to it, your essay will become stronger.

C. Tone and word choice
Some phrases are too informal or emotional for an academic essay:
“Kids are dieing for our country”
“drink a beer”
“do anything an adult can do”

Try to sound more formal:
use young adults instead of kids
use consume alcohol instead of drink a beer
avoid “I would feel more like an adult” in the conclusion unless the essay is meant to be personal

Grammar and sentence-level issues
There are many language errors, but most can be fixed with careful proofreading.

Common patterns:
Spelling:  
  - “Every since” → Ever since  
  - “Independance” → Independence  
  - “dieing” → dying  
  - “affects” → effects (or better: “effects of alcohol”)  
  - “gonvernment” → government

Word choice/confusion:  
  - “condemned to go to war” sounds too strong; better: allowed to serve in the military or sent to war
  - “containing alcohol in the car” → possessing alcohol in a car

Grammar:  
  - “you can not” → cannot  
  - “two young” → too young  
  - “eighteen year old was allowed” → eighteen-year-olds were allowed

Sentence completeness:  
  - “when the government says that you are two young to do what all of the other adults.”  
    This sentence is incomplete. Better:  
    ...when the government says you are too young to do what other adults can do.

Example of a stronger revision
Original:  
“Kids are getting in too much trouble trying to hide their drinking from their parents.”

Improved:  
“Because drinking is illegal for people under twenty-one, many eighteen- to twenty-year-olds drink in secret, which may encourage unsafe behavior.”

This version is more formal and precise.

Final advice
When you revise:
Correct the conclusion so it matches your thesis.
Use more formal vocabulary.
Add evidence for each main claim.
Check grammar, spelling, and sentence structure carefully.
Consider one counterargument and respond to it.

You have a good foundation and a clear opinion. With better support and clearer language, this essay can become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear concern: children learn harmful gender stereotypes early, and society should change those messages so adults can develop healthier identities. You also use several sources, which shows ambition and engagement with course readings. To revise effectively, focus on argument clarity, organization, source use, and sentence-level accuracy.

Main argument: make your thesis more precise

Men and women are naturally different and should understand those differences.

These ideas can work together, but you need to explain the relationship clearly. At present, readers may wonder: Are you defending gender differences, criticizing stereotypes, or both?

Try writing a more specific thesis, for example:
Society should teach children that gender identity exists, but stereotypical roles should not limit what boys and girls can value, do, or become.

This version gives you a clearer line of argument for the whole essay.

Organization: build a logical progression
Your essay has interesting material, but the structure is sometimes hard to follow. A stronger outline might be:

Introduction
Explain the issue
Present your thesis clearly

Body Paragraph 1: Children learn gender stereotypes early
Use Masters and Johnson

Body Paragraph 2: Harmful effects in adulthood
Use Ferrell and Friedan
Show how rigid roles hurt both men and women

Body Paragraph 3: A better alternative
Explain what healthier gender education would look like
If you use Gray, explain exactly how his ideas support your thesis

Conclusion
Restate argument
Emphasize the need to change messages given to children

At the moment, some paragraphs jump too quickly from one source to another without enough explanation.

Masters and Johnson
Katha Pollitt

Betty Friedan (not Frieden/Fredian/Friedon)
John Gray (not Grey)

This is more analytical.

For instance, when discussing Ralph or Friedan’s homemakers, you could explain:

How earlier childhood messages may have contributed to those adult outcomes

This deeper analysis will make the essay more persuasive.

Tone and word choice
Some phrases sound too informal or too judgmental for academic writing:
“roller coaster ride”
“wonderful start” (used ironically, but unclear)
“just want quiet”
“scarry confession”

Try to use a more formal and precise tone. Also be careful with absolute statements such as “men and women walk, talk, drive, and grow in different ways.” This sounds too broad and may seem unsupported. Be more cautious:

A. Spelling

piont → point

You have a strong topic and a real argument here. With clearer structure and more precise writing, this essay can become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that legalizing some drugs, especially marijuana, could benefit society. You also try to discuss several areas—health, crime, prisons, government control, and personal freedom—which shows strong effort and range. To revise effectively, focus on (1) a clearer thesis, (2) better organization, (3) more careful reasoning, and (4) grammar/style accuracy.

Main argument and thesis
Your central idea is understandable, but the essay sometimes shifts between:
legalizing marijuana only
legalizing some drugs
legalizing all drugs

This makes the argument less precise. Choose one position and state it clearly in the introduction.

Possible revised thesis:
> Because prohibition has not stopped drug use, legalizing and regulating marijuana—and possibly some lower-risk drugs—could reduce crime, improve product safety, and allow governments to use tax revenue for public health.

This thesis is more specific and gives your essay a structure.

Organization
Right now, ideas appear in a somewhat random order, and some paragraphs contain too many different points. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Background: drug use continues despite prohibition
   - Thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Public health and safety
   - Regulation would improve purity and reduce dangerous contamination
   - Medical supervision / safer access
   - Needle programs could reduce disease transmission

Body Paragraph 2: Crime and prisons
   - Illegal markets create violence
   - Legal sales could weaken drug dealers
   - Fewer arrests for minor marijuana offenses would reduce prison overcrowding

Body Paragraph 3: Economic benefits
   - Tax revenue
   - Job creation
   - Funds for treatment and education

Body Paragraph 4: Counterargument
   - Some people worry legalization would increase use, especially among youth
   - Respond with regulation, age limits, education, and enforcement

Conclusion
   - Restate the argument clearly
   - Emphasize why legalization/regulation is more practical than prohibition

Reasoning and development
Some ideas are interesting, but they need stronger support and more careful logic.

A. Avoid overgeneralization
For example:
“Most violent crimes stimulate from selling illicit drugs.”
“Everyone who is using alcohol or drugs are just trying to achieve one goal, which is getting high.”

These statements are too broad. Use more cautious language:
“Some violent crime is linked to illegal drug markets.”
“Many users seek intoxication, although patterns of use vary.”

B. Be careful with controversial claims
Statements such as “If somebody does not want to live on this earth, why make them?” are too extreme and may weaken your credibility. Focus on policy, safety, and freedom rather than shocking language.

C. Include evidence
Your essay would be much stronger with facts, examples, or sources. For revision, add:
statistics about marijuana arrests
evidence from places where marijuana has been legalized
research on tax revenue or reduced incarceration
public health evidence about regulated markets and needle access

Use of counterarguments
You mention concerns about health and age limits, which is good. However, your responses are not fully developed. For example, saying age limits should maybe be removed is unlikely to persuade many readers. A stronger response would be:
> Although some critics argue that legalization would make drugs easier for teenagers to obtain, governments can reduce this risk through strict age limits, ID checks, school education, and penalties for illegal sales to minors.

Language and grammar
Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are many sentence-level errors. Focus on these patterns:

A. Subject-verb agreement
“The side effects of marijuana is” → “The side effects of marijuana are”
“People are going to use drugs regardless of what anyone say” → “what anyone says”
“The government claim” → “The government claims”

B. Word choice / spelling
“lease harmful” → “least harmful”
“pureness” → “purity”
“stimulate from” → “stem from”
“hann your body” → “harm your body”
“constitution liberty” → “constitutional liberty”

C. Sentence fragments
“Issues such as permanent damage to the brain and other parts of the anatomy.”
This is not a complete sentence. You could write:
> Opponents of legalization often mention issues such as permanent brain damage and harm to other parts of the body.

D. Run-on or awkward sentences
Some sentences are too long or loosely connected. Break them into shorter, clearer ones.

E. Formal tone
Avoid phrases like:
“get high off of”
“why not try something new?”
Use more academic wording:
“become intoxicated”
“it is reasonable to consider alternative policies”

Final advice for revision
When revising, do these three things first:
Decide exactly what you support: marijuana only, or marijuana plus some other drugs.
Reorganize into clear topic paragraphs with one main idea each.
Add evidence and use a more formal tone.

You have a strong willingness to argue and many relevant ideas. With clearer structure and more precise language, this essay can become much more persuasive.

“It s” → “It’s”
“b t U” appears to be a typing error
“teacher's assistants” → usually “teaching assistants”
“they fill up so quickly” → “sections fill up so quickly”

“allowed domain over a classroom” → “allowed to teach a class”
“invoke the student to ponder” → “encourage students to think about”
“ample amount of English” → “sufficient English proficiency”

“professors… they”
“students… they”

These phrases sound angry, but they weaken your credibility. In academic writing, strong arguments are usually more persuasive when expressed calmly.

Instead of “This was a case of greed,” write:

For example, after mentioning the city spent money on downtown projects, explain:

“Los Angeles Rains” → probably Rams
“Tennessee Cherokees” may be inaccurate
“late march of 1983” → late March 1984 if referring to the Colts’ move

Check all names and historical details.

Common issues
Apostrophes
“it's” should often be its
“1980's” should be 1980s

Word choice
“most beloved team” → most beloved team
“consciously devastated” → deeply devastated
“abdication” is the wrong word here; better: abandonment or betrayal
“rapid” → probably rabid

To revise effectively, focus on clarifying your argument, improving organization, and correcting language problems.

Main strengths
Meaningful topic and strong voice: The essay engages seriously with pressure from society, family, and gender roles.
Use of examples: Betty Friedan and Ralph provide concrete cases rather than only abstract claims.
Argumentative intention: You are not just describing a problem; you are trying to persuade the reader that individuals should make independent choices.

Thesis and argument
Your main claim appears strongest in the first and last paragraphs: people should not let others decide their life path. However, the thesis is still a little broad and indirect. Try to state it more clearly near the end of the introduction.

For example:
> Social expectations about career and family often push men and women into choices that do not fit their true values; therefore, individuals should make life decisions based on their own priorities rather than society’s approval.

This will help the reader understand the essay’s direction.

Also, some parts of the essay seem to assume one “better” path, especially for women (for example, staying home with children first). Be careful: if your argument is about personal choice, then your examples should support freedom of choice, not one fixed solution for everyone.

Organization
The essay has many interesting ideas, but the structure is sometimes hard to follow. I suggest this pattern:

Introduction
   - Explain the problem of social pressure
   - Present your thesis clearly

Body paragraph 1: Women and social expectations
   - Friedan’s influence
   - Pressure to “have it all”
   - Result: dissatisfaction or conflict

Body paragraph 2: Men and social expectations
   - Ralph’s story
   - Pressure to be breadwinner/successful worker
   - Result: distance from family and lost personal dreams

Body paragraph 3: Counterargument / complexity
   - Acknowledge that practical concerns matter: money, family needs, stability
   - But argue that practical choices should still reflect personal values

Conclusion
   - Restate argument
   - Emphasize self-knowledge and balanced decision-making

Try to make each paragraph begin with a topic sentence that clearly states its main point.

Use of evidence
You mention quotations with “,” but the quotations themselves are missing. If this is a source-based essay, you must include the actual quotation or remove the marker. Also, explain the evidence more directly. Do not assume the reader knows Friedan or Farrell well.

For example, after mentioning Ralph, explain:
what his story shows,
how it supports your claim,
why it matters today.

Style and tone
Sometimes the tone becomes too conversational or rhetorical:
“But what if it’s wrong?”
“What’s not to like...?”

These can work in moderation, but too many rhetorical questions weaken academic style. Replace some with direct statements.

Example:
> Instead of asking “What if it’s wrong?”, write “Such choices can be deeply damaging when they do not reflect a person’s real goals.”

Also, avoid overgeneralization. Phrases like “nothing has changed” are too absolute. A more careful claim is stronger:
> Although social attitudes have changed in some ways, many people still feel trapped by expectations about success, gender, and family.

Language issues to revise
There are frequent grammar, word choice, and punctuation problems. Common patterns:

Word choice
“erected of them” → expected of them
“reouired” → required
“actuality prefer” → actually prefer
“tine-in” → tie-in or better: internet connection / remote work arrangement

Grammar
“as young people being pressured” → as young people, being pressured / when they are young and pressured
“Ralph... and others like him, are likely” → subject agreement and punctuation need checking
“fear women are willing” → probably few women are willing

Punctuation
There are extra spaces, comma problems, and occasional awkward sentence boundaries. For example:
“being ,pressured” → remove comma
Avoid unnecessary commas before “that”
Some long sentences should be divided into two shorter ones

Final revision advice
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis
Reorganize paragraphs around one main point each
Make sure each example clearly supports the thesis
Reduce rhetorical questions and make the tone more academic
Edit carefully for grammar and word choice

Overall, this essay shows strong engagement with the issue and good potential. With a clearer structure and more precise language, your argument will become much more persuasive.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0006.2.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: men and women can be different, and those differences should not always be treated as negative or discriminatory. This is a bold argumentative position, and you use many examples to support it. You also show energy and confidence in your writing, which is important in persuasive essays.

That said, to make the essay more convincing in an academic setting, you need to improve focus, logic, tone, organization, and language accuracy.

Main strengths
Clear position: The reader can understand your main claim.
Use of examples: You include sports, advertising, restaurants, and entertainment.
Strong voice: Your essay sounds engaged and opinionated, which helps argument writing.

Main problems with argument
A. Thesis is too broad and unclear
Your introduction says men and women are naturally different, that law should treat everyone equally, and that society should “enjoy” these differences. These ideas are related, but not yet precise enough.

Try to make your thesis more specific. For example:
> Although men and women should receive equal legal rights and opportunities, recognizing some physical and social differences between the sexes should not automatically be considered discrimination.

This is clearer and easier to defend.

B. Some examples do not prove your point well
Several body paragraphs rely on examples like strip clubs, Hooters, and sexualized advertising. These examples are controversial, but more importantly, they do not automatically prove that appreciation of difference is fair or harmless. A reader may argue that these industries objectify women or reinforce stereotypes.

To strengthen your essay, you should:
explain why these examples support your argument,
address the opposing view fairly,
or replace some examples with stronger ones, such as:
  - separate sports competitions,
  - different marketing to men and women,
  - social preferences that are not discriminatory.

C. Overgeneralization
You often make large claims without evidence, for example:
“Most guys do not want to have men in small outfits serving them food.”
“The main point of wearing cologne or perfume is to attract the opposite sex.”

These are too absolute. In academic writing, avoid assuming all men or all women think the same way. Use softer language:
“many people,”
“some consumers,”
“in many cases,”
“often.”

Organization
The essay would be stronger if each paragraph had:
a topic sentence,
an example,
explanation of how the example supports the thesis.

Right now, some paragraphs feel like a list of opinions.

A possible structure:
Introduction: equality under the law, but recognition of difference is not always harmful.
Body 1: physical differences in sports may justify separate competition.
Body 2: appreciation of beauty or attraction is not always sexism.
Body 3: businesses sometimes market to male or female preferences; this can be debated but is not always unfair.
Counterargument paragraph: some forms of “enjoying differences” can become exploitation or discrimination.
Conclusion: balance equality with recognition of difference.

Adding a counterargument is especially important for a university essay.

Tone and academic style
Your tone is often too informal or provocative for academic writing. Phrases like:
“trashy people”
“lust after the opposite sex”
“Maybe open one called buttocks for women”

make the essay sound emotional or joking rather than analytical.

Try to use more neutral academic language. For example:
“socially stigmatized”
“sexual attraction”
“gender-targeted marketing”

This will make your essay sound more mature and persuasive.

Language issues
There are many grammar, spelling, and word-choice errors. Some frequent problems:

A. Confusing word choice
“underlining driving force” → underlying driving force
“resent social mood” → recent social mood
“increase social well” → increase social well-being
“built up faithful” → built up a loyal following
“scene as important” → seen as important
“sexes” → often should be sexist
“were” → sometimes should be where
“there” / “their” / “they’re” are often confused

B. Sentence structure
Some sentences are awkward or run-on. Example:
> “The fact that there are two sexes in the situation the obvious answer to the problem could not be reached.”

Better:
> Because sex became an issue in the situation, the school did not choose the option that seemed most practical.

C. Agreement and articles
“a women” → a woman
“There has been several lawsuits” → There have been several lawsuits
“the have” → they have

One important content issue
Be careful with the difference between:
recognizing difference
and
justifying unequal treatment.

Your essay is strongest when discussing separate sports competition. It is weaker when it seems to defend hiring or funding decisions based mainly on sex. Make sure you clarify that appreciating difference should not remove fairness or opportunity.

Final advice for revision
For your next draft:
Rewrite the thesis to be more precise.
Keep 2–3 strongest examples only.
Add one counterargument and respond to it.
Use more neutral academic tone.
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and word choice.

You have an interesting argument, but it needs clearer reasoning and more careful language to succeed as an academic essay.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0006.3.txt =====
Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that welfare reform should combine Democratic and Republican ideas. This is a strong starting point because your thesis is specific and you organize the essay around several proposed changes. You also use sources throughout, which shows good academic effort. For revision, focus on clarifying your argument, improving paragraph development, and correcting language/mechanics.

Strengths
Clear thesis: In the introduction, you state the main claim and list the reforms you support.
Relevant sources: You include evidence from several sources rather than relying only on opinion.
Logical structure: The essay moves through major points such as training programs, time limits, and funding cuts.

Main Issues to Revise

A. Make the thesis more precise and balanced
Your thesis says the issue “can be solved” by combining both parties’ ideas. This sounds too absolute. In argumentative writing, it is usually better to avoid claiming that a complex social issue can be “easily solved.”

Current idea:  
“The welfare problem could easily be solved…”

Better:  
“A more effective welfare system could be created by combining time limits, required training, and reforms to cash-assistance policies.”

This sounds more academic and realistic.

B. Develop analysis, not only summary
Many body paragraphs present quotations, but sometimes the essay does not explain how the evidence supports your argument. After each quotation or example, add 1–2 sentences of analysis.

For example, after discussing Project Match, explain:
Why is this program successful?
Does it show that training works for all recipients, or only some?
What conditions are necessary for success?

This will make your argument stronger and more thoughtful.

C. Avoid overgeneralization and unsupported assumptions
Some claims are too broad or may sound unfair, for example:
“There would no chance for recipients to become dependent…”
“This would save the government a lot of money and make people support themselves.”
“Mothers on welfare are having more babies at the government’s expense.”

These statements need either stronger evidence or more careful wording. Welfare policy is complicated, and readers may question arguments that seem to blame recipients without acknowledging structural issues such as low wages, lack of childcare, or limited job opportunities.

Try language such as:
“may reduce long-term dependency”
“could encourage employment for some recipients”
“critics argue that…”

D. Improve paragraph unity
Some paragraphs include more than one main idea. For example, the paragraph about spending cuts also shifts into AFDC and teenage mothers. Consider separating these into distinct paragraphs:
Why spending cuts are proposed  
Why AFDC is controversial  
Why benefits related to additional children are debated

This will improve readability.

Use of Sources
You include many quotations, but there are several problems:
Some quotations are dropped into the paragraph without introduction.
Punctuation around quotations is often incorrect.
Some source names are misspelled: Silverstein is sometimes written as Sliverstein; Whitman appears as whitman.

Try to introduce and explain quotations more smoothly:
Weak: “Welfare is part of the problem…”
Better: Silverstein argues that “welfare is part of the problem and not the solution” (14), suggesting that long-term cash assistance may discourage independence.

Also, use quotation marks only for exact words. If you paraphrase, quotation marks are not needed.

Language and Grammar
Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are frequent grammar and mechanics errors. Focus especially on:

A. Articles and plurals
“less people” → fewer people
“their own family” → their own families or their own family members

B. Word form / phrasing
“heatedly argued welfare issue” → hotly debated welfare issue
“spread across the United States” → expanded across the United States
“women should get more money for having more children” → additional welfare payments for having more children

C. Sentence structure
Some sentences are awkward or incomplete:
“If these time limits were enforced there would no chance…”  
  → If these time limits were enforced, there would be no chance…

D. Capitalization and spelling
highschool → high school
life time → lifetime
tax payers → taxpayers
Capitalize proper nouns and source names consistently.

E. Quotation punctuation
There are many quotation mark and comma errors. Example:
`"Everyone agrees that the welfare system should be changed, but 'how, is the big question"`
This should be corrected carefully according to your citation style.

Content Suggestion
To strengthen your argument, acknowledge at least one counterargument. For example:
Some critics argue that strict time limits may hurt children if jobs are unavailable.
Then respond by explaining why training and limited support should still be maintained.

This will make your essay more persuasive and academically mature.

Revision Priorities
Rewrite the thesis to sound more precise.
Improve body paragraphs by adding analysis after evidence.
Reduce overgeneralized claims.
Fix paragraph organization.
Edit grammar, spelling, and quotation punctuation carefully.

You have a solid foundation and a clear opinion. With stronger analysis and more careful language, this essay can become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you believe dorm curfews and limits on overnight visitation are unfair and should be changed. The paper also includes specific examples from your own experience, which is a strong feature in argumentative writing. These personal stories make your ideas feel real and help readers understand why the issue matters.

What you do well

Clear opinion
Your thesis is understandable: students in college are adults and should have more freedom, especially about overnight guests and curfews.

Good use of examples
You use several examples:
your male classmate helping with homework
your cousin visiting during homecoming
safety concerns about students walking across campus at 2 a.m.

These examples support your argument and give the essay emotional force.

Strong sense of audience
You are writing in a persuasive way, trying to convince readers that the rule is unreasonable. This is effective for an argumentative essay.

---

Main areas to revise

Make your thesis more focused

Right now, the essay argues several related points:
overnight visitation should be allowed
curfews are unfair
co-ed dorms have advantages
safety is a concern
relatives should be allowed to stay

These ideas are connected, but the essay would be stronger if your main claim were more precise.

For example, you could write a thesis like:

“College dormitories should end the 2 a.m. curfew and allow overnight visitation because students are adults, the current policy is inconsistent, and forcing students to leave late at night creates safety problems.”

This gives the essay one central argument.

---

Improve organization

Your paragraphs sometimes jump from one point to another. Try this structure:

Introduction
Introduce the issue
State your thesis clearly

Body Paragraph 1: Students are adults
College students should be trusted to make responsible decisions
Roommate agreement can help manage problems

Body Paragraph 2: The current policy is inconsistent
Some dorms have more freedom than others
This creates unfairness

Body Paragraph 3: Curfew creates practical and safety problems
Students forced to leave at 2 a.m.
Walking alone late at night can be dangerous
Intoxicated students may be especially vulnerable

Body Paragraph 4: Personal example
Cousin visiting from the military
Relatives and guests may need a place to stay

Conclusion
Restate the main argument
Suggest a reasonable solution, such as weekend overnight visitation if full change is not possible

---

Use a more formal academic tone

Some phrases are too informal for an academic essay, for example:
“chill out”
“sneak around”
“quality time”
“this should be one thing that does”

Try replacing them with more formal language:
“spend time”
“avoid breaking the rules secretly”
“maintain personal relationships”
“this policy should be reconsidered”

Also avoid emotional wording that may weaken your credibility. For example, instead of “treated as if I were age thirteen,” you could write “treated as though I were not capable of adult responsibility.”

---

Strengthen grammar and sentence clarity

There are many language errors, but most can be fixed through careful editing. Pay special attention to:

Word choice / spelling
“he treated” → be treated
“sigu.her mate guest” → this seems to be a typing error; revise clearly
“bad to go” → had to go
“They’re actions change” → Their actions change

Sentence fragments and punctuation
Example:
“I guess if I were a resident of one of the ‘special’ dorms A could, co-ed.”
This is unclear and incomplete.

You might rewrite it as:
“I assume that if I lived in one of the co-ed dormitories, I would have more freedom to host overnight guests.”

Capitalization
“In” in the middle of a sentence should not be capitalized.
“Is” in “at 2 a.m. Is dangerous” should be lowercase.

Agreement and pronouns
“A friend of mine that's a Bates House resident” → “A friend of mine who is a resident of Bates House”
“If a person chooses to do whatever in their room” → better: “If a student chooses what to do in his or her room…” or “in their room” if your instructor accepts singular “they.”

---

Develop reasoning more carefully

Some claims need more explanation. For example, when you say students are adults, explain why that means dorm rules should change. When you mention safety, explain how the current policy increases risk. A strong argument does not only state opinions; it connects them logically.

Also, consider briefly addressing the other side. For example, the university may argue that curfews protect students or reduce disturbances. Then you can respond by saying that adult students can still be held responsible through guest registration, roommate consent, and quiet-hour rules.

---

Final suggestion

Your essay has a strong core idea and effective personal examples. The biggest improvements will come from:
writing a clearer thesis,
organizing each paragraph around one main point,
using more formal academic language,
carefully editing grammar and punctuation.

You have good persuasive material. With revision, this can become a much stronger argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: teachers, especially high school teachers, deserve more recognition and reward. This is a strong and meaningful topic, and your personal appreciation for teachers gives the essay warmth and sincerity. You also include several relevant points: teachers help students personally, they work hard, they are underpaid, and society often undervalues them. These ideas give your argument a good foundation.

Strengths

Clear thesis and purpose  
   From the beginning, readers understand your main claim: teachers should be recognized and rewarded more.

Personal examples  
   Your example about your favorite high school teacher is effective because it makes the essay more vivid and believable.

Passionate voice  
   The essay shows genuine feeling. This helps persuade readers because they can see the issue matters to you.

Main Areas to Revise

Improve organization
Right now, many paragraphs repeat similar ideas about appreciation, rewards, and dedication. Try grouping your ideas more clearly.

A possible structure:
Introduction: present the main argument
Body 1: teachers’ emotional and academic impact on students
Body 2: why high school teachers face special challenges
Body 3: why teachers deserve better pay and public respect
Body 4: who should recognize teachers and how
Conclusion: restate argument and end strongly

Also, make sure each paragraph begins with a topic sentence that states its main point.

Make the thesis more precise
Your thesis is understandable, but it can be more focused. For example:

Possible revision:  
“Teachers, especially high school teachers, should receive greater appreciation, public recognition, and better pay because they shape students’ lives, face difficult classroom challenges, and contribute greatly to society.”

This version clearly previews your main reasons.

Reduce repetition
Several sentences repeat the same message, such as “teachers deserve reward” or “teachers are dedicated.” Instead of repeating, develop each point with explanation or evidence.

For example, after saying teachers are dedicated, explain:
What kinds of extra work do they do?
Why is high school teaching especially difficult?
How does teacher support affect students’ success?

Use a more formal academic style
Some phrases are too conversational for an argumentative essay.

Examples:
“go an extra mile” → go the extra mile
“I guess they have to be that way” → High school teachers may need to be especially dedicated because...
“put up with a lot” → deal with many challenges
“They too deserve their flowers” is expressive, but may sound too informal or culturally specific in academic writing.

Try to use clear, formal vocabulary throughout.

Support claims with broader evidence
You use personal experience well, but the essay relies heavily on it. To strengthen the argument, add more general reasoning or examples. For instance:
teacher workload beyond class hours
emotional labor of teaching
teacher shortages caused by low pay
the long-term influence of teachers on society

Even if you do not use outside sources, you can still explain these points in more detail.

Sentence-Level Language Notes

Grammar and wording
Here are some examples to revise:

“These teachers really care and.try to work with their students.”  
  → “These teachers genuinely care and try to work with their students.”

“A material gift or a kind word of expression.”  
  → This is a sentence fragment.  
  Better: “Rewards can take the form of a material gift or a kind expression.”

“There are a limited amount of teachers...”  
  → “There is a limited number of teachers...”  
  (“number” is used for countable nouns like teachers.)

“teacher’s limitations”  
  → “teachers’ limitations” if you mean limitations affecting teachers generally.

“Everyday at least one student...”  
  → “Every day, at least one student...”

“something they’ve done good”  
  → “something they have done well”

“because it teachers that have helped made these figures”  
  → “because it is teachers who have helped make these figures”

Avoid sentence fragments and punctuation problems
There are several fragments and punctuation errors, such as:
“The students. should because...”
extra periods, apostrophes, and spacing issues
quotation mark problems near the end of a paragraph

Read carefully for proofreading, especially:
commas
apostrophes
sentence boundaries
missing words

Content Suggestions

A few ideas need more careful wording:
“Teachers seem to have a mother instinct” may sound too broad or gendered. Consider:  
  “Many teachers provide care and guidance beyond academic instruction.”
“students in high school seem to be the hardest to deal with” may sound like an unsupported generalization. Explain *why* high school can be challenging.

Final Advice
For revision, focus on:
creating a clearer paragraph structure,
writing a more specific thesis,
cutting repetition,
improving grammar and formal style,
adding more explanation to support each reason.

You have a strong topic and sincere voice. With better organization and more polished language, this essay can become much more persuasive and academically effective.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0007.3.txt =====
Feedback Report

You have chosen a clear and important topic, and your essay shows effort to use sources and discuss more than one side of the issue. You also include specific examples such as the V-chip, Senate proposals, and violent cartoons. This gives your essay a good starting point. For revision, the main areas to improve are thesis, organization, use of evidence, and language accuracy.

Clarify your main argument
Your essay is introduced as an argumentative essay, but your position is not fully clear. At different points, you seem to say:
TV violence is a serious problem for children,
some people disagree about its effects,
censorship proposals may work,
violence is necessary for storytelling,
children will eventually see violence anyway.

These ideas do not fully fit together. Decide what you really want to argue. For example, you could argue:

TV violence can negatively affect children, but parental supervision is better than government censorship, or
Because TV violence is common and harmful, stronger controls such as the V-chip are necessary.

Your final sentence sounds closer to the first position, so if that is your view, make it clear in the introduction.

Improve organization
Right now, the essay feels like a list of facts and opinions rather than a logical argument. Try using this structure:

Introduction
Introduce the topic
Give brief background
End with a clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Why TV violence is a concern
Explain how common it is
Use evidence about children and viewing time

Body Paragraph 2: Attempts to control TV violence
Explain the V-chip and government proposals
Discuss whether they are effective

Body Paragraph 3: Counterargument
Explain why some people defend violence in TV shows
Respond to that view

Conclusion
Restate your argument
Give a final judgment or recommendation

Also, use topic sentences at the beginning of each paragraph so the reader knows the paragraph’s main idea.

Use evidence more carefully
You include citations, which is good, but sometimes the evidence is not explained enough. After giving a fact or source, explain how it supports your argument.

For example:
“With an average national TV viewing time of 7 1/4 hours daily…” — Explain why this matters. Does more viewing increase children’s exposure to violence?
“The most violent cartoons are…” — Explain why cartoons are especially important if children are your focus.

Be careful with unsupported claims, such as:
“can sometimes cause the child to become violent”
This is a strong claim and needs solid evidence. If you do not have strong evidence, use more cautious wording:
“may influence aggressive behavior in some children”

Make your argument more analytical
Some sentences are too general or sound informal. For example:
“well that is strictly up to the viewer to decide”
This weakens the argument because it avoids analysis. Instead, explain your own view.

Also, this sentence is important but underdeveloped:
“The cause for television violence is to add texture to the show and not to poison children’s minds.”
This could become a strong counterargument if you explain it more clearly: writers may use violence for realism, conflict, or dramatic effect, but this does not remove concerns about child audiences.

Revise language and grammar
Your meaning is usually understandable, but some sentences need correction for grammar, word choice, and style.

Examples:
“violent tv shows” → violent TV shows
“Some of the proposals to antiviolence” → Some anti-violence proposals
“Some television shows show violence” → repetitive; use depict violence
“made for television movies” → made-for-television movies
“how many parents actually watch television with their teenager?” → how many parents actually watch television with their teenagers?

Sentence-level issues:
“An exhaustive examination of the broadcast networks' programming during the 1995-1995 season…”  
  Check the date; it is probably 1994–1995 or 1995–1996.
“sinister combat violence' pervades…”  
  The quotation mark is incorrect.
“whomever is writing the television show” → whoever is writing the television show

Try to avoid very long sentences with many ideas. Shorter, clearer sentences will strengthen your argument.

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion is too brief and sounds dismissive of the problem. Instead of saying children will eventually see violence anyway, explain what society should do now. For example:
parents should supervise children’s viewing,
broadcasters should give clearer warnings,
government censorship should be limited.

Final advice
For your next draft, focus first on one clear thesis. Then organize each paragraph so it directly supports that thesis. After that, revise sentences for grammar and clarity. You have good material here, but it needs a stronger central argument and more careful development.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: government should continue, and perhaps increase, support for the arts. You also show strong engagement with the topic and offer several different kinds of reasons: historical, social, economic, educational, and therapeutic. This breadth is a real strength. To revise effectively, focus on clarity, organization, and language accuracy.

Main strengths

Clear argumentative stance from the introduction.
Wide range of supporting ideas: communication, community, tourism, creativity, therapy, prison programs.
Ambitious vocabulary and formal tone, which suits an argumentative essay.
Personal engagement: your comments on art as relaxation make the essay more sincere.

Biggest issues to revise

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your introduction states that arts funding should be kept, “if not receive more, support,” but the exact reasons are not clearly previewed. Add a thesis that lists 2–3 main arguments.

Possible revision:
“Government should continue to fund the arts because the arts preserve culture, strengthen communities, and provide important educational and economic benefits.”

This will help the reader follow your structure.

B. Improve paragraph organization
Right now, many paragraphs contain interesting points, but the essay sometimes feels like a list of reasons rather than a tightly organized argument. Try this structure:

Introduction + thesis
Arts preserve human culture and communicate values
Arts strengthen social bonds and communities
Arts bring economic benefits
Arts improve education, creativity, and mental health
Counterargument + response (for example: some say arts funding is less essential than other spending)
Conclusion

Also, begin each body paragraph with a clear topic sentence.

Example:
“First, government funding for the arts helps preserve culture and communicate social values.”
“In addition, the arts produce measurable economic benefits for local communities.”

C. Use stronger evidence
Many claims are interesting, but they are often general. Academic argument becomes stronger with specific examples, statistics, or sources.

For example:
Instead of saying museums attract tourists, mention a city or museum.
Instead of saying psychologists have shown art develops creativity, cite a study or organization.
If you discuss prison art programs, give one specific program as evidence.

Without evidence, some statements sound too broad.

Language and style issues

A. Grammar and sentence form
Some sentences are awkward or ungrammatical. A few examples:

“which should keep” → “which should be maintained” / “which should continue to receive”
“it is interpretable” → “they are understandable”
“which directly can influence” → “which can directly increase”
“Most often, the greatest influence on an artist stems” → good idea, but simplify for clarity
“pschosanalysts” → “psychoanalysts”
“not meant be valued” → “not meant to be valued”
“in a ever-more complex” → “in an ever more complex” or “in an increasingly complex”

Watch especially for:
article use (a/the)
verb forms
singular/plural agreement
word choice that sounds unnatural in English

B. Word choice
Sometimes the vocabulary is advanced but not quite natural. Simpler wording is often stronger.

Examples:
“rests in the views of anthropology” → “can be explained from an anthropological perspective”
“impress the globe” → “impress the world”
“productive measures” → “constructive benefits”
“diffuse some violent situations” → “reduce violence” or “help prevent violent behavior”

C. Avoid overgeneralization
Some claims are too absolute:
“art is something which produces a positive, constructive analysis…”
“some works are not meant be valued as art…”

These statements may weaken your essay because they ignore disagreement. In argumentative writing, it is better to acknowledge complexity.

For example:
“Although some publicly funded works are controversial, this does not justify reducing support for the arts as a whole.”

Content suggestion: add a counterargument
A strong argumentative essay should address the other side. You mention defense cuts at the beginning, but you do not really engage with the common objection that public money should go only to “essential” services.

You could add:
“Critics argue that government funds should prioritize healthcare, education, or infrastructure. However, arts funding represents a relatively small portion of public spending and yields cultural, educational, and economic returns that benefit society as a whole.”

Sentence-level examples

Original:  
“Yet, there are some areas, such as government support for the arts, which should keep, if not receive more, support from government agencies.”

Revised:  
“However, government support for the arts should be maintained and, in some cases, even increased.”

Original:  
“These are very basic, but it is interpretable by nearly everyone in a precise, pictorial form.”

Revised:  
“Although such drawings are simple, they can be understood by nearly everyone through visual expression.”

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:

Write a clear thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs around 3–4 main reasons.
Add specific evidence.
Simplify awkward sentences and correct grammar.
Include one counterargument and response.

You have strong ideas and a serious tone. With clearer structure and more precise English, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you strongly support a salary cap for professional football players. This is good for an argumentative essay because the reader always knows what you believe. You also give several reasons for your opinion, such as fairness, fan facilities, greed, and team competition. The essay is passionate and easy to follow at the paragraph level.

What is working well
Clear thesis and organization
   - In the introduction, you state your main claim and list your reasons.
   - Most body paragraphs focus on one main idea.

Strong voice
   - Your writing sounds confident and engaged.
   - Rhetorical questions help show your feelings and involve the reader.

Use of examples
   - You use examples such as doctors, stadium conditions, strikes, and Deion Sanders. These help make the essay more concrete.

Main areas to improve

Make the argument more logical and balanced
Many of your claims are strong, but some are too general or not fully supported. For example:
“football players don't do anything that is considered rewarding”
“All you have to be able to do is run and catch balls”
“the whole reason why [OJ Simpson] was let off was because he was a popular football player”

These statements sound emotional, but they are not persuasive because readers may think they are exaggerated or unfair. In academic argument, it is better to:
avoid attacking a group of people,
explain causes carefully,
support claims with evidence.

Revision advice:  
Instead of saying football players “do nothing but entertain,” say that their salaries may be disproportionately high compared with socially necessary professions such as doctors or nurses.

Use stronger evidence
Your essay relies mostly on opinion. To be more convincing, include:
salary comparisons,
examples of stadium funding problems,
evidence about competitive balance between rich and poor teams,
facts about labor disputes or salary caps in sports leagues.

Also, some claims are factually questionable, such as:
“A professional football player makes more money in one year than a doctor does in a lifetime.”
This is probably not true in most cases.

Revision advice:  
Use more careful language:
“Some star players earn more in a single season than many doctors earn over many years.”

Improve paragraph focus
Some paragraphs become too long and include several different ideas. For example, the long paragraph about doctors also includes charity, Christmas, and rich teams funding organizations. This weakens unity.

Revision advice:  
Try this structure:
Paragraph 1: salary inequality compared with socially valuable professions
Paragraph 2: high salaries encourage greed and labor disputes
Paragraph 3: teams should invest more in fan facilities
Paragraph 4: a salary cap could improve fairness among teams

Avoid problematic wording
A few expressions are offensive or outdated in modern English, especially “mentally retarded.” Do not use this term. A better choice is:
“people with intellectual disabilities”

Also, some wording is too informal:
“wee” → typo for “we”
“let off”
“play ball”
“hate has taken over”

Try to use a more neutral academic tone.

Language corrections

Grammar and word choice
Some common issues:
subject-verb agreement: “A doctor, or a nurse work” → “A doctor or a nurse works”
possessives: “other peoples lives” → “other people’s lives”
prepositions/articles: “go to” / “in the stall” / “the following year”
word form: “affective” → “effective”
spelling: “ant other team” → “any other team”
verb tense: “Football use to be” → “Football used to be”

Sentence style
Many sentences are repetitive because they begin with:
“One reason…”
“In many ways…”
“The main reason…”

Try combining short sentences and varying openings.

For example:
Original: “The one reason why they go on strike is because of the money. So. why don't we stop giving them so much money.”  
Better: “Because salary disputes are a major cause of player strikes, a salary cap could reduce conflict between players and teams.”

Suggested thesis revision
Your current thesis is clear, but it can sound more academic. For example:

Revised thesis:  
“Professional football players’ salaries should be capped because excessively high wages create social inequality, reduce funds available for fans and facilities, and allow wealthy teams to dominate competition.”

Final advice
For your revision, focus on:
Using a more formal tone
Supporting claims with evidence
Avoiding overgeneralizations
Dividing ideas into clearer paragraphs
Editing grammar and word choice carefully

You have a strong foundation because your opinion is clear and your essay has energy. If you make your reasoning more precise and academic, this essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that stronger gun control is constitutional and necessary to reduce violence. This is a solid argumentative foundation. You also use outside sources and try to support your claims with examples, statistics, and legal discussion. These are important strengths in academic writing.

What you do well

   - Your main claim is consistent throughout the essay: gun regulation is constitutional and can reduce crime.
   - The introduction gives readers a strong sense of your argument.

Use of evidence
   - You include statistics, historical background, and references to the Brady Bill, the NRA, and Supreme Court interpretation.
   - This shows research and helps make the essay more persuasive.

Strong conclusion
   - The ending returns to the constitutional issue and public safety, which gives the essay a sense of closure.

---

Main areas to improve

Organization and repetition
Many ideas are repeated, especially:
gun control reduces crime
the Constitution allows regulation
waiting periods and background checks are helpful

Because of this repetition, the essay feels longer than necessary and sometimes loses focus.

Suggestion
Try reorganizing the paper into clear sections:
Introduction + thesis
Constitutional argument
Social harms caused by gun availability
Policy solutions (background checks, registration, waiting periods, licensing)
Counterargument and response
Conclusion

Also, some paragraphs are too long and contain several ideas at once. Each paragraph should have one main point.

---

Development of argument
Some claims need more explanation. For example:
“Law-abiding citizens do not need handguns” is a strong claim, but it may sound too absolute unless you explain it carefully.
“Most courts” and “the U.S. Supreme Court” are mentioned, but the legal discussion is oversimplified and should be presented more precisely.

Suggestion
When making a strong claim, add:
evidence
brief explanation
why it supports your thesis

For example, after a statistic, explain what it shows and why it matters.

---

Use of sources and citation
You use many source references, but there are problems:
citations are inconsistent: Cozic / Cosec, Close Up Foundation / CUF
some quotations are dropped into the sentence without enough integration
the essay relies heavily on sources, so your own voice is sometimes less visible

Suggestion
Check every author name and page number carefully.
Introduce sources clearly:  
  *According to Cozic,...*  
  *Cole argues that...*
After using a quotation, add your own explanation.
Make sure you follow one citation style consistently.

---

Language and grammar
Your essay is understandable, but there are many sentence-level errors. Here are the most important ones:

a. Capitalization
Do not capitalize common nouns in the middle of a sentence:
“Gun Control” → gun control
“Guns” → guns
“Federal Legislation” → federal legislation

b. Spelling and word form
Examples:
“sawed of shotguns” → sawed-off shotguns
“hand gun” → handgun
“can not” → cannot
“definatly” → definitely
“riffles” → rifles
“licencing” → licensing
“transferees” may be acceptable, but check whether purchasers is clearer

c. Grammar
Examples:
“buyers backgrounds” → buyers’ backgrounds
“an individuals right” → an individual’s right
“persuasiveness of a statistic” → the persuasiveness of a statistic

d. Sentence clarity

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you believe abortion should be strictly regulated, with some exceptions such as rape and danger to the mother’s life. This is a good foundation for an argumentative essay because the reader can understand your viewpoint. You also try to support your ideas with examples, especially the story about your cousin, which gives the essay a strong personal dimension.

However, to revise this essay successfully, you need to improve focus, tone, organization, and language accuracy.

Main strengths
Clear opinion: Your stance is strong and consistent.
Use of examples: You include practical examples, moral reasoning, and a personal story.
Willingness to consider exceptions: This makes the argument more complex and thoughtful.

Most important issues to revise

A. Tone and word choice
Your essay often uses very emotional, harsh, or violent language, which can weaken academic argumentation. In university writing, even when discussing sensitive topics, you should sound calm, respectful, and precise.

For example:
“play the game”
“horseplay”
“kill it”
“wild maniac”
“vicious sperm”
“put into jail for murder”

These phrases sound judgmental and informal. They may make some readers stop trusting your argument.

Better approach: use neutral language such as:
“engage in consensual sex”
“terminate a pregnancy”
“victim of rape”
“legal penalties” or “criminal responsibility”

B. Thesis and consistency
Your introduction says abortion decisions should not concern only women, and that there should be strict rules. But later, you also support abortion in some cases. This is not a problem, but your thesis should show this complexity clearly.

Possible revised thesis:
> Although abortion should not be treated as a casual solution to unintended pregnancy, it should remain legally available in limited cases such as rape or serious risk to the mother’s life.

This would make your position more precise and balanced.

C. Organization
Right now, some paragraphs are too long and contain many different ideas. Try to give one main idea per paragraph.

A clearer structure could be:

Introduction – topic + thesis  
Paragraph 1 – responsibility and contraception  
Paragraph 2 – moral objection to abortion after consensual sex  
Paragraph 3 – exception: rape  
Paragraph 4 – exception: risk to mother’s life  
Paragraph 5 – personal example of your cousin  
Conclusion – restate position

Also, use stronger topic sentences to guide the reader.

Problems with evidence and logic
Some claims need better support or more careful wording. For example:
Statistics about contraception should be cited from reliable sources.
The claim that welfare can solve financial problems is too simple and needs evidence.
The story about your cousin is powerful, but one personal example cannot prove that abortion generally causes infertility.

In academic writing, avoid making a general conclusion based on one case. You can say:
> My cousin’s experience made me concerned about the possible physical and emotional consequences of abortion.

This is more careful and persuasive.

Grammar and sentence issues
There are many grammar problems, especially with:
subject-verb agreement
articles
word form
sentence boundaries
pronouns
spelling

Examples and corrections
“a sensible, well-minded women” → a sensible, thoughtful woman
“is said to ninety-five percent effective” → is said to be ninety-five percent effective
“that not very fair” → that is not very fair
“how are financially unable” → who are financially unable
“if is she was raped” → if she was raped
“I does think” → I do think
“govermnent” → government
“should be prevent” → should be prevented

Also, some sentences are too long and become confusing. Break them into shorter sentences.

Style advice for a stronger academic essay
Avoid repeating “I feel” too often. Replace with more formal phrases:
  - I argue that
  - This essay contends that
  - In my view
Avoid absolute statements like “no excuse.” They sound too strong unless you prove them carefully.
Use transitions:
  - First,
  - Furthermore,
  - However,
  - In contrast,
  - For example,
  - Therefore

Final revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis so it reflects your nuanced position.
Remove emotional/informal language and use neutral academic wording.
Reorganize paragraphs around one main point each.
Correct grammar and spelling errors.
Make claims more careful and evidence-based.

Overall, your essay shows strong personal engagement and a clear viewpoint. With a more academic tone, better organization, and careful editing, it can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear opinion and a consistent focus: you argue that sex is overused in the media and that this influences teenagers in harmful ways. You also use specific examples from television, music, and magazines, which is good for an argumentative essay. The main revision goal is to make your argument more academic, organized, and precise.

Strengths
Clear position: From the first sentence, readers know what you believe.
Relevant examples: TV shows, songs, and advertisements help illustrate your point.
Strong sense of purpose: You are not only criticizing media, but also arguing for more responsible messages about protection and safe sex.

Main issues to revise

A. Make the thesis more specific
Right now, your main claim is repeated many times (“sex is overrated in the media”), but the essay needs a stronger thesis that shows the full argument.

For example:
> Sex is overused in television, music, and advertising, and this repeated portrayal presents unrealistic ideas about relationships while failing to promote responsibility and safe sex.

This version is more academic and gives your essay a clearer direction.

B. Improve organization
Your essay has paragraphs about TV, radio, and magazines, but the argument becomes repetitive. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Topic
   - Why it matters
   - Thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Television
   - Explain how TV normalizes casual sex
   - Give 1–2 examples
   - Explain why this matters

Body Paragraph 2: Music and magazines
   - Show how lyrics/titles and advertising use sex to attract attention
   - Give examples
   - Explain the effect on audiences

Body Paragraph 3: Impact on teenagers
   - Argue that media often shows sex without consequences or discussion of protection
   - Explain how this can shape teenagers’ attitudes

Conclusion
   - Summarize argument
   - Suggest what media should do differently

C. Use a more academic tone
Some phrases sound too conversational, such as:
“Now radio is almost as bad as television.”
“Nothing but a sheet.”
“That’s not reality.”

In academic writing, use more neutral wording:
“Radio also frequently uses sexual themes.”
“The advertisement emphasizes the model’s body rather than the product.”
“Such portrayals are unrealistic and incomplete.”

Also avoid directly addressing the reader too often with “you.” Academic essays usually prefer general statements.

Development of ideas
Your argument would be stronger if you explained how media influences teenagers instead of only stating that it does. For example:
Does repeated exposure normalize risky behavior?
Does it create unrealistic expectations about relationships?
Does it separate sex from emotional or health consequences?

Be careful not to make claims that sound too absolute, such as:
> “their only conception of sex is through the media.”

This is too broad. A better version:
> “For some teenagers, media may become an important source of information about sex, especially when communication with parents is limited.”

This sounds more accurate and persuasive.

Grammar and language corrections
There are many sentence-level errors. Focus especially on these:

A. Capitalization
“Sex Is used…” → Sex is used…
“Its funny…” → It’s funny…

B. Verb forms and word choice
“R. Kellye, bluntly saids…” → R. Kelly, bluntly says…
“soak and wet” → soaked and wet
“your selling” → you’re selling
“precautions measures” → precautionary measures or simply precautions

C. Article and plural use
“in the large number of those who promote sex” → among the many forms of media that promote sex
“show some programs and ads, that don’t center around sex” → show some programs and ads that do not center on sex

D. Sentence structure and punctuation
You have many comma splices (two full sentences joined only by a comma). Example:
> “I have ever read, has ads featuring sex.”

Also:
> “I understand that ‘sex sells’, but why can’t we have more decent ads and programs, without sex.”

Revise for cleaner punctuation:
> “I understand that ‘sex sells,’ but why can’t we have more decent ads and programs without it?”

Style improvements
Avoid repeating the same phrase too often, especially “sex is overrated.”
Use transitions such as Furthermore, In addition, Moreover, However, As a result.
Replace emotional phrasing with analytical language.

For example, instead of:
> “The media leaves nothing to the imagination.”

You could write:
> “Contemporary media often presents sexuality explicitly rather than suggestively.”

Final advice
When revising, focus on:
Writing a stronger thesis  
Reorganizing paragraphs logically  
Reducing repetition  
Using a more formal academic tone  
Correcting grammar and punctuation errors  

You have a solid foundation and a clear argument. With revision, this essay can become much more convincing and polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear and interesting topic: the psychological and social influence of clothing. You also show strong effort in using sources and organizing the essay by age groups and themes. The paper has good material, but it needs revision in thesis, organization, tone, source use, and grammar to become a stronger academic argument.

Main strength
A major strength is that you include many perspectives: self-image, adolescents, consumers, the elderly, and modesty. This gives your essay breadth and shows that fashion is a complex social issue.

Main problem: unclear central argument
Right now, the essay reads more like a collection of observations and quotations than a focused argumentative essay. Your introduction suggests that clothing influences how people judge others, but later the paper also discusses identity, consumer behavior, peer pressure, modesty, and age. These are related, but the essay needs one clear claim.

Suggestion
Create a thesis like this:
> Clothing is not merely a personal choice; it shapes social judgment, influences self-identity, and reflects the pressure of cultural expectations across different stages of life.

This gives you a clear line of argument for the whole essay.

Organization
The body paragraphs are separated by topic, which is helpful, but the progression is not always smooth. Some paragraphs begin with a clear point, while others suddenly introduce quotations or examples.

How to improve
Use a clearer structure such as:

Introduction + thesis
Clothing and first impressions/social judgment
Clothing and self-identity
Adolescents and peer pressure
Consumer behavior and fashion trends
Age, modesty, and cultural expectations
Conclusion

At the beginning of each paragraph, write a topic sentence that directly supports the thesis.

For example:
> Adolescents are especially vulnerable to fashion pressure because clothing often determines peer acceptance.

Use of evidence
You use many sources, but often the quotations are dropped into the essay without enough explanation. For example, the Robert Herrick poem is interesting, but you need to explain more clearly how it supports your argument. Also, some long quotations interrupt the flow.

Suggestions
Introduce each source clearly.
Explain in your own words what the quotation means.
After quoting, add 2–3 sentences of analysis.

For example:
> Herrick’s poem presents disorder in dress as attractive rather than improper. This suggests that clothing communicates personality and emotion, not just social status. In this way, fashion can influence desire and perception.

Also, make sure your citation style is consistent. Right now, the references such as “(84)” or “(Ryan 295)” are not always clear.

Tone and word choice
Some language is too informal, judgmental, or inappropriate for academic writing. For example, “slut” and “pimp” are offensive and weaken your credibility. Academic essays should analyze stereotypes without repeating harmful labels casually.

Better alternative
Instead of:
> One may judge them to be a slut, a pimp…

You could write:
> People may form unfair stereotypes about a person’s morality, social role, or personality based only on clothing.

Also avoid phrases like “one doesn’t know what style he/she wants.” Try to use more natural and consistent wording.

Grammar and sentence issues
There are many grammar problems, especially with:
subject-verb agreement
articles
word form
sentence clarity
capitalization

Examples
“how that person feel” → “how that person feels”
“she probably feel sexy” → “she probably feels sexy”
“one doesn’t know what style he/she wants to wear and this is where fashion designers feed”  
  → awkward; better: “Many people are uncertain about their personal style, and fashion designers often profit from this uncertainty.”
“Clothing has strong affect on adolescents”  
  → “Clothing has a strong effect on adolescents.”
“The needs for clothing are the same”  
  → “The clothing needs are similar”
“older men and women”  
  → capitalize if beginning a sentence: “Older men and women”
“fell self-pity”  
  → “feel self-pity”
“every day lives”  
  → “everyday lives”

You also have some run-on sentences. Try writing shorter, clearer sentences.

Development of ideas
Sometimes your examples are too general or based on personal speculation:
> “if I were a person who dresses the way society wants me to dress…”

This sounds hypothetical and weakens the academic tone. Instead, discuss the broader social pattern directly.

For example:
> People who dress according to dominant social expectations may receive greater acceptance, while those who reject those expectations may be excluded.

Conclusion
Your conclusion repeats earlier ideas but could be more analytical. Instead of ending generally, restate the thesis and explain why the topic matters.

Possible direction
You might conclude that fashion is not superficial; it shapes identity, social belonging, and cultural values.

---

Revision priorities
Write a clear thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one supports that thesis.
Replace informal/judgmental language with academic wording.
Explain quotations more fully.
Edit carefully for grammar and sentence structure.

You have a strong topic and useful source material. With clearer argumentation and cleaner language, this essay can become much more convincing.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-009.4.txt =====
Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that abortion should be the woman’s choice. This is a good start because your opinion is consistent from beginning to end. You also try to address opposing arguments, which is important in argumentative writing. To revise successfully, focus on organization, clarity, grammar, and support for your claims.

Strengths
Clear thesis/position: You clearly support the pro-choice view.
Awareness of counterarguments: You mention anti-abortion arguments such as rape and the “genocide” comparison.
Strong conclusion: Your ending returns to your main point and gives the essay a sense of closure.

Main problems to revise

A. Improve thesis and focus
Your thesis appears in paragraph 2:  
“the decision to have an abortion or not should be left entirely up to the woman…”

This is a workable thesis, but it needs to be more precise and academic. For example:

Revised thesis:  
*Abortion should remain legal because the decision belongs to the pregnant woman, especially since each pregnancy involves personal, medical, and social consequences that only she can fully understand.*

This version is clearer and previews reasons.

B. Strengthen organization
Right now, some paragraphs are hard to follow because ideas jump suddenly. A clearer structure would be:

Introduction: background + thesis  
Body paragraph 1: women’s bodily autonomy and personal consequences  
Body paragraph 2: special cases such as rape  
Body paragraph 3: response to anti-abortion comparison with genocide/Nazis  
Conclusion: restate thesis + broader implication

Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that states its main idea.

Example:  
*One reason abortion should remain legal is that the pregnant woman is the person most directly affected by the decision.*

C. Develop evidence more carefully
Some claims are too general or unsupported. For example:

*“If a child is born unwanted, he is automatically psychologically damaged for life…”*  
This is too absolute. “Automatically” and “for life” are very strong claims and need evidence. Without evidence, the sentence sounds exaggerated.

A better version:  
*Children who are born into difficult or unwanted circumstances may face emotional and social challenges, which is one reason some women consider abortion.*

Also, when discussing rape, explain the reasoning more fully instead of only stating it.

D. Use a more academic tone
Avoid emotional or overly absolute language when possible. For example:
“Society should not be considered Nazis...” is too dramatic.
“it is obvious” can sound informal.

Try more formal wording:
*Comparing abortion to genocide is inaccurate because genocide refers to the systematic destruction of a specific group, while abortion concerns an individual reproductive decision.*

Language and grammar issues

A. Sentence boundaries / comma splices
You often join two complete sentences with a comma. Example:

Original:  
*These debates began over two decades ago, they have done nothing, but enlarge throughout the years.*

Better:  
*These debates began over two decades ago, and they have only grown over the years.*  
Or:  
*These debates began over two decades ago. Since then, they have only grown.*

B. Word choice and form
Some words are incorrect:
“pro-choiceor” → *pro-choice or*
“proffers” → probably *pro-lifers*
“this categories” → *these categories*
“its hard” → *it’s hard*
“there tendencies” → *their tendencies*
“woman women” → likely an editing mistake; remove repetition
“anti-abortionist” → more natural: *anti-abortion activists* or *opponents of abortion*

C. Articles and plurals
As a Chinese L1 writer, pay special attention to a/an/the and singular/plural forms:
*the lives of humans* → *human lives*
*a woman’s personal decision*
*some anti-abortionists think…*

D. Awkward phrasing
Examples:
*“The two names for this categories are either…”*  
  → *People generally identify themselves as either pro-choice or pro-life.*
*“According to the situation”*  
  → *Because she understands her own situation best…*

Content issues to reconsider
Be careful not to oversimplify the opposing side. For example, you say anti-abortionists “ignore” rape cases, but many do discuss them, even if they disagree. Representing the other side fairly will make your argument stronger.

Also, the paragraph beginning *“Although if you analyze…”* is incomplete and confusing. It seems you wanted to discuss rape as an exception, but the sentence is not finished. Revise this paragraph completely.

A possible revised opening
*Abortion is one of the most controversial moral and legal issues in modern society. People usually divide themselves into two groups: pro-choice and pro-life. Although both sides present strong opinions, abortion should remain legal because the decision involves the woman’s body, health, and future, and these consequences are best understood by the woman herself.*

Final advice
For revision, focus on:
Writing a clearer thesis  
Organizing each paragraph around one main idea  
Adding explanation and evidence  
Correcting grammar and word choice  
Using a more formal academic tone

You have a strong opinion and a clear purpose. With better organization and more careful language, this essay can become much more persuasive.
