Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: television exposes children to sexual content, violence, and profanity too early, and media producers should provide more child-appropriate programming. You also show strong concern for children’s development, and your use of examples makes your argument feel sincere and personal. For revision, focus on clarity, organization, evidence, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear argument: Your main claim is understandable from the beginning.
Strong sense of purpose: You care about the topic, which gives the essay energy.
Use of examples: References to talk shows, cartoons, and your own children help readers see what you mean.
Awareness of audience: You try to persuade parents and media producers, which is effective for an argumentative essay.

Main issues to revise

A. Narrow and clarify the thesis
Right now, the essay repeats similar ideas: children watch adult content, they copy it, and parents/media should stop this. Try to state one focused thesis near the end of the introduction.

For example:
“Because young children cannot fully understand sexual and violent content on television, broadcasters should provide more age-appropriate programs during after-school hours, and parents should supervise what children watch.”

This is stronger because it gives:
the problem,
the reason,
the solution.

B. Improve organization
Your essay has many ideas, but they are not clearly divided. Group similar points into separate body paragraphs.

A possible structure:
Introduction – problem + thesis  
Body paragraph 1 – children have easy access to adult TV  
Body paragraph 2 – children may misunderstand and imitate what they see  
Body paragraph 3 – not all “children’s” programs are suitable  
Body paragraph 4 – what parents and TV stations should do  
Conclusion – restate argument and suggest action

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence.

Example:
“Young children are often exposed to adult television because many programs shown after school are not designed for them.”

C. Use stronger evidence
Some claims sound too extreme or unsupported, such as:
“Approximately ninety eight percent of television programs are not for children.”
“That only leaves a small two percent…”

If you do not have evidence, avoid exact statistics. Readers may doubt your credibility. Instead write:
“A large number of television programs shown after school are aimed at adults rather than children.”

Also, personal experience is useful, but an argumentative essay becomes stronger when you add:
research on child development,
studies about media influence,
information about TV ratings or scheduling.

D. Avoid overgeneralization
Some statements are too broad:
“Children see people on television as role models…”
“Once interested then wants to experiment.”
“The child will think it’s all right to have sex and get pregnant…”

These claims may be true in some cases, but not always. Use more cautious language:
may
can
are likely to
may misunderstand

This makes your argument sound more academic and reasonable.

Language and style

A. Tone
Some phrases are informal, such as:
“Big people shows”
“mom’s cooking or cleaning”
“what can you say but no”

For academic writing, use more formal vocabulary:
adult-oriented programs
unsupervised viewing
inappropriate content

B. Grammar and word choice
There are many sentence-level errors. Common problems include:
subject-verb agreement,
article use,
word form,
punctuation,
spelling.

Examples:
“make the children to want” → “make children want”
“How is a parent suppose to explain” → “How is a parent supposed to explain”
“a man and women” → “a man and a woman”
“its up to the parents” → “it is up to the parents”
“some where” → “somewhere”

C. Sentence control
Some sentences are very long and include several ideas. Break them into shorter sentences for clarity.

Example revision:
Original:  
“With these television programs on every day the child starts to watch his or her favorite show and once interested then wants to experiment.”

Possible revision:  
“When children watch these programs every day, they may become attached to particular shows. After repeated exposure, they may also become curious about the behaviors they see.”

Content suggestions
Be careful with the paragraph about homosexuality. As written, it may sound biased or unclear. If you keep this point, focus on the issue of age-appropriate explanation of complex topics, not on suggesting that homosexuality itself is harmful.

For example:
“Some topics shown on television are complex and may be difficult for very young children to understand without parental guidance.”

Final advice
For your next draft:
Write a clear thesis.
Divide the essay into logical paragraphs.
Remove unsupported statistics.
Use more formal, cautious language.
Edit carefully for grammar and punctuation.

You have a strong topic and a clear concern. With better organization and more precise language, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear purpose: to persuade readers that recycling is necessary to protect the environment. You also include many specific examples (plastic, glass, batteries, motor oil, printer cartridges), which shows strong effort in research and support. The conclusion returns to your main message effectively. For revision, focus on sharpening the argument, improving organization, and correcting language/style issues.

Main strengths
Clear topic and position: You consistently argue that recycling is important.
Good range of examples: The essay shows that recycling involves more than paper and cans.
Use of evidence: You cite sources and statistics, which strengthens credibility.
Sense of urgency: Your tone makes the issue feel important.

Biggest revision priority: strengthen the thesis and argument
Right now, the essay sometimes reads more like an informational report than an argumentative essay. You list many recyclable items, but the central claim is not always precise.

Improve the thesis
Your introduction should state:
your position
your main reasons

For example:
> Recycling should become a normal part of daily life because it reduces landfill waste, conserves natural resources, and prevents toxic pollution.

This kind of thesis gives your essay a clear structure.

Make each paragraph support the thesis
Instead of only describing one material at a time, explain how that material supports your argument. For example:
Plastic recycling reduces waste and conserves resources.
Recycling glass and steel saves energy.
Proper disposal of batteries, oil, and refrigerators prevents toxic pollution.

This will make your essay more analytical and persuasive.

Organization
Your essay would be stronger with a clearer paragraph structure.

Suggested structure
Introduction: environmental problem + thesis  
Body paragraph 1: recycling reduces landfill waste  
Body paragraph 2: recycling conserves resources and energy  
Body paragraph 3: proper recycling/disposal prevents toxic pollution  
Body paragraph 4: practical actions consumers can take  
Conclusion: restate argument and call to action

At present, some paragraphs are too long and cover several ideas. Try to give each paragraph one main idea with:
a topic sentence
evidence
explanation
a concluding sentence

Use of sources
You use many quotations, but sometimes they are dropped into the paragraph without enough explanation. After each quote, explain:
what it means
why it matters
how it supports your argument

For example, after a statistic about landfill space, explain how this proves recycling is necessary.

Also, some of your sources seem old or possibly unreliable (for example, references to the Twin Towers date the material). If possible, use more current and authoritative sources.

Style and tone
Your tone is generally persuasive, but some sentences are too emotional or too informal for academic writing.

For example:
“please make the effort to recycle” → too direct/informal
“the world would not be in danger” → too broad and absolute

Try for a more formal academic style:
> Consumers should make greater efforts to recycle paper products when reuse or plastic alternatives are not possible.

Also avoid overgeneralizations such as “Plastic bags are much better for the environment than paper bags” unless you fully prove that claim. This is a complicated issue, so use more careful wording:
> In some cases, plastic bags may require fewer natural resources to produce than paper bags.

Language and grammar issues
You have many good sentences, but there are frequent grammar and word choice problems. Common issues include:

A. Subject-verb agreement
“the human population needs to educate themselves”  
  Better: people need to educate themselves or the population needs to educate itself

B. Word choice
“hangars” → hangers
“fun” → full
“fight bulb” → light bulb
“m the normal trash” → in the normal trash

C. Sentence structure
Some sentences are awkward or unclear:
“There are many possible ways for this to be achieved, one of which being recycling.”  
  Better: There are many ways to achieve this goal, and one of the most effective is recycling.

D. Punctuation
Do not use semicolons incorrectly:
“This includes but is not limited to; plastic bags...”  
  Better: This includes, but is not limited to, plastic bags...

E. Clarity
“Recycling needs to become commonplace uniformly” sounds unnatural.  
  Better: Recycling needs to become a common practice everywhere.

Content accuracy and precision
Be careful with factual claims:
Some statements are too absolute.
Some information may be outdated.
A few terms are imprecise, such as “household toxics.” A better term is household hazardous waste.

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these steps:
Write a clear thesis with 2–3 reasons.
Reorganize the body paragraphs around those reasons.
Use evidence more selectively and explain it more.
Replace emotional language with formal academic style.
Edit carefully for grammar, word choice, and punctuation.

You already have strong material and many useful examples. With better organization and a more argumentative structure, this essay can become much more effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a consistent main idea: society associates pink with girls/females and blue with boys/males, and advertising helps reinforce this stereotype. You use familiar examples, especially from commercials and baby products, which makes your argument easy to follow. This is a strong starting point.

What is working well
Clear focus  
   The essay stays on one main issue: color stereotypes and gender.

Relevant examples  
   You mention shaving gel, deodorant, diapers, bottles, and baby clothing. These examples support your point that media and consumer products reinforce gender stereotypes.

Logical basic organization  
   You move from women, to baby girls, to baby boys, and then to a conclusion. This gives the essay a simple structure.

Main areas to revise

Make your thesis clearer
Right now, the essay begins with questions, but your argument is not stated strongly enough. Add a clear thesis at the end of the introduction.

Possible thesis:  
*In American culture, pink is often associated with femininity and blue with masculinity, and advertisements, baby products, and social expectations continue to reinforce these gender stereotypes.*

This will help the reader understand exactly what you are arguing.

Improve paragraph structure
Some paragraphs are very short, and some ideas repeat. Try this structure:

Introduction: introduce the topic and thesis
Body paragraph 1: commercials aimed at women
Body paragraph 2: baby girls and pink
Body paragraph 3: baby boys and blue
Conclusion: explain what these stereotypes mean socially

Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence.

Example topic sentence:  
*Advertising often reinforces the idea that pink is a feminine color.*

Develop analysis, not just description
You give many examples, but sometimes you only describe them. Explain why the example matters.

For example, instead of only saying a commercial uses pink, add analysis like:
*This use of color suggests that femininity is linked with softness and delicacy.*
*The product design teaches consumers to connect women with certain colors and behaviors.*

This will make your essay more argumentative and academic.

Avoid overgeneralization
Some sentences sound too absolute, such as “Commercials always show this stereotype” or “Pink is almost always worn by baby girls.” In academic writing, softer claims are usually better.

Use phrases like:
*often*
*frequently*
*in many cases*
*commonly*
*tends to*

This will make your writing sound more accurate and thoughtful.

Language and grammar issues to correct

Capitalization and sentence beginnings
Several sentences begin with lowercase letters.

Incorrect:
*because it is a very soft color*
*on the Secret deodorant commercial*
*once a baby has developed its looks*
*of course*

Correct:
*Because it is a very soft color...*
*In the Secret deodorant commercial...*
*Once a baby has developed its features...*
*Of course...*

Sentence fragments and punctuation
Some sentences are incomplete or joined awkwardly.

Incorrect:
*Why? because it is a very soft color...*

Better:
*Why is pink considered feminine? One reason is that it is seen as a soft color...*

Also, be careful with comma splices and repeated short sentences. Try combining ideas for smoother flow.

Word choice
A few phrases sound unnatural in English.

*pictured as being soft and tender* → *portrayed as soft and tender*
*brought from the hospital* → *brought home from the hospital*
*developed its looks* → *developed clearer physical features*
*get away with it* → *be socially accepted*

Grammar corrections
Examples:
*Men do not usually wear pink because it is stereotypically considered a feminine color.*  
  Good sentence.

*Also commercials for feminine products, such as sanitary napkins, also have...*  
  Better: *Commercials for feminine products, such as sanitary napkins, also use...*

*baby boys when brought from the hospital are usually dressed in blue*  
  Better: *Baby boys, when brought home from the hospital, are usually dressed in blue.*

*males can not wear pink*  
  Better: *males cannot wear pink*

*(jam referring to babies)*  
  This seems to be a typo. Maybe you mean: *(I am referring to babies).*  
  However, in a formal essay, it is better to rewrite the whole sentence.

Style suggestions
Try to use a more formal academic tone. For example, instead of asking the reader questions at the beginning, you could start with a statement:

*Colors often carry social meanings. In many cultures, pink is linked to femininity, while blue is linked to masculinity.*

This sounds stronger and more formal.

Final advice
When revising, focus on:
adding a strong thesis,
organizing paragraphs more clearly,
explaining the significance of your examples,
correcting capitalization, grammar, and punctuation,
using a more formal academic tone.

You have a solid idea and useful examples. With clearer analysis and cleaner language, this essay can become much stronger.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You have a clear position: you strongly support drug legalization and try to show several benefits, such as reduced crime, lower costs, less prison overcrowding, and improved public health. Your essay has energy and conviction, which is important in argumentative writing. To improve it, focus on organization, development of ideas, use of evidence, and sentence-level accuracy.

Main strengths
Clear thesis and position: The reader understands what you believe.
Relevant supporting points: You mention cost, crime, prisons, and health, which are strong areas for an argument.
Ambitious topic sentences: Many body paragraphs begin with a clear claim.

Biggest revision priority: develop your ideas fully
The largest problem is that many paragraphs are incomplete or underdeveloped. Several places include “”, which means important explanation or evidence is missing. Because of this, your argument often feels unfinished.

For revision, use this pattern in each body paragraph:

Claim → Explanation → Evidence → Example → Link back to thesis

For example, in the paragraph about crime:
Claim: Legalizing drugs would reduce crime.
Explanation: If drugs are regulated and cheaper, users may be less likely to steal to afford them.
Evidence: Add research, statistics, or expert opinion.
Example: A country or state where regulation changed criminal patterns.
Link: Explain how this supports legalization.

Right now, some paragraphs only have the claim.

Organization and structure
Your essay would be stronger with a clearer structure.

Suggested structure
Introduction
Background
Thesis statement

Body Paragraph 1: Legalization would reduce black-market profits  
Body Paragraph 2: Legalization could reduce some kinds of crime  
Body Paragraph 3: Legalization would reduce prison overcrowding  
Body Paragraph 4: Legalization would save money and generate tax revenue  
Body Paragraph 5: Legalization and public health

Conclusion
Restate thesis
Summarize key points
End with a strong final thought

Also, avoid repeating the same point in different paragraphs unless you show a new angle.

Use of evidence
You include one citation (“Duke 231”), but most claims are unsupported. In academic argument, strong opinions are not enough. You need:
statistics
expert sources
research studies
examples from real policies

Some claims are too broad or doubtful without evidence, such as:
legalization would reduce alcohol and tobacco use
users would become responsible for their own debts
many prisoners would become “useful members of society”

These ideas may be possible, but you must prove them carefully.

Logic and precision
Some sentences are unclear or logically weak. For example:
“They have come to reality” is not natural English; perhaps you mean “They have come to realize”.
“The drug users would be responsible for their own debts” is unclear. Do you mean users would pay the cost themselves instead of taxpayers paying enforcement costs?
In the health paragraph, you write: “If drugs were to increase by a factor of two or three, the deaths and diseases... would be much lower.” This is confusing because increased drug use would not automatically lower deaths. You need to explain that regulated purity, safer access, and medical supervision could reduce harm.

Be careful not to make claims that seem exaggerated.

Style and tone
Try to make your tone more academic and less emotional. Phrases such as:
“it’s time we stop wasting money”
“face the truth”
“real criminals”

can sound too informal or simplistic. In academic writing, it is better to sound balanced and precise.

For example:
Instead of “face the truth”, write “reconsider current policy in light of available evidence.”
Instead of “real criminals”, write “violent offenders.”

Grammar and language issues
Your meaning is often understandable, but many sentences need correction. Focus on:

Articles and word choice
“on the society of America” → “on American society”
“beneficiary to America” → “beneficial to America”

Sentence structure
Some sentences are fragments or awkward:
“Similarly, when heroin, they become alcohol abusers.”  
  This sentence is incomplete and must be rewritten.

Word forms and collocations
“drug prosecution” may be better as “drug law enforcement” or “drug-related prosecution”
“socially and economically margin” → “social and economic marginalization”

Punctuation and spacing
Remove extra spaces: “it' s” → “it’s”
Avoid commas in the wrong place: “that ,drug prohibition”

Thesis revision
Your thesis could be more formal and specific. For example:

“Although many Americans believe prohibition controls drug abuse, legalizing and regulating drugs could reduce black-market crime, lower incarceration rates, improve public health, and save public money.”

Final advice
When revising, do these three things first:
Fill in every incomplete section.
Add evidence to each body paragraph.
Rewrite unclear sentences for accuracy and academic tone.

You have a strong topic and clear opinion. With fuller development and more careful language, this could become a much more persuasive argumentative essay.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay shows strong personal engagement with the topic. You clearly care about gun violence, and your use of local examples and family experience gives the paper emotional power. Readers can see that this issue matters deeply to you. For revision, focus on making your argument more precise, organized, and academically supported.

Strengths
Clear position: You strongly argue that stricter gun control is needed.
Relevant examples: The school shooting, bank robbery, Mr. Glover’s death, and your uncle’s death all help illustrate the seriousness of gun violence.
Emotional impact: Personal and community-based examples make the essay persuasive on a human level.

Main areas to improve

A. Clarify your thesis
Your thesis is understandable, but it is too broad and somewhat awkwardly phrased:

> “Gun Control needs to implemented because it would mainly reduce crime, save lives and lessen the fears of innocent ones.”

Revise it to be more grammatical and specific. For example:

Possible revision:  
*Gun control should be strengthened in South Carolina because stricter laws can reduce violent crime, protect innocent lives, and increase public safety.*

This gives you three clear points for your body paragraphs.

B. Improve organization
Right now, the essay mostly lists examples one after another. Try organizing each body paragraph around one main reason.

A stronger structure could be:

Introduction + thesis
Body paragraph 1: Gun access contributes to school violence
Body paragraph 2: Gun access increases public crimes such as robbery
Body paragraph 3: Gun violence destroys families and communities
Body paragraph 4: Address opposition and explain why stricter laws are still necessary
Conclusion

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that connects to your thesis.

C. Use evidence more carefully
You include quotations, but some problems reduce their credibility:
Some sources are old, local, or unclear.
“Personal Recollection” and “Interview With Mom” are emotionally important, but in an academic essay they should support—not replace—stronger outside evidence.
The statistics about “hate crimes” do not clearly connect to your argument about general gun crime.

Try to:
Explain each quotation more clearly.
Use statistics directly related to gun violence, firearm deaths, or gun laws.
Make sure every source clearly supports your point.

For example, after quoting a source, explain:
what happened,
why it matters,
how it supports stricter gun control.

D. Avoid overgeneralization
Some claims are too absolute, such as:
“Heinous crimes involving guns are at an all-time high.”
“Anybody can just walk in with a gun and commit a robbery.”
“No one but the policemen and people in the military would be allowed to carry guns.”

These statements may sound emotional rather than logical unless you provide proof. In academic argument, be careful and precise.

For example:
Instead of “all-time high,” say “a serious and growing problem.”
Instead of “anybody,” say “some offenders are able to obtain guns too easily.”

E. Address the opposing side fairly
You mention the NRA, but only briefly. A stronger argumentative essay should show that you understand the other side before refuting it.

For example:
Some people believe gun ownership is a constitutional right.
Others argue that guns are needed for self-defense.

Then explain why you still support stricter control:
background checks,
waiting periods,
limits for minors,
better storage laws.

This will make your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Language and grammar issues

Your meaning is usually clear, but there are many sentence-level errors. Focus on these:

Common problems
Spelling: “goverment” → *government*; “techers” → *teachers*; “memebers” → *members*; “misue” → *misuse*; “acquaintace” → *acquaintance*
Missing words: “needs to implemented” → *needs to be implemented*
Capitalization: “Gun Control” does not need capitals in the middle of a sentence.
Word choice: “innocent ones” is awkward; use *innocent people* or *law-abiding citizens*.
Sentence structure: Some sentences are run-on or repetitive.

Example revision
Original:  
*This crime was an act of nonsense.*

Better:  
*This crime was senseless and could have been avoided.*

Style suggestions
Use a more formal tone. Avoid phrases like “create trouble” or “use their heads instead of the trigger.”
Try not to rely too much on emotional wording alone; combine emotion with logic and evidence.
Integrate quotations smoothly into your own sentences.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong foundation because it is sincere and passionate. To improve it, revise for:
a sharper thesis,
clearer paragraph organization,
stronger academic evidence,
fair treatment of opposing views,
grammar and sentence-level correctness.

If you make these changes, your essay will become much more convincing and effective.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0011.4.txt =====
Here is a revision report to help strengthen your essay.

Overall evaluation

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that capital punishment should not exist in the U.S. judicial system. You also organize your body paragraphs around three main counterarguments—cost, retribution, and deterrence—which is a strong argumentative structure. This gives your essay a clear direction.

However, to make your argument more persuasive in academic writing, you need to improve tone, evidence, grammar, word choice, and sentence clarity. Right now, the essay sounds passionate, but sometimes too emotional or informal for an academic argument.

Strengths

Clear thesis and position: readers know from the introduction that you oppose capital punishment.
Logical organization: each body paragraph addresses one supporting claim by death penalty advocates.
Awareness of counterarguments: this is important in argumentative writing.
Good attempt to use evidence: statistics and studies are mentioned, which is appropriate for this essay type.

Main revision priorities

A. Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases are too emotional, conversational, or sarcastic. For example:

“obscene form of punishment”
“ridiculous”
“Let me throw a couple of statistics at you”
“Correct me if I’m wrong”
“pull their plug”

These expressions weaken your credibility because academic argument usually sounds calm, precise, and evidence-based.

Better approach: replace emotional language with neutral wording.

Example:
“Advocates of this obscene form of punishment…”  
  → “Supporters of capital punishment often argue…”
“these reasons are as ridiculous…”  
  → “these reasons are not supported by evidence”

B. Support claims with specific sources
You mention statistics and studies, but you do not identify where they come from. In academic writing, evidence must be traceable.

For example:
“it costs five times the amount…”
“They have done studies in states…”

Ask yourself:
Who found this?
In which state or country?
When?
What was the source?

Without sources, readers may doubt the accuracy of your evidence. When revising, add specific references such as government reports, academic studies, or legal research.

C. Improve grammar and word choice
There are many language errors that make the essay harder to read. Some common issues:

Confusing word forms / wrong words
“it’s existence” → its existence
“untill” → until
“Ounce society” → Once society
“inprison” → imprison
“recieves” → receives
“midevil” → medieval
“deterent” → deterrent
“murdeers” → murders
“deference” → deterrence
“detramental” → detrimental

Sentence structure problems
Some sentences are too long or awkward. For example:

“How this statement can be valid is due to the reasons that…”
  → This is unclear and unnatural.

Better:
“This is because individuals sentenced to death often spend many years on death row and go through repeated appeals, which increases legal costs.”

Subject-verb agreement / plural errors
“There are a couple of reason’s this has no effects…”
  → “There are a couple of reasons this has no effect…”

D. Make your reasoning more precise
Some arguments are interesting but not fully developed.

For example, in the retribution paragraph, you argue that capital punishment is revenge rather than justice. This is a good point, but you should explain it more logically and carefully. Instead of rhetorical questions, use clear reasoning:

What is the purpose of punishment in a legal system?
How is retribution different from revenge?
Why does the state’s use of killing create a moral contradiction?

This will make your point stronger.

Organization suggestions

Your structure is good, but the introduction and conclusion can be improved.

Introduction
Your thesis should be more direct and formal. For example:

Possible revision:
“Although supporters of capital punishment argue that it reduces costs, provides just retribution, and deters crime, these claims are not supported by evidence. Capital punishment is costly, morally problematic, and ineffective as a deterrent.”

This is clearer and more academic.

Body paragraphs
Try this structure in each paragraph:
Topic sentence
Opposing view
Evidence
Explanation
Link back to thesis

Conclusion
Do not simply repeat earlier points. End by reinforcing the significance of your argument, for example:
what this means for justice,
public policy,
or human rights.

Final advice for revision

When you revise, focus on these steps in order:

Replace informal and emotional language with formal academic language
Correct grammar and spelling
Add reliable sources for statistics and studies
Rewrite unclear sentences for conciseness
Develop analysis instead of using rhetorical questions

You already have a solid argumentative framework. If you make your language more formal and your evidence more specific, this essay will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an important ethical issue and shows clear interest in the debate about euthanasia. You include definitions, background, and examples such as Dr. Kevorkian, which helps readers understand the topic. Your conclusion also shows that you have a position. For revision, focus on making your argument clearer, more consistent, and better supported.

Clarify your thesis

Your introduction ends with a question: whether doctor-assisted suicide should be legalized or whether doctors should follow the Hippocratic Oath. However, your own position is not clearly stated until the final paragraph, and even there it is somewhat mixed.

At the end, you say:
doctors should not decide whether a patient dies,
but physicians may cooperate with a patient’s decision.

This sounds partly against and partly in favor of physician-assisted suicide. Try to write one direct thesis statement in the introduction. For example:

If you are against legalization: “Although terminally ill patients deserve compassionate care, physician-assisted suicide should not be legalized because it conflicts with medical ethics and gives doctors too much power over life and death.”
If you support some form of it: “Physician-assisted suicide should be legal for terminally ill patients under strict conditions because it respects patient autonomy and relieves unbearable suffering.”

Improve organization

Right now, the essay has useful information, but the paragraphs do not always build a logical argument. A stronger structure would be:

Introduction: define euthanasia briefly and present your thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: explain types of euthanasia only if necessary for your argument.
Body Paragraph 2: present the social/religious/legal opposition.
Body Paragraph 3: discuss Kevorkian as an example.
Body Paragraph 4: explain the Hippocratic Oath and medical ethics.
Body Paragraph 5: respond to opposing views.
Conclusion: restate your position clearly.

Also, some parts summarize sources rather than argue. Make sure each paragraph begins with a topic sentence connected to your thesis.

Develop analysis, not just summary

You mention William F. May and Joseph Fletcher, but you mostly report what they said. After each source, explain:

Why is this point important?
How does it support your argument?
Do you agree or disagree with it?

For example, after discussing May’s four forces, add analysis such as: “These social forces show why euthanasia remains controversial, but they do not by themselves prove whether physician-assisted suicide is ethical.”

Fix incomplete quotations and missing information

Several places contain , which makes the essay feel unfinished. These missing quotations are especially important because they seem to be key evidence. Before submitting a revision, make sure to:
include the complete quotations,
cite them correctly if required,
explain them in your own words.

Without these parts, the essay’s credibility is weakened.

Be careful with definitions and accuracy

Some statements are too broad or unclear. For example:
“There are very few legal objections to this form of euthanasia.”
“It is widely practiced in hospitals all over America.”
“This type of euthanasia is occurring more frequently because it is seen as the easiest way out.”

These claims need evidence, and the phrase “easiest way out” sounds judgmental rather than academic. Try using more neutral language:
“Some people view suicide as a way to escape unbearable suffering.”
“Withdrawal of life support is legally accepted in many cases in the United States.”

Also, “euthanasia” and “physician-assisted suicide” are not always exactly the same. If your essay is mainly about doctors helping terminally ill patients end their lives, use the term physician-assisted suicide consistently.

Sentence-level language improvements

Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are grammar and word-choice problems. Some examples:

“Those patient's” → “Those patients”
“would rather die than to continue” → “would rather die than continue”
“Many people leave in their will expressed wishes” → “Many people express in their wills”
“Christains” → “Christians”
“modern societies outlook” → “modern society’s outlook”
“break throughs” → “breakthroughs”

Also watch pronouns such as he, his, and one. Try to be consistent and formal. For example:
“the patient… his mind” → “the patient… their mind” or rewrite in the plural.

Strengthen the conclusion

Your final paragraph has a thoughtful idea, but it needs to connect more clearly to the essay. State your final claim directly and summarize your main reasons. Avoid introducing a new idea too suddenly.

Final advice

For your revision, focus on:
writing a precise thesis,
organizing paragraphs around that thesis,
adding analysis after source material,
completing missing quotations,
revising grammar and word choice.

You have a strong topic and useful material. With clearer argument and more careful editing, this can become a persuasive academic essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: the wild card is good for baseball. You also organize the paper around several reasons: it adds teams, increases excitement, and creates more competition. This gives your essay a solid argumentative structure. You also use many specific examples from the 1995 season, which is a strength because it makes your argument concrete rather than abstract.

What is working well

Clear thesis and purpose  
   In the first paragraph, you state your claim clearly. Readers understand your main argument immediately.

Good use of examples  
   You support your ideas with real teams, players, and playoff situations. This helps show knowledge of the topic and gives the essay credibility.

Logical overall organization  
   The essay moves from explanation of the old/new system to reasons why the wild card is beneficial. This progression generally makes sense.

Main areas to revise

Improve focus: reduce too much background detail
A major problem is that too much of the essay explains the playoff system instead of arguing why the wild card is beneficial. For example, the long quotation about Division Series rules and the detailed explanation of home-field advantage do not strongly support your thesis. They interrupt the argument.

Suggestion:  
Keep only the background your reader needs. Then spend more space analyzing the benefits of the wild card.

Make paragraph topics clearer
Some paragraphs begin with one idea but then move into another. For example, the paragraph about the new system and home-field advantage becomes confusing because the connection to your thesis is weak.

Suggestion:  
Start each body paragraph with a clear topic sentence tied directly to your thesis:
The wild card allows more star players and more cities to appear in the playoffs.
The wild card keeps fans interested longer by creating an additional late-season race.
The wild card increases competition by forcing stronger teams to survive more playoff rounds.

Strengthen analysis, not just description
Sometimes you list facts but do not fully explain why they prove your point. For instance, listing many players is interesting, but the reader needs more explanation of why their presence helps baseball financially, culturally, or competitively.

Suggestion:  
After giving evidence, add 1–2 sentences of analysis:
How does this increase fan interest?
Why does that matter for baseball?
How does this support your claim?

Watch grammar and sentence structure
There are several sentence-level errors that make the essay less smooth. Common issues include missing words, incorrect word forms, punctuation, and awkward phrasing.

Examples:
“the team from each league with the best record the three Division winners”  
  → should be: “the team from each league with the best record among the non-division winners”
“The new playoff system includes eight teams and three rounds.”  
  This is clear and effective.
“The two winners of the division series in each league then meet in second round action.”  
  → better: “The two winners of the Division Series in each league then meet in the League Championship Series.”
“baseball fans why they loved the game”  
  Since your essay is written in present tense argument, consider consistency: “why they love the game.”

Correct wording and mechanics
There are some noticeable language mistakes:
“National Leagued Championship Series” → “National League Championship Series”
“post season” → usually “postseason”
“as made evident” → better: “as shown by”
“Andres Galaragga” → likely “Andrés Galarraga”
Final sentence: “losing popularity to football and baseball” → should probably be “football and basketball”

Also check capitalization consistency for terms like Division Series, League Championship Series, and World Series.

Use sources more smoothly
You include quotations and citations, but sometimes they feel dropped into the essay without enough introduction or explanation.

Suggestion:  
Introduce sources clearly and explain them afterward. Also make citation style consistent.

Suggested revision plan

Shorten the background in paragraphs 1–4.  
Delete or reduce the home-field advantage section unless you can clearly connect it to your thesis.  
Reorganize body paragraphs around your three strongest reasons:
   - more teams/stars/cities
   - more fan excitement at the end of the season
   - more competitive playoffs
Add analysis after each example.
Edit for grammar and word choice sentence by sentence.

Final comment

This essay shows strong knowledge of baseball and a clear argumentative intention. Your biggest revision task is not finding more evidence; it is selecting the best evidence and explaining it more directly. If you simplify the background, sharpen the paragraph focus, and polish the grammar, your argument will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

You have a clear and passionate position: the media often abuses the public’s “right to know” and harms privacy, fairness, and even safety. Your voice is strong, and the essay includes many concrete examples, which helps readers understand what concerns you. The conclusion also clearly returns to your main idea. These are good strengths for an argumentative essay.

Main revision goal: make the argument more focused
Right now, the essay reads more like a series of complaints than a carefully organized argument. You mention several different problems:  
declining newspaper quality  
military security  
invasion of privacy  
sensationalism in trials  
damage to political candidates  
unfair treatment of individuals  

These points are related, but they need a clearer controlling thesis. Try to state your main claim early and make every paragraph support it.

Possible thesis:
*Although the media has an important duty to inform the public, it often crosses ethical boundaries by prioritizing profit and sensationalism over privacy, fairness, and public safety.*

This thesis is stronger because it is balanced and specific.

Improve organization
The essay would be stronger if you grouped ideas by category instead of moving from one example to another.

Suggested structure:
Introduction
Briefly introduce the issue
End with your thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Media and public safety
Grenada
Desert Storm
Bosnia

Body Paragraph 2: Media and personal reputation
Surgeon General candidate
USC English Department example
Colin Powell

Body Paragraph 3: Media sensationalism and profit
O.J. Simpson trial
newspapers/TV making money from scandal

Body Paragraph 4: Privacy and ethical limits
celebrities/public figures
where the line should be between public interest and private life

Conclusion
Restate argument
Offer a reasonable final thought or solution

This structure will help readers follow your logic more easily.

Develop analysis, not only examples
You have many examples, but sometimes you do not fully explain how they prove your point. After each example, add 2–3 sentences of analysis.

For example, after discussing military reporting, explain:
why wartime reporting should have limits,
how journalism can conflict with national security,
what ethical responsibility reporters should have.

This will make the essay more academic and persuasive.

Use a more formal argumentative tone
Your passion is effective, but some sentences sound too conversational or emotional for academic writing.

Examples:
“How dare they endanger lives...”
“Watch out, future warriors...”
“It was all to sell more newspapers...”
“Let it be.”

These expressions are vivid, but in an academic essay, calmer wording is more persuasive.

For example:
Instead of “How dare they endanger lives...”  
write “Such reporting may endanger soldiers and raises serious ethical concerns.”

Also avoid overgeneralizations such as:
“Nobody is perfect.”
“We don’t see any good news anymore.”
“The media’s freedom of speech will be the downfall of personal liberty.”

These are strong claims, but they need evidence or more careful wording.

Address the other side
A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge a counterargument. For example, you could admit that:
the press is essential in a democracy,
the public does need information about government, war, and public officials,
investigative journalism can expose corruption.

Then explain that the problem is not freedom of the press itself, but the misuse of that freedom. This will make your essay more balanced and sophisticated.

Sentence-level language issues
Your meaning is usually clear, but there are grammar, word choice, and punctuation problems. Some likely come from drafting quickly rather than language ability alone.

Common issues:
Word choice / collocation
“take a subscription” → buy/purchase a subscription
“dragging up all kinds of dirt” → digging up scandals/private details
“on this reason” → for this reason
“right to know start to tread on” → right to know begins to infringe on

Grammar
“The media took a perfectly good candidate” → understandable, but more formal: The media undermined a well-qualified candidate
“The past is past” → The past should not automatically define a person’s future
“none of the media’s business” → correct possessive form

Agreement and pronouns
Be careful with pronoun consistency:
“a person… their” is common in modern English, but keep it consistent.

Punctuation and quotation marks
There are several punctuation errors and inconsistent quotation marks/apostrophes. Proofread carefully.

Spelling/typing corrections
There are many small errors that reduce credibility. Examples:

fives → lives
inunune → immune
hwnburgers → hamburgers
ofv → off
medi&s → media’s
gwne → game
dodt → don’t
ad that → all that

Final advice
Your essay has a strong central concern and an authentic voice. For revision, focus on:
writing a clear thesis,
organizing by themes,
explaining each example more fully,
using a more formal tone,
correcting grammar and spelling.

You already have good material. With stronger structure and a more academic style, this could become a convincing argumentative essay.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0013.2.txt =====
Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you believe marijuana should be legalized. This is a good start for an argumentative essay because the reader can easily understand your main claim. You also use personal experience and examples, which make your writing sound engaged and sincere. However, to make the essay more convincing in an academic setting, you need to improve organization, evidence, tone, and language accuracy.

Main strengths
Clear opinion: Your argument is easy to identify from the beginning.
Strong voice: The essay sounds personal and confident.
Relevant topic sentences: Many paragraphs stay connected to marijuana legalization.
Use of examples: You try to support your ideas with experience, media examples, and possible medical uses.

Main problems to revise

A. The essay relies too much on personal stories
Personal experience can help, but in an academic argumentative essay, it should not be the main evidence. For example, stories about your parents, your childhood, or classmates smoking before studying are interesting, but they do not prove that marijuana should be legalized.

What to do instead:  
Use facts, statistics, expert opinions, and research. For example:
medical uses of marijuana
economic benefits of legalization
comparison with countries or states where it is legal
evidence about addiction or health risks

B. Some claims are too broad or unsupported
Several statements are strong but have no proof, such as:
politicians use drugs themselves
government officials keep marijuana illegal for profit
marijuana has nearly no long-term side effects
marijuana is not a drug

In academic writing, unsupported claims make your argument weaker.

Suggestion: If you cannot prove a claim with reliable evidence, either remove it or soften it.  
For example:
Instead of “marijuana is not a drug,” write: “Some people argue that marijuana is less harmful than many illegal drugs because it is derived from a plant and has recognized medical uses.”

C. Organization needs improvement
Right now, the essay feels like a series of thoughts rather than a carefully planned argument.

A stronger structure could be:

Introduction
   - background on marijuana debate
   - thesis statement

Body Paragraph 1: Medical benefits
   - treatment for pain, glaucoma, nausea, etc

Body Paragraph 2: Problems with criminalization
   - arrests, prison costs, unequal enforcement

Body Paragraph 3: Economic benefits of legalization
   - taxation, regulation, reduction of illegal market

Counterargument paragraph
   - acknowledge concerns about abuse or health risks
   - explain why legalization with regulation is better

Conclusion
   - restate thesis
   - summarize main reasons

D. Tone is too informal for an academic essay
Phrases like “weed,” “talking out of his head,” “mega bucks,” and “light up” sound conversational. Academic writing should be more formal.

For example:
“weed” → marijuana or cannabis
“mega bucks” → large profits
“talking out of his head” → speaking without evidence
“light up” → smoke marijuana

Also, avoid directly attacking politicians unless you have reliable proof.

Language corrections

Here are some important grammar and wording issues:

“Although, that is the most political stance…”  
  → “Although this may be the most politically acceptable stance, …”

“The Columbian suppliers”  
  → “The Colombian suppliers”  
  (“Columbian” is incorrect here.)

“in european countries”  
  → “in European countries”  
  (capitalize proper nouns)

“I a conversation”  
  → “I had a conversation”

“used in it's leafy form”  
  → “used in its leafy form”  
  (“its” = possessive; “it’s” = it is)

“their are nearly no long lasting side effects”  
  → “there are almost no long-lasting side effects”

“when their stressed out”  
  → “when they are stressed out”

“class mates”  
  → “classmates”

“study c ession”  
  → probably “study session”

“critisize”  
  → “criticize”

You also need to work on run-on sentences. Many sentences are too long and combine several ideas without correct punctuation. Try using shorter sentences.

Thesis revision
Your current thesis is too simple: “I think it should be legalized.”

A stronger thesis might be:

“Marijuana should be legalized because it has recognized medical benefits, criminalization is ineffective and unfair, and legal regulation would provide greater social and economic benefits than prohibition.”

This thesis is more academic and gives your essay a clear plan.

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:
Create a clear essay structure.
Replace personal opinion with researched evidence.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Correct grammar, punctuation, and spelling.
Add a counterargument to show balance.

You have a strong sense of purpose, and that is valuable. If you combine that strong voice with better evidence and organization, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

You choose a clear and important argument: journalists should protect confidential sources, and shield laws matter. Your essay shows good effort in using examples, legal history, and outside sources. The paper already has a strong position. For revision, focus on thesis, organization, style, and sentence-level accuracy.

Strengths
Clear opinion: You strongly argue that reporters should not be forced to reveal sources.
Use of evidence: You include several cases (Maryland, South Carolina, Watergate, California), which gives the essay credibility.
Awareness of counterargument: You mention that judges sometimes need information, which is useful in argument writing.

Main issue: make the thesis sharper
Right now, your position is repeated many times, but the central thesis is not stated in one precise sentence. Readers need that early.

For example, at the end of the introduction you could write something like:

“Because confidential sources are essential to investigative reporting and public knowledge, shield laws should strongly protect journalists from being forced to reveal their sources except in very limited circumstances.”

This is better because it:
states your claim clearly,
gives a reason,
allows for a nuanced argument (“except in very limited circumstances”).

Improve organization
The essay has many examples, but they sometimes feel like a list. Try this structure:

Introduction
Explain the issue briefly.
Define shield laws.
Present your thesis.

Body Paragraph 1: Why confidentiality matters
Trust between reporter and source
Why anonymous sources help the public learn important information

Body Paragraph 2: Legal background
Maryland’s first shield law
Growth of state shield laws

Body Paragraph 3: Limits and problems
South Carolina case
Explain that protection is uneven and not federal

Body Paragraph 4: Additional examples
Ohio search
California fine
Watergate or Charles Stuart case

Body Paragraph 5: Counterargument and response
Judges may need information for justice
But compelling disclosure harms press freedom and future reporting

Conclusion
Restate argument
Emphasize public interest
End with a strong formal statement

This structure will help your reader follow your logic.

Use evidence more analytically
Often you summarize cases, but you do not always explain why they prove your point. After each example, add 1–2 sentences of analysis.

For instance, after the Twila Decker example, explain:
what this case shows about the weakness of state shield laws,
why this weakness may discourage sources from speaking.

A useful pattern is:
Example → explanation → connection to thesis

Tone and academic style
Your conclusion becomes emotional and informal:
“Protect the Shield Laws.”
“Don’t make people scared to talk.”
“If people feel like a reporter can be trusted, the stories will be much more interesting.”

These sound persuasive, but not fully academic. Use a more formal tone:
“Shield laws should be strengthened to preserve public access to important information.”
“Without confidentiality, valuable sources may refuse to speak.”
“As a result, the public may lose access to significant investigative reporting.”

Also avoid statements that are too absolute, such as:
“A journalist's job is to get the story no matter what.”
This can sound ethically problematic. Instead:
“A journalist’s role is to gather and report information in the public interest.”

Sentence-level issues
There are many grammar, punctuation, and formatting problems. Main patterns:

A. Comma errors
“Keeping the sources secret, especially in a courtroom setting has always been…”
  - Add comma: “setting, has” is wrong; actually better: “Keeping sources secret, especially in a courtroom setting, has always been…”

B. Double punctuation / spacing
“example,,”
“Swasy,, was”
extra spaces before/after punctuation

Clean these carefully.

C. Word form / grammar
“the way other states handle cases… is varied greatly”
  - Better: “varies greatly” or “differs greatly”
“The shield law does not, however provide”
  - Better: “The shield law does not, however, provide…”

D. Capitalization and quotation errors
“deep Throat” → “Deep Throat”
Some quotation marks are missing or incorrect.
Some symbols appear corrupted (�). Fix all formatting.

E. Pronoun style
Repeated “he/she” sounds awkward. In modern academic English, use:
“they” / “their” for a singular person
or
plural nouns: “reporters… they…”

Citation and integration
You use sources, which is good, but quotations need smoother integration. Introduce them clearly and explain them. Also keep citation style consistent. For example:

According to Henry, “…” (56).

Do not insert long legal quotations unless necessary. The Maryland law quotation is too long. You can summarize most of it and quote only the key phrase.

Priority revision plan
Write a stronger thesis.
Reorganize by topic, not just by example.
Add analysis after each case.
Make tone more formal and less emotional.
Edit grammar, punctuation, and formatting carefully.

You have a solid foundation and strong supporting material. With clearer structure and more polished language, this can become a persuasive academic argument.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position: women should be allowed to attend The Citadel. Your essay also shows strong personal engagement, especially when you discuss equality and public education. The main idea is understandable, and you give several reasons to support it. For revision, focus on three areas: organization, development of arguments, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear thesis and stance: Your essay consistently argues that excluding women is unfair.
Relevant reasons: You discuss tradition, physical ability, equal access to public education, and the idea that coeducation does not cause distraction.
Strong voice: Sentences such as “Everyone does deserve the chance to try” are persuasive and sincere.

Organization and structure
Your essay has good points, but they need a clearer structure. Right now, some ideas repeat, and the opening is not fully clear.

Introduction
Your introduction should:
explain the issue,
give background,
state your thesis clearly.

For example, instead of starting with several grammar errors, you could write something like:

> In recent years, courts have debated whether women should be allowed to attend The Citadel. Because The Citadel is a publicly funded school, it should not exclude women. Tradition, concerns about physical strength, and fears of distraction are not strong enough reasons to deny women equal access.

This gives readers a roadmap.

Body paragraphs
Try to organize each paragraph around one main reason:
Paragraph 1: Tradition is not a sufficient reason.
Paragraph 2: Women are capable, and individuals should be judged by ability.
Paragraph 3: Public funding means equal access.
Paragraph 4: Coeducation does not create serious distraction.

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence and then include explanation or examples.

Conclusion
Your essay currently ends suddenly. Add a short conclusion that restates your main argument and leaves the reader with a final thought.

Development of ideas
Some of your points are good but need more careful explanation.

A. Tradition
Your point is strong, but the example “you would still have black slaves” is very extreme. It may distract readers. Instead, explain more calmly that many social traditions have changed when they became unfair.

B. Physical ability
This is one of your better arguments. You rightly say not all women can handle The Citadel, but not all men can either. This is a strong logic point. Develop it further:
admission should be based on standards,
anyone who meets the standards should be allowed in.

C. Public funding
This is an important argument and should be explained more precisely. You argue that public schools should not discriminate. Good. However, the paragraph about schools being separated by ethnicity becomes exaggerated and less connected to your main point. Stay focused on The Citadel and equal protection/fair access.

D. Distraction argument
Your comparison to coeducational colleges is effective. To strengthen it, explain that students must learn to study and work with both men and women in society.

Language issues to revise
Your essay has many grammar and spelling errors. These do not hide your meaning, but they weaken your credibility. Focus especially on:

Subject-verb agreement
“there has been one question” → “there has been one question” is okay, but later:
“That questions is” → That question is
“There has been many objections” → There have been many objections

Word form / wrong word
“as a women” → as a woman
“less superior” → inferior or less worthy
“publically funded” → publicly funded
“excellence discipline” → excellent discipline

Spelling
Examples:
appearred → appeared
alao → also
desciniinate → discriminate
pubfically → publicly
poliish → Polish
shouln't → shouldn’t
eqaul → equal
learening → learning

Sentence boundaries and punctuation
Some sentences are too long or joined incorrectly. For example:

> “That may be true in some cases, but others might be able to handle the challenges, Everyone does deserve the chance to try.”

This should be:
> “That may be true in some cases, but some women might be able to handle the challenges. Everyone deserves the chance to try.”

Style suggestions
Avoid phrases like “and so on,” which sound informal and vague.
Be careful with emotional statements; balance them with logic.
Capitalize proper nouns: The Citadel.

Revision priority
Write a clearer introduction with a thesis.
Separate body paragraphs by main reason.
Add a conclusion.
Correct grammar and spelling carefully.
Support each point with clearer explanation.

You already have a solid argument. With better organization and sentence-level editing, this essay could become much more persuasive and polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: a stay-at-home wife should be respected as an equal partner in marriage and should share ownership of family property. This is a strong, arguable thesis, and your writing shows genuine engagement with the topic. You also try to support your argument with examples from media, family life, and social attitudes, which is a good strategy for argumentative writing.

What is working well

Clear central argument  
Your main claim is easy to understand. From the introduction, readers know that you oppose the idea that a wife who stays home is inferior.

Strong sense of purpose  
Your essay consistently returns to the ideas of equality, respect, and shared ownership. This gives the paper unity.

Use of examples  
You include several kinds of evidence:
a TV example,
your mother’s daily routine,
public attitudes toward stay-at-home wives,
legal/financial concerns about property ownership.

This variety helps show that you are thinking broadly about the issue.

Main areas to revise

Narrow and modernize the thesis
Right now, the essay focuses only on “the wife” staying home and “the husband” earning money. This makes the argument sound less balanced and sometimes too general. Since your main point is about equality in marriage, try to present it more precisely.

For example, you could revise your thesis to something like:

“In a marriage where one spouse stays home to manage the household and children, that partner should be treated as an equal because domestic labor is valuable, the arrangement is usually a shared decision, and both partners contribute to the family’s well-being.”

This version is more formal and avoids repeating “wife” too often.

Improve organization
The essay has many good ideas, but some paragraphs repeat the same point or move suddenly to a new idea. Try using a clearer structure:

Introduction
background
thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Domestic labor has real value  
Body Paragraph 2: Staying home is often a mutual decision  
Body Paragraph 3: Equal partnership should include shared ownership/legal protection  
Body Paragraph 4: Society unfairly judges stay-at-home wives  
Conclusion: Restate argument and significance

This will make your reasoning easier to follow.

Strengthen evidence
Some examples are effective, but others are weak or incomplete.

The television example includes “”, so readers cannot understand the conversation. Replace it or summarize it clearly.
The argument about paying a housekeeper $4.25 per hour feels outdated and too specific. Instead, explain more generally that domestic labor would cost money if outsourced.
Your example about your mother is vivid, but it partly discusses a working mother, not a stay-at-home wife. You should connect it more clearly to your argument: if this labor is exhausting even after paid work, then full-time domestic work also deserves respect.

Use a more academic tone
Some phrases sound too conversational or judgmental for an academic essay. For example:
“watch the soaps”
“If the guy didn’t like the idea...”
“pay more attention to business of their own”

Try more formal alternatives:
“watch television”
“If the husband disagreed with that arrangement...”
“should avoid judging private family decisions”

Also, avoid quotation marks when they are not necessary. For example, writing “his” possessions is effective once, but repeating quotation marks too often weakens the style.

Language and grammar points

Here are common patterns to revise:

Compound adjectives
“stay home wife” → stay-at-home wife

Word choice
“can not” → cannot
“loose the house” → lose the house
“no where” → nowhere
“over heard” → overheard
“fiance” → fiancé (if male) / fiancée (if female)

Sentence structure
Some sentences are long and run-on. Break them into shorter sentences.

Original:  
“The husband then went on to say that he viewed the car as his because he took care of it, She then took the argument in a different direction.”

Better:  
“The husband then argued that the car was his because he maintained it. The wife responded by taking the argument in a different direction.”

Parallel structure
“washed, dried, and put away” is good parallel structure. Try to use this consistently.

Subject-verb and verb form
“before started another working day again” → before starting another workday again
“some type arrangements” → some type of arrangement

Final advice
Your essay has a solid core argument and clear conviction. For revision, focus on:
writing a sharper thesis,
organizing body paragraphs more logically,
replacing weak examples with clearer evidence,
making the tone more formal,
carefully editing grammar and spelling.

You have strong ideas; now the main task is to present them in a more organized and academic way.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0014.4.txt =====
Feedback Report

You have chosen an interesting and important topic, and your essay shows clear engagement with the debate about marijuana legalization. You also try to include history, health effects, and social consequences, which gives your essay a broad scope. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Clarify your purpose and thesis
Your introduction says the passage is not trying to convince the reader, but your conclusion clearly argues that legalization is the “only logical answer.” This creates a contradiction.

If this is an argumentative essay, you should state your position clearly in the introduction.
Example of a clearer thesis:  
  “Marijuana should be legalized because its medical benefits are recognized, its risks are lower than those of alcohol and tobacco, and legalization could reduce crime and increase tax revenue.”

This will make your essay more focused and persuasive.

Improve organization
Right now, the essay moves from history to health to crime, but some paragraphs are not well connected, and some ideas appear suddenly.

A stronger structure could be:

Introduction + thesis
Historical and legal background
Medical and physical effects
Comparison with alcohol and tobacco
Social and economic benefits of legalization
Counterargument + response
Conclusion

Also, begin each body paragraph with a clear topic sentence. For example:
“One important reason to legalize marijuana is its medical value.”
“Another argument for legalization is that prohibition contributes to crime.”

Use more reliable and precise evidence
You include many claims, but some are too broad, outdated, or unsupported. For example:
“NO ONE has ever died from marijuana”
“Many people are killed every day because of drug transactions”
“Most of what people smoke nowadays is not just pure Marijuana”

These are very strong statements, so they need trustworthy sources. In academic writing, avoid unsupported generalizations. Also, some facts may be inaccurate or need updating.

Suggestions:
Use recent academic or government sources.
Give exact source information when quoting.
Explain how each piece of evidence supports your argument.

For example, instead of saying “any doctor will inform you,” write:
“Studies have shown that marijuana has lower overdose risk than alcohol and tobacco, although it still has health risks.”

This sounds more academic and careful.

Address counterarguments fairly
A strong argumentative essay should consider the other side. At the moment, your essay mostly supports legalization and does not seriously discuss objections.

You could include concerns such as:
impaired driving
addiction or dependency
effects on adolescent brain development
public health regulation

Then respond to them:
“Although marijuana use may create health and safety concerns, legalization would allow the government to regulate quality, age limits, and distribution more effectively than prohibition.”

This will make your argument more balanced and credible.

Strengthen academic tone
Some phrases are too informal or conversational:
“even the president admits to smoking it”
“look at what generation is office now”
“ask yourself”

Try to use a more formal tone. For example:
“Public attitudes have become more accepting in recent decades.”
“This issue can also be compared to alcohol prohibition in the 1920s.”

Also, avoid writing directly to the reader too often. Academic essays usually focus more on analysis than conversation.

Grammar and sentence issues
There are many sentence structure, punctuation, capitalization, and word choice errors. Some common patterns:

A. Capitalization
Do not capitalize marijuana in the middle of a sentence unless it begins the sentence.

B. Word choice and spelling
Some errors:
govermnent / goverwnent → government
suffers → sufferers or patients
Pgychology → Psychology
reacted to smoking → related to smoking
elevate much of the crime → eliminate or reduce much of the crime
is office now → is in office now
eanount → amount

C. Possessive/determiner errors
it’s purpose → its purpose

D. Sentence fragments and comma problems
Example:
“The first thing to look at when deciding about a stance on Marijuana, the history of Marijuana in the United States.”
This is not a complete sentence.

Possible revision:
“The first thing to consider when deciding one’s stance on marijuana is its history in the United States.”

E. Parallel structure
Example:
“Some short term effects of smoking Marijuana include, the ‘high’, and increased heart rate, and some loss of coordination.”
Better:
“Some short-term effects of smoking marijuana include a feeling of euphoria, an increased heart rate, and loss of coordination.”

Conclusion
Your conclusion is clear, but it is too absolute: “there can only be one logical answer.” In academic argument, it is better to sound confident but not extreme.

For example:
“Overall, the evidence suggests that legalization would offer more benefits than continued prohibition, provided that marijuana is carefully regulated.”

Final advice
Your essay has a clear opinion and several strong ideas. For revision, focus on:
writing a clear argumentative thesis,
organizing ideas into logical paragraphs,
using accurate and reliable evidence,
addressing counterarguments,
correcting grammar and style issues.

You have a good foundation; with clearer structure and more precise academic writing, this essay can become much stronger.

“now what kind of idea was that?”
“It is not fair!”

These phrases sound emotional rather than analytical. In academic writing, try to explain ideas calmly and precisely.

For example, instead of:
> Things were fine and dandy until women started getting a little “uppity” in their thinking.

Write:
> Traditional gender roles began to be challenged when women demanded equal rights and greater independence.

This sounds more formal and persuasive.

Use of evidence
You include some useful examples, but many claims need stronger support. For example, when discussing:
the Bible,
media influence,
children’s stories,
social expectations,

you should explain how these examples support your argument. Also, if possible, use more reliable evidence such as:
historical facts,
academic sources,
statistics,
studies about gender roles or media representation.

You do include one source (“Everywoman’s Bill of Rights”), which is good. However, the quotation should be introduced more smoothly and connected more clearly to your argument.

Language and grammar
There are many grammar and word-choice problems that sometimes make the meaning unclear. Common issues include:

a. Missing words
“when were strictly in the home” → “when women were strictly in the home”
“Men felt that belonged in the home” → “Men felt that women belonged in the home”

b. Wrong word forms/spelling
“warm” → “women”
“particulary” → “particularly”
“appropiate” → “appropriate”
“glamourous” → “glamorous”
“intellingence” → “intelligence”
“priorties” → “priorities”

c. Subject-verb agreement
“Another thing about women in present society are…”  
  → “Another thing about women in present society is…”

“Setting double standards in a society are like…”  
  → “Setting double standards in a society is like…”

d. Sentence structure / run-on sentences
Example:
> Women were getting restless, they no longer wanted to be only in the home...

This should be revised:
> Women were becoming restless. They no longer wanted to remain only in the home.

e. Pronoun/reference problems
Be careful with words like he, they, this, that so the reader always knows what they refer to.

Stronger conclusion
Your conclusion repeats your feelings, which is good, but it could be more effective if it returned to the main argument in a more formal way.

Possible direction:
> In conclusion, although feminism has expanded women’s opportunities, double standards continue to shape how women are judged in the family, in the media, and in everyday life. A fair society should evaluate people as individuals rather than according to gender stereotypes.

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs by topic.
Remove informal/sarcastic language.
Fix grammar and spelling carefully.
Add clearer evidence and explanation.

You have a passionate argument and many relevant ideas. With better structure and more formal language, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position against book censorship and shows strong personal engagement with the topic. You use examples, legal cases, and references to authors to support your argument, which gives your essay energy and seriousness. Your main task in revision is to make the essay clearer, more organized, and more grammatically accurate so your ideas can have stronger impact.

Strengths

Clear opinion: Your position is consistent: censorship harms education and freedom.
Relevant examples: You mention court cases, organizations, and specific authors, which helps create an argumentative essay rather than only a personal opinion piece.
Passion and voice: The essay has emotion and conviction, especially in the conclusion.

Main areas to revise

A. Improve the thesis and focus
Your introduction asks several rhetorical questions, but the thesis is not stated directly enough. A stronger thesis should clearly say what you believe and why.

For example:
> School boards, parents, and censorship groups should not have the power to ban books because such actions restrict intellectual freedom, weaken education, and violate the spirit of the First Amendment.

This gives the reader a clear roadmap.

B. Organize the body paragraphs more logically
Right now, some paragraphs feel disconnected. Try this structure:

Introduction + thesis
Historical/background context of censorship
School censorship example 1
School censorship example 2
Public library censorship example
Conclusion

At the start of each body paragraph, write a topic sentence that explains the paragraph’s main idea.

Example:
> Censorship in schools is especially damaging because it limits what students are allowed to learn.

C. Use evidence more carefully
You include useful evidence, but sometimes it is not fully explained. After each example, tell the reader why it matters.

For instance, after the *Ms.* magazine example, explain how the court decision shows that censorship often comes from discomfort rather than a valid educational reason.

Also, check whether facts, names, and citations are accurate:
“People for the American Way” (not “People of the American Way”)
“freedom of speech” (not “freedom ofspeech”)
“curriculum” (not “curriculwn”)

D. Adopt a more formal academic tone
Some sentences are too conversational for an academic essay.

Examples:
“Did you know that…” → too informal
“they go as far as to bum them” → should be “they go so far as to burn them”
“try to stop the world for turning” → should be “try to stop the world from turning”

Try to avoid dramatic questions and use direct statements instead.

Language issues to correct

A. Sentence structure and grammar
There are many grammar problems that make the essay hard to follow. Focus especially on:

Subject-verb agreement  
  “The censorship of literary works range” → “The censorship of literary works ranges”

Articles and prepositions  
  “in a the case” → “in the case”  
  “under an previous agreement” → “under a previous agreement”

Verb forms  
  “was unable to provided” → “was unable to provide”

Plural/singular nouns  
  “student's education” → “students’ education” or “student education”

B. Word choice
Some words are incorrect or unnatural:
“openingly” → “openly”
“high students” → “high school students”
“baimed text” → “banned text”
“causal walk” → “casual walk”
“dishonesty” → “injustice” or “disservice”
“restrain” → “restraint”

C. Punctuation and formatting
There are many punctuation problems:
Extra commas: “In New Hampshire,,”
Incorrect apostrophes: “authors rights” → “authors’ rights”
Quotation marks and titles need consistency.
Avoid symbols like “<?>” in a final draft.

Problems likely influenced by Chinese language patterns
As a Chinese speaker, you may naturally write long sentences connected by ideas rather than by clear English grammar. In English academic writing, readers expect:
one clear main idea per sentence,
clear connectors,
explicit subjects,
careful tense control.

So when revising, shorten long sentences and check whether each sentence has a clear subject, verb, and object.

Suggested revision priorities

Write a clear thesis in the introduction.

Explain your evidence more fully.

Revised:
> In conclusion, school boards, some parents, and religious groups often attempt to restrict social change by banning books they consider offensive.

This version is clearer, more formal, and grammatically correct.

Overall, your essay has a strong argument and good potential. With better organization and careful editing, it can become much more persuasive and academically effective.
