Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You chose an important and complex topic, and your essay shows effort in using historical sources, quotations, polls, and different viewpoints. This is a strong starting point for an argumentative essay. To improve the paper, focus on clarity of argument, organization, use of evidence, and language accuracy.

Main argument: make your position clear
Your essay discusses many opinions about the Confederate flag, but your own argument is not clear until the conclusion. Even there, the claim is still vague.

For example, your conclusion says:
“there is no reason to fight over something that was established so many years ago”
“If we let these people have their way it will only lead to bigger controversy”

This suggests you support keeping the flag, but you do not clearly state why. A stronger thesis should appear at the end of the introduction and give your main reasons.

Example of a clearer thesis:
> Although many people view the Confederate flag as part of Southern heritage, it should not be displayed on government property because its historical meaning is closely tied to slavery and racial inequality.

Or, if you want to defend the flag:
> Although the Confederate flag is offensive to many Americans, removing it from all public spaces ignores its role in Southern heritage and risks simplifying a complicated history.

Choose one clear position and build the whole essay around it.

Organization: create a logical structure
Right now, the essay feels like a series of quotations and survey results. Try organizing it like this:

Introduction  
   - Brief background  
   - Clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Heritage argument  
   - Explain why some Southerners support the flag  
   - Use 1–2 quotations only  
   - Analyze them

Body Paragraph 2: Racism/slavery argument  
   - Show why others see the flag as racist  
   - Use the Alexander Stephens quotation as strong historical evidence  
   - Explain its significance

Body Paragraph 3: Public opinion / current controversy  
   - Use polling data carefully  
   - Explain what the data shows

Conclusion  
   - Restate your position  
   - Explain why your argument matters today

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that connects to your thesis.

Use evidence more effectively
You include many quotations, which shows research, but often they are inserted without explanation. In academic writing, do not let quotations speak for themselves. After each quote, explain:
Who is speaking?
Why is this evidence important?
How does it support your argument?

For example, the Alexander Stephens quotation is your strongest piece of evidence because it directly links the Confederacy to white supremacy. After quoting it, explain that this speech shows the Confederate government openly defended racial inequality, so the flag cannot be understood only as “heritage.”

Also, some evidence seems contradictory or unclear, especially the polling paragraph. Make sure the numbers are correct and fully explained. If a statistic seems surprising, you should comment on it, not just report it.

Tone and fairness
An argumentative essay should sound reasoned and academic. Some phrases are too emotional or too general, such as:
“there is no reason to fight”
“these people”
“it will only lead to bigger controversy”

Try to sound more precise and respectful. Acknowledge the other side fairly, then respond with evidence.

Language issues to revise
Your essay has many grammar, capitalization, spelling, and punctuation errors. These make the argument harder to follow. Pay special attention to:

Capitalization
Correct proper nouns and official names:
United States
United Daughters of the Confederacy
Confederate flag
State House
African Americans

Spelling/word form
Some examples:
grand father → grandfather
fory → forty
siad → said
todl → told
unacceptabe → unacceptable
an the Capital grounds → on the Capitol grounds

Sentence structure
Some sentences are incomplete or run on too long. For example:
> “In defense of the flag, Senator Campbell said…”

This should be followed by analysis in a complete sentence. Also check quotation punctuation carefully.

Word choice
Sometimes your wording is unnatural in English:
“still showing today” → still displayed today
“our ancestors lives” → our ancestors’ lives
“to what they thought were their rights” → for what they believed were their rights

Citation and formatting
Your citations are inconsistent and sometimes incomplete, for example:
missing closing parenthesis
inconsistent spacing
unclear source names

Use one citation style consistently if your teacher requires it.

Final advice
Your revision should focus on quality, not quantity. You do not need so many quotations. Use fewer sources, but explain them better. The key question is: What exactly are you trying to persuade the reader to believe?

Once your thesis is clear, choose only the evidence that supports it directly.

If you revise carefully, this essay can become much stronger because you already have useful material and an interesting topic.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that frivolous lawsuits waste public money and that lawmakers should reform the civil court system. This is a strong topic for an argumentative essay because it is focused, practical, and debatable. You also include specific examples, which helps make your argument vivid. For revision, you should work mainly on organization, academic tone, evidence, and language accuracy.

Strengths

Clear opinion: Your main claim is easy to understand.
Relevant examples: You mention prison lawsuits, fake accidents, workers’ compensation, and school discipline. These examples show you are thinking broadly about consequences.
Sense of urgency: The essay communicates that this issue matters to taxpayers and the legal system.

Main revision priorities

A. Make the thesis more precise
Right now, your main idea appears in several places, but it should be stated clearly in one sentence near the end of the introduction.

For example:
“Because frivolous civil lawsuits waste taxpayer money, increase insurance costs, and delay legitimate cases, lawmakers should pass reforms to limit abuse of the court system.”

This thesis is stronger because it gives three reasons that can organize your body paragraphs.

B. Improve organization
At the moment, the essay reads more like a list of complaints than a carefully structured argument. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Background on rising legal costs
   - Clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Cost to taxpayers
   - Court buildings, judges, staff, jurors
   - State costs defending weak lawsuits

Body Paragraph 2: Economic effects
   - Insurance fraud
   - Higher insurance premiums
   - Effects on wages and medical costs

Body Paragraph 3: Harm to justice system
   - Delays for legitimate civil cases
   - Impact on criminal courts and jail overcrowding

Body Paragraph 4: Solutions
   - Faster dismissal of frivolous suits
   - Financial penalties
   - Better fraud investigation
   - Professional punishment for scams

Conclusion
   - Restate thesis
   - Emphasize need for balanced reform

Use a topic sentence at the beginning of each paragraph so the reader can follow your logic.

Use a more academic tone
Some sentences sound conversational or emotional, which weakens your credibility in academic writing.

Examples:
“Seems like everybody is suing everybody these days.”
“begging for people to call them”
“ridiculous cases”

These phrases sound informal and subjective. Replace them with more neutral language:
“There appears to be a growing number of lawsuits.”
“Many attorneys advertise aggressively for personal injury cases.”
“some claims may lack legal merit.”

Also be careful with absolute statements such as “everybody,” “just about everything,” or “all of these lawsuits.” These are too broad and may sound exaggerated.

Support your claims with evidence
This is the most important content issue. Many claims may be true, but they need evidence. For example:
“It costs millions of tax dollars…”
“Hundreds of lawsuits are filed by inmates…”
“It now takes from three to five years for a civil case to be tried.”

In an academic essay, you should provide:
statistics
court data
government reports
news sources
expert commentary

Without evidence, the essay sounds based mainly on opinion or anecdote. Also, some examples (such as the underwear case or ice cream flavor case) may sound sensational unless you document them carefully.

Address the other side
A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge counterarguments. For example, you could admit:
Some lawsuits that seem minor may protect constitutional rights.
Civil lawsuits can help injured people receive justice.
Judges already have some power to dismiss weak claims.

Then explain why reform is still needed. This makes your argument more balanced and persuasive.

Language and grammar issues
There are many spelling and typing errors, such as:
“govermnent” → government
“taxpayers dollars” → taxpayers’ dollars
“corporation of lawyers” → cooperation of lawyers
“then to challenge it” → than to challenge it
“payoff” → pay off or payout
“worker's compensation” → often workers’ compensation
“ffivolous” → frivolous

You also need to check sentence boundaries. For example:
“Are all of these lawsuits necessary? Seems like everybody is suing everybody these days.”
Better:
“Are all of these lawsuits necessary? It seems that many people now turn to litigation too quickly.”

Because you are a Chinese L1 writer, pay special attention to:
articles: a, an, the
plural forms: lawsuit/lawsuits, cost/costs
verb forms: “have become,” “are filed”
possessives: taxpayers’ dollars, workers’ compensation

Suggested revision strategy
Write a new thesis.
Group your ideas into 3–4 body paragraphs by reason.
Remove informal or emotional wording.
Add evidence for each major claim.
Include one counterargument.
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and punctuation.

Final comment
You have the basis for a persuasive essay, but to make it effective in an academic setting, you need to move from strong personal opinion to structured, evidence-based argument. If you revise for organization, tone, and support, your essay will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear opinion and a strong sense of urgency. You argue that partisan conflict caused the 1995 government shutdown and harmed ordinary Americans. This is a good foundation for an argumentative essay because you have a debatable claim, historical context, and specific examples. For revision, focus on clarity, organization, accuracy, and sentence-level correctness.

Main strengths
Clear position: You consistently argue that political parties and unwillingness to compromise damage government.
Concrete example: The 1995 shutdown gives your essay a real case to analyze.
Awareness of audience: You try to show why the issue matters to ordinary Americans, not just politicians.

Thesis and argument
Your central claim is understandable, but it needs to be stated more precisely. Right now, the essay sometimes sounds like it argues:
there should be no political parties at all, and
the real problem is that politicians refuse to compromise.

These are related but not exactly the same. Decide which claim you want to defend.

A possible revised thesis:
The 1995 government shutdown shows that intense partisanship and refusal to compromise can damage the American government and hurt ordinary citizens.

This thesis is easier to support than the broader claim that political parties themselves should not exist.

Organization
The essay would be stronger if it followed a clearer structure:

Introduction
Introduce Jefferson’s idea
Explain the 1995 shutdown
State your thesis

Body paragraph 1: Background
Explain fiscal year, budget process, and why the shutdown happened

Body paragraph 2: Causes
Describe the conflict between Congress and President Clinton
Show how partisan disagreement stopped action

Body paragraph 3: Effects
Explain consequences for workers, services, and the economy

Conclusion
Restate argument
Explain why compromise is necessary

Right now, some information is repeated or placed in confusing order. For example, the emergency spending bill and shutdown timeline could be explained more smoothly in one section.

Use of evidence
You include dates, numbers, and examples, which is excellent. However, some facts need checking or clearer explanation. For instance:
“October 1- 1962 was the first time the United States government shutdown.” This should be verified.
“This could cause a depression” is too dramatic unless you provide evidence.
“ending with the president of the United States” is unclear and probably inaccurate.

Try to distinguish between fact and opinion. Phrases like “I feel” appear many times. In academic argument, it is usually stronger to state the claim directly:
Instead of: I feel the American people elect representatives...
Write: Americans elect representatives to prevent this kind of political failure.

Style and tone
Your tone is passionate, but some expressions are too informal for academic writing:
“like two kids fighting on a playground”
“So who’s right?”
“Well...”

These phrases sound conversational. Try a more formal style:
Both sides behaved stubbornly and prioritized political advantage over compromise.

Also, avoid emotionally loaded words like “tragic” unless you explain them carefully.

Grammar and sentence issues
There are many sentence fragments, punctuation problems, and capitalization errors. These make your ideas harder to follow.

Examples:
“This statement that was made by Thomas Jefferson...”
  - Correct: This statement was made by Thomas Jefferson...
“This event is the shutdown of the United States government and it will not end until government officials. decide...”
  - Remove the period after officials.
“The power of the United States Should be equally divided.”
  - should should not be capitalized.
“When the constitution was written, it designed...”
  - Better: When the Constitution was written, it established...
“How does this effect the ordinary person”
  - Use affect (verb), not effect (noun).
“this situations”
  - Should be this situation or these situations.

You also need to watch:
articles: the president’s cabinet
plurals: points of view
verb forms: the government began to shut down
spelling: government, fighting, Thurmond, nonessential

Quotation and attribution
Your opening quotation is interesting, but the sentence after it is incomplete:
“This statement that was made by Thomas Jefferson...” is a fragment.

Also, make sure the quotation is accurate and properly introduced. For example:
Thomas Jefferson argued that partisanship could divide representatives and weaken government.

If you cannot verify the exact wording, it is safer to paraphrase.

Final advice for revision
When you revise, focus on these priorities:
Write a sharper thesis
Reorganize the essay into clear paragraphs
Use formal academic tone
Correct grammar, punctuation, and capitalization
Check factual accuracy

You have a strong topic and a clear concern about the effects of political conflict. With better organization and more careful editing, this essay could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that abortion should remain a legal choice. This is a good start for an argumentative essay because your opinion is easy to identify. You also use several different reasons, such as rape, poverty, personal freedom, illegal abortions, and adoption costs. These are relevant ideas. For revision, the main areas to improve are thesis clarity, organization, evidence, logic, and language accuracy.

Strengthen your thesis
Your introduction gives background, but the sentence “Hapeo povertyg personal choice and illegal abortions are examples why people should maintain their right to decide” is unclear because of language errors. Your thesis should state your claim and main reasons more directly.

For example:
“Abortion should remain legal because women have a right to bodily autonomy, banning abortion can harm victims of rape and women in poverty, and illegal abortions create serious health risks.”

This version is clearer and prepares the reader for the body paragraphs.

Improve organization
Right now, your essay reads more like a list of points than a carefully developed argument. Try this structure:

Introduction: background + thesis  
Body Paragraph 1: bodily autonomy/personal choice  
Body Paragraph 2: rape and trauma  
Body Paragraph 3: dangers of illegal abortion  
Body Paragraph 4: poverty/adoption/social consequences  
Conclusion: restate argument and explain why it matters

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence. Then explain the point, give evidence, and show how it supports your thesis.

Use stronger evidence
Many claims in your essay are serious, but they need evidence from reliable sources. For example:

“One in four women experience rape or attempted rape…”
“Lower income families also tend to have five children on average…”
“The average cost to support a child is one hundred twenty-five thousand dollars…”

If you include statistics, you should cite where they come from. Also, some numbers seem too broad or possibly inaccurate, so check them carefully. In academic writing, unsupported statistics can weaken your argument.

Be careful with logic and tone
Some arguments need more careful explanation. For example:

“By offering abortion to the lower income people you are helping to maintain a balance…”  
  This sounds problematic because it suggests controlling the number of poor people. That can seem unfair or even discriminatory. A stronger argument would be:  
  “Women in poverty may face greater financial and social burdens from an unwanted pregnancy, so access to abortion can protect their health, education, and economic stability.”

“Would be able to love him or her?”  
  This is emotional, but in an academic essay you should avoid assuming that all women in this situation feel the same. Instead, focus on trauma and the need for personal choice.

“if they remove this right who is to say they will not try to remove other rights”  
  This is a slippery-slope argument. It may be more convincing if you explain specifically why reproductive rights are connected to individual liberty.

Revise sentence-level grammar
There are many grammar and word-choice problems that make some parts difficult to understand. Here are a few examples:

“today�s society” → “today’s society”
“Many people believe it should be illegal. Many others…”  
  Good meaning, but you can combine them for better flow.
“being force to give birth” → “being forced to give birth”
“life time” → “lifetime”
“they won this right in the case of Rowe versus Way” → “women gained this right in the case of *Roe v. Wade*”
“If the remove this right” → “If they remove this right”
“sky racket alsop” → “skyrocket also”
“eightteen” → “eighteen”
“adoption homes” → better: “the foster care system” or “adoptive care” depending on what you mean

Also, remember:
Capitalize the first word of every sentence: “they” → “They”
Use punctuation carefully, especially after introductory phrases and between independent clauses.
Avoid sentence fragments such as “Would be able to love him or her?” This should be “Would you be able to love him or her?”

Make your conclusion stronger
Your essay ends with new claims about tax money and infant mortality, but these ideas are not fully developed. A conclusion should not introduce major new arguments. Instead, briefly restate your main points and end with a strong final statement.

For example:
“In conclusion, abortion should remain legal because it protects women’s autonomy, reduces harm in cases of rape and poverty, and prevents the dangers of unsafe illegal procedures. Although abortion remains controversial, the decision should belong to the individual rather than the government.”

Final advice
Your essay has a clear opinion and addresses an important issue. For your next draft, focus on:
writing a clear thesis,
organizing each paragraph around one main idea,
using reliable evidence,
avoiding weak or unfair logic,
correcting grammar and word choice.

You have a strong foundation; now work on making your argument more precise, academic, and persuasive.
